# Chasing Rhinos With The Swami. Volume 2

## ... PRELUDE

I’m of two minds: should I stay in the UK and deepen my relationship with George Harrison, remain behind as his trusted guide and keep him pumped up in Krishna consciousness? Or should I take off on a wild adventure to India, a rhino-chase with spiritual master and the merry pranksters? It was no contest, really. First, it was Prabhupada’s desire that I join him, and second, we would finally see the land of Krishna, the source of the philosophy and culture that now shaped our lives. But I was in great anxiety as we prepared for the trip. Lord knows how long we’d be gone—and I was leaving George out there alone. How would he fare?

Over the past few months, George and I had had many discussions about the crowded conditions at Bury Place and “finding an ashram for the devotees.” He had experienced the pleasures of country living at Friar Park and told me he would find a similar place for the Krishnas.

When I see him in late September to say good-bye, George again promises to look for suitable properties while I’m gone—he’ll liaise with Mukunda, he says, who will be remaining in London. The Bury Place temple is in good hands with Mukunda holding down the fort.

The plan was that the American devotees would fly to London, join the European contingent, and we would all fly together to India. Mukunda and Tamal Krishna had befriended an Indian gentleman in London named Dipak Jashapara, who managed an airline (Brothers Air Services Company, or BASCO) that flew between Brussels and Bombay. It was arranged that we would all meet in Brussels on October 1, 1970, and travel together to India on Jashapara’s BASCO airlines. The fare was cheap—about £50 per person one-way.

We’re elated, and start making preparations, like getting visas and shots. Gurudas buys a slide projector. We’re allowed one suitcase each, so we pack a few clothes (no pants—dhotis only), kartals, a toothbrush, a couple of books. Malati insists we bring her giant “Prabhupada’s World Sankirtan London” banner, so we roll that up and stuff it into a suitcase. On the way to Brussels it finally sinks in: We’re on our way to India! To meet Prabhupada!

This will be the first time we are in the field with our spiritual master, together, on a big mission. We’re on safari, on the campaign trail, a maximum camp-out. In San Francisco, we lived there and the Swami came to visit. In London, we did reconnaissance, built a fort, and Prabhupada came to stay for a while and attend our programs. But in India, Srila Prabhupada will command every aspect of the expedition from day one. This is a bold new mood in our spiritual master’s ever-changing eternal play: Srila Prabhupada leading the charge, showing us how to do it. We will be an army now, with our commander-in-chief, the ultimate general, calling the shots and, like a father, caring for our everyday needs.

Prabhupada’s going global! The great international rhinochase begins!

## One

In early September 1970, in a well-coordinated attack, Palestinian guerillas hijacked four airliners from separate European and Middle Eastern airports. Three of these planes were flown to a  remote airstrip in the Jordanians desert, 425 passengers and crew were removed as hostages, and the planes destroyed by explosives. Pan Am flight 93, a New York-bound Boeing 747, was diverted to Cairo when the Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine (PFLP) terrorists aboard were told the plane was too big to land in Jordan. 175 passengers were evacuated moments before the jumbo jet exploded all over the tarmac at Cairo International Airport. Civil war broke out in Jordan, the Sixth Fleet stood off the coast of Lebanon, and President Nixon was ready to send in US bombers. The world was shocked by this huge affair.

On September 27, Egypt’s beloved President Gamal Abdel Nasser summoned the warring national leaders to Cairo to broker a settlement. By late afternoon on the 28th, the conflict was resolved, the hostages released, and Nasser went home, to die that evening of a heart attack. Egypt and much of the rest of the world went into mourning for Nasser, and on October 1, five million Egyptians cried and beat their chests as Nasser’s mortal remains were solemnly taken through the streets of Cairo. Extreme passions filled the air as Arab and non-Arab heads of state flew in for the funeral. Security at Cairo airport was at an all-time high.

On the afternoon of October 2, not many noticed the tiny, two-propeller DC-3 with BASCO markings slowly steer its way through barricades and wreckage on the runway at Cairo International, dwarfed by giant jets from almost every nation, to park in front of the multitiered transit terminal. Few remarked the small gaggle of Hare Krishna devotees that stumbled from the fuselage to emerge in unfamiliar heat, escorted by khaki-clad men armed with Kalashnikovs to a shaded compound in front of the terminal. When these strange people began clanging cymbals, clapping hands, and singing and dancing around, their guards began to pay attention. What in Allah’s name was going on? Within minutes, dozens of soldiers and policemen surrounded the robed dervishes, and hundreds of stranded passengers ran to the airport’s balconies to witness the first wave of the World Sankirtan Party—eleven souls oblivious to war and politics on a journey to the East to meet their spiritual master.

Malati: This was the first wave of Western devotees to travel to India. We were the first members of ISKCON’s World Sankirtan Party, and it was on Prabhupada’s personal order and invitation that we were together. It actually became very second nature for us to immediately begin harinam sankirtan wherever we were. That was why we were going to India; it was under the banner of the World Sankirtan Party.

Our excellent adventure had begun the week before. In the last days of September 1970, our India-bound troops, in twos and threes, had flown into cold and rainy Brussels, Belgium, from various parts of the world and found lodging in cheap hostels near the Zaventem airport. Some of us stayed at the apartment of Dipakjashapara, who managed the Brussels office of Brothers Airline Services Company (BASCO) and who was friends with the London devotees. BASCO was headquartered in Aden, in the People’s Republic of South Yemen. They operated a small fleet of battered DC-3s and DC-6s, flying cargo and a few passengers from Brussels to Bombay and points between on a rather flexible schedule, and as soon as their plane was ready, we would leave for India.

Jashapara’s apartment became the HQ for our makeshift journey. Gurudas was in charge of logistics, but there was no real plan. Prabhupada was in Calcutta, and we thought he had made arrangements for us in Bombay but weren’t really sure. We had one-way tickets (£50 each). We were on the Swami’s magic carpet ride wherever it may lead us. Surely Krishna would take care of everything.

The first morning after our arrival, Mr Jashapara, who always looked like he was in pain even when he was smiling, said, “Yes, we will certainly go, Mr. Gurudas-ji, but just now there is slight delay.” We made a couple of dry runs: we’d pack, call for taxis, then Dipak would phone and say there was some mechanical problem or the weather was bad and we’d unpack and find ways to fill our time. (We didn’t know anything about hijackings, Nasser’s death, or the explosive situation in Cairo, and Dipak didn’t mention them to us.)

Finally, early in the morning on October 2 we got the call: the BASCO flight to Bombay was leaving in two hours. A flight to Brussels from Los Angeles with four devotees aboard was delayed, so we hastily agreed that I would stay behind to guide the strays to Bombay on another BASCO flight leaving the next day, Krishna willing. I said goodbye to Malati and my two-year-old daughter, Saraswati, to my friends Gurudas, Yamuna, and Chidananda, and to the devotees I’d just met: Giriraj, Rishi Kumar, Nanda Kumar, Gopal, Aravinda, and Madri. Eleven brave souls flying off to India.

So I wasn’t there when the first batch of devotees arrived in Cairo, but later, I saw Gurudas’s four-minute 8mm movie of their reception while the BASCO flight refueled. Dozens of men surround the devotees. They’re mostly soldiers in olive-green berets or police in peaked caps or secret service guys in white dress shirts and sunglasses—each with a brush mustache and startled, puzzled looks on their faces, a mix of suspicion and curiosity. Some are laughing, white teeth showing below mustaches, as the saffron-robed and sari-clad figures raise their arms and twirl and dance to the maha-mantra. Six-foot-four Chidananda in a torn white T-shirt sets up an altar on a little table in the shade. He has pictures of Prabhupada and Radha-Krishna, a few leaves plucked from a nearby tree, some chanting beads, and a small murti of Lord Jagannath. (Jeez, in front of all these Muslims he even bows down before these heathen idols!)

Toddler Saraswati puts down her little yellow plastic bucket and rubber ball, grabs Yamuna’s hands, and they jump up and down. Onlookers discuss this strange display behind cupped hands. Hundreds of transit passengers gather above on the balconies, digging the show. A lone mullah, dressed in white, observes from a distant rooftop. And there’s mild-mannered Giriraj in his owlish glasses boldly explaining to the soldiers that Krishna is another name for Allah.

Nanda Kumar: I took a walk. I just didn’t want to watch. I took a walk around the plane, chanted to myself, prayed for protection. And when I came back around the plane I looked over. The first thing 1 saw were two machine guns leaning up against a bus and Giriraj holding one guy in each hand—and these Egyptian military guys were jumping up and down chanting Hare Krishna with big grins on their faces!

As the ecstatic devotees file back to the plane for the Cairo-Aden leg, Chidananda distributes potato-chip prasad from a little basket into the eager hands of his locked-and-loaded escorts.

Meanwhile, Durlabh, Kaushalya, Rebatinandan, and Dinanath arrived in Brussels from L.A., and we left together for Bombay on a second BASCO flight early on the morning of October 3. Our two-prop DC-3 had been stripped down for cargo, leaving only a few hard seats bolted to the floor for passengers. Boxes and bales were lashed around us and wrapped in sisal netting. Aboard there were also chickens, a goat, and a requisite fat lady in a black hijab accompanied by her screaming kids. There was no door to the cockpit, so you could watch the two pilots at work from your seat, a tinny babble of languages squawking over the radio. Tail and wing flaps were operated by mechanical cables exposed in the fuselage by our seats—you could see them moving back and forth as the pilots banked. There was drinking water and a dipper in a barrel. You did your business in a bucket behind a burlap curtain. I had never met these four California devotees, but we become friends quickly in the excitement of this unusual flight.

The first thing I noticed from my porthole window was the Swiss Alps above me as we sailed about five hundred feet over Gotthard Pass on our way up to twenty thousand feet cruising altitude. At the DC-3's top speed of 207 miles per hour, it was going to be a long trip. We landed for refueling at Cairo early in the afternoon—“Look! The pyramids!”—and were puzzled by the wreckage of a jumbo jet scattered on the airstrip. Black cloth hung from the buildings, and there was an air of intense paranoia—soldiers everywhere. Some of the guards recognized us from the same crazy group that had landed here yesterday, and they waved and said, “Hare Krishna!” But we were only five and not emboldened to mount the display of our illustrious predecessors.

We disembarked at the Aden airport late in the afternoon, stepping into blazing heat. Must have been 120°F—the tarmac burned our feet through the soles of our shoes. There was absolutely no shade, and we had to wait outside while, barrel by barrel, workers refueled the plane. Eventually, Durlabh noticed a single tall palm tree, the trunk of which cast a long thin shadow to the east, and we hurried over to stand in a row in the meager shade, chanting and laughing, hopping up and down on one leg or the other for two hours to keep our feet from scorching.

\[Six months later, BASCO airlines would be nationalized and renamed Yemen Airlines when Aden became part of the Peoples Democratic Republic of Yemen. Over the next twenty years, Yemen Airlines would suffer four hijackings, three plane crashes, and one plane shot down over Damascus.\]

Nearly twenty-four hours after leaving Brussels, in the predawn of October 4, we landed at Santacruz Airport in Bombay. The air was warm, humid, and smelled of fuel and sewage and, when the men who met us slipped garlands over our heads, the deliciously exotic aroma of tuberoses, a scent I will evermore associate with Bombay.

For the airport of a major city it was strangely empty. The jet age had not yet arrived in India, it seemed. On the way to town we passed through coconut groves, rice paddies, and sprawling shantytowns with their thousands of small bamboo huts roofed with corrugated tin and plastic sheeting. A veil of blue smoke from cooking fires hung in the air. Even at this early hour there were people everywhere—and cows—and are those water buffalo? Swarms of tiny black-and-yellow taxis beeped and swerved into every available gap in the traffic. Child beggars stood at stoplights even as the sun rose to reveal the Arabian Sea to our right. We swung around and stopped in front of the six-story Seksaria House, 74 Marine Drive, a hundred feet from the bay, in the heart of Bombay city.

India!

Our reunion with the first wave of devotees was hilarious, each of us recounting our BASCO adventures.

“But when is Srila Prabhupada coming here from Calcutta?” I asked.

“We re not sure. There’s a big conference of yogis in Bombay next week—Sadhu Samaj, or something like that—and he might come for that. We re supposed to wait here for him.”

“What’s the story with this house? Who are these people?”

Apparently, a man named Mr. Dandharia had met Prabhupada in Calcutta, and Prabhupada had asked him—and Dandharia had arranged with his uncle here in Bombay, a Mr. Kailash Seksaria—to put us up for a while. In India, wealthy families live together, generations under one roof—brothers, uncles, cousins. This was a large family of pious Krishna devotees.

“They gave us a whole floor. You won’t believe the food!”

“I dig the elevator—it’s an Otis. American, right?”

“Yeah, but Giriraj won’t use it. He thinks it’s may a. He always climbs the stairs to the third floor.”

And then breakfast arrived. We sat in rows on the marble floor of the main room as servants placed before each of us a large green leaf (“Yeah, they serve meals on banana leaves”). The women heaped rice and some kind of dumplings, pancake-looking things, and sauces in little silver dishes. (“What’s this?” “Lemon pickle,” “And this?” “Dunno. Just eat it. You’ll love it.”) At the end of the meal they gave us each a silver tumbler filled with a thick, orange juice. I took a trial sip. Oh my God, it was so good I couldn’t believe it. What is this stuff? “Mango nectar—fresh-squeezed mangoes mixed with milk.” I drank glass after glass. When the woman came around again, I fought Rebatinandan for the last drops in the pitcher.

Now we were full of good prasad and excitement. This is it! We’re in India at last, our home ground, and Prabhupada is coming. Were the World Sankirtan Party. Let’s hit the streets!

Seksaria arranged kirtan for us at the Madhavbaug temple, a Laxmi-Narayan temple in the oldest part of Bombay. Laxmi, we knew, was really Radha in Her goddess-of-fortune aspect. Laxmi was also slang for money and the source of our English word “lucky.” Narayan is an expansion of Krishna, Madhava another name for Krishna, so this should be good.

As our fleet of cars wended through the pedestrian-packed bazaars of old Bombay, we saw tiny shops and stalls selling everything under the sun: bright-colored textiles on one street, grains and nuts on another, heaps of spices to right and left. Our senses were assaulted by the noise and color, the frantic energy of an ancient land with so many people in it. We saw what we imagined was Vedic culture in action, what Prabhupada called varnashram-dharma—the division of society into four classes: there’s a. guy stripped to his loincloth, shiny with sweat, sinewy legs and back straining to push a ton of scrap metal balanced on a two-wheeled cart—he must be ashudra, the “legs” of society. Society’s belly, the vaishya class, was sitting cross-legged behind stacks of goods for sale—fat-jowled merchants, their mouths red with—“What is that stuff, Kailash?” “Paan, betel nut. It stains the teeth red.” But where were the kshatriyas, the warriors, the arms of society? We didn’t see many cops, and the few we saw didn’t look much like the warriors we’d read about in the Bhagavad-gita. And the few sadhus we saw, monks and holy men, were mostly begging. Up close we didn’t see much enlightenment. Then where was society’s head, the brahmanas? We were beginning to see for ourselves what Prabhupada had told us many times:

Because at the present moment there is a scarcity of brah-manas, spiritual guides, and kshatriyas, administrative men, and because the entire world is being ruled by shu-dras, or men of the manual laborer class, there are many discrepancies in society. It is to mitigate all these discrepancies that we have taken to this Krishna consciousness movement. If the brahmana class is actually reestablished, the other orders of social well-being will automatically follow, just as when the brain is perfectly in order, the other parts of the body, such as the arms, the belly, and the legs, all act very nicely.

When we get out of our cars before the stone portal of the Madhavbaug temple, people on the street surround us, amazed to see white sadhus. We start chanting Hare Krishna right away, dancing with glee. Our hosts then lead us into the temple. We’re joyous to see Radha and Krishna there, although saddened to see Them somewhat neglected compared to our magnificent Radha-Londonishvara. There is one cool thing though: people are taking turns tugging at a rope connected to an elaborate pulley system that moves a big sheet of canvas hung over the Deities from the ceiling as a fan. There are very few worshipers in the place, and most of them are white-haired women. What’s going on? Where are the hordes of blissed-out Vaishnavas we had expected?

And thus it begins to dawn on us, too, what Srila Prabhupada has explained to us over and over—the reason for and purpose of our mission: “Unfortunately, Indian people are so much bewildered that they are giving up their God conscious culture. They want to imitate the West. My mission was that I shall go to America, and if some of the American boys and girls, younger section, would accept it, then I’ll bring them here to show these Indians that how great their culture is. That is my policy.”

Back outside, we restarted the kirtan. We were in India just twenty-two years after Mahatma Gandhi’s assassination. Nehru’s daughter was now the Prime Minister of India, and most middleaged and older men were still dressed in traditional white cotton kurtas and dhotis, white or black Nehru caps tight on their heads. Rishi Kumar addressed the huge crowd that had gathered, telling them, in a Brooklyn accent, that “millions” of Westerners were now chanting Hare Krishna and that we’d come to India to revive Krishna consciousness in the land of Bharat. We broke off singly to talk to people and answer questions.

We were just getting into it, preaching up a storm out in the hot sun, when Seksaria’s liveried servants approached us with silver trays bearing glasses of ice-cold lemonade. Some of the people around us were in rags—we guessed they scarcely got enough to eat in a day—so we tried to ignore the proffered drinks, but the servants insisted, so most of us grabbed a glass and downed the lemonade in one gulp, hoping we were not setting a bad example. On the way back to Seksaria’s for a ten-course lunch, we discussed how to polish our act.

And so began a whirlwind of daily sankirtan around the city, mostly in what is called the Fort district. Bombay occupies a northsouth island peninsula, much like Manhattan, surrounded on three sides by the Arabian Sea. The Fort district sits at the southern tip, like the Battery in New York. The buildings in Bombay are impressive and large, but only a few stories high; the highest building in town seems to be the twelve-story Mafatlal House in Churchgate, right around the corner from Seksaria’s place. We staged rousing sankirtan in places like Crawford Market, Elphinstone Circle, Flora Fountain, Colaba, Zaveri Bazar, and caught the flavor of post-colonial Bombay.

The British created Bombay from seven islands back in the mid-1700s to serve as their main port for trade from the West. Although the city seemed old to us, it wasn’t really ancient India. In 1970, just twenty-three years after the British had left, Bombay still had the ambience of an English city: Cooperage Road, Garden Road, Rope Walk Lane, and Forbes Street; grand buildings like the High Court and Victoria Terminus train station; and open parks like Oval Maidan, Brabourne Cricket Grounds, and Mahalaxmi Race Course (where thoroughbreds still ran). Wide sidewalks lined the streets, shaded by porticoes and mature trees—giant banyans, mango and jackfruit trees, coconut palms, blue-flowered jacarandas, yellow flames, and fragrant frangipanis.

Prabhupada had told us that in ancient times, Indian kings had ruled the planet. Vedic culture had spread to every corner of Asia, Europe, and even the Americas. Since time immemorial, India was known to the world as a place of great riches. Columbus was trying to find India when he accidentally came to the Americas. Alexander the Great invaded India in 326 BC, and subsequent waves of Muslim and British armies had plundered India and stripped it of its treasures. Timur, the Mongol conqueror, sacked Delhi for gold, gems, and elephants in 1398. Nader Shah came from Persia with his legions in 1739 and looted the Peacock Throne, the most precious single object on the planet with its two tons of gold embedded with thousands of rubies, emeralds, diamonds, and pearls. We could see in Bombay’s bazaars the faint echo of such wealth. There were blocks of vendors selling only perfumes and rare oils, and in Zaveri Bazar there were hundreds of shops promising jewels and gold behind barred and gated doors. It was still here, that fabulous wealth, but more cautious now after millennia of plunder.

Bombay was a maze of marketplaces, small streets and alleys, diagonals and dead ends, linked by a few long wide avenues streaming in all directions. The roads swarmed with pedestrians, bicycles, horse- and hand-drawn carts, rickshaws, and the occasional elephant or cow. Motorized traffic was light, mostly fourseater 1964 Fiat 1100s, manufactured in India, with a sprinkling of larger Ambassadors (modeled on the 1956 Morris Oxford), and a few big ’50s American cars.

Huge billboards were mounted all over Bombay, advertising items like “Cinthol, India’s Only Deodorant Soap (Hurry While Stocks Last!),” “HERO Deluxe Mopeds, with 6 Months Guarantee,” “Kohinoor Vest, an Ornament for the Manly Chest,” “Chandan Wardrobes, the Wedding Gift That Shows Daddy Cares,” “Regent King Cigarettes, Moments of Happiness’”—and Bollywood films like Johny Mera Naam and even American films like Mackenna’s Gold, starring Gregory Peck. Seeing these billboards, we realized what Prabhupada had told us: India had slipped from the Vedic standard and was now very much into the Western concept of “I am this body.”

We were on fire with preaching zeal. Prabhupada had come to change all this, and we had come to help him! So from the first day of our arrival our mission was fixed: demonstrate to India the glories of their own Krishna culture, as taught to us by their native son, A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami. We got the sense that Vedic society was still just under the surface and that it wouldn’t take much to convince these people, who after all were born into this ancient tradition. We could see that most Indians, whether Muslim, Christian, Parsee, Jain, Sikh, or Hindu, were still largely guided by spiritual values, and we saw proof of this everywhere—on calendars, storefronts, and the altars that decorated every home and business. We saw some images of Krishna, Rama, some of Laxmi, but most people seemed to be worshiping Shiva, Ganesh, Kali, and Durga. We knew there must be Vaishnavas in Bombay—and we longed to meet them.

Yamuna: The center of our activity every single day was nagar-sankirtan. That was our main engagement—sometimes twice a day. This kirtan party was so wonderful. We were like a family, a tight family, and each person played a role. The heartbeat of our kirtan was the mridangas of Dinanath and Rishi Kumar—they kept the rhythm going. And we were shoulder to shoulder as godbrothers and godsisters with so much affection and respect for each other. We felt as if Srila Prabhupada was our father and we were his spiritual children, and there was such a nice mood in this early sankirtan leg of Srila Prabhupadas mission in India.

**\* \* \***

We were immersed in a day-and-night round of street sankirtan, lavish meals at important homes, and public programs. We didn’t have books to distribute, and we didn’t have much personal knowledge of the scriptures, only a basic grasp of the philosophy. When we spoke, therefore, it was always simple: “I am not this body but pure spirit soul. Krishna is the Supreme Personality of Godhead.” That Krishna was the Supreme Person seemed to be a new idea to many of the people we preached to—India used to know it, but the people seemed to have forgotten it. It was a simple truth to prove:

“You are following Bhagavad-gita, correct?”

“Yes.”

“Who is speaking Bhagavad-gita?”

“Krishna.”

“It takes a person to speak something, right?

“Yes.”

“Then Krishna is a person.”

“Yes.”

We each went out to buy chaadars, big Indian shawls you could use as blankets, and this gave us our first taste of bargaining in Indian shops. The guys from the East Coast were better at it. To me, the prices were already so ridiculously cheap compared to what you’d pay in the US that I usually just paid whatever the shop owner asked.

Srila Prabhupada was in Calcutta, on the other side of India. An express letter from him arrived at Seksaria’s for Gurudas: “Please accept my blessings to you all. You are welcome in India. Now my going to Bombay is postponed and my enclosed letter will speak for itself. Now if your preaching work in Bombay is going nicely, then you can stay there for some time and do preaching work as they are doing here in Calcutta. Otherwise, you can immediately come here and work jointly.” Enclosed with this letter was the carbon copy of another letter Prabhupada had sent to organizers of the Bharatiya Sadhu Samaj, a major conference of holy men that would be held in Bombay in a few days’ time, which read: “Please accept my greetings. In continuance of my telegram dated October 2nd, 1970, and letter dated October 3rd, 1970, I beg to inform you that although my party from London has reached there in Bombay, I am unable to join you there in Bombay because there was no arrangement for my passage. Therefore, if my disciples there can do the work, then I do not require to go to Bombay leaving aside my important engagements here.”

Oh,. no! They hadn’t sent Prabhupada a plane ticket! Immediately, by phone and telegram, we and our Bombay hosts conveyed to Srila Prabhupada what was going on in Bombay, including the excitement and positive response to our arrival, and urged him to come. We also sent him a plane ticket. He couldn’t resist. The next day we received a second telegram telling us that he would be flying to Bombay the day after tomorrow, on October 8, in the late afternoon. We were so excited we could barely sleep.

Prabhupada had very much wanted to open a branch of ISKCON in his native Calcutta. In September he had written to Gargamuni, “We shall open a very gorgeous branch in Calcutta. Calcutta is my birthplace, so all of you as my disciples do something unique in Calcutta. That will be fitting service to your Spiritual Master.” Prabhupada had gone to Calcutta with two American disciples back in 1967. He’d left Achyutananda and Ramanuja—joined byjayapataka in 1968—to establish ISKCON in this capital of Gaudiya Vaishnavism. Prabhupada had returned to Calcutta in August 1970 with another five Americans, but despite their best efforts they were unable to gain much of a foothold there. The disciples moved around town from one small apartment to another, looked at several big buildings, but negotiations for space and land were slow. Prabhupada wrote to one of his godbrothers, “Unfortunately they are loitering in the streets of Calcutta without having a suitable place.” So when Prabhupada received our news that Bombay was very receptive, perhaps he thought it better to get something big going elsewhere in India, then return to Calcutta in force later.

Prabhupada is coming! We made a plan: there wasn’t enough time to get many devotees mobilized to go to the airport, so Kailash and I would go out to Santacruz in two cars to meet Srila Prabhupada and escort him back to Marine Drive. The devotees would greet him en masse on the sidewalk in front of Seksaria’s house. Gurudas would stay behind to organize the reception.

**\* \* \***

I hadn’t seen Prabhupada since December 1969—almost ten months earlier. This would be a bigger, more important Prabhupada, one who had dozens of temples and now maybe five hundred disciples. Would he still notice me? Kid around with me? Or would he be distant, so busy now that I could not approach? I could never be anything like Srila Prabhupada—so pure and surrendered, so in touch with Krishna—but I was convinced I had never done anything so exciting and meaningful in my life, and that if there was any hope at all for me, I had to continue on this path, working as hard as I could to spread Krishna consciousness in the world. My strategy to achieve this was simple: I would try to stay as physically close to Prabhupada as possible, hover in his presence. Who knows? Maybe if I work hard enough at this, I might develop a tiny spark of love for Lord Krishna.

And then, there he isl Floating down the airplane’s steps I see a new version of our spiritual master: the maharaja, at home, king of all he surveys, the owner of India: head held high, effulgent, unhurried, serious, and dignified. And there’s Tamal Krishna, following him down the stairs.

I place a garland around Prabhupada’s neck and he is glad to see me. The intimacy is still there. I say quickly, “The devotees are waiting for you in Bombay.” Our host’s Ambassador, strung with marigolds and mango leaves, waits by the plane like a wedding car. Prabhupada sits in front, and Tamal and I jump in the back.

“Heard you got hitched to a California girl, Tamal,” I whisper.

“Yeah, Madri and I got married in France last March. But I don’t know...”

All the way into town I watch the back of Prabhupada’s effulgent, newly-shaved head bobble with the potholes—his proud profile as he half turns to question or laugh as we regale him with the huge possibilities of preaching in Bombay. I watch the lightning-bolt vessels at his temples as they shoot back from his eyes and feel the coolness and calm that surrounds him even in this stuffy car. The fragrance from his tuberose garlands is intoxicating. His presence sends tingles of delight through my shoulders. How could anyone be this gorgeous?

“Prabhupada, there is so much wealth in this town. They say that Bombay pays 80 percent of India’s taxes. We are meeting so many big men here, and they have a deep desire to serve Krishna. They have a sweet, almost innocent love for the devotees. They are accustomed to think and do big things. Accomplishment is a way of life here, and dreams are made every day in Bombay like nowhere else in India. It’s like a cross between New York and Los Angeles—India’s financial and entertainment centers rolled into one.” Yes, Prabhupada’s going with the idea now: “Forget Calcutta for the time being. Let us focus on Bombay.” I somehow get the idea that this was Prabhupada’s plan all along.

Prabhupada knows about Bombay, of course. He had a business here once, and he collected his passage for America from these very streets. On Peddar Road, as we pass Kemps Corner, he points to the left and says, “Just down there, Grant Road—my office was there, next to Multani temple. It was not a very nice district.”

Bunched on the sidewalk in front of the Seksaria’s house the crowd of about fifty devotees and Bombay well-wishers erupts into thunderous kirtan, blocking the traffic on Marine Drive. The “Prabhupada’s World Sankirtan” banner is flying and, as Prabhupada steps out of the car, everyone leaps and bangs kartals and drums and shouts “Hare Krishna!” Garland after garland is offered and settles into a heap around his neck, and he looks each of his disciples in the eyes and, without speaking, welcomes each of us to India.

After Prabhupada gets settled, the devotees gather around him in his first-floor apartment. All his windows are open and warm, gentle breezes waft in from the Arabian Sea, tousling the hairs of his tiny sikha. He’s glowing and relaxed. We’ve been told that the monsoon season is nearly over, but today clouds gather and a light rain threatens. Outside, we hear the occasional beep-beep from a passing taxi. The pressure is off now: our father, best friend, the meaning of our lives—he’s back with us. How great he looks! Bits of sandalwood paste and red kumkum cling to his unlined brow. He looks healthy and strong, but thinner than I remember him, slimmed down for action, playful, barking orders, in total command. His hooded eyes are mellow and satisfied and moving over us one by one with obvious pleasure.

“So? What is your idea, this Bombay city?”

“It’s not so much different than Los Angeles, Srila Prabhupada—everyone very busy to make money.”

“Yes. We can practically see. Everyone is working with his body in Bombay city. A very poor man is also in Bombay city, and a very rich man is also there. Both of them have the same facilities to work, but we find that one man is working very hard day and night. Hardly he is getting his morsel of food. Another man, simply by going, sitting in the office, earning thousands and thousands. Why? Because the difference of the field of activities. The bodies are different. Because one has got a certain type of body, his destination is already there. Karmana daiva-netrena jantur dehopapattaye. We can study this thing—that somebody is living in a poor slum and another man is living in a very palatial building. Therefore the body is made according to our past karma, and that is called destiny. Your happiness and distress according to the body is already settled up. It is not possible by natural way to improve or dis-improve it. It is already settled up. Therefore shastra says, tal labhyate duhkhavad anyatah sukham. Happiness derived from sense enjoyment can be obtained automatically in course of time, just as we obtain miseries even though we do not desire them.

“People in Calcutta and Bombay also do this every day. They work very hard in the office or factory, and again they spend three or four hours in transportation returning home. Then they retire at ten o’clock and again rise early in the morning to go to their offices and factories. This kind of hard labor is described in the shastras as the life of pigs and stool-eaters. Nayam deho deha-bhajam nriloke kashtan kaman arhate vid-bhujam ye: ‘Of all living entities who have accepted material bodies in this world, one who has been awarded this human form should not work hard day and night simply for sense gratification, which is available even for dogs and hogs that eat stool.’”

Prabhupada. Always keeping things in perspective and enabling us to see the world through his eyes of rare wisdom, spoiling forever our impulse to gratify the body’s senses. Next ,he tells us that in earlier times, the maharajas would keep elephants as a symbol of their great wealth. “And above all precious elephants, the white elephant was supreme. When a maharaja happened to find an English wife, they used to say he was keeping a ‘white elephant.’ Now you American boys and girls, you are my white elephants. Everyone is seeing my dancing white elephants.” We roar with laughter—but the name sticks: Prabhupada’s dancing white elephants.

“And you, Miss Saraswati, you are happy here in India?” She’s two years old now, and shy, but honors him above all others. Then, “Achaa. So what is our program?” Prabhupada is ready to go. Let’s take this town to pieces.

The next morning, Friday, October 9, the English-language newspaper Times of India, with five million readers, reports on page 7:

U.S. ‘HARE KRISHNA’ CULT MEMBERS IN CITY

The group of Americans, including women with babes in arms, who have been attracting the attention of people in Bombay by chanting “Hare Krishna” on the streets, belong to the International Society for Krishna Consciousness and have been specially flown to Bombay to participate in the seventh all-India conference of the Bharat Sadhu Samaj, to be held in the city from Sunday. Nearly 500 delegates are expected to participate in the conference, which will discuss “moral problems.” Many of the “sadhus” from abroad have adopted the dress and hair arrangement of Indian pundits.

On the night of Saturday, October 10, Kailash hosts an inaugural meeting on the spacious open roof atop Seksaria Bhavan. Maybe two hundred people (“Check that out, Tamal, some of these guys are in suits and ties!”) are seated in rows of rented chairs. Our devotees sit cross-legged on oriental rugs spread out in front. Prabhupada faces us, sitting very straight on an ornate, high-backed, but narrow armchair, his feet not touching the floor. Bhaktivedanta Swami is formally introduced to Bombay’s finest, and he gives his usual hard-hitting speech: “Just see the results for yourselves. Please help us spread this Krishna culture. It is your duty.” Chidananda speaks, then Yamuna speaks, and then our amplified kirtan blasts long and ecstatic over rooftops from Churchgate to Nariman Point. After kirtan, the chairs are folded, and tables laden with sumptuous food appear, the guests mingling with devotees as a hundred questions are asked and answered.

The next morning, the Sunday Times announces our presence on page 11 with a photo of Madri, Yamuna, and Malati dancing on the street and Saraswati clowning for the camera from her pram:

Is it a stunt or an elaborate leg-pull? The sceptics of the Krishna cult are legion. But watching members of the cult at close quarters, following every movement of theirs and getting to know their philosophy and sentiments, one cannot but conclude that they are a very serious lot. A group of Americans, including women with babes in arms, belonging to the International Society for Krishna Consciousness (ISKCON), has been moving around Bombay during the past few days, chanting Hare Krishna Hare Krishna Krishna Krishna Hare Hare, to the accompaniment of cymbals and drums. \[They\] evoke in the spectator a sense of incredulity. Can the materialistic West... have turned at last to embrace the spiritualism of the East?

I met several of the Kirtan-chanting Americans (who have come here to attend the seventh all-India conference of the Bharat Sadhu Samaj which begins here today) and was at once struck by their sincerity and utter surrender to the cult they have adopted. The Vaishnavas of Mathura could not be so guileless, I thought, as this band of Bhakti enthusiasts.

It’s October 11, 1970, and now the party really begins. A fence of colorful cloth surrounds a huge area of white sand on Chowpatty Beach, a mile up Marine Drive from Seksaria Bhavan. Above the elaborate stage, banners proclaim “The 7th Annual All-India Conference, Bharatiya Sadhu Samaj” in English, Marathi, and Hindi. We don’t know much about spiritual politics in India. Prabhupada has told us that some “so-called yogis and swamis” are “impersonalist rascals,” selfish and vain, and that they are responsible more than anyone else for India’s increasing corruption. He says that we should go down there, have sankirtan, and that he will speak on the fifteenth—four days hence—on the last night of the Samaj. We are gun-shy to see so many big, big gurus up on the stage or seated in front in their saffron, white, and yellow outfits. We note the variety of tilak markings on their foreheads, and that some have shaved heads and others long, matted hair. We see skinny, semi-naked babus smeared with ash and carrying tridents and staffs (“Wow, these must be the holy men we’ve heard so much about!”), and we feel as we sit on the sand that every eye in the place is on us.

Gujarat governor Mr. Shriman Narayan opens the conference: “India can never achieve peace and prosperity if it pursues only material progress.” He calls for a revival of spiritual values to “fight the forces of evil.” Sounds good to us. Other men speak in Hindi or other tongues, and we don’t understand much. Those of us from London are used to playing to big crowds—screaming fans ar rock concerts—so when it’s our turn, we overcome our shyness and spring into kirtan, gradually building the tempo as thousands of amazed onlookers join in, some even rising to dance.

October 11 is Ramachandra Vijayotsava, celebrating Lord Rama’s victory over the demon Ravana, so some of us stay behind at the beach to watch the fireworks that follow the main event.

Malati: They had one firework, display that replicated Sita, Ram, and Hanuman. Another showed Krishna and Arjuna on their chariot. It was the first time any of us had witnessed such spectacular fireworks.

The reporters on deadline rush to file their stories, and on the morning of October 12 we read in the Times of India: “A lively feature of the function was the rendering of ‘kirtan’ by 14 American sadhus belonging to the Bhakti cult, who chanted ‘Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna, Krishna, Hare Hare’.”

That’s all. There’s no mention of Bhaktivedanta Swami, just his dancing white elephants.

Over the next few days we read in newspapers that the Bharatiya Sadhu Samaj had passed resolutions urging “noninterference by the government and political parties in the affairs” of mathas (monasteries) and mandirs (temples) and a ban on cow slaughter. Religion and politics seemed inextricably alloyed in India: “Presiding over the subjects committee meeting of the conference, Swami Akhandananda Saraswati called for a United front of Sadhus for the cause of nation-building.”

Meanwhile, Kailash and Gurudas organize a press conference, to be held in Prabhupada’s room the evening of October 13. Prabhupada tells the twenty-five reporters who attend:

Human society is no longer bounded by geographical limits. Just like we are traveling all over the world—not only once, but twice, thrice in a year. You can go to London from Bombay within nine hours. And practically, while I am in India, all my disciples are coming here from different parts of the world. There are facilities. So these, my disciples, practically millions of people in Western countries, are chanting Hare Krishna and taking up this, your Indian spiritual culture. Now you have forgotten. You want to be like them—after money and motorcars. In one thousand years you cannot have motorcars like they have got. Now they want Krishna; that is all. They want your spiritual treasure house, which you have neglected. This is your wealth, Bhagauad-gita. Why you do not give?

Then, on a rickety projector, Durlabh shows an 8mm silent film of the 1970 Ratha-yatra festival in San Francisco, with Gurudas narrating. Crossing a white sheet on the wall rippled by a ceiling fan, the reporters see the huge rath car, Srila Prabhupada sitting proudly in the center, Lord Jagannath above and behind him, swirling devotees, thousands of Americans celebrating this most ancient and revered of India’s pageants, and they are stunned. But again, the press is intrigued more by the novelty of Western sadhus, and we read this article in the Times on the morning of October 15:

U.S. MILLIONAIRE’S SON SEEKS SOLACE IN KRISHNA

Giriraj, the 23-year-old son of a U.S. millionaire, told newsmen in Bombay on Wednesday that he had renounced his father’s riches to seek solace in the Krishna Consciousness movement. The bespectacled youth, who has forgotten his Christian name, is among the group of 15 people who are now in Bombay to attend an all-India conference of the Bharatiya Sadhu Samaj.

The leader of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness is Swami Bhaktivedantaji Prabhupada. The Swami told newsmen that he had set up his headquarters in Los Angeles to spread the movement at the behest of his guru. The Swami said he was trying to open branches in the Soviet Union also. To the question “who financed his movement,” he said, “Lord Krishna.”

Late that same afternoon, Prabhupada accompanies us over to the Sadhu Samaj on Chowpatty Beach. Despite our hosts’ protests, he insists that his devotees sit onstage with him. “These Western Vaishnavas are every bit as spiritually advanced as the other yogis and swamis here. I want them up here with me,” he says, and the festival organizers finally agree. As we file onstage, all but one of the other yogis and swamis reluctantly leaves—there’s just not enough room. Prabhupada introduces us to India as authentic yogis. He is proud of us. Fie is showing us his country and at the same time showing his countrymen what he has been doing in the West. Our spiritual master is perched stage right in the same gilded, red-velvet chair that he’d sat in at Seksaria Bhavan, his disciples around him, with his books and photos of his gurus prominently displayed in a row on a white cloth below him. More than ten thousand citizens are seated in chairs or standing on the vast enclosed beach. Vertical fluorescent tube lights blink on as dusk settles.

Prabhupada starts right in with a kirtan. It heats up quickly and we begin to dance around the stage: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, buwump, buwump ...

Devananda Swami, Kartikeya Swami, and Bruce, a Japanese-Canadian boy, had just arrived by train from Calcutta. Now there are more than twenty of us. Imagine how the scene must have looked to thousands of Bombay citizens at a time when the West was practically unknown to India: four beautiful American women dressed in bright saris; a bubbling, blue-eyed, two-year-old shouting hariboll and bouncing in her mother’s arms; a handsome African-American, his saffron flowing against his dark skin as he pounds the drum with both arms and throws his head back in ecstasy; sixteen tall, Western men, confident, devout, obviously intelligent and well-educated, two carrying the staffs of swami initiation; ajapanese boy, serene and Buddhalike. This Bhaktivedanta Swami has done something no one else has done, for sure.

Malati and a few devotees jump off the stage—there’s not enough space for all of us to dance. Then Prabhupada beckons us all to get off the stage and he comes down too. We hit the sand and spontaneously dance up the aisles, urging the seated audience to join us—and they do. Thousands rise and chant Hare Krishna. In glee and surprise they leap into the aisles and dance, many weeping openly to see their way of life so joyously embraced, all bodily distinctions erased. Prabhupada dances and claps his hands in front of the stage, raising his arms in the air, and we’re transported to Lord Chaitanya’s time, when even the deer and the tigers danced. Dinanath and Rishi Kumar bring the kirtan to a rousing crescendo. And then we stop. No one is sure what’s next, were all so filled with timeless bliss.

After a moment we file back up onto the stage and the crowd settles. Something has changed in the atmosphere. Prabhupada sits before the “Chicago Radio” microphone and begins speaking in English, because “otherwise my students will not understand.” He keeps it short and simple, talking about how many Westerners are hungry for India’s knowledge, so “Now you please help me spread this Krishna consciousness movement.” His disciples’ presence speaks volumes.

But there’s obviously tension among the Sadhu Samaj organizers. They want us out of there so they can go on with their dry speculation and talk of social reforms. So we hustle away, leading sankirtan all the way down Marine Drive to Seksaria Bhavan. Bombay came to hear the yogis speak, and here was Prabhupada and his dancing white sadhus stealing the show. No one could put on a show like we could. Nobody even came close.

A reporter for the Times of India Weekly said of this October 15 meeting:

The magic of Krishna’s name has reached millions of young Americans groping for a way out of the morass of materialism. A group of these idealists is now in this country to gain first-hand experience of the land of the Lord’s birth.

Low clouds threatened a downpour. It had already begun to drizzle and many in the 10,000-strong crowd on Bombay’s Chowpatty Beach were on their feet. For five consecutive nights they had listened to heady spiritual oratory and now the all-India session of the Bharatiya Sadhu Samaj was heading for a tame end. An elderly sadhu announced a half-hour program of sankirtan “by our brothers and sisters from across the sea.”

It was indeed a memorable farewell to the rain. A group of twenty Americans, members of the Hare Krishna delegation, took over the dais. The air was filled with the beating of mrdangas, the clash of cymbals and the music of the Mahamantra...

The falterers returned to their seats. The drizzle had now become a welcome accompaniment to the devotional music. The huge crowd joined in the chanting. Even the policemen succumbed to the Hare Krishna magic. There were also some in the press enclosure who were not immune to the atmosphere. One greying reporter whom I had always regarded as a particularly unsentimental person said to me in an emotion-packed voice: “Do you realize what is happening? Very soon Vaishnavism is going to sweep the West. The Hare Krishna movement will compensate for all our loss at the hands of the padres through the centuries.”

**\* \* \***

That night, after the meeting at Chowpatty Beach, Prabhupada held court in his room. His windows were again open to the fresh sea breeze, and we could hear cars swooshing by on the wet pavement outside. The devotees were gathered around their general, reliving his latest victory.

Prabhupada said, “This Bombay, they are mad after money and cinema, but if you scratch the surface you will find devotees—so many devotees. They have been misled. Baba-this and Pandit-that—so many so-called gurus are coming here for raising money in Bombay. You have remarked? ‘You just give me ten rupees and I will make you God!’ \[Everyone howls with laughter.\] ‘Yes, you come, and if you have got money, we shall also take it.’ ” \[More howling.\]

Serious and intense in public, Prabhupada let his hair down (so to speak) with us in his rooms. Sometimes when you stared at Srila Prabhupada’s face, it seemed to change or morph from one person—the man we knew so well—to another, say, a youth—or for a flickering moment a beautiful woman—then back again, lifting the veil to his familiar beckoning grin as if to say, “You see, I am not this body.”

He quoted shastra one moment and told a joke the next. Laughter was our constant companion around Prabhupada—joy in this wild adventure of devotional service. Life with Krishna is fun, playful, and full of juice, Prabhupada often told us, and it certainly seemed like the characters and events we experienced in India were straight from a cosmic comedy. “Nothing is accidental,” he said. “It is Krishna’s play,” and to us it seemed so funny.

Then Prabhupada surprised us by telling us that twelve years ago, in 1958, he had refused to speak at a similar, weeklong “Bhagawat Week” on the very same Chowpatty Beach, because “these sacred topics should be discussed only by liberated persons and not by pretentious, mundane pseudoswamis.”

Prabhupada continued, “Now one thing: they will see you on the street chanting and dancing and they will think, ‘Oh, they are just like all other sadhus, begging for attention, some money.’ No, in India now it has come like this, chanting on the street we will seem like beggars. Best thing is we approach big men: ‘Krishna has given you everything. You have got nice house, nice business. Now you give something to Krishna. We have got so many books. We have got so many temples to build. Why you don’t join us for your life? No one else is doing, showing the greatness of India’s spiritual culture to the world. Become a Life Member, take our books, help us build temples around the world, and when you go there, anywhere, you stay in our temple as honored guest.’”

Then Prabhupada revealed his latest battle plan, holding up a handwritten schematic for what he called the “Life Member Program.” We passed this paper around, and his simple idea sunk in. It’s a stroke of genius, this idea to enroll influential Bombay citizens as Life Members. From a small core group of prominent Life Members, their friends would join and the program would expand exponentially; books would get distributed and a strong base would be built for spreading Krishna consciousness in India. An electric idea. The World Sankirtan Party was evolving to a new strategy. Devananda would type up Prabhupada’s schematic and give us each a copy. Prabhupada had done it again.

That night, I lay on my straw mat surrounded by godbrothers in various stages of sleep or near-sleep. The streetlight outside on Marine Drive cast palm-frond shadows around the darkened room. You could hear the faint Minnie-Mouse squealing of Hindi film music from a late-night kulft stand down the road, low voices talking somewhere on the seaside promenade, a passing bicycle bell.

“Psst! Tamal, you still awake?”

“Yeah.”

“Good plan, huh, this Life Membership deal?”

“Yeah. Go to sleep.”

“How much is 1,111 rupees?”

Grnnnn...

“OK, then, Hare Krishna.” Before sleep, though, I worked out that 1,111 rupees was about $140. Hmmm.

We received copies of the Life Membership plan the next day:

(A)    LIFE MEMBER receives all our books thus far published as well as all future publications, as well as a lifetime subscription to BACK TO GODHEAD. He is also free to visit any of our temples, take prasadam there, and stay there if he likes. Price is 1111 rupees.

(B)    DONOR MEMBER receives all our literature thus far published as well as a lifetime subscription to BTG. Price is 555 rupees.

(C) SUBSCRIPTION MEMBER receives a lifetime subscription to BTG. Price is 222 rupees.

(D) COMMON MEMBER receives a one-year subscription to BTG. Price is 33 rupees.

**\* \* \***

Kailash Seksaria became the first Life Member. Then he arranged a get-together with his important friends to meet Prabhupada one on one in order to make them Life Members. Other prominent Bombay men signed up too: G. D. Somani, Hari Krishna Agarwal, Harlal Gupta, Mohanlal Khetan.

Right away, Srila Prabhupada wanted to register The International Society for Krishna Consciousness as a charitable trust in India and, with the help of his new Life Members, he drew up a Memorandum of Association listing, at the bottom, the “names, addresses and occupations of the members of the first Bureau of the Society” as follows: A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami (Sanyasi); Shyam Sundar das Adhikary (Architect); Tamal Krishna das Adhikary (Composer); Gurudas Adhikary (Teacher); Rishi Kumar das Brahmachary (Economist), all of us living at “74, Marine Drive, Bombay-20”.

The next few days were a blur. We attended three, four, even five programs a day—at “Shreeniwas House,” the corporate headquarters of the Somani family industries, or in a schoolroom with children or an auditorium full of older women. Wherever we went, Prabhupada made a short and very humble appeal, showed the audience his books, and prepped them for the Life Member program soon to be developed. “Dante nidhaya trinakam padayor nipatya” he said, “you are very learned and intelligent and pious people. I stand with a straw in my teeth and I beg you to please chant Hare Krishna.”

He was dynamic, in a hurry as he walked majestically to the dais, spoke, and quickly exited, his white elephants dancing in his wake. His hosts held open the door to his Ambassador and he was off to the next gig.

Saraswati’s job at these gatherings was to sing and dance, and Prabhupada carried her in his car—often she sat on his lap—to all the programs. She broke the ice at the meetings, melted India’s heart, and she was soon the darling of Bombay.

All of us were developing a real fondness for these Indian people—you might even say we were starting to love them. Their endearing qualities and gentle manners were such a far cry from Western skepticism and rude, me-first behavior that sometimes it felt like we had arrived from the future into a past when people were more innocent, connected by social graces, and showed their thanks by giving small gifts.

But as young Americans, we often shared the joy at our own way of life by making jokes that sometimes bordered on the sacrilegious: “Hey, Gurudas, I found out what that red stuff is in all the stairwells. You know, in those office buildings?”

“Yeah?”

“It’s spit, man. These guys spit betel nut juice on the way up and down the stairs ’cause it’s dark in the corners!”

Or, “Hey, I met a guy today. Says the Indian army has invented an atomic hand grenade. I said, ‘Well, how far can you throw it?”’

Or, “D’you see all those vultures circling around that hill? They say the Parsee people put their dead in a big pit up there and let the vultures eat ’em.”

Or, “Have you seen those sadhus over by Crawford Market? One guy’s had his arms raised for years. He never sits down and he’s got legs like an elephant. You throw him coins in a little pot. Man, it’s a tough way to make a living!”

Fascinated by the exoticism of India, I wrote my first aerogram to George Harrison in London: “The Swami is with us in Old Bom-bay, gateway to our Motherland! Keeping us secure and cared for in this most foreign of all lands, he is our dearest Friend, praising our little accomplishments arid always ready with a joke to keep us laughing and merry—Swamiji: our Commander-in-Chief, ordering us into battle—our Chairman-of-the-Board, meeting the people of Bombay and teaching us the arts of strategy, diplomacy, and negotiation—our Tour Guide, ‘Yes, this was the British High Court, over there where Gandhi lived, and here I had my shop selling tonics’—Prabhupada, the Master Manager, managing the tiny strengths of a handful of inexperienced 20-year-olds into a potent small army, storming the beach, the Swami and. his rag-tag band of White Elephants, your friendly invaders.”

**\* \* \***

Prabhupada had been in Bombay for only a few days, but he was taking the town by storm. It seemed like almost every minute some new opportunity would appear, and Srila Prabhupada was flooded by ideas—and offers—how to expand his India program. India’s most famous Bollywood star, Dev Anand, came to see Srila Prabhupada, and photos of Anand posing with devotees were plastered all over the film magazines. There was no fixed plan—at least we couldn’t see one. Everything seemed totally spontaneous, impromptu, improvised. We left all decisions in Prabhupada’s hands. “Any place, big or small, I am like a cow. I just give milk,” he told us.

Sometimes he asked one of us to speak, catching us off guard. There might be five thousand people out there, but he would suddenly turn and say, “Shyamasundar, you will say something?” The general was training his hesitant, introverted officers, forcing us to the front lines.

Prabhupada’s success in mobilizing the pious people of Bombay was instant and overwhelming. By October 21, Prabhupada was writing letters to devotees like Hamsaduta in Calcutta: “Regarding our Indian centers, it is already decided that in Bombay we must have a center first and negotiation is going on seriously. If we cannot open many centers in India, at least one center must be opened in Bombay before we go back to the States.”

Kaushalya: Srila Prabhupada taught us how to drink, water without touching the glass with our lips and how to peel bananas with our teeth—that is, without using our left hand. He said, “Everyone will be watching you very carefully, so you should do these things nicely.” He was tireless. He went from engagement to engagement to engagement, and then in the evenings he gave more public lectures, and every morning we got up early and had arati and morning class on the Bhagavatam in his room. We did that wherever we were.

Yes, this was different—it was a traveling show now, unlike in the past where we took one-day excursions with Srila Prabhupada, then returned to home base. No, this was a roadshow; we had no fixed abodes. We were on the move, a gypsy caravan, a moveable feast around Bombay, with nothing but day-to-day planning.

Kailash Seksaria took lavish care of us, but we knew his largesse could not last. Soon, three things happened: (1) some of us moved with Prabhupada into Mr. Lalan’s house, just off Nepean Sea Road on Malabar Hill; (2) organizers for the Vedanta Sammelan, another weeklong conference of holy men, invited Prabhupada and some of the devotees, all expenses paid, to Amritsar, twelve hundred miles north—nearly two days by train; Prabhupada accepted, and tickets were booked to leave Bombay on October 19; and (3) a man named Manoharlal Agarwal offered to house Prabhupada and his disciples on the premises of his Sita-Rama temple in Chembur, a northern Bombay suburb.

I liked Bombay a lot. I felt at home here. Bombay was much like a time-capsule mix of 1950s Los Angeles and London. There was a wonderful, old-time nostalgia in the bustling bazaars and shady alcoves, where mold and mildew clung to many buildings. I felt like I’d been here before. Bombay was the leading city in India, with its classy, moneyed people and perfect winter weather—a place where dreams were built. As Americans, we had some advanced knowledge about how the material world worked. In that respect we were way ahead of these people. Products for which manufacturing was already mature in the West—simple things like making razor blades or radios—were still in their infancy in India. (And I admit, as children of capitalism, we were always thinking, “Hey, I could get rich here if I opened a business. With a billion customers, I could make Frosted Flakes or sneakers, spray deodorant—even hula hoops—and be in on the ground floor of this new economy.”)

I could see another big rhinoceros coming up; I could see that Prabhupada was sorting out who would lead the various projects unfolding in India. I wanted to stay and serve in Bombay, this city I’d come to love, so I decided to try to impress Prabhupada with how good a job I could do here.

Tamai Krishna thought a lot like me, and he liked ambitious projects. His forceful energy could help create something grand in Bombay. On the eve of Prabhupada’s departure to Amritsar, Tamai and I met with him and suggested we stay behind in Bombay. “We have these new Life Members here, and someone has to collect their checks. We have all these new introductions—potential Life Members—to follow up on and to keep the ball rolling. And we need to find a new place for you to live for when you come back.”

Prabhupada agreed, so Tamal and I stayed behind with our wives and Saraswati, sad at separating from our master and our dearest friends, but knowing that Prabhupada would be more pleased by our active service in Bombay. He had enough elephants for his roadshow.

**\* \* \***

On October 19, Prabhupada and twelve devotees departed by train from Bombay’s Victoria Terminus, bound for Amritsar. After they left, Tamai and I wandered the streets of the Fort District, the dark, cool, arcades of Bora Bazar, Kalbadevi, Marine Lines, giving ourselves a crash course in navigating the byzantine warrens of old Bombay. Some streets were still known by their English names, some had unpronounceable Indian names, but there were no numbered addresses. “Oh, you want Modi Building? It’s that one over there.” We sought out the offices of wealthy industrialists and business owners in order to make Life Members.

One day, on Dadabhai Naoroji Road, I was spotted by a runner from Mr. Lucky’s shop. Lucky Footwear was a tiny shop, barely wide enough for its floor-to-ceiling stack of shoeboxes against one wall and two chairs so customers could try on shoes. The runner left me there, and I sat with the handsome, mild-mannered proprietor with the tiny scar on his cheekbone. He introduced himself as Muhamed—“They call me Mr. Lucky”—and asked if I wanted to change money. The bank rate was 7.5 rupees per dollar, but he was offering 10, maybe 11, even 12, for bigger dollar amounts. He had read about us in the newspapers, he said, and liked what we were doing. Right off we agreed that Allah is the same as Krishna, the one God with many names.

I’d heard about the Bombay mafia. Post-independence India was struggling with coherent economic policies, and there were strict Reserve Bank of India controls on foreign exchange. Foreign money was scarce, resulting in a thriving black currency market dominated by a few “dons” in the manner of 1930s Chicago. I was naive—Mr. Lucky saw that—but he knew I was an aspiring holy man, so he treated me with respect. As I sat, another runner came in with two European tourists—a man and a woman—and they disappeared behind a curtain at the back, reappearing a few minutes later, the woman stuffing cash into her purse. We nodded, a little embarrassed—and I began to get the picture. Thus began what became a long and enduring friendship with Mr. Lucky, one of Bombay’s black-market kings.

Going out the door I spotted a nifty pair of white plastic chappals (sandals) in his tiny window and went back inside and bought them. Mr. Lucky seemed surprised by a cash sale for his shoes. He whacked the dust off of them with a rag, wrapped them in a box he then tied with string, and gave me a ten-rupee discount. I imagined him selling only one pair of shoes a day.

Tamal and I were still staying at Mr. Lalan’s house on Malabar Hill, but we knew it was a temporary situation, so we went out to Chembur to inspect Mr. Agarwal’s Sita-Rama temple, called Ram Sharanam. He offered us three simple, quiet, and spacious rooms, but Chembur was far—maybe fifteen miles through traffic from the downtown action. We scrambled around Fort, Malabar Hill, and Colaba districts looking for more central headquarters. Two letters arrived from Prabhupada, still in Amritsar: “I am very anxious to know your situation; whether you have removed to the Rama Temple or where you are stationed now?”, and, “I was thinking of going to Delhi and Vrindavan, but as you say that my presence in Bombay will be more beneficial, we shall start from here at 6:35 a.m., reaching Bombay central station next day 31st October, Saturday by 4:40 pm.”

Yipes! That was only five days away! So we bit the bullet and started fixing up the Chembur place for all of us. Madri and Malati organized the kitchen and ramped up the Sita-Rama Deity worship while Tamal and I ran around Bombay gathering everyone’s possessions and stocking the new digs with the stuff we’d need.

Prabhupada returned to Bombay on October 31 and, back from the wars, seemed happy and tranquil in our suburban dwelling. We heard his news: the Golden Temple at Amritsar is the most sacred shrine for the Sikhs, and Prabhupada had been honored at the Vedanta Sammelen in a big way. Then the biggest news: Gurudas, Yamuna, Giriraj, Durlabh, Gopal, and Bruce were dropped off in New Delhi. “Yes,” Kaushalya told us, “Prabhupada told them to get down from the train when we stopped there and to get something going in Delhi.”

When I heard that Gurudas had been posted to Delhi I was deeply relieved, in a way. I’d heard that Delhi was a real dump compared to delightful Bombay. Although I hadn’t traveled outside Bombay since I’d arrived in India, I was convinced that Bombay was the best city in India for promoting Krishna consciousness—and I was now a head honcho there. In the spirit of transcendental com-petition—our unspoken desire to please him the most—I wanted to bag this big Bombay rhino for Srila Prabhupada.

**\* \* \***

The Ram Sharanam temple was on 18th Road in the suburb of Chembur. Here, the devotees ate, slept, worked, and worshiped under the same roof with Srila Prabhupada. This quiet life, with Prabhupada at home, went on week after week. His door was always open, and we breezed in and out of his room. Saraswati was always the first one in his room every morning, and if she didn’t show up he asked, “Where is Saraswati?” He supervised every aspect of the girls’ cooking and Deity worship practices and paraphernalia. He translated books and wrote letters. There was a wonderful feeling of family routine. “Walk softly; our father is resting.” “He wants fresh coconut water after his nap. Can you get him some dabs at the corner stall?”

On November 4,1970, Prabhupada wrote to Sudama in Tokyo:

Now I want to simply begin translating. Here I am situated in a temple of Sita-Ram in a nearby suburb of Bombay. The atmosphere is very relaxing, such as I have not found anywhere else in India thus far. So in every way it is very conducive to my translating work and beginning today I will spend all my time translating. Tamala Krsna and Shyamasundar are here with me as my liaison officers and the rest of the GBC members are spread all over the world. So please work closely together and do everything in cooperation.

On most days the men were pounding the streets of Bombay, signing Life Members. We would cram ourselves into a train at

Chembur and forty-five minutes and twelve stops later stumble out onto the platform at Victoria Terminus. These trains were already packed when they arrived at Chembur, and at each station after our stop more men squeezed aboard until you were jammed into a sea of smelly bodies and looking into a hundred staring eyes. One day I felt something in my book bag and reached down to grab the hand of a child pickpocket. He pulled away quickly, and I couldn’t move to catch him. There were separate cars for women, but they described similar conditions and were often groped in the crowds on the platform—what the locals called “Eve teasing”.

Tamal Krishna formed one team with Dinanath while I teamed up with Rishi Kumar, a tough guy from Brooklyn. We competed to see which team could make the most Life Members.

The Life Member program was Prabhupada’s perfect idea for spreading Krishna consciousness in India. In America and Europe, sankirtan in public places was an effective means for distributing literature, collecting funds, and spreading Krishna philosophy, but in India the streets were lined with unkempt, begging sadhus, and Prabhupada didn’t want us to be compared with them. India was a top-down society: whatever the leaders did, the rest of the population seemed to follow. And 1111 rupees was a lot of money in a country where per-capita income was about a thousand rupees a year. So we started at the top.

Rishi Kumar and I made cold calls, simply showing up at a business tycoon’s office and asking for him. If his secretary told us he was busy, we often just opened his office door and barged in. Knowing that a soft spot for sadhus was bred into every Hindu’s heart, we’d make an open display of American-style friendliness, selling this multimillionaire, this CEO of vast divisions of industry, our new product. Most of the men we met were convinced by the sacredness and purity of Prabhupada’s mission. If they weren’t, we sold them on prestige—being part of an elite international club of top-class men. And we showed them the high quality of our books. (We had only one sample of about three books, but we assured them that many more books would be in their hands soon.) Rishi Kumar and I had a bad-guy/good-guy act. If someone showed resistance, Rishi would pound on the man’s desk and shout: “But you must become Life Member. Your friend So-and-so said you would, and Mr. So-and-so and Mr. So-and-so are already Life Members. Don’t you want to be on the list with Bombay’s finest?” Then I would pipe up and say, “Please excuse my godbrother’s behavior—he has given his life to his guru.” You know, the soft sell. A large percentage of the men we met would tell their secretary to write a check, and some would even say, “Please come to my home. I want you to meet my family.”

We became fearless because Prabhupada was fearless. At every public meeting he said straight out to his countrymen: “You are fallen down. You have forgotten everything. You are copying these Westerners.” Or, “You have a few cars; they have millions. You will never have as many cars as they have. You will never have as much opulence as they will. You have your own opulence, and that is your spiritual knowledge, and you have thrown it in the garbage heap.” He gave it to them straight in the face. Every one of those meetings was followed by silence, but people knew he was right. Prabhupada was fearless. And that same fearlessness was in us.

It was all so new and strange, this stomping around in an extremely foreign land. During those early weeks in Bombay I sometimes asked myself, What are you doing here? But soon the relish of our daily work erased these thoughts of other places and other things I might be doing, and I was seized by a strong desire to outshine my godbrothers in pleasing Srila Prabhupada. Thinking of Prabhupada, who waited at home for my spectacular report, I’d pick up the pace and run to my next appointment.

To look snappy for potential Life Members, we bought better clothes, shopping at Cottage Emporium on DN Road. What a great shop. They had sandalwood soap for Prabhupada and real tilak. We also bought “VIP” briefcases and a number of colorful book bags—which one we’d carry on a particular day would depend on the client we were planning to approach. As we patrolled the busy sidewalks, we noticed small cups of milk sitting at the base of banyan trees, and learned that sacred cobras lived hidden in the roots.

At first we grabbed lunch on the street—fried samosas and jelabis—but we soon got diarrhea and learned to buy roasted peanuts in the shell and cellophane packets of mung dal and potato chips instead. We discovered rich, green sugarcane juice at street-side stalls with hand-turned cranks that pressed the juice from the stalks. There were flies all over the place, and the vendors dipped the glasses in a bucket of dirty water between uses—so a big mistake! We so,on switched to bottled Fanta or Limca. Finding suitable toilets downtown was also a considerable challenge.

We gradually realized that we were experiencing some of the same struggles that had shaped our beloved guru, and we felt we understood him better. Our love for him deepened as we saw how he had risen above all this confusion and taken his masters message west.

Prabhupada seemed super-charged. This was his turf, his show, and the afterburners were lit, making up for lost time. Prabhupada was seeing the seeds he had so carefully planted in past decades—and with such great endeavor and suffering—now growing quickly and opening to the sun.

In the late afternoons we rushed back to Chembur, jubilant to make our reports to Srila Prabhupada. Even Ratan Tata, the biggest Parsee industrialist in India, could not resist our pitch. Prabhupada knew who most of these men were, and he would tell us, “Oh, yes, he is making automobiles,” or “He is making steel,” or “I know his brother in Calcutta,” or “Sri Sahadevan—yes, he has a big, big circus—Apollo Circus. It is famous all over India.”

Early each morning Prabhupada spoke in the temple room from Srimad-Bhagavatam, his children gathered around. I watched the spray of wrinkles at the ends of his eyes, like tiny feathers spread in flight. And his eyes—so doelike, calm, much like the eyes we saw on the white, humped cows strolling in the streets, soft and liquid and loving, with long curling lashes. Even the little dewlap under his chin was lovable. Sometimes, around his eyes you could see black outlines, almost like mascara, like the eye of Horus on an Egyptian fresco. His dark eyebrows were thick, unruly, and they swept up and down with changes in his moods, rising as Krishna flung His chakra at some fleeing demon or lowering as he explained to you across his desk, step by step, how to open a bank account in India.

At Chembur, Prabhupada lectured from the Sixth Canto, the story of Ajamila and the cruel conditions of the material world:

adhuneha maha-bhaga  
yathaiva narakan narah  
nanogra-yatanan neyat  
tan me vyakhyatum arhasi

Now kindly tell me how human beings may be saved from having to enter hellish conditions in which they suffer terrible pains.

He used the example of disease. “According to the symptoms of the disease, you have to undergo treatment. That is called prayaschitta, atonement.”

One may know that sinful activity is injurious for him because he actually sees that a criminal is punished by the government and rebuked by people in general and because he hears from scriptures and learned scholars that one is thrown into hellish conditions in the next life for committing sinful acts. Nevertheless, in spite of such knowledge, one is forced to commit sins again and again, even after performing acts of atonement. Therefore, what is the value of such atonement?

He told us, “That’s all right. Krishna will take away all your sins, but you must guard against continued sinning. That is the process.”

Devotees asked Prabhupada questions—these were more dialogues than lectures—and they always led to practical matters, particularly how to live and act in India. For example, Prabhupada instructed us on monkeys: “But if you have got a stick they will not come. They are afraid of stick. The dogs also, they are afraid of stick. Therefore in such places one should carry in hand one stick. That is safe,” and, “Door must be closed always. Otherwise you cannot eat, you cannot live very peacefully—monkey will come.”

We were mesmerized as Prabhupada spoke on those golden mornings over the raucous cawing of crows outside, coo-cooing of pigeons in the courtyard. Changing expressions moved across his golden face, like the play of cumulus and sunlight over a varied landscape. One moment he was from our world and the next from a world we couldn’t penetrate, the self-contained knower of everything. Then suddenly he’s tossing a ball to you in a friendly game of verbal catch—maybe a fastball to see if you can catch it. He might break from describing Krishna’s vast cosmic design to real concern for your upset stomach: “Just try little bicarbonate soda, one spoonful in water.” None of us had ever seen such depth of personality. Prabhupada’s vitality seemed to increase each day. He was constantly talking to people. We never saw him sleep, and he ate very little, even though he encouraged us to eat huge amounts.
