We were so excited, so full of expectation of great things ahead. Of course, there were down periods when we all felt it was too difficult, living like this—“I just want to go back to where there are dishwashing machines and hot water”—but living this close to Prabhupada just made everything easy, light.

We were always joking. The devotees in Bombay were a diverse lot, but we were united around Prabhupada. We could see humor in one another’s personalities and constantly poked fun at each other. Gradually, we developed and shared in running jokes about the people we met. Whoever they were—whether celebrities or street sweepers—these people became part of our amusement. We had nothing, but our austere lives became painless because we were having so much fun. Sure, sometimes we were chauffeured in limos and served huge banquets. But the next day we’d be packed like sardines in garlic-breath train cars with only a handful of chickpeas for lunch. We felt joy because we could see the joy Prabhupada experienced as he sported in the world in which we had been immersed. We’d go to a meeting and he would be grave, even stern, chastising his countrymen for allowing Krishna consciousness to fade, and then we’d return to Ram Sharanam, sit with him, and he’d recall something funny that had happened at the program and break into uproarious laughter. The jolliness—his and ours—drew us deeper and deeper into his tender net.

Mr. Ramchand Chhabria had a gem business in Hatton Garden in London, and we knew him from the Bury Place temple. His main home, however, was in Bombay, and he sometimes visited Prabhupada in Chembur or invited us all to lunch at his home on Malabar Hill. No one spoke softer or more slowly than Mr. Chhabria. Compared to him, our introspective and reticent Giriraj was a motormouth.

Another character, a Mr. Sada Jiwat Lal (which we mispronounced as Sadaji-i^it-lal), a huge man, very fat and jolly, also loved Prabhupada and his devotees. Sometimes he cried when Prabhupada spoke. Sada had a chain of sweet shops called Punjabi Chandu Halwai, with branches all over Bombay. He was always laughing; he was the personification of a big sweetball. His shop at Teen Batti, a crossroads on Malabar Hill, became our favorite hangout when we were in town. He let us gorge on free milk-and-sugar sweets like burft, pera, sandesh, rasagulla, rasamalai, rabri, and gulabjamuns.

When Sada Jiwat Lal heard about our problems on the trains—pickpockets, Eve teasing—he offered to loan us his 1952 Chevrolet four-door coupe and his driver. I told him no driver was required because I used to own a ’52 Chevy myself.

Prabhupada vouched for my driving skills, and the next day we had a car. But learning to herd this huge machine through the crowded streets of Bombay was a white-knuckle experience. I’d move slowly up the left side of the road, one hand on the horn, dodging rickshaws, cows, bicycles, and oblivious pedestrians. There are few stoplights at intersections in Bombay, no posted street names, and no maps, and I often found myself heading off for some destination on the other side of Bombay without the slightest clue how to get there.

But now with a car, at least Prabhupada could go on morning walks somewhere besides the immediate neighborhood. We drove to places like Worli Sea Face or Shivaji Park, where his presence—trailed by his eager band of white elephants, their ears cocked to hear his every word—caused quite a stir.

We’d heard all kinds of stories. For example, in India, if you hit someone with your car—or worse, if you hit a cow—the people dragged you from the vehicle and stoned you to death. One day, I was cruising at about 2 mph through the packed Kalbadevi marketplace when suddenly an old, white-haired man jumped in front of me and blam\\ I hit him. He flew over the hood and flopped back down onto the street. I killed the motor, put my arms up around my head, and went face down onto the steering wheel, waiting for the bricks to fly. I heard all this shouting—a hysterical mob —and thought I was a dead man for sure. When I finally raised my head I saw a crowd around the old man, whom someone had dragged to the sidewalk. He was lying on his back and the people were yelling at himl Chastising him? Yes, one woman even hit him with her handbag. “Sadhu!” she said, pointing at me, still frozen in the car. Yes! They were blaming him for stepping in the way of a sadhu'. I got out, lifted the old guy to his rickety legs, embraced him, and said loudly, “Hare Krishna!” I then slipped him a ten-rupee note; The crowd went wild, like they were watching a miracle play. India.

**\* \* \***

As we worked these crowded neighborhoods to make Life Members, we were often reminded of Srila Prabhupada’s own struggles in these very streets. Prabhupada was initiated by his guru, Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati, in Allahabad in 1932 and given the name Abhay Charanaravinda das. Srila Bhaktisiddhanta’s Society was called the Gaudiya Math, and there were about sixty Gaudiya Math centers scattered around India. It took us years to sort out the details of Srila Prabhupada’s life before he came to us in the West, but we were given bits and pieces of it as we roamed Bombay’s streets with him.

“My Guru Maharaja ordered me, ‘You do this’ \[spread Krishna consciousness\]. But I thought, ‘Let me first of all become a rich man. Then I shall do.’ In the beginning I was thinking, ‘Now my godbrothers have taken sannyasa. They are begging from door to door. Why shall I beg? Let me earn money and start Krishna consciousness.’”

In 1935, Abhay set up his business selling patent medicines on Grant Road in Bombay. He met some of his godbrothers there and found a two-story building for their headquarters on Gowalia Tank Road. In October 1970, Prabhupada opened an account with the Central Bank of India branch on Gowalia Tank Road—home savings account no. 14538—and as we breezed out the door of the bank with him he pointed down the street and said, “Yes, our Gaudiya Math was just over there.”

By 1938, his Bombay business had failed and he returned to Calcutta. In January 1957 he came back to Bombay, this time as an honorary professor of the Bhagavad-gita at Bharatiya Vidya Bhavan, a school of Vedic theology. But he felt restricted there by the open opposition of his impersonalistic peers, and soon resigned. While in Bombay, he tried to revive, without much success, the League of Devotees, a society he had formed in 1953 in Allahabad. When we saw the crumbling building at 93 Narayan

Dhuru Street, his old headquarters in the cacophonic center of Bombay’s biggest nuts, bolts, and hardware market—we were doubly appreciative of his earlier struggles.

On Sunday November 8, 1970, the two-page lead story in the Times of India Weekly was entitled “Special Report: Hare Krishna, Krishna Hare.” The headline was printed in a stylized typeface that made the English letters look like devanagari script. Prabhupada had Tamai Krishna read the article to him line by line. He liked the article so much that he asked to have it copied and sent out to all his ISKCON centers. Millions of Indian citizens read:

Das took me to His Divine Grace at once. I found him seated beneath a huge portrait of Krishna, a kindly old man with hypnotic eyes ... Swamiji pointed out that the Hare Krishna mantra is not to be taken for an ordinary song or anything associated with the mundane. “It is a pure transcendental sound vibration of the Absolute and has been upheld as such since time immemorial by the great sages and by the Vedic scriptures.” He explained that the chanting of the mantra purified the mind. That is why he had asked his followers to go to places and “broadcast” the Lord’s names. The chanting, he said, takes place regularly in the streets of 20 cities in the US, Canada, Britain and West Germany. “Soon the mantra will be heard in every town and village in the world.”

On November 9, Sumati Morarjee, owner of Scindia Steamship Lines and the woman who had sponsored Prabhupada’s 1965 voyage to the West, held a homecoming celebration for him in the conference hall at Scindia House on Morarji Road in Ballard Estate, near the Alexandra Docks in east Bombay. Her staff and many important Bombay families were present. It was like the closing of a five-year circle.

Among the Scindia House crowd that night were two special people: a woman named Vijayantimala and her husband, Dr. Chamanlal Bali. Vijayantimala was a Bollywood superstar and a classical dancer, and she was so beautiful it took your breath away. When after the meeting the couple spoke briefly with Prabhupada, we'could see that Prabhupada was struck by the couple’s obvious beauty, grace, and humility—they were like swans among ducklings. Both were born in pious Vaishnava families. Vijayantimala was known for turning down major roles if there was explicit sexual content. When they gave us their address on Nepean Sea Road and said they would like to become Life Members, Rishi Kumar and I were over there bright and early the next morning to sign them up and savor the company of this extraordinary pair.

On November 10 we read about the Scindia House program in the Times of India:

FOUNDER OF KRISHNA CULT FETED

Before and after his speech, his foreign disciples held Harikirtan, chanting “Hare Krishna, Hare Rama,” accompanied by cymbals and drums with such fervor that it compelled audience participation. Saraswati, two-year-old daughter of Malati, added her voice to the chorus with great abandon. Swami Prabhupada recalled how he was helped by Mrs. Sumati Morarjee, executive director of Scindia, to go to the US by Scindia’s Jaladuta on August 15, 1965, and he related the progress of the movement overseas. He announced his desire to found a centre in Bombay.

In the close-up photo at the top of the article, Prabhupada is about the happiest you’ll ever see him—laughing, looking above the camera and to his right, all teeth showing, his smile lights up the universe.

On November 13 Prabhupada wrote to Rupanuga in Buffalo, New York:

We are just now receiving great publicity and it is reported that Bombay has now got its atmosphere filled with Krishna Consciousness. It is a fact, and the important members of the Bombay community are appreciating our Movement. For the present I am more prominent than all swamis. People are appreciating—What are these other swamis? They cannot go outside India. There is a Bengali saying that a jackal is king in a small forest. The story is that a jackal became king in the forest by fooling the other animals for some time, but he remained always a jackal and his ruse was at last exposed.

**\* \* \***

Despite the whirlwind of events in India, Srila Prabhupada expertly managed the worldwide expansion of his movement through the mail. On November 7, he wrote to Gurudas in New Delhi: “Things in London are not as they should be. London temple is one of the most important centers in our society and it should not suffer for lack of good management. So I want you and Yamuna to both be prepared to go back to London in the near future ... but for the time being you should remain here and see what can be done in Delhi.”

Great Britain was my GBC zone. I was supposed to manage things there. In London, Janaki was depressed now that her sister and best friends were gone, and this was distracting Mukunda. And it seemed the young, inexperienced men we d left in charge were struggling to maintain Bury Place. Of course I’d do whatever Prabhupada wanted, but I really didn’t want to go back to London. It was winter there, and I wasn’t very good at managing large organizations anyway, so I redoubled my effort to please Srila Prabhupada in Bombay. (Luckily, the London situation improved, and neither Gurudas nor I had to return to England.)

Prabhupada also wrote to Krishna das in Berlin: “Regarding Russia, I am very anxious to establish a center there in Moscow and if I am invited, I can go there ... This correspondence should be conducted very cautiously with Russia. You can ask them some questions, like: What is your ideal ultimate goal of life? What is the difference between animal and human life? Why is religion accepted by all kinds of civilized societies? What is your conception of the original creation? In this way questions may be put to find out what is their standing. We do not grudge an atheist provided he has got some philosophical standing. In this way try to elicit some answers from the Professors. I want very much a center in Russia.”

From vast international strategies down to the smallest detail of a devotee’s personal care, Srila Prabhupada was on top of everything. On November 17, he advised Bhaktajan in New York City, who had a skin affliction:

Regarding the ointment which you are finding so useful, the formula is as follows:

Hard Paraffin (candle wax) 1/2 ounce

Soft Paraffin (Vaseline) 3 ounces

Resin (or rosin) 1 ounce

Oil of Eucalyptus 1/2 ounce

Chlorophyll

Melt the two paraffins and the crushed resin in a small pot, stir constantly. Then all melted and mixed up, add the oil of eucalyptus and stir it in. Let the mixture cool somewhat, but keep stirring or the resin will separate and harden on the bottom. Just before the mixture begins to solidify, add enough dry chlorophyll to make the ointment slightly dark green—not very much required. Stir the mixture thoroughly until it is completely cooled into an ointment.

On November 28, he wrote to Gargamuni, who was in Florida: “Here in Bombay we are getting good response. Our expenditure is very heavy because Bombay is like a European or American city. But Krishna is providing us with all facilities. It requires a little intelligence how to keep pace with Krishna and the world simultaneously.” Prabhupada’s travel plans in India were always very fluid. He would receive invitations from distant cities and dispatch a devotee or two to go there and scope it out, communicating with them by letter or telegram. If a program looked promising in that place, Prabhupada would often request the host to send tickets for him and his traveling team.

On November 15, Prabhupada wrote to Jayapataka in Calcutta: “I have already applied to the Magh Mela Committee and have received a letter of acceptance from them.” Magh Mela was some kind of traditional camp-meeting with millions of sadhus, which would take place in January-February near Allahabad in central India.

In response to an invitation, Prabhupada had sent Kirtanananda to Gorakhpur, a city near Nepal in northern India, to try to make Life Members and get something going there. On November 24, he wrote to Kirtanananda: “Already we have over thirty life members in Bombay.” (Which really meant, “How many Life Members have you made? Why you are not making more?”)

On the same day he wrote to Madhudvisa in Calcutta: “Your friend Jariwala has invited us to go to Surat in full strength. You may all go there directly or come to Bombay and we shall go together.”

Prabhupada was setting the scene for his traveling show. By late November 1970 there were at least five destinations competing for Prabhupada’s presence: Indore, Vrindaban, Gorakhpur, Surat, and the Magh Mela in Allahabad.

On December 2, he wrote to his godbrother Puri Maharaja in Rajahmundry, a city in eastern Andhra Pradesh state, South India, adding another destination to the list:

Perhaps you are my only Godbrother who has appreciated my humble service to the cause of Guru Gauranga. All my other Godbrothers are very much envious, as I can understand from their behavior ... Tomorrow I am going to Indore; from Indore I shall come to Surat. From there I shall go to Gorakhpur. From Gorakhpur I shall go to Allahabad, and most probably from Allahabad I may go to Vrndavana ... I shall go to your place from Vrndavana or from Allahabad, and we have to arrange in that way. We are thirty men and women all together. Whether you want us all to visit? Perhaps if we go from Vrindaban we shall have to take advantages of the Grand Trunk Express.

During this period, Prabhupada had to seriously consider how to deal with his godbrothers. We ran into them in most places in India. Some were grudging and obstructive, apparently thinking that Prabhupada had violated the strict rules of Vaishnava behavior by initiating meat-eaters and women and associating openly with such fallen persons. Many godbrothers were upset, too, that he’d adopted the title “Prabhupada,” “master at whose feet all other masters sit,” a title they felt should be reserved only for their guru, Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati. But Prabhupada waved off their ill-informed objections and carried on. The travel plan was getting tighter.

**\* \* \***

Prabhupada was invited to the Gita Jayanti Mahotsava, another weeklong sadhu convention in Indore, five hundred miles northeast of Bombay and about fifteen hours away by train. Amritsar, Surat, Indore—I had no idea where these places were and didn’t even try to find out. To me, they were just cities somewhere deep in India, maybe north of us somewhere. All I knew was that Bombay was on the west coast, Calcutta on the east coast, and Delhi about halfway between.

Before his departure for Indore, again Tamai Krishna and I asked Prabhupada if we could stay behind. All right, he said, “but you should write me letters to Indore daily, what is your progress.” Prabhupada left for Indore on December 3, taking only four devotees with him from Bombay. Hamsaduta, Gurudas, and a few other devotees from Calcutta and Delhi would meet him there.

An article appears in the Bombay Times of India on December 5:

BOMBAY WILL BE EASTERN H.Q.

OF KRISHNA FOLLOWERS

The saffron-clad American devotees of Lord Krishna, who came to Bombay a month ago, have decided to make the city their permanent home. Tamai Krishna das Adhikari, a native of New York City and a graduate of Queen’s College, told this reporter of their plans between recitals and dances of Ramdhun at a kirtan organized by Trimurti on Friday.

“We are looking for a plot or a building to establish our headquarters. It must be in the heart of the city; we do not want to go to the suburbs.” He said that Bombay would be the headquarters for the International Society for Krishna Consciousness for the East and Los Angeles for the West. Bombay is the right place for our headquarters. The weather is nice, the people are intelligent, they speak English, and they are influential.” He said that since their arrival in the city a hundred life members from Bombay residents had been enrolled. He showed some of the books on Lord Krishna’s teachings, all glossy productions. He said 100,000 books would shortly arrive from the U.S.

Two-and-a-half-year-old Saraswati, her auburn hair flowing, joined in the recitals on the lawns of a modern apartment building on Nepean Sea Road. Later she went to sleep on the lawn.

“That is my wife,” said Tamal, pointing to a young American dressed in a sari. “Our guru, Srimad A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, encourages marriages.” The guru was in Indore. He was to address a large meeting there the same evening.

Our first priority in Bombay was to find suitable headquarters. We didn’t want to keep crashing at somebody else’s place. We spread the word to our Life Members that we were looking for a permanent place and asked them to help. We scoured the city. I mostly looked around Malabar Hill, the Beverly Hills of India. The maharajas and rich industrialists built their Bombay homes on Malabar Hill along Nepean Sea Road, Mount Pleasant Road, or near the Hanging Gardens, with sweeping views of the Arabian Sea and the city spread out below. After India’s independence in 1947, and under the new socialist government, most of the maharajas were no longer receiving huge incomes from their ancestral lands and were going broke. Their elegant Victorian or Art Deco homes on the Hill were now shabby, and many were for sale. To my eye, they were splendidly seedy: Greco-Roman statuary and carved cherubs black with mold, long tiled drives encircling fountains that no longer worked, huge banyan trees shading the lawns, and open verandas and shady porticos filled with trash. We looked at the king of Nepal’s unkempt mansion and the palaces of the kings of Gwalior and Baroda. What did we have—a few thousand rupees from life memberships? We tried all kinds of elaborate negotiations—a few thousand down, monthly installments, maybe so-and-so will co-sign—but despite our strong desire to see Srila Prabhupada in palatial surroundings, these properties were way beyond our means.

Meanwhile, Ramchand Chhabria phoned and said he owned a small hotel in Apollo Bunder, downtown Bombay, and he offered to house some of us for a while. Would we like to see it? We were ecstatic the moment we saw his Sea Palace Hotel. It was the perfect place. Just a block down the waterfront from Bombay’s finest hotel, the Taj Mahal, and close to the Gateway of India, the Sea Palace Hotel was the best location possible for our Life Member recruiting program. It was central, sunny and cool, facing the harbor, and the cooks knew how to make spaghetti marinara and tomato soup with croutons. Krishna came through again! As ex-hippies, we were used to small, crowded quarters, meals on the fly and sleeping rough, so to us the Sea Palace Hotel was really posh. Now we had wheels and a cool place to stay—and Bombay was our sweetball. Mr. Chhabria gave us one large room upstairs for the men and a smaller room for the women and my daughter. There was a separate, windowless room off the main dining room for our meals. And every day was a delight—wow! Cornflakes and fresh papaya for breakfast, grilled cheese sandwiches with ketchup for lunch, sliced white bread, sort-of-macaroni and spaghetti, and vanilla milkshakes. After weeks of rice, dal, and chapatis, it was paradise.

Within two months of arriving in India, our Krishna team had spread out all across the country. Planning anything was now almost impossible. Modern communications technology had not yet arrived in India. Telegraph was about the fastest way to reach people, but the language in telegrams often arrived garbled. To make a long-distance telephone call meant waiting in line for hours at the central post office at Flora Fountain and then usually being cut off mid-sentence. So our plans were mostly transmitted by letters, which usually took days to reach their recipients and, because we were all moving around so much, mail was often lost. Planning required total faith in Krishna’s magic.

On December 13, Tamai Krishna and I received a letter from Prabhupada, who was still in Indore:

From your letter everything is encouraging, still you should post me daily one letter. I am anxiously awaiting your full report from Bombay in letters ... Regarding Surat, how can we go? We have not received any money. So it should be postponed. We have received two telegrams, but no money.

I have received one telegram from Gurudasa in Delhi reading as follows: “CCP granted also official letter clearing books waiving demurrage sent to all ports, trying Indira 18th—Gurudasa.”

So I am very anxious to know what you have been able to do in the matter of receiving the books. Because of the proposed meeting with Indira Gandhi on the 18th instant, our Gorakhpur program is not fixed up. If you have already purchased tickets in the meantime, you may go there as planned, but our arrival from this end is not yet ascertained. Please keep me informed by daily letters as you promised before my leaving for Indore.

There was a full-scale port strike in Calcutta. It had been going on for months, and thousands of Prabhupada’s books were stuck in a warehouse. On top of that, the government wanted to levy a huge tax on imported books. Storage costs and demurrage were mounting up. Gurudas pleaded with the central government in Delhi, trying to get the books exempted from tax and cleared through customs. He was also attempting to arrange a meeting between Prabhupada and Indira Gandhi. I was so glad Gurudas and Tamal Krishna were dealing with this kind of stuff. Their confrontational, tough New York manner—I saw it many times in London—had a way of resolving big problems quickly: “Hey! Yes, you. Look at me. We re gonna get this thing done, OK?”

For millennia, foreigners had plundered India. It had taken relatively few Muslims or Brits to rule this heavily populated country because both regimes created a huge civil service. If you employ everyone in your government, the people can’t revolt. Indian government bureaucracy was now a complex system of smoke and mirrors. Also, after thousands of years of practice, "small cheating” had been honed to an art in India. We learned this the hard way, and Prabhupada was constantly berating us for being so ignorant in the hands of Indian businessmen and government agents.

As a result of Prabhupada’s December 13 letter, Tamai Krishna decided to go to Calcutta to try to clear the books from the port. On December 15, Gurudas managed to bully his way through the musty halls of New Delhis bureaucracies and get a letter from T.B.C. Nebudgadi, Joint Chief Controller of Imports and Exports, allowing our books to be released without duty.

Also on December 15, the devotees at the Sea Palace Hotel received a telegram from Prabhupada telling us to come to Surat. Someone must have sent him money and tickets. He would arrive in Surat from Indore on December 16, he told us, and we were to meet him there as soon as we could wrap things up in Bombay and buy train tickets. We would find him “c/o Bhagubhai Jariwala, Motorcycle Building, Begumpura, Surat.”

(Unknown to us, on December 16, which was Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati s disappearance day, in faraway London, George Harrison sent an Apple limo and driver to the Bury Place temple with £100 and a note to Dhananjaya asking him to buy food for a celebratory feast.)

Shortly after noon on December 17, our crew gathered at Victoria Terminus for the five-hour train ride to Surat. We spent our waiting time marveling at the antics of cat-sized rats in the stations rafters.

By now, more devotees had arrived from the US, including Tushta Krishna and his wife, Tulsi Krishna dasi. And two Indian devotees, Sagar Maharaj and Amritananda, joined us from Calcutta. Now there were twenty-three of us—our entire Bombay contingent in holiday mood—crowding onto the train and anxious to be reunited with Srila Prabhupada and our fellow disciples in Surat.

Aboard the train I wrote a letter to George filled with stream-of-consciousness impressions of my first trip into the real India:

Miles of slums coming out of Bombay. Garbage heaps w/ dozens of children climbing on them, stuffing paper in burlap sacks. Steam engine pulling this thing—black soot blow-by coating everything in the cabin. Engineers blasting the air horn every two minutes. Village after small village pattern the entire landscape, like trees that distance themselves so each gets the right amount of water. The village people seem so innocent. No tractors yet; bulls to pull plows. November is harvest season. Green, lush. Thatches of rice stalks drying in the sun, women bent over the paddies to their calves in water planting rice shoots for a second crop. Eye-burning rectangles of sunlight from the flooded paddies. I feel a great kinship, affection for them. Not so different from my own farm days in Oregon. How everyone is suffering in the material world, but these people seem happier than the city folk. Their faces light up to see us, startled, but in a good way. They immediately smile, even laugh, ladies hiding white teeth in the cowl of their saris. Mutual astonishment: East meets West meets East. Bright-clothed women with pots on their heads, bangles and bracelets the length of their arms, skinny kids in uniforms marching to school, skinny men in turbans and ragged dhotis. Project yourself from a barred, open train window to a faraway field where it’s quiet, clicking palm fronds overhead, white egrets, herons poised and waiting for frogs, the air smelling of earth and water, grass and cow dung—you can almost see young Krishna running through the fields with his pals. What’s that—those big red piles in that field? I ask a new friend—“Chili peppers,” he says. “D’joosee that goat up in the tree?” Fields of ripe cotton and tomatoes being plucked by hand. Thinking: as each property owner dies and divides his land among his many sons, each generation gets less and less—must be why they’re crowding into those slums in the city. Passing small temples—you can see the flag on top—Hanuman, Durga mostly. Look, Saraswati! Camel! A real, live camel pulling a huge wagonload of cotton bales wrapped in burlap. Train stations: packed and buzzing with families on the move—these Indian people like to travel—the clamor of loudspeakers announcing the next train—shouting of hawkers for tea and samosas—men lined up pissing on a wall ... Sunset as we slow to approach Surat: a fuzzy, red-orange ball on the western edge suspended in blue-black haze—the smoke from 100,000 cooking fires. Wail of shenai from a roadside radio. Seeing it for the first time: Eternal India.

Thousands of years ago, Surat was called Suryapur. Krishna used to stop by here on His way to Dwaraka, about four hundred miles—maybe eight days by horse-drawn chariot—to the northwest. Surat was an important port city during Roman and Mogul times, being the western endpoint for the Silk Road. Then the harbor silted in, and the British built a better port at Bombay. Surat, now home to half a million people, is famous for its textiles, especially its silk brocades and gold embroidery work (jari). “Jariwala,” the surname of the family who will be hosting us, means a vendor of fabric embroidered with gold thread.

When we stepped down at Surat Station, we were met by dozens of citizen-devotees carrying flags and banners, throwing flower petals, shouting Krishna’s names, and heaping us with fragrant garlands. It was as if the five-hour train journey north had sent us hurtling through a time warp and we had stepped out into 3000 BC, the time when Krishna and Balaram were entering Mathura.

We’d read about this in Prabhupada’s Krsna book:

The houses were decorated with costly jewels, and each and every house had nice compounds of trees bearing fruits and flowers. The corridors and verandas of the houses were decorated with silk cloth and embroidery work in jewels and pearls. In front of the balcony windows were pigeons and peacocks walking and cooing. All the grain dealers’ shops within the city were decorated with different kinds of flowers and garlands, newly grown grass, and pleasing flowers like narcissus and roses. The entrance doors of the houses were decorated with water pots filled with water. Water mixed with yogurt, sandalwood pulp, and flowers was sprinkled all around the doors, which were also decorated with burning lamps of different sizes. Over all the doors were decorations of fresh mango leaves and silk festoons.

When the brothers were passing through the streets, all the brahmanas in the neighborhood went out with sandal water and flowers and respectfully welcomed Them to the city. He proceeded down the street of the marketplace, where the citizens were prepared to receive Him with various kinds of presentations, especially betel nuts, flowers, and sandalwood pulp. The mercantile men in the market worshiped Krishna and Balarama with great respect.

The scene became even more surreal when we passed into the old city. Signs proclaiming the arrival of Bhaktivedanta Swami and his entourage lined the narrow streets. Shop and home entryways were decorated with palm branches and colored patterns. We were led with great fanfare to the home of Bhagubhai Jariwala, in the medieval district in Surat’s core. There we saw elaborate textiles draped over floors and furniture and garlands of flowers and mango leaves everywhere. The extended Jariwala family, their friends, and the city’s officials were shouting “Hare Krishna!” in welcome.

Prabhupada was waiting for us on the balcony of the house. He was ten feet high and glowing, his shoulders held back to support the many garlands around his neck—the master of all he surveyed.

As soon as They reached the precinct of his house, the florist immediately came out and with great devotion fell down on his face to offer his respectful obeisances. He offered a nice seat to Krishna and Balarama and asked his assistant to bring out flowers, betel nuts, and pulp of chan-dana. The florist's welcome greatly satisfied the Lord.

This entry into Surat immediately gave us the sense that we were doing something historic. This was a real first, something that had never happened before. Our Prabhupada, His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami, was unique in history, leading his tiny foreign army on a friendly crusade into deepest India.

We began to see Prabhupada as a great saint here in his homeland, relaxed, the true spiritual father of India, taking his place on the balcony to address flushed and excited devotee crowds like the Pope at St. Peter’s, his forehead plastered with sandalwood paste, the rooms and halls behind him suffused with the aroma of gardenias and tuberoses. Prabhupada was loving it—and we were loving him more each second. To see our father, our best friend, our master, so well treated, so revered by a whole city—it made us dizzy with new realizations. Prabhupada was a saint! The word for saint in Sanskrit comes from the root sat, “one who knows and abides by the truth.” Prabhupada was being venerated for his virtue and knowledge, as a living example of the truth. And seeing these angelic citizens of Surat, we realized, too, what the world might be like if everyone became a devotee of Krishna.

Once we were settled, we caught up on the news from Indore: Prabhupada had enrolled the king of Indore, Maharaja Bharatsingh, as a Life Member, but his check had bounced. Also, Prabhupada had placed an ad in the Indore newspapers:

Wanted: qualified Brahmins to preach Bhagavad-gita all over the world. Educated candidates accepted without any discrimination of Caste and Creed. Life Member can send any member of his family for being trained up as qualified Brahmin who can preach Bhagavad-gita as it is all over the world. Apply ISKCON.

At Mr. Jariwala’s house, Prabhupada lectured at 4:30 each morning in his room. Despite the early hour, people packed in to hear him, spilling out into the hall. His lectures were followed by the most sumptuous breakfasts we’d ever had, served with pomp and flourish on banana leaves spread on the floor. By 8 a.m. we were out of the house for all-day kirtan on the streets of heaven. Each day we’d go to a different section of town. Roads were cordoned off, and extensive advertising assured that thousands of devotees joined us to chant and dance in sankirtan. The town leaders draped bright saris, tied together, over the roads, shading the chanters from the hot sun. Fresh-cut banana trees were fastened to lampposts, and the pavement was freshly washed and adorned with colored riceflour rangoli designs. The city’s devotees standing on balconies showered us with rose petals and rice, and those in the streets buried us under mounds of garlands and anointed us with sandalwood and bright red kumkum paste along with smudges of black ash from sacred fires. As we walked past people’s homes, we were often pulled inside and given cold lassis or sweets before rushing back into the streets to continue our chanting parade.

There were maybe thirty of us, Prabhupada’s disciples from America, Europe, and India—our African-American Dinanath, jumping and whumping his mridanga, giant Chidananda, beautiful Kaushalya, Malati, and Yamuna, their eyes closed in bliss. Saraswati with her little blond pigtails, wriggling on my shoulders, her arms in the air, shouting “Haribol” There was Japanese Bruce (later, Bhanu das), whacking his kartals in devotional satori. We had ex-linebackers, ex-Vietnam machine gunners, ex-pompom girls, ex-motorcycle queens circulating these ancient streets in gusts and eddies, hour after hour, roaring Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare.

We’d straggle back by late afternoon, eat an even better meal than we’d ever had in our lives, maybe nap for an hour, then head off to our evening program. The city fathers had sealed off the intersection of Station Road and Kot Safil Road, the two main streets of town, and erected a stage in the center of the crossing. Here, night after night, blocking traffic in every direction, a square mile of seething humanity hung on Prabhupada’s every word. Prabhupada usually asked one of his disciples to also speak, and one night he asked Kaushalya to sing the eighteen verses of Sri Ishopanishad. After the show hundreds of Surat’s citizens would line up to touch Prabhupada’s feet and accept a piece of sugar candy prasada from his hand. What a spectacle. The mayor, Vaikuntha Shastri, declared a municipal holiday and closed the schools for Prabhupada’s visit. Gujarat’s Chief Minister, Hitendra Desai, introduced Prabhupada at the opening ceremonies. “Welcome to Bhaktivedanta Swami and members of the Hare Krishna Movement” was proclaimed on banners hung throughout the city.

Kaushalya: When I finished singing, Prabhupada called me closer to him on the stage, and I started to offer my obeisances. Suddenly, he grabbed my head and put it on his lap, patted me on the back, and rubbed my head. I felt like a puppy. It was really special.

After the evening celebrations in Surat, we gathered in Prabhupada’s room. One night I asked him, “Prabhupada, we have seen some of these huge outdoor programs they have in Bombay with big tents. They go on for a week, something like that Sadhu Samaj meeting, and with big lights and advertising. Some of the Life Members think—well, we also think—that it might be a good idea if we put on one of these tent festivals in Bombay ourselves. What do you think?”

“Oh, you have found a good place?”

“We’re thinking maybe Cross Maidan—that big park between Churchgate Station and Flora Fountain, right in the heart of Bombay. There was some kind of Mayavadi festival there a couple of weeks ago, and thousands of people were going in.”

“Yes, it is a good idea. Bombay is the leading city in India. If I speak like this continuously, many days, it will change everything. At least one week. The Life Members will help you. Yad yad acharati shreshthas tat tad evetaro janah. And I will speak there morning and night, and everyone is welcome. No admittance fee. You make big publicity. I will show you—”

And Prabhupada sits forward, takes his glasses out of their little plastic case, and picks up his Cross ballpoint pen. He sketches for a moment and says, “You make like this, one big hall with a stage where we will sit, and here make a nice temple, and we will install Radha and Krishna Deities. Decorate everything nicely. And behind, some space for pujaris—and prasadam. There must be prasadam for every person who comes. We shall call it: ‘Bhagavata Dharma Discourses.’”

At last, a big project! “Bhagavats Dharma Discourses” in Bombay—a major rhino for sure.

**\* \* \***

On December 25, several of us rushed back to Bombay to find a more permanent place to live, make more Life Members, and get started on this big festival idea, the Bhagavata Dharma Discourses. Prabhupada stayed on in Surat, saying he would join us later. Meanwhile, he dispatched some devotees to Allahabad to begin preparing for January’s month-long outdoor program, the Magh Kumbha Mela.

We moved back into the Sea Palace Hotel. There was a surprise package waiting: George Harrison had mailed me a copy of his new album, All Things Must Pass. We didn’t know any of this at the time, of course, but the album had been released on November 27 and had become the top-selling album in the world. By December 26, “My Sweet Lord,” a single from the album, was Number One on the US hit parade, where it stayed for four weeks. Hundreds of millions of people around the globe heard the Hare Krishna mantra, most for the first time, and many must have been thinking, “Wow, one of the world’s most famous people is singing Hare Krishna. Let me see what this is all about.” All Things Must Pass, the first triple album in history recorded by a solo artist went six times platinum, selling more than six million copies. “Hare Krishna” was now a household term all over the earth. But we couldn’t find anyone in Bombay with a record player, so we couldn’t listen to it.

From the Sea Palace Hotel we divided into teams and fanned out: some would be hustling Life Members, others looking for a temple property, and some networking with Life Members to secure a venue for our Bhagavata Discourses.

For that we would need a large tent, in India called a pandal. Pandals are basically huge, four-sided tents framed with bamboo poles and covered in canvas. They’re large enough to accommodate a stage and space to seat thousands of people.

We wanted to use Cross Maidan as the venue, but we had a few other parklike public areas in mind in case Cross Maidan wasn’t available. In the meantime, Gurudas was working on getting ISKCON registered in India.

I had returned the ’52 Chevy to Sada Jiwat Lal before going to Surat, so we were now taking taxis everywhere. Rishi Kumar was appointed treasurer and kept a tight rein on the money.

“Hey Rishi, I need twenty rupees today for cab fare.”

“Twenty rupees?! The bus is two rupees. Here’s three—one extra for a Fanta. Knock yourself out.”

Traveling by taxi in Bombay was a hair-raising experience. Each taxi dashed to occupy any empty space, and together they swarmed like migrating fish up a stream. There were many near misses on the road but remarkably few crashes. Inside most cabs—on dashboards or hanging from the mirror—there were small pictures, statuettes, or medallions representing the driver’s religious faith. We met Mary with Jesus, Sai Baba, Shiva, Ganesh, and occasionally Krishna holding a flute with a cow by His side. Muslim drivers often had an Arabic prayer pin-striped on the dash. Hindus and Christians had little altars with flashing Christmas tree lights or a burning stick of incense or one of the one-rupee jasmine garlands thrust through drivers’ windows by urchins at traffic light stops. The drivers were sometimes silent and brooding and sometimes broke into voluble chatter, happy when we replied in broken Hindi. Many took the opportunity to practice their English. India was the land of “small cheating,” so we watched the meters like hawks. There was a double system of fares read off a paper chart—justifying charging more than what the meter said—and we had to be careful. Whenever I traveled in taxis with Gurudas, I had to restrain him from yelling at the driver and occasionally threatening to slug him.

In response to the November 8 Times of India Weekly article (“Special Report: Hare Krishna, Krishna Hare”) a total of seven letters to the editor had appeared in subsequent issues. These letters from Indian citizens addressed topics such as caste, Hindu traditions, modern science, and politics. Some labeled the Hare Krishna devotees “beggars,” “escapists,” and “sentimentalists.” Others defended us. I had sent clippings of these printed letters to Srila Prabhupada in Indore and Surat, and he had been considering his reply. Finally, on December 30, Prabhupada mailed me his very long response to the letters and asked me to sign my name to it. Times of India Weekly published his letter with very little editing on January 17,1971. This, in small part, was Prabhupada’s reply to the Indian public:

Since the Hare Krsna movement devotees have come to India, many kinds of discussions have taken place amongst various types of readers. I, the undersigned, one of the members of the Governing Body, wish to clear up the situation for public understanding... According to the authority of Bhagavad-gita, all the American, European, Canadian, Australian, and Japanese boys and girls who are accepting this cult belong to the topmost brahmana class. According to Bhagavad-gita there is no caste system by birth ... The stress is given to the quality and practical life ... Whether the Indian brahmanas will accept them as brahmanas or not does not matter... This is a cultural movement, and if the whole world accepts this cult, even though Indian brahmanas do not accept, it will do no harm at all. The tendency now is generally that even without changing their status as Hindus, Muslim, etc., people are having a social intercourse with others. The religious or national bar does not hamper social intercourse. We are not striving for social or political unity, but if Krsna consciousness is accepted there will automatically be political, social, and religious unity.

**\* \* \***

Srila Prabhupada returned to Bombay on the Flying Ranee express train from Surat on the morning of January 4,1971, and we picked him up and rushed him to lunch at a new Life Member’s home. Mr. Madhanlal Vakaria had a 33 % record player, so I brought All Things Must Pass for Prabhupada to hear. The sound quality was poor, but Prabhupada smiled and seemed to relish every word as I moved the needle around to the tracks he had indeed inspired, such as Awaiting on You All” and “Beware of Darkness.” Although Prabhupada couldn't understand many of the lyrics, he heard the maha-mantra chorus in “My Sweet Lord” and went “Ooooh!” with his eyebrows raised.

Prabhupada stayed overnight in a room adjacent to ours at the Sea Palace Hotel. The next morning we crossed the street with him and walked four hundred yards east along the harborside promenade to the Gateway of India. He spoke of the British era and how this massive arch was built to commemorate the 1911 visit to India of King George V and Queen Mary. The arch served then as the ceremonial gateway for viceroys and Bombay’s British governors. Just twenty-two years earlier, in February 1948, Britain had finally withdrawn from India, and the last British troops exited through this arch.

The following evening Srila Prabhupada flew off to Calcutta to organize Jayapataka’s team, who would travel to Allahabad in a few days to manage the ISKCON camp at the Magh Kumbha Mela. With all the action going on in Bombay, I was resigned to staying behind and working there—it probably meant I wouldn’t see Prabhupada for the next few months.

Tushta Krishna had spotted a “Flats to Let” sign in front of a newly-constructed high-rise at 89 Warden Road, at the base of Malabar Hill near Breach Candy, a couple of blocks from the Arabian Sea. The building was called “Akash Ganga,” Hindi for “Milky Way.” Like London’s Bury Place, Warden Road was close to the center of a major city. We went to look at the two empty flats comprising the seventh floor—sounds of construction still echoing throughout the building—and they were perfect, with a 360° view like an oceanside condo in Miami or Rio. The two flats together cost Rs. 3000/-($375) per month—a huge amount in those days. Prabhupada wrote from Calcutta and told us to go for it—his logic being that making three new Life Members a month would cover the cost.

As with most official negotiations in Bombay, Gurudas and Rishi Kumar took the lead. The landlord, Mr. Billimoria, wasn’t too sure about us. The sixth floor of Akash Ganga, one floor below our apartments, was home to the Pakistan Consulate, and trouble was already brewing between East Pakistan and India. President Nixon was supporting Pakistan at the time, and it was widely rumored that we were American CIA agents. (Two months later, there would be riots in the parking lot of the building and the consulate closed.)

On January 12, several Bombay devotees took off by train for Allahabad to join Srila Prabhupada and the devotees at the Magh Kumbha Mela. Gurudas, Rishi Kumar, and I stayed behind at the Sea Palace Hotel to negotiate for the Akash Ganga apartments, to work on ISKCON’s registration, and to nail down a site for the Bhagavata Dharma Discourses. We would miss the grand rendezvous in Allahabad, which left us feeling lonely and neglected.

Then a few days later, Gurudas got a telegram:

COME WITH PARTY ALLAHABAD IMMDETLY BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI

Hooray!

The grand Vaishnava celebration in Surat had opened our eyes and would be a hard act to follow—but we hadn’t seen anything yet...

## Two

It was all about surrender. From the moment we stepped onto that DC-3 bound for Bombay, we’d pretty much let the river of India sweep us along, surrendering ourselves to its ageless allure. Little by little we renounced expectations normally governed by the rules of cause and effect: If I do this, then this will happen. It seemed you couldn’t really plan ahead much in India. Things just happened. Giving up our attachment to the ways we thought things should be, we surrendered to the way they were and, slowly, we deferred to the gentle but frantic energy of the people and culture around us and let Krishna’s magic be our guide.

In Bombay, at least I had found some comfort in the vestiges of familiar things: tiny luxuries like fast-moving taxis, the English language, flush toilets, ticking clocks, candy bars, and airmail stamps. But all that changed on the cold dawn of January 18, 1971, when I passed by horse-drawn tonga through Daraganj Gate in Allahabad and looked north and east over the hundred-square-mile encampment called Ardh Kumbha Magh Mela. That morning I was gripped by an extraordinary feeling. I knew one thing for sure: here, I would have to surrender everything.

As far as the eye could see, spread out on the great sandy alluvial plain before me, tens of thousands of pandals and tents, erected just days before, were arranged in blocks with wide streets between. Four weeks from now the entire pole-and-canvas megalopolis would be dismantled and removed, leaving scarcely a trace of its fifty million visitors. Two mighty rivers, the Ganges from the north and the Yamuna from the west, joined and flowed southeast, trisecting this vast encampment. Hundreds of columns of wood smoke rose into a dark-blue haze stretched across the horizon above a soon-to-rise red-orange sun.

Our ISKCON camp was somewhere down in that sea of humanity. Malati, Saraswati, and most of the Bombay-based devotees had already been here for a week. I had left Bombay the day before—having stayed back a few days to finish some work—and had traveled alone in an overnight second-class coach to Allahabad Junction. On the nine-hundred-mile, twenty-four-hour train ride I’d had time to read up on Ardh Kumbha Magh Mela. This is what I learned:

In the predawn of history, with demonic forces on the rise, the demigods who ruled the creation approached the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Krishna, and asked for His help. Krishna says in the Bhagavad-gita, “Whenever and wherever there is a decline in religious practice and a predominant rise of irreli-gion—at that time I descend Myself.” He thus took the form of a colossal tortoise named Kurma and dove into the Ocean of Milk, the cosmic ocean, resurfacing with a great mountain on His back. He then asked the demigods to use the mountain as a churning staff and Vasuki, king of serpents, as a giant churning rope, and with these tools to robustly churn the Milk Ocean. Like butter churned from milk, the demigods would extract amrita, the elixir of immortality, from the ocean. Drinking this amrita would allow the demigods to prevail over the demons.

The demigods complied, and the elixir was collected in a pitcher, or kumbha. The demons, of course, fought hard to acquire it. During the battle that followed, a drop of amrita spilled to the earth in what is now Allahabad, in the very spot where the three most sacred rivers in India meet. The confluence of these rivers, the Ganga, the Yamuna, and the Saraswati, is called the

Triveni or Sangam. Every twelve years since that long-ago event, auspicious planets enter the jug constellation (Aquarius), filling it with amrita, and once again the nectar of immortality pours out on the Triveni.

There are four Kumbha Melas in India, but this particular Mela (festival) always happens during the month of Magh (January-February) in Allahabad. Because it takes place halfway between the twelve-year cycle and is, at least cosmologically, considered smaller than the main Kumbha Mela, the word ardh (“half”) is added to its name. In January of 1971, the Ardha Magh Kumbha Mela at Allahabad was the largest human gathering for a peaceful purpose on the planet.

My India guidebook even had a quote from Mark Twain in it. Twain braved the Kumbha Mela in 1895: “It is wonderful, the power of a faith like that, that can make multitudes upon multitudes of the old and weak and the young and frail enter without hesitation or complaint upon such incredible journeys and endure the resultant miseries without repining. It is done in love, or it is done in fear; I do not know which it is. No matter what the impulse is, the act born of it is beyond imagination.”

The city that has grown around the confluence of the holy rivers was renamed Allahabad (“City of God”) by its Muslim conquerors, but it was originally known as Prayag, “Place of Sacrifice.” The well-documented history of Prayag boggled my mind: after creating the world, Lord Brahma offered his first sacrifice to Krishna here. Millions of years ago, Lord Rama and His devotee Hanuman spent time in Prayag, at the ashram of the great sage Bharadwaj, before proceeding to nearby Chitrakoot. Five hundred years ago, Lord Chaitanya taught His great disciple Rupa Goswami the science of devotional service at Dashashwamedh Ghat in Allahabad, and a plaque marked “Rupa Shikshsthali” commemorates the event. There is another plaque, this one bearing the footprints of Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, at Allahabad’s Veni Madhava temple, where Lord Chaitanya sang and danced during

His stay in the area. Lord Chaitanya also spent three nights at the Nag Vasuki Temple, in Daraganj, just near the Mela campgrounds.

Prabhupada himself lived and ran a business—Prayag Pharmacy—in Allahabad from 1923 to 1932 and again from 1948 to 1953. On November 21, 1932, at the Allahabad branch of the Gaudiya Math, Prabhupada was formally initiated by his guru. While here, he owned a big Buick and had a driver, and to create a little side income he lent the car out to his nephew, who drove it as a taxi. Prabhupada told us that in 1953 his employees had stolen his money and pharmacy inventory—practically everything he owned. This catastrophic event, he said, caused him to remember Krishna’s statement from the Tenth Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam: “If I especially favor someone, I gradually deprive him of his wealth. Then the relatives and friends of such a poverty-stricken man abandon him. In this way he suffers one distress after another. When he becomes frustrated in his attempts to make money and instead befriends My devotees, I bestow My special mercy on him.” From that moment, Srila Prabhupada abandoned Allahabad and dedicated his life to preaching Krishna consciousness, preparing himself to one day find us in America.

**\* \* \***

Since time immemorial, people have gathered for spiritual cleansing at this confluence of sacred rivers, believing that anyone who comes here and bathes at the auspicious moments when life force (amrita) pours from the heavens is guaranteed forgiveness of sins, promotion to the heavenly planets—or at least a good job with the Indian Railways.

The Magh Mela Committee had allotted ISKCON a 100' x 100' plot, well-situated near Daraganj Gate and very close to the famous, two-million-year-old Bade Hanuman temple. The Mela seemed like the perfect place to showcase our Krishna consciousness movement for the spiritual leaders of Hindu India. Everyone would be there, and we could catch them all in one shot. In fact, Prabhupada had been anticipating attending the Mela and, unknown to all but a few of his disciples in Calcutta, had applied for a campsite months ago, paying eight thousand rupees in advance for the pandal infrastructure. (Events seemed so chaotic in India that we often couldn’t see how carefully Srila Prabhupada was planning his mission.)

The sun rises at about 7 a.m. in January in Allahabad and sets at about 5:30 p.m. Daytime temperatures average 68°F, but nighttime lows are around 38° or less. Every day starts with a chilling fog. Huffing out little bursts of steam, I tell the tonga driver, “Hare Krishna Mandir. Chalo! Jelde, jeldel—let’s go! Hurry, hurry!” He knows right away where the place is. Twenty-five devotees were already living here; some had arrived from Surat earlier in January, then ten more from Calcutta, and now the Bombay contingent. It must have been chaos as they tried to set up the tents and other structures.

Madhudvisa Swami: This was the first time that all the devotees in India had come together. There had been devotees in Calcutta for three years, and some had settled in Bombay a few months earlier. There were also devotees in Delhi. We were spread out, preaching in our different places. Prabhupada wanted to reorganize all the preaching in India, so he had all of us meet him in a central location, at Kumbha Mela.

Prabhupada had arrived in Allahabad on January 12. A few days later I arrived to an already well-established camp. In the center was a 90' x 60' brightly colored pandal, twenty feet high, reminiscent of a circus tent with its six-inch vertical stripes in red, yellow, blue, orange, and purple. Huge red carpets had been spread over the sand floor. There were also three smaller tents, residences for the men, women, and Srila Prabhupada, and a flimsy corrugated tin shack, which served as the kitchen. The compound was enclosed on three sides by a ten-foot tin-sheet fence, the front open to the pilgrim traffic on Triveni Road.

Prabhupada had brought with him a set of twenty-four-inch brass Radha-Krishna Deities, and Their Lordships now stood on an elaborate, three-tiered altar at the center-back wall of the pandal, Dressed in beautiful, gold-brocaded silk and draped with pearls and bright-red rose garlands, Their faces framed by jeweled crowns, Radha and Krishna looked stunning against a dark-red, crushed-velvet backdrop embroidered with golden floral designs. These were the first ISKCON-installed Deities I’d seen since arriving in India almost four months earlier, and I rushed to fall at Their lotus-like feet. They seemed to wink at me over tall white candles and the yellow and orange marigolds heaped around Them, as if to say, “Glad you could make it. Were having a ball.” Nanda Kumar, stripped to the waist, was sitting to the side of the altar, polishing a ghee lamp.

“Jeez, Nanda Kumar, aren’t you freezing in here?”

“Yeah, but you get used to it.”

There are maybe fifty people lined up in the main tent waiting to greet the Deities. Everyone is wrapped in blankets, sweaters, chaadars, shawls. I’m delighted to see the familiar faces of devotees with whom I’d already shared so many amazing adventures: Dinanath, Kaushalya, Durlabh, Chidananda, Hamsaduta, Himavati, Bruce. And there are a few Indian devotees now—they must be from Calcutta. Hey, there’s Jayapataka and Achyutananda. (After three tough years as pioneers in Calcutta, these vets spoke Hindi and Bengali and knew their way around, giving confidence to us greenhorns.) There’s Madhudvisa, now a Swami, whom I haven’t seen since San Francisco. Ah, and there’s Tamal Krishna.

“Where’s Prabhupada, Tamai?”

“He just finished his morning lecture. He’s in his tent. He’ll want to see you right away. Come on—I’ll take you there.”

"Saraswati!” My daughter does a double take, sees her dad, and comes running into my arms, squealing like a piglet. “You wanna go see Prabhupada with me?” She grabs my hand and leads the way through a flap at the back of the tent.

“Jeez, Tamal, it’s so bloody cold!”

“Yeah, there was ice on top of the water buckets this morning.

But everybody gets a wool blanket.”

“Who were those naked guys sitting back there, with the big black beads and metal sticks?”

“Those are the Naga Babas—Shiva guys. You can tell by the tridents. They’re covered in ashes, totally renounced, but they smoke pot. They hang out here in the early mornings ’cause we’re the only place that’s open. Prabhupada has us pop the tent flaps and blow the conch at 4 a.m. This place is packed for mangal-arati—you won’t believe it.”

We pass a tiny, ramshackle enclosure—the kitchen shack—and I hear the clattering of pots along with muttered imprecations.

“That’s Rebatinandan. He’s head cook. It’s a tough deal, cooking out here for forty-five devotees. Plus Prabhupada wants to feed rice, puris, and chutney to everyone who comes to the pandal. That’s like seven hundred people a day.”

“Get out!” I hear Rebati shout behind the tin wall, and Madri rushes out, wiping tears from her eyes.

We knock on a pole outside Prabhupada’s tent. “Yes?” I raise the flap and there he is, seated on a cushion before a low table covered with papers and documents. He wears a saffron wool watch cap and a russet sweater, a gray chador draped over his shoulders. His eyebrows shoot up and a huge smile lights his face. We bow and rise. Prabhupada is a little rumpled this early in the morning, gray stubble strengthening his face. In the low light from a lantern he looks thirty years old, his eyes flashing with intensity. He is the commander on campaign—in full control, dashing off orders and advice to his army of children around the world. He sets down his pen and says, “Oh, and here is our Saraswati!” and he reaches over and pinches her already apple-red cheeks. She hides her head in my dhoti, shy and happy to see him.

“You are keeping well, Srila Prabhupada?”

“Yes, only some slight congestion in the head from the cold, but it is nothing. We are accustomed. Sarva-karma-phaladyagam, prahus tyagam vichakshanah: a little trouble we can manage for Krishna, what is that? And what is the news, Bombay affairs?”

“All’s going well, Srila Prabhupada. We’re trying for the Cross Maidan field for the Bhagavata Dharma Discourses for late March or early April—ten days. Sadaji and other Life Members are helping with municipal permissions.”

“What will be the cost, total cost? How can you pay?”

“It’s going to cost a lot of money, Prabhupada, but we have this idea to create a souvenir book for the festival. Businessmen will pay to put their advertisement in the book.”

“Hmm. That is a standard procedure.”

“And I think we have got that Akash Ganga apartment, seventh floor, on Warden Road. The owner of the place knows George’s friend, Ravi Shankar, so he likes us. Gurudas and Rishi Kumar have stayed back a couple more days to finish the lease.”

“Yes, I have approved expenditure of eighteen thousand rupees—six months’ advance rent.”

Prabhupada’s tiny electric heater is having little effect on the cold room, and wisps of mist punctuate our conversation. I love when he’s wearing those reading glasses, his eyes magnified with infinite knowledge. Relief floods my body, knowing all will be well now that I’m in the presence of my spiritual master. Removing the glasses he says, “Today, just now, we shall go for taking bath in Ganga. Later we shall discuss. You are ready?”

He points to his metal trunk and tells his secretary, Devananda, to take out two big bags of aluminum coins—and to bring them with us. On the way out, Tamai tells me this is one of the most auspicious days for bathing according to astrological calculation, so it’s going to be a madhouse at the beach. But Prabhupada wants to go.

“Are you kidding me?” I think. “We’re going for a swim in this cold?” Surrender, remember? So I grab my towel and join the group.

It’s about 7 a.m., and maybe twenty devotees, some running to catch up, fan out to the sides and behind Srila Prabhupada as he bounds from the pandal onto the sixty-foot-wide Triveni Road, looking spiffy in a long pink hooded overcoat someone made for him. He turns left and slows to an amble, the orange sunrise brightening his face. His head held high, he looks stern as he taps his black mahogany cane on the pavement and his battalion breaks into song. The streets are nearly deserted at this early hour, the camps just starting to stir. We can hear a few loudspeakers in the distance cranking out scratchy prayers or morning ragas on shenai, but our whump-whump of mridangas and the clash of our kartals and the full-throated chorus of Krishna’s names predominates down the lines.

Between drumbeats, Madhudvisa explains to me, “We’re on the west bank. That’s the Ganges about five hundred yards ahead.”

It’s so wide! Reminds me of the Columbia River in the Oregon of my youth.

“Down there—” he says, “that way’s south—maybe half a mile or so is the Yamuna River. The two rivers meet over there. That’s called the sangam”

“Have you seen it yet?” I ask.

“No, but some devotees have. We can go later, if you want—hire a boat.”

The rising sun is now burning off the fog, and we smell smoke from ten thousand cooking fires. There’s murmuring on all sides. Ageless India is stirring. Pilgrims wrapped in blankets stumble onto the road, disheveled, soot-smeared, singly or in groups, rushing even at this hour to bathe in the river or find food, water, or wood, mumbling prayers as they walk, and astounded by our rollicking sankirtan.

My battered copy of Cook’s Travelers Handbook describes the bridge we see across the Ganges and to the north of us. Two kilometers long, Daraganj Railway Bridge was built by the British in 1857. The old map also shows a pontoon bridge made of small boats laid side to side—yes, there it is ahead.

Madhudvisa says, “And over there—you see that flag? That’s the Bade Hanuman temple. It’s very old. Hanuman’s lying down in there, carved in stone, about six feet long. They say the deity was found there, buried in the sand, and during monsoons he’s completely submerged by the Ganges.”

Our own Hanuman is here too—our gregarious Hanuman das from French Canada, so ugly he’s beautiful. “Shyama-sun-DAR, maintenant, you are so bee-yoo-tiful! I loff you!” he says, with his huge, half-closed green eyes. And Saraswati shouts, “Hariboll” in her little yellow sweater, arms raised, bouncing on my shoulders, her two short pigtails sticking out and golden in the sun. Achyutananda, banging the mridanga beside me, knows how to wrap a brown turban around his head Indian-style. Hanuman and Achyutananda get into a kirtan duel now—Hanuman with kartals, Achyuta on drum, leaping back and forth in each other’s faces, working up the crowd. Many of us hardly know each other, but we see in this timeless sankirtan soul-parade what must be an eternal bond, deep friendship from lifetimes past. And our master leads us on.

Sadhus fall at Prabhupada’s feet, interrupting our march, and Prabhupada laughs and blesses them all. He’s enjoying this excursion, showing us how it must have been in ancient times, in those stories from Srimad-Bhagavatam of immense spiritual assemblies—thirty-three thousand sages gathered to hear truth at Naimisharanya, the armies massing at Kurukshetra—still living in Technicolor and 3-D right before our eyes. The flow of people thickens into a crowd as we near the river. We begin to see poor and sick people sitting by the side of the road, little tin pots on the sand before them. Devananda passes out coins to us, and we each put a handful into our bead bags. Prabhupada motions for us to put a coin in each beggar’s pot. I pull out a coin. What is this—ten annas? Jeez, it’s one anna. At a hundred annas to a rupee, and a rupee worth thirteen American cents—“C’mon, Hamsaduta, make this leper’s day and give him two cents!”

As we proceed we begin to see sadhus and monks, grouped by denomination, along the side of the road. Their tilak., dress (or lack of it), and other symbols tell us what sect they are from. Their eyes look far away as our coins clink in their battered bowls; each face is a gateway to a million ageless tales.

As we near the river, yogis appear, lining the path and doing tricks. “What! Malati, d you see that?” There’s a boy-man lying on a wooden plank with a dozen foot-long spikes penetrating his torso, his eyes focused on eternity. The wounds are long-healed—must be something he’s done most of his life. There’re more of them—it’s a gathering of men on spikes. Their handlers stare at us and we stare back at them, trying to unravel in our minds this testament to sacrifice and the power that’s supposed to come from it. Is it worth it? Do these guys get anything more than a meager living for the incredible pain they’ve undergone? This yogi stuff is no longer a fairytale, son.

We see other yogis buried in the sand with only an arm showing, their fingernails a foot long and curling around their wrists. There are levitators, a row of cross-legged men seated above the ground. One guy’s almost two feet up! Wait a minute. We glance at Prabhupada. He marches past, looking the other way, like, “Come on, lads, let’s go to the beach.”

Kaushalya: Prabhupada had told us, “Nou must be very careful because there are very powerful yogis here,” and that many of them have very powerful siddhis—like they had the power to mystically move between places. He said, “You must be very respectful, very careful.”

Now we were really seeing ancient Vedic India, a land of magic, yogis, and tapasya, voluntary austerities performed to gain spiritual force. Prabhupada says most of these yogis are bogus, but some are the real thing; a few are hundreds of years old, he says, and still appear young and fresh. They gather for the Mela, then return to their seclusion, living with tigers in the mountain jungles. If you make eye contact with one of those thousand-yard stares, it almost blows you backward. Some guys are standing on one leg; the other, after years of performing this austerity, is a shrunken appendage of bone and skin. “Wait’ll George gets a load of this!” I think.

But we are subdued by it all, too, respectful, and we’re getting it. Prabhupada has taught us that one has to perform many austerities in order to develop yogic powers—but for what? Each yogi has a little pot by the road to collect a few paisa from people as they pass. “They undergo so many hardships just to earn a little money. It is a business,” Prabhupada says. Where’s the bliss, the joy, from all that austerity?

You only see bliss on the faces of the Krishna bhaktas, an uproarious, noisy pack moving against the throng to the river. The crowd parts as we reach the water’s edge. A sea of people is already submerged to the waist before us. Steam rises from the glacier-born stream. We look to Prabhupada. He says, “In 1928, I remember I was touching the water and it was so pinching that it was cutting your body. But as soon as you actually get a dip—one ... two ... three—then you immediately come out, and it is all right.” He removes his hood and cap, reaches down, takes a handful of Ganga water and sprinkles it on his bare head. He motions us to go on in—he’s had his bath, and it looks like he’d prefer to sit this one out.

We shed our layers, setting them in a well-guarded pile, and timidly look for an opening into the—whoa! freezing!—but shallow water, the holy mother of all waters, whose very touch brings liberation and forgiveness of sins. Each of us is focused on our own sacred encounter here, and some of the Indians move aside slightly to accommodate our huge white bodies. Most of us are dipping with our hands and sprinkling Ganga water over our heads, but we see others pouring water over themselves with little pots—men in skimpy gamchas, women draped in wet and clinging saris. The bolder ones among us dip under completely as we move to thighdeep, hip-deep, and then—we’re Americans after all—we dive forward and swim out past the bathers, exuberant with the cold and laughing out loud to feel the bone-deep blessing. We look back to the bank and see Prabhupada laughing with us.

**\* \* \***

We were all newcomers to India, but somehow we had organized and were running our camp in the midst of this whole strange show. There were constant glitches in these extreme close quarters, and Prabhupada sometimes got angry with the way we did things, but he guided us in this way, showing us the meaning of personal sacrifice. He himself was rising at 4 a.m. for mangal-arati and taking cold-water bucket baths. After his morning walks he shaved, shirt off, in the weak early sun, holding his little mirror in his left hand, his Gillette in the other, while just a few feet from him we ran around, preparing breakfast, chanting rounds, taking our own baths, and otherwise getting ready for another action-packed day.

Our group was an unlikely mix of single men and women, married couples and sannyasis, with Prabhupada as our rock-hard center. We each knew that if Prabhupada was happy with our behavior the rest would take care of itself. We felt a deep need to demonstrate to everyone at Kumbha Mela the legitimacy and strength of Prabhupadas movement and prove to them that his ISKCON was the advent of what other sadhus could only wish for: that their own beliefs were now being shared and practiced in a pure manner by people from a world they could scarcely imagine.

We learned from Prabhupada how to bathe with a few cups of water without undressing completely; how to clean our backsides with water, and how to share a tiny toilet with fifty others while preserving everyone’s modesty. We learned to cook for hundreds on tiny wood fires, to rise in the dark and cold against all temptation to sleep, and to overlook each others faults and seek only their good cheer.

Perhaps alluding to our sinful pasts, which still lurked in us, Prabhupada lectured in the early mornings on the Faust-like story of Ajamila. Ajamila was a sinful man, but at the moment of death he cried out Krishna’s name “Narayana!” The Yamadutas, enforcers of the god of death, came to take Ajamila away, but Krishna’s agents showed up too and made a counterclaim for his soul. What follows is the ultimate discussion on sin and atonement. Prabhupada said:

sarvesham apy aghavatam  
idam eva sunishkritam  
nama-vyaharanam vishnor  
yatas tad-vishaya matih

So by chanting Hare Krsna mantra one becomes free from sinful reaction. Not only that, he becomes very soon a devotee. Yatas tad-vishaya matih. Yatah means simply by chanting. Here it is clearly stated, nama-vyaharanam, chanting of the holy name ... So not only he becomes free from all sinful reaction of his life, but yatas tad-vishaya matih: his mind becomes fixed up in the lotus feet of Krsna.

Krishna’s deputies win the debate, and Ajamila eventually goes back to Godhead.

Prabhupada’s lectures at Kumbha Mela are intimate, and devotees feel free to raise their hands and ask questions mid-lecture. For every question, Prabhupada has an immediate answer, which he backs up with a scriptural quotation. There are many thousands of verses in the Vedic literature, and Prabhupada seems to remember them all. We are blown away by this—so amazed by his knowledge and so proud of him. Much of what Prabhupada says is over our heads, but he repeats his points constantly, and some of it begins to penetrate our hard hearts. He’s also preparing us to preach in India. I notice the devotees’ questions are more detailed, more aligned with advancing understanding. Rebatinandan, Yamuna, and Hamsaduta ask good questions about the soul and the different degrees of heaven. Me—I just remain stupid. I’m not very good at memorizing verses or understanding a thing in detail. But I get the main points and they sink in. What I know best is that Prabhupada knows everything. My instincts run to action, so I think, “Let Prabhupada take the stage, and let me help him prepare the biggest stage possible. This will be my service.”

At night, Prabhupada speaks in Hindi to the hundreds of Mela attendees who fill our pandal and spill out into the wide street in front. We distribute huge pots of halvah and other prasada to all who enter.

In January in this part of India, there’s a weak easterly monsoon called the retreating monsoon. Sure enough, it rained for a couple of days, light but constant, while we were there. The ground turned into a sea of mud. Everything leaked, and wet sand clung to our bodies, clothes, cookware, and beds. The Naga Babas, thin, ash-smeared, dreadlocked, and naked, were particularly affected, and crowds of them often took refuge in our main tent, sticking their tridents in the sand as they sat around the entry. One day, Tamal Krishna saw about ten babas lighting a chillum (hash pipe) and politely asked them to leave. Their red eyes stared back at him, but there was no other response. With emphatic gestures and a louder voice, Tamal again asked them to leave. Again there was no response. Finally, Tamal grabbed one of their sacred iron tridents and heaved it from the tent. We heard it clatter on the road outside. Silence gripped the tent. If ever there was a time when Black Doom would strike us from the sky, this was it. Five or six big American men slowly gathered, ready to jump in and fight if it came to it. Minutes passed while the opposing forces glared at each other—Krishna Cowboys vs. Shiva Bullies. But then as one the babas rose and padded away, Radha and Krishna smiling from Their altar and watching them disappear down the street.

Most mornings Srila Prabhupada took his walk early, passing through the neighborhood with his troop of singers. While other sankirtan parties went out during the day, spreading the maha-mantra around vast reaches of the Kumbha Mela, Prabhupada met people in his tent, wrote letters to disciples around the world, and otherwise caught up with business that had been postponed during the manic first months in India.

A few weeks earlier, on December 6, 1970, Times of India Weekly had published a letter to the editor:

\[The ISKCON devotees\] explained ... that Krishna was above all religions, the Universal Teacher, the Supreme Man, the personification of the Absolute Truth. If it is so, then why can’t they go to Pakistan and China for the chanting of Krishna’s name and ask them to vacate aggression, the soil of this land where the Great Master was born!”

This idea seemed to stick in Prabhupada’s mind, and he started discussing with us his plan to send a preaching mission to Pakistan. Personally, the idea terrified me, and no one else volunteered to go either. Pakistan was two different nations back then, West Pakistan and East Pakistan, separated by a thousand miles of north India. In January 1971, both halves of the nation were on the verge of civil war. On January 18 Prabhupada wrote to Gargamuni Swami in Florida:

People sometimes inquire if we have gone to Pakistan. And I am thinking—why not? Both India and Pakistan are in great distress because of this false partition and I am sure the whole matter can be put to rights by proper application of our principles of Krsna Consciousness.

You can immediately make arrangements to go there to East Pakistan, and Brahmananda Maharaja to West Pakistan, each assisted by one brahmacari ... Now you will have to ply your good intelligence for serving Krsna very diligently and soberly in foreign land.

Whoa, we thought. This is an impossible mission. Such penance! But they did it. Gargamuni and Brahmananda left the US and took off for the two Pakistans. More on that later.

A few days later, on January 23, Prabhupada wrote to Kshirodakshayi in London:
