In the meantime our program for touring India has been going with all success in every place we are invited. Now we have come to the Ardha Kumbha Mela at Prayag and we have got undisputed prominence amongst all groups here in the large gathering.... Regarding the Regent’s Park land, I am very hopeful of your efforts to secure permission from the Queen.

**\* \* \***

One morning, after a night of rain, Prabhupada arranges to take us to the Allahabad chapter of the Gaudiya Math, where he had been initiated thirty-eight years earlier. There are still puddles in the low spots, but the sun is bright in a turquoise, rain-washed sky. A train of nine bicycle rickshaws makes its way from the pandal, snaking slowly up Mahatma Gandhi Road, with Srila Prabhupada in the lead rickshaw. He’s wearing his best duds and fresh tilak. All his clothes are in saffron color—his chaadar, even the pilot’s cap fastened by two pearl buttons at his chin.

The rest of us are crammed two or three to a rickshaw. It’s uncomfortable to see the malnourished driver working so hard to haul us up the hill, but we accept now the Indian concept of service: “This is my job, and I’m proud of it.” Prabhupada, in the lead, pulls up his sun cover, but most of us leave ours down to welcome the sun after so many days of rain.

“Sri Rupa Gaudiya Math” stands in bold letters above the arched entrance at 77 Tularam Road. Srila Prabhupada helped develop this temple almost four decades earlier. He was initiated here as “Abhay Charanaravinda das,” in this very room we’re visiting today, and he’s happy to be reunited with the familiar Sri Sri Radha-Madhava Deities and a couple of his aged godbrothers. Lord Chaitanya stands on the altar next to Krishna—the first time most of us have seen Gauranga as the object of temple worship. And we all feel a living connection with Srila Prabhupada’s physical past. It’s like the closing of a circle, or meeting long-missed friends. We have rousing kirtan and a parikram around the Deity room, and then we eat lunch before heading out for the next adventure: a boat trip on the Ganges to the holy sangam.

Madhudvisa, Devananda, and I walk about a mile from the pandal to the confluence of the Ganges and Yamuna rivers. From here, looking across to the far banks, the white tents that mark the Mela are visible for miles. We agree to the standard ten-rupee fee for a small dugout canoe, with rowers fore and aft, and paddle out to the actual sangam, where the sacred rivers meet. Hundreds of miles from its Himalayan sources, the silt-laden Ganga is very wide here, but it narrows just before entering the Yamuna, and so picks up speed. Were surprised to see that the Yamuna is bigger, slower, and deeper than the Ganga, and very clear. The Ganga slams into the quiet Yamuna, and when you add the Saraswati River, which enters from below through a hole in the earth, you get a violent confluence of currents: the mile-wide Triveni and—at its center—a magnificent whirlpool a hundred feet across.

“Look! There’s where the Yamuna comes in!”

“Wow! There’s the whirlpool!”

“Is that a body?” We watch as a gaily-wrapped cadaver floats by and slips silently over the edge and down into the spinning currents. The boatmen are careful now; they slow and maneuver us along the standing lip of the curling water, and we stare into the abyss of power, where the underground Saraswati joins and throws the massive volumes of the other two rivers into a circle.

The millions-of-years-old Rig Veda describes the important rivers in ancient India from the Ganges in the east to the Indus in the west in a clear, geographical sequence: Ganges, Yamuna, Saraswati, Indus. About twelve thousand years ago, earth’s movements and melting glaciers from the previous ice age altered north India’s topography, and the Saraswati disappeared beneath the sands of Rajasthan’s Thar Desert. It’s still found deep underground in paleo channels, and now reemerges at the sangam from below, enhancing the sacred turbulence.

This is the magic vortex into which yogis dive and reemerge in other places.

“I have personally seen,” Prabhupada says, “that they take bath in the Ganges and come up in the seven sacred rivers. They go down in the Ganges and come up in the Godavari River. Then they go down and come up in the Krishna River, and go down—like that.”

**\* \* \***

Late in the morning of January 24, Prabhupada took us on another adventure, this time to the ashram of Prabhudutt Brahmachary. We traveled upstream by boat for a mile or so along the east bank of the Ganges to an Allahabad suburb called Jhusi. Prabhudutt Brahmachary was well known for translating the Chaitanya Charitamrita into Hindi, and he was a stout old Vaishnava in Prabhupada’s disciplic line. Earlier in January, devotees had spent a week at his ashram while preparing our pandal across the river.

We set out in three fifteen-foot wooden rowboats with canvas sunroofs. Prabhupada and his attendants were in the lead boat, and the rest of us crammed into the two following boats. As we slowly paddled across the wide Ganga, Malati described an incident that had happened while she was staying with the other devotees at Prabhudutt’s ashram a couple of weeks earlier: Saraswati had slipped and fallen into the pit at the outhouse! Malati rushed her—yuck!—covered in excrement, to the bank of the Ganges to wash, and the sandy bank beneath them caved in. Suddenly our little Saraswati was alone in the current, drifting away! Malati can’t swim. Panicked, she shouted Krishna’s name. Then a tall, skinny Shiva baba, who was sitting in meditation nearby, slowly rose and, wading storklike out into the stream, hefted our daughter into his arms, dumped her in the sand at Malati's feet, and then walked back to his chillum in the shade as if nothing had happened.

On the east shore we saw an elephant painted in bright, jewellike patterns, chained to a huge plane tree, eating leaves from the tree’s limbs that had been lopped off above by its mahout and dropped to the ground at its feet. It looked to us like the whole tree would be finished in a couple of days, and we guessed they’d just move over to the next tree and repeat the process.

As we neared the ashram’s tiny dock, the tension we each felt from the crowded pandal and the noise and bustle of the Mela began to flow out and away through our hands and feet as we trailed them overboard in the cool Ganges water. We stayed at the ashram all day and held some mellow kirtans. Prabhupada was casual but at the top of his game as he spoke to the assembled Vaishnavas. We watched him lounge on white cushions, draped in garlands of aromatic orange and yellow marigolds, relishing the winter flowers. A warm, gentle breeze rustled palm leaves overhead, and birds called. We were served a sumptuous, mid-afternoon meal, and then we napped for an hour in the quiet, spacious ashram. As evening fell, we descended the hill to our waiting boats. Once more, Srila Prabhupada had displayed to all who had seen him the full cognizance, beauty, and sheer calm force of his fully-realized life.

**\* \* \***

On January 28, a few of us hopped a train back to Bombay to take possession of Akash Ganga and to start work on the big pandal show we hoped to stage at Cross Maidan. Before leaving the Kumbha Mela, we met with Srila Prabhupada several times in his tent to discuss the Bombay program. I described to him my hopes for the Bhagavata Dharma Discourses show. “Prabhupada, thousands of people will come every night when you speak. We’ll have kirtan in the pandal all day, gorgeous Deities, sell hundreds of books. We’ll advertise all over town. It’ll be the biggest show in Bombay, ever.”

I could see he was a little skeptical, but he encouraged me, told me to “chase the rhinoceros,” and I was more determined than ever. I could sense that some devotees considered my big plans just another of “Shyamasundar’s wild schemes.” I also sensed that most of the devotees just wanted to stay in Prabhupada’s company and travel with him around India. I did too, of course—personal association with our spiritual master was blissful—but I had experienced an even higher bliss when Prabhupada looked into my eyes after I’d done some big service—once I’d captured a rhino—and said, “You have done very well.” There was no bliss higher than this. Prabhupada himself had set that example; just see all the impossible rhinos he had bagged for his own spiritual master! From my experiences in San Francisco and London—the Mantra Rock Dance concert, the Ratha-yatra festivals, Bury Place, the Beatles (“My Sweet Lord” right now the #1 song in the world)—I knew that the sky was the limit and anything could be done. I had already realized the supermost bliss of seeing Krishna’s hand removing obstacles to service so “impossible” that He must personally interfere. I wanted to show Prabhupada off to a bigger audience, the movers and shakers of India, and these people lived and worked in Bombay.

I described to Srila Prabhupada our progress so far. We had applied to the municipality of Bombay for permits to use the Cross Maidan grounds. We would print a program souvenir filled with paid adverts by well-wishing businessmen. A full-page ad would cost them a thousand rupees. At a hundred pages, we’d make one lakh rupees, and we thought that would be enough for everything. There were professional pancLzZ-building companies in Bombay that could erect these huge constructions almost overnight—you just had to have the money. We would advertise on billboards and banners across Bombay’s main roads, and we planned to pass out flyers on busy streets and at train and bus stations and plaster the newspapers with ads.

“Shyamasundar, you will be the overall in-charge. You just divide up yourselves, who will do the different tasks. Krishna will help you. Let us see.” With that, Prabhupada bade us goodbye, and our skeleton crew—Madhudvisa, Rishi Kumar, Yamuna, Tushta Krishna, Tulsi Krishna, Malati, young Saraswati, and me—hit the rails for Bombay. Prabhupada would be traveling to Calcutta after the Mela, and then maybe on to Mayapur or Gorakhpur or maybe Madras. He said he would come to Bombay when we called for him.

**\* \* \***

We moved onto the seventh floor at Akash Ganga. Views through the south- and west-facing windows were fantastic: the posh manors on Malabar Hill; a vast expanse of the Arabian Sea stretching to the horizon; on the right, down Warden Road, were Breach Candy Swimming Club and the US Consulate. There was still construction noise from the floors above us and the place smelled of fresh paint. At first there was a little privacy, but as more and more devotees arrived the place became crowded, so we moved the men into one flat and the women into the other. The girls prepared Prabhupadas quarters and set up a simple altar for our daily worship and classes, while the boys hit the streets to raise enthusiasm and money for the festival.

On February 4, Prabhupada, still in Calcutta, wrote a letter to Hayagriva in the US:

Our touring program here in India is going on and I have just come from Allahabad where we attended the Ardha Kumbha Mela celebrations for three weeks. Our camp was very highly appreciated by all the big officials and they have said that ours was the most successful presentation. Even the members of many Mayavadi camps had to appreciate our movement’s activities and they have joined our Kirtan also, even though I strongly defeat all their arguments in daily lectures. One influential man of the Allahabad community has expressed it that “never before have we heard personalism so strongly preached.” Now we are registered in India and we have got our tax exemption permit. Our new address of Bombay ISKCON is given above. The building is in the most elite section of Bombay and the rent is Rs. 3,000 per month.

While we poured ourselves into festival preparations, Prabhupada traveled from Calcutta to Gorakhpur to attend an extended program with his dancing white elephants. We couldn’t keep track of his whereabouts—there was little communication. We hoped he would soon be coming back to Bombay.

The front windows at Akash Ganga looked out over the Arabian Sea. The February weather in Bombay was perfect; there was brilliance to the air reminiscent of Los Angeles, and we all bought cheap sunglasses. I borrowed the ’52 Chevy again, and we made our daily rounds. As I had done in London with my little red Fordson pickup, I drove the Chevy into town each morning and dropped devotees off on various street corners near that day’s scheduled appointments. Then I might go over to Sada Jiwat Lal’s office to see if there was progress on getting permits for the pandal or visit a Life Member or two to hustle an ad for the souvenir. We would all make regular pit stops, brief moments of respite from the burning sidewalks of the Fort, pausing at one of Sadaji’s sweet shops for refreshment or, if we were anywhere near the Ballard Docks we’d stop to see Mr. Choksi, Sumati Morarji’s secretary at Scindia House. Years back, Choksi had helped Prabhupada meet and befriend Mrs. Morarji, and he always dropped whatever he was doing when we came by, giving us cold drinks and boosting our morale with ardent discussion.

Often, if I was in the downtown Fort area I would stop by to chat with Mr. Lucky. We became close, in a way. He would tell me what the Koran says about something and I would tell him what the Gita says about the same thing. He explained how foreign cash was converted into luxury goods and gold in Dubai and then smuggled back into India on fishing boats. And Lucky kept me informed about world affairs, such as the war brewing in Pakistan, and he explained how such affairs affected currency markets. “War is good for business,” he said.

One day, he said, “I want to show you something. Do you have time?” He then took me for a drive in his white Ambassador. His driver threw me threatening looks in the rearview mirror. We arrived at a warehouse somewhere in north Bombay. Lucky waved to the plainclothes guards, who were stationed for blocks around, until we swept into a chain-link-fenced compound and parked in front of a huge hangarlike building. He opened several locks on a heavy door and switched on the overhead lights inside. There were stacks of crated television sets, refrigerators, and cases of foreign cigarettes piled high as far as my eyes could see. I was speechless, deeply impressed. Then he pointed to a nearby steel vault, fumbled with more keys, and unlocked the door to Aladdin’s cave: gold bars, stacked in all sizes, from tola (ten-gram) biscuits to kilo ingots, baskets of gold watches, rings, and bracelets. The little room was full. Mr. Lucky was a cold-faced guy—never showed emotion—but that day he smiled, and I saw his dimples and gold-capped teeth.

After visiting Lucky or a Life Member, I’d have lunch. For me, that meant a quick stop at the Parsee Dairy on Princess Street, beneath the Marine Drive flyover. I’d grab a quarter kilo of fresh homemade cheese (paneer) and a sealed plastic bag of buffalo milk. In my car parked in the shade under the overpass was a loaf of sliced white bread, a jar of Major Grey’s Chutney, and a knife.

Cheese and chutney sandwiches every day, offered to a little picture of Radha-Krishna on the dashboard and wolfed down before dashing to another appointment. I was a regular Bombay-walla.

Parking in front of some office building in the Fort, I would pay a kid two or three rupees to watch the car and maybe wipe down the windshield with a dirty rag. Everyone in India has a job; you don’t deny them if they want to work. This was a difficult concept for us to grasp, with our egalitarian Western tradition of self-reliance. In India, you don’t carry something yourself but let someone else do it—their self-esteem depends on serving in some way. It’s been this way for thousands of years. Yoh are the Sahib. That’s your job—they’ll carry your bags, bring you water, and wash your clothes. That’s their job. We hired a woman to clean the Akash Ganga flats every day, and met dhobi-wallas (laundrymen) at the door each morning with our bundles of dirty clothes—that’s the system.

Undermanned and desperate to succeed, we worked the back lanes of the Fort, the palatial mansions on Malabar Hill, and hard-to-fmd addresses in north Bombay. Then we came together at night and wondered where all our effort was going: we had not yet secured permits for Cross Maidan, and we couldn’t give Prabhupada a confirmed date; the few thousand rupees we’d collected hardly made a dent in the total budget we would require. One morning at Akash Ganga, I looked in the bathroom mirror and saw for the first time permanent wrinkles on my forehead. I was only twenty-eight years old! But I realized that these wrinkles were some sort of rite of passage: anxiety and aging are something all mortals must accept.

We finally got some good news: the books had arrived! Aside from a few personal copies, we had no books. These books were the very essence of Prabhupada’s teachings, and without them it had been very difficult to convince the people we approached. Thousands of books printed in Japan had finally arrived at the Calcutta docks, and in the first week of January Srila Prabhupada had traveled there to clear them through customs and store them safely in a Scindia warehouse. He had then directed that six cases of books be sent by boat to the Scindia godown (warehouse) at the Alexandra docks in Bombay.

Now, a month later, Rishi Kumar and I hurried over to the harbor. It took us hours to negotiate the sprawling docks and godowns. Finally, we saw the word “ISKCON” painted on several heavy, four-foot-square wooden cases. We hastily pried open the top of the first crate, pulled out a few Krsna books, and saw they’d been chewed on by rats. Almost every book had chew marks, and some of the books were so damaged we couldn’t save them. Never mind. We picked out the least chewed books—Krsna, Bhagavad-gita, Teachings of Lord Chaitanya, Nectar of Devotion—and distributed them to our Life Members, greatly lifting their enthusiasm—and ours.

Near the end of February 1971, we finally got the best news of all: Cross Maidan was ours from March 25 through April 4. Still, when we weighed what we’d done so far against what remained to be done—it was overwhelming. We couldn’t do it, not without more help. Prabhupada had gone first to Gorakhpur and then to Benares. We imagined he was back in Gorakhpur, but we weren’t sure if he’d continue on from there to Calcutta or would come to Bombay. I sent telegrams to both Calcutta and Gorakhpur, hoping one of them would reach him:

CROSSMAIDAN CONFIRMED MARCH 25 APRIL 4

STOP MUST CANCEL UNLESS WE GET MORE MEN.

On February 28, Prabhupada wired me back:

SENDING SEVEN STRONGMEN IMMEDIATELY PROCEEDING THERE IS NO QUESTION OF CANCELLING CROSSMAIDAN.

BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI

Tamal Krishna, Gurudas, Giriraj, Chidananda, Gopal, Bhanu, and Sudama Vipra arrived by train at Victoria Station a few days later, and the Bombay crew was relieved. We had about three weeks to get everything ready. We divided up the work: Tamal Krishna would take charge of physical installations, setting up the pandal, cooking, and bookstall arrangements; Giriraj and Rishi Kumar would sell ads for the souvenir and be responsible for the finances; Madhudvisa would be program director for the stage and daily programs; I would supervise advertising; Yamuna would take care of the Deities; Gurudas knew Bombay and would assist in all departments. Everyone was assigned a task. Collectively we threw ourselves into the Cross Maidan project and it took on a life of its own. It was bigger than anything else we’d ever done.

**\* \* \***

Cross Maidan (may-daZzn, an ancient word for “playing field”) is the middle section of a 1-mile-long, 150-acre swath of open lawn and trees that runs north-south through the center of downtown Bombay, much like Central Park in New York City. The entire park—named “The Esplanade” by the British—is composed of Cooperage Cricket Grounds and Azad, Cross, and Oval Maidans. Cross Maidan is bounded on the west by Churchgate Railway Station. Just to its east is Victoria Terminus. Veer Nariman Road borders Cross Maidan’s five acres to the south and, as it is situated between two railway stations, carries the heaviest pedestrian traffic in Bombay. It’s the perfect spot for a spiritual circus.

Tamal Krishna contacted Mr. Bhattar at RMB Event Management and asked him to meet with us. Together we discussed a plan for the grounds.

“We’ve got a total area that’s five hundred feet long by four hundred feet wide. The main tent, just here in the center, backing on to the north fence, should be huge. Let’s make it 250 feet square. How many people will that hold, Mr. Bhattar?”

“Give or take, sitting and standing, about 10,000 persons.”

“Perfect! But it’s March. Summer season has started, and it’s getting hot. It’s already in the 90s out there. How do we keep such a big tent cool?”

“You will need fans—ceiling fans for the audience, portable fans onstage, and openings in the tents for air circulation.”

“Main thing is the stage. Prabhupada wants it to be very big—maybe about sixty feet long and thirty feet wide, with plenty of room for kirtan. And with a temple for the Deities, maybe just here.”

“And what about rugs? You will need rugs. I have many kinds of rugs.”

“OK, rugs everywhere. And what do you think, Mr. Bhattar, do we need chairs?”

“No, audience will sit on the ground on rugs. You may have a few chairs for VIPs near the front of stage.”

“OK. And on one side we’ll build book tables and a Life Membership table — ”

“Yes, we have many tables for you. And we can erect some small stalls.”

“Hey, what do you think, Tamai? Maybe we can put up some stalls and rent them out to, like, incense sellers, or for snack shops to make some extra cash.”

“Yeah, good idea, but we’d better ask Prabhupada first. And another thing: where are we going to cook?”

“Oh, yeah, forgot about that. We’d better move the main tent forward, build a cook shed in back, add another fifty feet—make it 300' x 250'. That still gives us a big courtyard out front to put up some displays about ISKCON around the world. And Prabhupada likes those dioramas.”

And so the Cross Maidan Festival quickly took shape.

Giriraj, Rishi Kumar, and Tushta Krishna sold advertising space in our Hare Krishna Festival souvenir: Rs. 1000/- for a full page, Rs. 500/- for a half page, and Rs. 300/- for a quarter page. If you wanted a front or back cover, they cost Rs. 5000/- each. We told business owners that “Everyone who donates gets a free copy, and all of your friends are doing it—let them see your name in there too!” The paid advertisers composed mostly sentimental messages like “Best Wishes for Hare Krishna Festival! Century Rayon, Industry House, 159 Churchgate Reclamation, Bombay-2.” We even got a quarter-page ad from Muhamed at Lucky Footwear, wishing us “Felicitations!”

Sales were brisk, but we were spending money as fast as these boys could bank it, and the pressure on them increased.

Some months ago Prabhupada had personally convinced Mr. R. D. Birla, a Bombay business tycoon, to provide two sets of marble Deities for our Bombay temple, but we had no clear idea when They might arrive from Jaipur, where They were being carved. But that didn’t stop us. We knew Radha and Krishna had to appear, just like They had in London a few days before Their installation at Bury Place in December 1969. In fact, Yamuna and Madhudvisa worked out an entire daily program for the festival built around the Deities’ schedule, like mangala-arati at—what?—4 a.m.?

“No, no. That’s way too early, we’ve gotta give Bombay people some time to wake up and get into town.” ’

“OK, how about 6 a.m.?”

“City law says we have to close down by 10 p.m. So, if we start at 6 in the morning, that’s sixteen hours a day times ten days—that’s a lot of time to fill!”

“Yeah. Prabhupada will speak—what?—maybe two hours a day? And we’ll have aratis, sankirtan, slideshows—and what else?”

“When Prabhupada gets here, we’ll ask him. But where is he? When’s he coming? And where are the Deities?”

Finally, on March 8,1 received a letter from Prabhupada, from Calcutta:

I’ve received one telegram from Hamsaduta. It is understood he has personally gone to Jaipur to take the Deities, and he will personally take them to Bombay, so there is no anxiety for the Deities. You can go on preparing the throne and dress very gorgeously. As scheduled, I shall reach Bombay by the 15th March and I shall let you know the exact time in due course.

Whew, man, that’s cutting it close—we need Prabhupada’s advice on this thing. Meanwhile, I was having fun with the advertising. There were not many restrictions in Bombay yet regarding commercial advertising, so we had a crew of men painting signs on canvas and wood, eighty-foot banners to stretch over major intersections, smaller signs for lampposts along every major road in town. Indian sign makers are very skilled and cheap. Red and blue letters on a white backdrop was our motif. Driving around, we’d say, “Oh, look, there’s an empty spot. You can see that from a hundred yards coming and going”—and we’d plaster yet another part of the town with “Hare Krishna! Cross Maidan,” or “Bhaktivedanta Swami: Bhagavata Dharma Discourses,” or “Prabhupada is Coming!” or “Save Your Life Now!” Corny stuff, American-style splashy, but we could feel Bombay beginning to buzz.

One tall, enthusiastic Parsee boy named Noza Mancherjee began to hang out with us at Akash Ganga and lend a hand at the pandal. (The Parsee people—Zoroastrians—had fled Persia and come to India centuries ago, and their Bombay descendants were an affluent and influential community, so we were glad for Noza’s help.) When Noza (later initiated as Mahamsa das) saw us running around putting up signs, he told us that his cousins owned an advertising company called Selvel. I could have kissed him. I’d seen the word Selvel on almost every billboard (or “hoarding,” as it is known in India) in town. Noza convinced his relatives to donate more than thirty major billboard sites around the city. Now we had huge hoardings, fifty to seventy feet long and twenty or thirty feet high, mounted at the best locations in Bombay. Large-size mechanical printing didn’t yet exist, so teams of skilled sign painters on scaffolds worked day and night with paints and brushes to create huge text and images—their reproductions of Prabhupada’s face were astoundingly lifelike.

Prabhupada arrived at Santacruz Airport on March 15, and as we drove him into Bombay, everywhere he looked he saw HARE KRISHNA FESTIVAL! WORLD PREACHER OF BHAKTI CULT: A. C. BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI! When he turned to me in the car with that special grin of his, I had never felt better in my life.

**\* \* \***

Ten days until D-Day and we were working around the clock, running back and forth from Cross Maidan to Akash Ganga to confer with Prabhupada, who had taken charge of every detail:

“Yamuna, just you place Radha and Krishna like this. The Indian people—they are not accustomed. They may take objection that a woman is conducting the worship, so Nanda Kumar will bepujari”

“Yes, it is all right to hire brahmin cooks provided they have all utensils and are very clean. Everyone who comes must take a little prasadam”

“No, we shall not rent out booths to outside vendors.”

“Flowers—there must be many, and every day fresh.”

“This balloon is a good idea, Shyamsundar. What will be the cost?”

“And our Giriraj—how much money you have made today?”

I had found a guy who fabricated light plastic, helium-filled balloons, and challenged him to make us one five meters (sixteen feet) in diameter. He artfully cut and fastened together dozens of flat white plastic triangles to create the largest balloon to date in India. He painted “HARE KRISHNA” on either side around the waist of the balloon in bold red letters three feet high. Six blue triangles tapered toward the apex on each side. We held our breath when it made its maiden voyage, but it slowly rose two hundred feet in the air, floating high above Cross Maidan, tethered by six white ropes. In a harness below the big balloon, the manufacturer had hung flashing white lights that read:

H

A

R

E

К

R

I

S

H

N

A

When he turned the lights on, we let out a collective cheer. You could see that balloon from one end of Bombay to the other—from Nariman Point to Tardeo.

As we counted down the days to the festival, the devotee population exploded—it seemed that when news of our adventures in Surat and at the Kumbha Mela hit temples in the West, devotees thought, “Hey, those guys are having all the fun. Lets go to Bombay!” They also came from all over India. Soon we had more than sixty devotees. Akash Ganga was packed to the ceiling. Some devotees moved into cheap hotels while others set up camp at Cross Maidan. Those who weren’t working on the pandal went out with the daily street sankirtan parties, passing out thousands of flyers to advertise the festival.

Prabhupada liked his room at Akash Ganga, which was on the west side facing the sea and next to the temple room. He had a little balcony and would often sit out there gazing at the ocean, chanting japa or answering questions from us like, “What will the daily themes for your discourses be, Prabhupada?” or “How much should we charge for Krsna book?”

We invited our Life Members and their families to a get-together at Akash Ganga on the evening of March 17, just a week before the Cross Maidan event. The place was jammed. Prabhupada thanked them for their help with the festival, but he didn’t mince words simply to placate them: “Just like in Bombay city everyone is working hard, very hard. Their mission is, ‘How I shall be able to construct a skyscraper building.’ That’s all. Why they are working so hard? Ten years, twenty years, fifty years, hundred years—then everything finished. Your skyscraper building, your nice wife, your apartment, your friends, everything that you have made, and you do not know where you are going.”

Prabhupada took his morning walks in the Hanging Gardens on Malabar Hill, where other prominent gentlemen strolled. From the moment we started up the wide stairs leading to the park, big industrialists, government leaders—the cream of Bombay society—rushed to greet him and touch his feet. There was a casual, imperial cadence to his walks at the Hanging Gardens, Prabhupada strutting, shoulders cocked, loose in the hips, like a Lipizzaner purebred, weight on his heels, feet flicking to the side. Bhaktivedanta Swami was the talk of the town.

A few days before the festival, we started hitting the newspapers with splashy, quarter-and half-page ads. We printed two hundred signs to post on city buses and commuter trains. One Life Member had a trucking company, so we posted big signs that read “Prabhupada and his Western Sadhus!” on the sides of his trucks. Another Life Member owned a twelve-story skyscraper on Mahatma Gandhi Road, just a few blocks from Cross Maidan, so with his encouragement we hired the Selvel company to paint “HARE KRISHNA” in ten-foot descending letters down a windowless side of the building. You could see it for miles.

Prabhupada came down to see the pandal on some days. As he walked around he’d ask, “What is the cost for this?”

“Four thousand rupees, Srila Prabhupada.”

“He is cheating you. Offer two thousand.”

He would also sit in a little dressing room behind the stage and point out defects in the construction or our arrangements and ask for adjustments. “Just make more space over here for putting shoes.” But mostly Srila Prabhupada heard our reports at night when we returned to Akash Ganga, and guided our crosstown project from his room on the seventh floor.

After conferring with him, Madhudvisa and Yamuna designed each day’s program and had it painted onto white signboards to be posted on either side of the entry into the main tent.

Events were moving fast now, and our individual responsibilities increased nearly to overload. And just when we thought it couldn’t get heavier, it got heavier. There was no time for existential thoughts like, “Where am I going with my life?” We were firmly lodged on Prabhupada’s magic energy-wave now, so let it flow, man, let it flow to wherever Krishna will take us. There was simply no time for negative things like tummy upsets or fevers or bad days of any kind. If there’s a problem, ignore it, man. Give it your extra shot, and Krishna will save the day.

And didn’t we see Krishna at work on this project! Our problems disappeared one by one. Key devotees just “happened” to show up at the perfect time, and money appeared out of thin air. So many coincidences and events came together with only minimal planning that there was just no question of who was in control. And it was such fun! Prabhupada always said that spreading Krishna’s name and fame should be a joyous experience, and he himself was always jolly and laughing. We couldn’t count the number of times we laughed until the tears came in the days leading up to the festival. We were a fine cast of characters, thrown together on an impossible, near-surreal errand for Krishna. We never knew what was going to happen next, but whatever it was, we knew it would be Krishna’s arrangement and therefore full of pleasure.

On March 24, the eve of the festival, Prabhupada held a press conference at Akash Ganga. The next morning the newspapers announced: “Hare Krishna Festival in City.”

**\* \* \***

The Hare Krishna Bhagavata Dharma Discourses Festival opened to the public on Thursday, March 25, 1971, and Bombay citizens poured into Cross Maidan by the tens of thousands. A fence of almost half a mile of dark-blue cloth, ten feet high, wrapped the perimeter, topped by thousands of flashing colored lights. People entered through twenty-foot-wide arched portals that we’d rented from a Bollywood movie set. There were three entrances on three sides of the grounds.

This is what they saw as they came in: a white canvas circus tent, 350' long, the roof tapering to a crown and hung around the edges with orange bunting, its vertical sides open at the top for air circulation. Two huge banners spread across the tent’s main entrance, one in Hindi and one in English, proclaiming:

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY

FOR KRISHNA CONSCIOUSNESS

FOUNDER-ACHARYA

HIS DIVINE GRACE

A. C. BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI PRABHUPADA

We also hung big WELCOME signs on both sides. Banks of floodlights lit the entire scene.

Floating two hundred feet above the courtyard was the sixteen-foot balloon, tethered by its ropes. Near the front entrance we’d placed a decorative fountain that sprayed water four feet into the air inside a twenty-foot-high ornate, columned gazebo, another

Bollywood touch. Potted palms and flowering plants were placed here and there throughout the grounds.

On white signboards posted on each side of the wide tent entrance people could read what the festival was all about, with the events and topics painted in blue and the times painted in red:

DAILY PROGRAMME

MARCH 25 through APRIL 4

6-00 AM MONGEL AROTIK & KIRTAN

7-00 to 8-00 Bhagavata Dharma Discourse by

His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

PRABHUPADA IN HINDI

8-00 AM Maha Prasada Distributed

8-30 to 9-30 Private Darshan With Srila

PRABHUPADA (by Appointment)

11-30 to 12-30 KIRTAN & BHOG AROTIK

12-30 to 2-00 PM General Darshan of

Sri Sri Radha Krishna Murtis

2-00 to 4-00 DEITY RESTING

4-00 to 4-15 DHOOP AROTIK

5-00 to 6-00 Private Darshan with

Srila PRABHUPADA (by Appointment)

6-00 to 7-00 Maha SANKIRTAN

7-00 to 8-00 Bhagavata Dharma Discourses by His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami PRABHUPADA IN ENGLISH

8-00 to 8-30 Cinema or Slide Show of Harinam Activities U.S.A. & ENGLAND

AUSTRALIA, GERMANY, JAPAN

8-30 to 9-30 Maha SANKIRTAN

9-30 to 10-00 SAYANA AROTIK

10-00 PM DEITY RESTING

Just inside the tent and to the left we’d shoved a long row of tables together and covered them with colorful ISKCON books for sale. We were printing Prabhupada’s books in America and Japan, so they were more expensive than books printed in India. But even at prices like Rs 70/- for Krsna book, Rs. 50/- for Bhagavad-gita, and Rs. 10/- for smaller books such as The First Step in God Realization, book sales were booming.

The forty-foot ceiling was strung with hundreds of orange triangular flags and supported by eight rows of stout blue-and-white striped poles placed at thirty-foot intervals. Dozens of ceiling fans whirred overhead. Vertical fluorescent tube lights were mounted on every other upright, and floodlights illuminated the stage, 250 feet inside the tent.

Center stage was dominated by breathtaking Radha and Krishna Deities—Krishna jamming on His flute and Radha, palm upraised, greeting the multitudes. They occupied a simple but elegant Deity house, ten feet wide by twelve feet high, which sat on a red-velvet, three-tiered altar. They were wearing bright silk dresses and crowns and jewels—a peacock feather in Krishna’s hair—and were draped in rose and tuberose garlands. The people of Bombay were clearly astonished to see such elegant Deities. Strands of fresh tuberoses and jasmine flowers hung like strings across the stage and before the Deities, dancing in the breeze created by electric floor fans. Above the altar we’d hung a pyramid of bright electric lights that flashed from side to side, top to bottom, in a hypnotic sequence.

An announcement is made, and thirty onstage devotees, flush from hours of kirtan, bow down to welcome His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada Maharaja.

Prabhupada ascends the stairs, stage left, hands his bead bag and glasses to an attendant, walks to mid-stage, stops to gaze at the Deities a moment, then bows, knees and head to the floor, before Radha and Krishna. He then turns and sits, legs folded, on his vyasasan, a low, red-upholstered armchair beneath a red, gold-fringed umbrella. As he settles himself, it’s totally silent in the vast arena.

He opens the Bhagavad-gita on the low table before him, dons his horn-rimmed glasses, taps the “Chicago Radio” microphone a few times, and clears his throat.

So ladies and gentlemen, I thank you very much for your taking so much trouble in participating with us in this great movement of Krishna consciousness. As I am repeatedly placing before you with all humbleness that this movement is very, very much essential, not only at the present moment, but also all the time.

Tonights theme is “Modern Civilization is a Failure, and the Only Hope is Krishna Consciousness.” Very quickly Prabhupada’s sonorous voice, his obvious erudition, and his cut-to-the-chase monologue weave a spell over the fifteen thousand Bombayites in attendance. What follows is a long, succinct picture of the temporary material world, the futility of our struggles within it, and “how to make the best use of a bad bargain.” Prabhupada quotes the Bhagavad-gita again and again, verses such as:

aham sarvasya prabhavo  
mattah sarvam pravartate  
iti matva bhajante mam  
budha bhava-samanvitah

I am the source of all spiritual and material worlds. Everything emanates from Me. The wise who perfectly know this engage in My devotional service and worship Me with all their hearts.

He continues:

So this Krishna consciousness movement is to educate people how to live in spiritual atmosphere, and then there will be peace and prosperity. If we want to learn some specific subject we have to accept a proper authority or a bona fide teacher. Similarly, if we want to learn the science of God, we have to approach a person who knows the science.

He explains that spiritual education means knowing how to use the things of this material world to serve Krishna:

There are two kinds of uses for everything. Just like this microphone is being used for spreading Krishna consciousness, and the same microphone can be used for singing a cinema song.

He pauses to drink water from a silver lota. Devotees behind him are cooling him with a rattan fan and a yak-tail fly whisk.

Even these European and American boys, you do not find they are correct to the principle, still they are sadhu because they have engaged themselves in Krishna consciousness. Even though you may find cause—because their forefathers did not teach them anything, they are learning new—so even you find some fault in their activities, don’t think that they are not sadhus.

He ends with:

We cannot realize Krishna, or God, by mental speculation or by material advancement. We have to render service to Krishna. Then Krishna will be pleased, and He will give you dictation from within how we can make advance in Krishna consciousness. And that is the successful life of human form of existence. Thank you very much.

The crowd is stunned. They seldom hear the truth made so clear in such a direct yet palatable way. Prabhupada motions for kirtan, and Saraswati jumps up and shouts “Haribol!”

This next kirtan was the loudest, most robust kirtan ever, with the entire audience on their feet dancing and chanting, sounding like a train roaring by at full speed.

Srila Prabhupada spoke mornings and evenings in the ten days that followed, giving long, profound lectures on such topics as “Kali Yuga,” “Krishna Not a Hindu God,” “Krishna Consciousness is Authorized,” “The Hare Krishna People,” and “The Duty of India.” Prabhupada also initiated six new disciples onstage in a Vedic fire ceremony, with his disciple Subal Swami conducting the rites.

The biggest attraction was probably the Vedic wedding ceremony Prabhupada performed on March 29 when he joined Padmavati dasi from Australia and Vegavan das from Sweden. The bride and groom looked so beautiful up there—people just couldn’t believe it. “From here,” said Prabhupada, in his address to the crowd, “they will go to Australia to preach. Just see. This is the real United Nations.”

Richard Slavin was in the audience during the festival, that same skinny kid whose outstretched hands I’d filled with runny yogurt in Amsterdam nearly nine months earlier. Richard had hitchhiked from Europe, penniless, across the Middle East to India, looking for a guru, and by now had examined dozens of yogis and spiritual leaders. When I told Prabhupada that Richard was sitting at the back of the crowd, he immediately sent Gurudas to fetch this dread-locked seeker from Chicago to sit with us onstage.

Richard: As Srila Prabhupada was speaking, I heard a voice within my heart proclaim, “This is your guru”

Richard told us he’d been carrying the picture of a beautiful blue god in his pocket for four months and, seeing Krsna book on our book-table, was finally able to identify Him—that’s how scarce Krishna was in India in those days. (We’ll catch up with Richard’s story later.)

A young Indian man in the crowd named Raghunath Bhagwan Patil also saw the devotees during the festival and later remarked,

“They were pretty dead serious, grave, convinced, knowledgeable, and I had the impression that if his followers and disciples were like this, I can only imagine that their guru must be far, far superior.” (Patil soon became Lokanath Swami, one of the great leaders in Prabhupada’s movement.)

Even though civil war had finally broken out between East and West Pakistan on March 26, and despite the ominous tension this caused in Bombay, people still flocked to Cross Maidan by the tens of thousands to hear Prabhupada speak and to observe firsthand the novel, international, spiritual spectacle in their midst. Perhaps a million people passed through the ISKCON pandal over a ten-day period.

Late in the afternoon of April 4, the final day of the festival, Radha and Krishna were escorted out of the pandal and onto the back of a magnificent chariot, with Srila Prabhupada seated proudly in front. They were then paraded two miles through the streets of downtown Bombay, to Chowpatty Beach. Awaiting Them at the beach was Their new throne, or singhasana, a huge, carved teak affair inlaid with silver that had been donated by devotees from the Mumbadevi and Maha Laxmi temples. Twenty-five thousand celebrants gathered on the sand to glorify Their Lordships as They were lifted onto the throne. Yamuna had arranged all of this—the funds, the Deities’ clothes and decorations, the cart, the elaborate eight-thousand-rupee throne. It was her special service to her beloved Radha and Krishna.

Late that night, we packed Radha and Krishna into two crates and brought Them from the beach by taxi back to Akash Ganga. But there was a problem. Our landlord, Mr. Billimoria, already alarmed by the large numbers of devotees occupying our flat, had heard many complaints about our loud kirtans, erratic hours, and the rows of orange robes hanging from the balcony to dry. Akash Ganga was supposed to be a high-class place. Perhaps he’d been listening to the rumors that we were CIA agents here to snoop on the Pakistan Consulate one floor below us. But Billimoria refused to let us bring the Deities up in the elevator, and when we pushed our way onto it anyway, he turned off the electricity.

A lively argument ensued, and Gurudas decked one of the watchmen with a well-thrown right. A noisy mob gathered. Just then, Sada Jiwat Lal arrived and calmly intervened, telling us to open the wooden crates so that everyone could see that this was the Lord and not a cache of AK-47s. “You should not keep the Lord waiting on the pavement!” he shouted, and the crowd began to sympathize.

Gurudas then hefted fifty-pound Krishna from the crate and began to carry Him up the seven flights of stairs. Two of us grabbed Radharani’s box and followed. The crowd turned on Billimoria, and electricity was restored to the lift.

Later that night, we dismantled the massive singhasana in sections and hauled it from Chowpatty Beach to Akash Ganga, where we took the parts up to the seventh floor by elevator without further incident.

The following day, beneath a large photo of the rath car in procession—the distinctive Victorian turrets of the Bombay High Court are visible in the background—the Indian Express reported:

FITTING FINALE TO THE HARE KRISHNA FESTIVAL

It was a grand, fitting finale to the 11-day Hare Krishna festival which attracted thousands of devotees at Cross Maidan in South Bombay.

The decorated idols of Radha and Krishna were taken in procession on a regal rath from the venue through Girgaum Road to Chowpatty in the evening.

Dozens of “Namasankirtan mandals” from all over the city spearheaded the procession with loud and ecstatic chanting of the Hare Krishna mahamantra followed by His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada in an elegant horse-drawn coach.

At Chowpatty, the four-foot tall deities, splendidly dressed and decorated with jewels and garlands, were displayed in their magnificent ‘singhasana’ (throne) donated to the Hare Krishna Movement by Madhavbaug and Mumbadevi temples.

The Prabhupada will deliver his final public message to the citizens before leaving in a few weeks on a preaching tour of the major cities of Russia.

**\* \* \***

The festival had been a resounding success. Cross Maidan put the Hare Krishna movement on the map in India almost overnight. Krishna culture was now so popular in India that government-owned Air India featured a romantic Radha and Krishna theme on its April 1971 timetable. Prabhupadas fame spread out from Bombay like a tidal wave, and for days after Cross Maidan Prabhupada kept saying, “Yes, this is the way.” He immediately dispatched Tamai Krishna, Giriraj, and Gurudas to Calcutta to arrange a similar pandal program there.

Srila Prabhupada had been in India since August 1970—nearly nine months—and during that time, his Krishna consciousness movement had grown rapidly around the world. So far he had managed everything by mail, but there was an urgent need for him to personally tend to his devotees overseas. Bombay, Amritsar, Indore, Surat, Calcutta, Allahabad, Gorakhpur, Benares, and Cross Maidan—for months he had been at the helm in India. Now other currents were pulling him in all directions. There would possibly be a pandal program in Calcutta, and in Bombay we had begun negotiations on a big property. But San Francisco and London were calling Prabhupada to attend their upcoming Ratha-yatras, and Bali Mardan was inviting Prabhupada to Malaysia and Australia. And there was also the prospect of visiting Russia.

Russia. This Iron Curtain country was always on Prabhupadas mind. In 1970-71, Russia and the Western world were locked in a duel seemingly to the death: no one knew who would drop the Bomb first. The world was divided into two camps: the USA and its capitalism and Russia and its communism. As early as 1967 Prabhupada had been saying he wanted to go to Russia, absolutely sure that Krishna consciousness would solve the problems of that beleaguered land and create world peace. He often said stuff like “The communists are first-class disciples of Kali; therefore our attention should be more upon them because of their serious materialistic fever.”

To me and the other devotees, this sounded like a pipedream. Russia was a godless state, sealed off from the rest of the world. Still, for two years Prabhupada had been urging Hamsaduta and Krishnadas in Germany to arrange a visa and speaking engagements for him in Russia. Finally, on April 1, Prabhupada got a letter from Krishnadas in Hamburg with an enclosed letter from a Professor Kotovsky in Moscow inviting Swami Bhaktivedanta to speak at the USSR Academy of Sciences. This letter had pleased Srila Prabhupada so much that he had opened his final April 1 lecture at Cross Maidan with this announcement:

Ladies and gentlemen, we thank you very much for your kindly coming here and participating in this noble movement of Krishna consciousness, or sankirtan movement. You’ll be very glad to know that today I have received one letter from my disciple, Sriman Krishnadas Adhikary, the temple commander of West Berlin. He was negotiating with Russian Cultural Society, and one very important professor, he has invited us to lecture in Russia, \[applause\] So very soon I shall be going to Russia with some of my disciples, \[applause\]

Since Indias independence from Britain in 1947, India had maintained a loose alliance with the Soviet Union based on the socialistic principles promulgated by the ruling Nehru—Indira Gandhi dynasty. By 1971, communist influence was seen all over India—from the hammer-and-sickle graffiti on walls to statues of Lenin and Marx in village squares. New Delhi, especially the Indian military, was firmly in the Soviet camp. Little wonder that the audience applauded when Prabhupada announced he would visit Russia. Prabhupada responded to Krishnadas immediately: arrange things in Moscow and get me a Russian visa.

On April 5, Prabhupada wrote a letter to all of Bombay’s Life Members, thanking them for making the Cross Maidan festival a “grand success” and outlining expansive plans for his future mission in Bombay. He then invited them to a meeting at Akash Ganga at 6:30 p.m. on April 26 “to discuss and to finalize plans to channel our united energies to achieve the goal.”

The “goal” was the Usman property on Narayan Dabholkar Road, high atop Malabar Hill, a run-down Arabian Night’s castle built in the 1920s on several acres by an old Mogul family now fallen on hard times. Negotiations were ongoing, but they wanted 2.5 million rupees for the place and it was doubtful that we could raise that much. Maybe the Life Members could help.

On April 7, Prabhupada went to Nasik, a city three hours to the northeast by train. He returned to Bombay two days later to a pleasant surprise: Gargamuni had arrived, safely back from the war in East Pakistan! Prabhupada was very happy to see him and questioned him late into the night. But where was his brother, Brahmananda? He was supposed to be in West Pakistan. No one knew for sure, and Prabhupada was very worried about him.

In the meantime we had another problem: our six-month India tourist visas were expiring. Near the end of March, devotees lined the benches in the musty Foreigners Regional Registration Office on the third floor of the Special Branch Building, behind St. Xavier’s College, in an old part of Bombay. After hours—sometimes days—of waiting, we were given C-forms to fill out. Requirements for visa extension were stringent, and many of us were faced with having to leave India. Prabhupada jumped in and wrote letters to police superintendents and the FRO: “I need these foreigners to help me. Please extend their visas.”

After nearly six weeks in Bombay, Srila Prabhupada was anxious to get back on the road. He threw a lot of lines into the water and waited to see who would bite. I was so busy with the affairs in Bombay that I wasn’t sure what was going on with Prabhupadas schedule, but by late April it looked something like this:

Malaysia and Australia near the end of April.

Calcutta in May. Tamai Krishna wrote that they had secured the Chowringhee Maidan, near Victoria Monument in the heart of downtown Calcutta, for a Bhagavat Dharma Discourses pandal program from May 14-24. Prabhupada promised to be there—he would fly to Calcutta from Sydney.

Russia. Prabhupada was to present his passport to the Russian Embassy in Bombay in order to get his visa, but his passport was about to expire, so he wrote Tamai Krishna to pick up a new passport for him in Calcutta. There was some bureaucratic mix-up, but he got the passport just in time for his trip to Malaysia.

Herculean! Iron man! How does he do it? This is the most forceful and energetic version of Srila Prabhupada we have ever seen. He’s so active that he seems superhuman. We marvel at how his mind moves, taking care of every aspect of what is now a worldwide movement while maintaining the highest personal standard of purity and discipline. He acquires and sends Deities all over the globe, writes, prints, and distributes books, raises and banks money—a hundred things at once. And yet he is always concerned for the well-being of each and every one of his disciples. He writes Karandhar on April 30:

This day the whole day has been to me an anxious time because I have heard very bad news from Pakistan. In a local paper it is published that four of our Hare Krishna Movement Missionaries were killed in Dacca by the Pakistani soldiers. I am very much anxious to know about Brahmananda. I had advised Brahmananda not to go to Pakistan after the civil war began there but I am afraid that he was very daring and might have gone against my instruction. So I am very very much anxious to know about Brahmananda. The whole day has been full of anxiety and still it is going on.

On May 1, Srila Prabhupada flew to Kuala Lumpur, taking Padmavati and Vegavan with him along with the second set of R. D. Birla’s marble Radha-Krishna Deities, which had just arrived from Jaipur. After programs in Malaysia, they would carry the new Deities onward to Sydney. With Prabhupada gone I started to feel antsy again. After three tough months organizing Cross Maidan, I wanted to travel somewhere, maybe chase another rhino for the Swami. The Usman property had been sold to another party—we just didn’t have the money for such a big place. My mind turned to London. I still wrote long, descriptive aerograms to George Harrison every couple of weeks, and he was always on my mind. He rarely wrote back though—George was not a letter guy—but we talked on the phone from time to time and he told me he was writing new songs about Krishna. Mukunda was in charge of the temple affairs in London and he saw George sometimes, so I wasn’t that worried that George would get lost. But England was my GBC zone, after all, so maybe I should go back there and lend a hand.

The Radha Krishna Temple 33 1/3 LP album, which we had recorded in London nearly a year earlier, was finally released on May 21, and Apple Records sent a few copies to me in Bombay. We would play it for Srila Prabhupada when he returned.

**\* \* \***

After his month-long whirlwind tour of Malaysia, Australia, Calcutta, and Rajahmundry, Prabhupada arrived back in Bombay on June 4, 1971. He jumped right in:

“Hamsaduta—my visa for Russia, what is the position?”

“Well, uh, I guess I could try here in Bombay—go to the Russian Embassy — ”

“No, no. For months you have been trying. You are, Krishnadas is—you are always trying. Shyamasundar, what do you think? Can you get me a visa for Moscow, with two attendants—total three men in our company?”

“Yes, Srila Prabhupada,” I said, not considering the nearimpossibility that Russia will allow a religious leader, a swami from India, and two Americans into their sealed-off world. In November 1956, Soviet leader Nikita Khrushchev had proclaimed to the West: “We will bury you!” America and Russia were avowed enemies; the world had witnessed the arms race, the space race, and for the past six years, the hopeless war against communism in Vietnam. Now, in June of 1971, it was just three years since an angry Leonid Brezhnev, current General Secretary of the Communist Party, announced the Brezhnev Doctrine: “When forces that are hostile to socialism try to turn the development of some socialist country toward capitalism, it becomes not only a problem of the country concerned, but a common problem and concern of all socialist countries.” In other words, don’t mess around with Russia or its allies or there will be war. A belligerent Richard Nixon was president in the US.

Still, on the morning of Monday, June 7, I went over to the Russian Consulate General in the Palm Beach compound at 42 Nepean Sea Road, on Malabar Hill, a ten-minute taxi ride from our Akash Ganga building on Warden Road. I was probably on a fool’s errand, but I was going through the motions—chanting out loud for Krishna’s help. Marx, Lenin, and Stalin glared down at me from the walls, but the consulate was empty except for its staff members, all Indians. Many of them had been to the pandal at Cross Maidan and were actually glad to see me.

I said, “Bhaktivedanta Swami has received an invitation to speak in Moscow and needs a visa.” All I had to show them was the now nearly two-month-old letter written to Krishnadas and forwarded to Prabhupada:

Moscow,

March 16, 1971

Dear Sir,

Referring your Letter to: Prof. V. V. Balabushevitch, dated 11/2/1970, I am to inform you that Professor V. V. Balabushevitch passed away on the 14th of December, 1970.

I thank you for your information about Swami Bhaktivedanta’s lecturing tour. If he would come to Moscow, the Soviet scholars doing research in ancient Indian culture will be very happy to meet him in the Institute of Oriental Studies, USSR Academy of Sciences. I would be thankful to you for the information on the dates of Swami Bhaktivedanta’s arrival and stay in the USSR.

Yours sincerely

G. G. Kotovsky

Head, Department of Indian and South Asian Studies,

Institute of Oriental Studies,

USSR Academy of Sciences,

Armyansky Perlk 2,

Moscow, USSR.

Right away the Consulate guy says, “This should not be a problem.

So I add, “We also need visas for his two attendants, both American citizens” (thinking Aravinda and probably Hamsaduta). The guy’s eyebrows shoot up and he says, “Wait here. I will be back soon.” I wait an hour, chanting on my beads under a lazy ceiling fan and picking through the Russian travel brochures displayed on a glass-topped table.

Finally, a woman appears and calls my name. She says she is the local “Intourist” agent and yes, our travel plan has been approved. We are allowed five days in Moscow, all three of us—a package deal: hotel, transport, meals, and guided tours. The only June dates available are June 20—25, and here is the price.

I tell her I'll check back with the Swami and return that afternoon, and she advises that they close for visas at three o’clock.

On the way back to Prabhupada, I think, “Hmm, five days is not enough. What can he do there in five days?” But I describe everything to him and he is jubilant. “Yes, we shall go on those dates. It is sufficient time.” Then he asks me, “Do you know this typewriting?”

Not sure where this is going, but I say, “Yes, I took a typing class in high school and typed all my papers in college.”

“Then you shall go with me as my secretary. Aravinda will go as servant, you will go as personal secretary. We shall fly to Moscow and then fly to Paris. Just make that arrangement.”

Whoa! My biggest dream—to scout the frontier with Prabhupada-is coming true! I tried to hide my glee, but Prabhupada must have seen that this was the happiest moment of my life. Later, I thought about my great good fortune. Out of billions of people, thousands of devotees, and maybe life after life, I’d aspired for this chance to be wing-man for the perfect man of God! Even as a child I had dreamed of traveling with a great sage, and now—oh, Krishna, You’ve done it again.

Aravinda was a cool-headed, thoughtful Jewish boy from Detroit. He had been Prabhupada’s servant for the past three months, typing all his letters. He was quiet, competent, and seemed to know what he was doing. I didn’t know him well, but in our few exchanges we seemed to get along—and best of all, he had a good, understated sense of humor. He’ll be a great traveling companion, I thought, and will teach me how things work in Srila Prabhupada’s inner circle.

Rishi Kumar gave me the necessary cash, and I dashed back over to Nepean Sea Road, handed over three passports, signed all the documents swearing that we would not commit criminal or subversive acts in the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics, and returned to Prabhupada with our stamped passports, a sheaf of airline tickets, and a printed itinerary.

While all this was going on, Prabhupada received a month-old letter from Professor Kotovsky, redirected to Bombay from Calcutta, stating that he would be leaving the Academy for his holidays near the end of June. Prabhupada told me, “Your first assignment as secretary—I want you to send a telegram to Mr. Kotovsky.” And he dictated the following:

VISITING MOSCOW JUNE 20TH TO 25TH.

PLEASE STAY I WISH TO MEET YOU.

BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI

The next day, Prabhupada dictated a letter to Kotovsky, to be sent under my name:

Bombay

June 10, 1971

My dear Professor Kotovsky,

Please accept our respectful greetings. On Tuesday, June 8th, 1971, on behalf of A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami, I sent you the following cable:

VISITING MOSCOW JUNE 20TH TO 25TH.

PLEASE STAY I WISH TO MEET YOU.

I am to inform you that Swami Bhaktivedanta has recently returned to India from a very successful lecture tour through Malaysia and Australia, and that he will be continuing on to lecture in France and England in the latter part of June. En route to these countries, he will halt in Moscow for a 5-day visit in your nation’s lovely capitol.

He will arrive in Moscow on Air India flight number

515 at 11:25 a.m. on 20th June, 1971, and he is scheduled to leave for Paris on the morning of 25th June.

Swami Bhaktivedanta would consider it a great pleasure and honor to meet with the Soviet scholars doing research in ancient Indian culture at the Institute of Oriental Studies, USSR Academy of Sciences, at any time between those dates which would be convenient for them.

As he will be leaving in ten days’ it is questionable if your postal reply would reach here before the departure date. With this in mind, may I humbly request you to cable whether or not you and your staff will be available to meet with the Swami, who is widely recognized as one of the world s foremost authorities on the Vedic culture.

I hope this finds you in good health.

Sincerely,

Shyamasundar das Adhikary

Secretary to A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

“You know, Prabhupada, they told me that we cannot bring any books—not even magazines—and no food, no religious pictures, nothing. And I got the idea at the Consulate that every move we make will be monitored by their secret police, the KGB.”

“Yes, that is the suspicion. Never mind. Anyone may watch. We are innocent. We shall be like the man on the crowded train: first, just let me put my foot here, yes, thank you. Now (he makes shifting movements on the cushion, chuckling), just shift a little more this way—then put up the leg. Finally, he lies down.”

Prabhupada often talked about communism. Real communism, he said, was spiritual communism, with God, not the state, at its center. If every citizen-devotee served Krishna, the community would automatically be self-governing-а true working-class paradise.

Prabhupada had answered a letter from Krishnadas in October 1970:

You write to say that “the communist goal is to achieve a paradise on earth,” but in the Bhagavad-gita Krishna has definitely stated that “from the topmost planet down to the lowest, all are places of misery where repeated birth and death take place”—so what kind of paradise can they achieve? Any society without religion is animal society.

He went on to say that religion without philosophy is sentimentalism or fanaticism, and that philosophy without religion is mental speculation.

So although the Russians may be very much advanced by throwing sputniks into the outer space, they are missing the real point—Krishna Consciousness, the science of God....When the scientist, philosopher, medical man will get the opportunity to study systematically what is God, what are His energies, what is my relationship, how to approach Him, then only there will be paradise. But paradise in the present condition of life, in ignorance of God, is like a parasite in temporary paradise.

Prabhupada had read Karl Marx in his youth, and he understood the concept of “historical imperative.” On April 23, Prabhupada had written in a letter to Professor Kotovsky:

This ancient Krishna culture and philosophy is the oldest in the world or in the universe. At least from a historical point of view it is not less than 5,000 years old.I think if you give me a chance to speak about the great Krishna culture and philosophy in your country, you will very much appreciate the simple programme with great profit. This culture is so well planned that it would be acceptable by any thoughtful man throughout the whole world.

Whatever reasons Prabhupada had for going to Moscow, Krishna had somehow made it possible. My mood was, “Whatever happens, it will be great.” But there wasn’t much time to prepare! I needed to pack the manual Olympia portable typewriter with its aluminum case, the Hitachi battery-powered cassette tape recorder (the size of a shoebox—it would fit in a book bag hung around my neck), and wait, this will be a historic occasion, so maybe I should pick up a camera and take some shots.

I ran over to the Fort district to shop for cameras. There were few cameras available in Bombay in 1971, and they all seemed very expensive. But finally I found a little Kodak Brownie box camera for two hundred rupees along with one roll of black-and-white Fuji film that would give me twenty-four images. The guy selling the camera explained how to use it: “Click this catch on the side, load film roll, close door. Now turn this lever to advance the film. No, no, to '1' position. Now look in the little window, aim it, push button, and that will be a very nice photograph.”

More good news: Brahmananda isn’t dead! Srila Prabhupada received a telegram from Brahmananda on June 8 from Karachi, and Prabhupada wired him back: come to Bombay immediately. Brahmananda arrived in Bombay from West Pakistan on June 14, to everyone’s great relief. Prabhupada hugged him like a long-lost child and then sat with him for hours in his room, catching up. Later that day, Aravinda told me that Prabhupada was sending Brahmananda to Africa!

I saw Big В in the hallway, a disconsolate look on his face, and he said, “Yeah, I failed. I didn’t distribute any books in Pakistan, made no Life Members, nada—I failed. Prabhupada wants me to go to Nairobi now, see what I can do there. He said if I go to Africa, we’ll have temples on every continent.” Prabhupada had been receiving letters for two years from Balmukund Parikh and other Indian Kenyan refugees living in London, urging him to start something in Nairobi.

I said, “Jeez, Brahmananda, Pakistan is all Muslims. There’s a war going on there. How does Prabhupada expect—I mean, you’re like our hero, the anchor ...” Trying to cheer him up. “Do you have to go right away?”

“He told me to rest for a few days here, and then go.”

“Wow, you just got here.”

“Whatever Prabhupada says I will do,” he said. “I’m just his yes-man.”

**\* \* \***

Our Moscow flight was scheduled for very early on the morning of June 20. We’d be stopping in New Delhi to change planes. Gurudas, Yamuna, and several other devotees were in Delhi, so Prabhupada instructed me to send Gurudas a telegram asking that he and the other Delhi devotees meet him in the airport waiting lounge between flights.

STOPPING TWO HOURS DELHI EN ROUTE MOSCOW ALL DEVOTEES MEET AIRPORT SUNDAY MORNING 20TH 730 Al 515 TRY BRING KARAN SINGH OTHER VIPS TO MEET ME NOTIFY SUBAL DAS— BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI

Dr. Karan Singh was the Maharaja of Kashmir and India’s current Minister of Aviation. That Prabhupada, thinking big at every moment!

The morning of the day before our departure, some of us stood on the balcony at Akash Ganga being lashed by near-hurricane winds and looking west to a wall of black clouds forty thousand feet high rushing in on us. It was the first day of the monsoon. I’d never seen it rain this hard. By noon, Warden Road was under three feet of water. Maybe they’ll shut down the airport! But by evening the floodwaters had receded, the stars had come out, and it looked like clear sailing for the following day.

I said goodbye to Malati and the others and held Saraswati in my arms, telling her I was going away with Srila Prabhupada—he’ll take good care of me, don’t worry!—and I would be back to see her very soon.

We had all seen James Bond movies back in the ’60s: On Her Majesty’s Secret Service and From Russia with Love. My scalp tingled thinking about the real-life Bond thriller about to play out: 007 and his trusty sidekick slipping behind the Iron Curtain on a wing and a prayer to wreak havoc and save the planet.

The master and I were about to infiltrate behind enemy lines.

## Three

In June 1971 the world was divided into two opposing ideologies, capitalism and communism. The Western bloc of capitalist nations was separated from the Eastern bloc of communists by a geographic line drawn north-south through Europe called the Iron Curtain. It was a Cold War. Both sides were hesitant to risk all-out nuclear holocaust, so they fought instead small hot wars in places like Korea and Vietnam, where the two blocs, led by the United States and Russia, struggled by proxy for world domination. The entire planet lived in fear that some incident would break the stalemate and unleash nuclear winter across our globe.

Through this perfect historical window stepped His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, determined to spread his ancient message of peace to both camps. Prabhupada arrived in Russia on June 20, 1971, and the short time he spent there could rightly be called fife days that changed the world.

DAY ONE: SUNDAY

A woman in red lipstick and a bouffant hairdo greets us outside the Sheremetyevo Airport immigration and customs gate. Pointing to the name tag on her sensible dark-blue suit she says, “Hello, very welcome to Moscow. I am named Katarina (Something-or-other)-vich, and I will be your INTOURIST guide. And this is Igor

(Something-or-other)-kov.” A liveried driver steps forward, loads our gear into a big black Chaika automobile that looks like a ’50s De Soto, and we race off down the wide avenues toward town. It’s Sunday afternoon and there’s almost no traffic. “Tomorrow is summer solstice,” Katerina tells us, “and daylight will be starting at 4 a.m. and sun will set after 10 p.m.”

Suddenly, a monster black ZIL limousine bearing diplomatic plates flies past on our left in a special center traffic lane, little flags flying from the grill. Over his shoulder Igor says, “V-I-P.” (I’m thinking, “Hey, maybe that’s Brezhnev, late for a meeting at the Kremlin.”) The wonderful wide, empty streets and verdant trees and lawns are a welcome sight after so many months in crowded, parched India.

We are glad to be moving—much relieved after the wild scene we’ve just left at the Moscow airport. We’re trying to be cool (keep your game face on, boy, you’re behind the Iron Curtain), but Aravinda and I are chuckling out loud, remembering the crazy stuff that’d already happened today.

So far, the whole trip has been an adventure. The Air India 747 stopped at New Delhi’s Palam Airport earlier this morning for a two-hour layover. Prabhupada was greeted with a noisy reception by the devotees, then he caught up on ISKCON affairs in New Delhi and Vrindavan and dispensed his advice. We left Palam at 9:30 a.m. for the six-hour flight to Moscow. Shortly after takeoff, the plane’s captain came into the cabin and introduced himself to Prabhupada as Captain Lal. He had seen us at Cross Maidan, he said. Captain Lal extolled the wonders of this brand new Boeing 747 and invited Swamiji to join him in the cockpit. Prabhupada was gone for half an hour. When he returned his eyes were big, like a little kid’s, and he told us about the plane’s instruments and how the pilots are flying this huge machine. Of course, he put it all into transcendental perspective: “So much arrangement they have made for flying machines in the sky. They are so proud. It is nice achievement, but there is no guarantee. At any moment there is one small thing—finished. And then where they will go? They do not know. Still, there must be pilot. Machine will not fly itself. Just like the material world, it is like this giant machine, it will not move without God as pilot.”

Inside the Moscow arrivals terminal, we stand in a long line for baggage inspection, with politsiya and soldiers all over the place. We’ve been warned not to bring books, periodicals, or religious paraphernalia of any kind. Prabhupada and Aravinda go through first with Prabhupada’s small bag. They are cleared, and they stand waiting for me as I heft my bulging suitcase up onto the counter—I’m carrying Aravinda’s stuff too, to save space—and when the KGB guy zips it open, right on top I see two books: a hardback Bhagavad-gita and an Easy Journey to Other Planets. (Malati! That rascal! She must’ve snuck ’em in!) My heart stops. Red warning lights flash in my brain. I imagine Siberia for years on end. The official picks up the Gita, opens it, and maybe twenty loose photographs of Krishna, Radha, Chaitanya, and Prabhupada fly out and scatter in all directions.

I’m looking back and forth from this mess to Prabhupada, who has this kind of serene look on his face as I’m trying to convey to him, “I didn’t know this was here. Honest! I’ll take the rap, don’t worry! You go ahead!”

More police run over while irritated passengers pick up the spilled photos and pass them back to me. The KGB guy is totally flustered, his face beet red, and he scrambles to pick up what’s left of the photos. Finally, he stuffs them all back into my suitcase, glares at me, and whips his hand toward the exit, like, get outta here!

Prabhupada mutters to me as we walk out, “So you have brought Bhagavad-gita? Very good.”

Leningradsky Avenue is lined mile after mile with uniform, concrete high-rises, the sameness uninterrupted until we get closer to the city, swing off Tverskaya Street, and stop in front of our hotel. “Kremlin and Red Square are just over there,” says Katarina, pointing. We are in the heart of Moscow. The National

Hotel is a throwback to the tsarist age. Opened in 1903, the blocksquare, six-story Victorian fortress is a remnant of gilded times, when four-horse carriages pulled up to the entrance and gowned ladies were escorted by dashing princes upstairs to the Imperial Ballroom. After the 1918 revolution and while the war-damaged Kremlin was being repaired, the National Hotel was home to the first Soviet government. Lenin, Trotsky, and Felix Dzerzhinsky, dreaded founder of the KGB, had rooms here.

We entered the vast, chandeliered lobby, and Katarina helped us check in. She handed me coupons for meals, and I informed her that we follow a strict diet—no meat or fish or eggs—and we would prefer to cook our own food. May we use a kitchen? (Aravinda had brought Prabhupada’s three-tiered cooker, his glass, a spoon, and two lotas.) These people were not very sophisticated—how could they know we weren’t big friends of Brezhnev’s, fellow comrades on the march to global communism?—so they relented and allowed us to use the servants kitchen on the second floor. Prabhupada is given room 356 on the fourth floor, and Aravinda and I have two small, plain bedrooms far from each other on other floors in the vast hotel. Prabhupada tells us it’s best if Aravinda stays with him, sleeping on the floor in his room, so I am stuck alone, on the second floor, just down the hall from the servant’s kitchen, seven minutes’ walk up stairs and down long corridors from Prabhupada—four minutes if I run.

While we unpacked and settled Prabhupada into his room, we heard military music in the street outside. From his window we watched, passing below, a hundred-foot ICBM rocket marked with a red star and supported by four trucks, end-to-end. This spectacle was followed by rows of goose-stepping soldiers, then tanks and huge, wheeled artillery. We were speechless. After the half-hour parade had passed, Prabhupada looked over to me from his chair by the window. I saw a tear running down his cheek. On this day, June 20, 1971, he said, “You cannot rule a nation by fear. This cannot last. Only little time. In twenty years, all this will be gone.” Later, we were told this impressive show of military might was a regular Sunday event.

Now Prabhupada had a place to eat and sleep, but I was deeply worried that he would be bored or disappointed. Introducing Krishna consciousness into Russia was one of his most important projects—and we had less than five days to accomplish something tangible. I threw a prayer into the wind. Surely Krishna would help; there was no other possibility. We had only two phone numbers, an iffy letter of invitation, a hotel room, and plane tickets out of here, and that was it. I prayed, “Please, Krishna, make this trip a success for Srila Prabhupada.”

It was Sunday, and Professor Kotovsky wouldn’t be at his office number until tomorrow (if he was even still in town), so my first job was to find food. The hotel staff had given me coupons I could exchange for food in state markets. At the front desk they told me there was a dairy shop open nearby, so I went there first. I stood at the end of a block-long queue. After some time, and seeing my exotic dress, a sympathetic citizen considered that I must be an overseas friend of communism and slipped me to the front of the line. I bought milk, yogurt, and cheese, and then went looking for vegetables. I roamed the near-empty streets for miles, asking people where to find food, pointing to my mouth, rubbing my stomach, holding up my shopping bag.. Finally, I found another long line waiting in front of a shop that sold veggies and bread, and again I was courteously escorted to the front of the line, this time by a babushka with a shy smile and steel teeth. I scored the last wilted cabbage and three small potatoes—the bread was sold out. This is high summer. Where is all the food?

DAY TWO: MONDAY
