It’s 5 a.m. and broad daylight when Prabhupada takes his first walk on the deserted streets of Moscow. As we walk from the hotel up Kremlevskiy Ploshchad toward Red Square, a few stout older ladies wearing layers of long skirts have just finished washing the Square, and they wave to us as they roll up long fire hoses. I’m fiddling with the Brownie camera and, knowing there are only twenty-four shots, I take time to compose a shot from behind Prabhupada as he strides up the fresh-washed cobblestones in front of the State Historical Museum and enters Red Square, the onion domes of St. Basil’s Cathedral in the distance.

As we stroll up the right side of Red Square, I take four more shots of Prabhupada looking around the Square. “That’s GUM, Prabhupada. It’s a world-famous department store. And this is the Kremlin, the seat of Soviet power.” And there before us is Lenin’s tomb. Hundreds of people have lined up through the night to view the embalmed corpse of Vladimir Lenin, the father of Russian communism. I snap two more photos as Prabhupada stands and gazes at this remarkable sight—the block-long queue of worshipers in heavy, dark clothes, the little house where two guards with rifles stand, waiting for the crypt to open. This is the mecca, the holy grail for communists everywhere. “Just see,” Prabhupada says, “we are always seeing beautiful Krishna dancing everywhere, and they simply want to see a dead body.”

I’m running around, trying to find good angles, seeing for the first time through a photographer’s eyes. I don’t want to waste our precious film, but this is it, the graphic highlight of our tour. “OK, Prabhupada, now slow down and stand just there.” He gazes intently at St. Basil’s domes, and I start to shoot. Then I get a better idea and lie down on the cobblestones at Prabhupada’s feet in the two o’clock position, pointing the lens up, there—no, stop laughing, Prabhupada! OK, therel Got it. Prabhupada looks down at me sprawled out on the wet pavement, laughing loudly, his whole body shaking, and he makes a joke about the devotee who will sacrifice anything, meaning: what a fool I am. He extends his sturdy black cane to help me up, but I laugh and wave it off, rising with youthful vigor. (St. Basil was also known as “The Fool for Christ.”)

Returning to Prabhupada’s room we see that Katarina has left a message: she is waiting for us down at reception. I run down, and ask her to please dial Professor Kotovsky for me at the School of Oriental Studies, USSR Academy of Sciences. She’s impressed. The professor picks up the phone, and I tell him, “Bhaktivedanta Swami is here. When can he meet you?” Kotovsky is a little surprised, hesitant, and do I detect fear? But after conferring with his colleagues he comes back on the line to say, “Yes, tomorrow you can come here, at 10 a.m.” I’m so happy as I run to Prabhupada’s room to tell him the news. At least the trip won’t be for nothing. Prabhupada will see the professor, and maybe he’ll line up some more gigs for us. Meanwhile, at least we’ve got some great photos of Srila Prabhupada in Red Square. Thank you, Krishna!

Prabhupada hands me Captain Lal’s business card and instructs me to phone and ask him if he can accompany us to see Kotovsky tomorrow. I run back down to reception, call Mr. Lal, and yes, he will pick us up at 9:30. When I hang up, Katarina is standing there. She asks, “Are you ready for your bus tour of Moscow?”

“I don’t know. I’ll ask the Swami.”

“So what do you want to do today, Srila Prabhupada? That lady, Katarina, wants us to go on a guided tour, sightseeing, on a bus with other tourists to see the high spots of Moscow.”

“Why not?” he says. “Let us go.”

The crowded bus winds through the streets of Moscow, Katarina speaking over the PA system about this or that landmark, the heroic battles, monuments to the success of the Soviet people’s struggle. Prabhupada is not very impressed. The gray buildings, people, and traffic are much like you’d see in any other dense metropolis, but here it all seems so cheerless, stark, and militant. Our first stop is just across the Moscow River from the Kremlin, near the St. Sophia quay. “Stand just there, Prabhupada.” He’s smiling, but not saying cheese as I frame him standing with Russia’s great monuments spread out behind him: the Cathedral of the Annunciation (left), Cathedral of the Archangel (center), and the

250-foot Ivan the Great Bell Tower (right). Srila Prabhupada, the intrepid transcendental tourist.

Proceeding down Komsomolskiy Prospekt, Katarina announces St. Nicholas Church, there, to the right. Prabhupada swivels in his window seat to see the huge church with an Orthodox cross on top. Its big doors are closed and two armed guards are examining the papers of a stooped, white-haired lady. “They have closed the doors. Now only some old people can go in,” he says. “This is the defect.”

A few miles later we reach the highlight of our tour: Moscow State University, Russia’s most prestigious institute of higher learning. The campus, home to fifty thousand students, covers two square miles on a bend of the Moscow River. Huge buildings extend out in a U-shape from a thirty-six-story central tower. The place was built by convict labor under Stalin’s first five-year plan. “Here they are simply learning to make bigger weapons. Such nonsense,” says Srila Prabhupada, whose interest in the tour is starting to wane. “Shyamasundar, let us go back to the hotel. You try to arrange.”

I tell Katarina that the Swami is not feeling well and we must return. She doesn’t like it, but she guides us to a taxi stand and we get into a cab. Practicing my one Russian word, I say, “National Hotel, spasibo.” As we speed back to the city, the loquacious driver wants to know who and what we are, so in a mix of English, French, and German words he gets the idea that Prabhupada is a saint, from India, and that Aravinda and I are his monk followers.

“Kree-shna.”

“Christos.”

“Ohhh.” His face lights up, and we can see that he’s a secret Christian. Prabhupada remarks that this communism is only on the surface, but deep inside the Russian people lives a strong spiritual longing. When we get to the hotel I realize we have no money, no rubles. The driver senses the confusion on my face and says, “Nyet, nyet!" meaning “Never mind,” and in a long, heartfelt monologue with the sign of the cross and gestures to heaven, our poor Russian working-class hero says he is proud to carry a saint in his car and refuses to take any money.

I go out again for food that afternoon. I return to yesterdays shop and join the line. This guy three persons behind keeps looking at me then averting his eyes. Finally, he steps out of the queue and walks to my side. “Psst! Are you yoga?” he whispers behind his hand. “Yes,” I say, “bhakti-yoga.” He tells me in broken English that he and his friends meet regularly to practice yoga. Would I come to his place tonight? He gives me the address, the apartment number, and says, “Hallway will be dark. Look for light under door.” Wow, this is really a James Bond scene, but maybe it’s what we’ve been hoping for. Wait’ll I tell Prabhupada! I tell the man I will be there at 10 p.m., and he wanders away.

The line moves up and, finally, I am happy to buy two loaves of heavy black bread. I ask the woman who takes my coupons, “Fruit? Where to buy fruit?” An onlooker tells me they have fresh cherries at a shop on the other side of Red Square and he draws me a little map. I walk a mile or so, get lost a few times, and as I’m nearing what I think must be the fruit shop, I encounter a group of fearsome young men wearing red armbands and blocking the sidewalk. One of the gang reaches out and shoves me against a wall. His friends join in—they grab my bead bag, yank at my sikha, yell at me in Russian, and one guy slaps my face!

“Hey, whattaryou doing! What’s goin on!” I shout back in English.

That slows them for a moment, but they continue to glare at me, harshly shouting, demanding something. I catch the word “dokument," and tell them “Dokument, hotel! Hotel!” They start to back off, realizing I’m a foreign tourist. “Marx goot, Lenin good” I say. That gets them—I must be a student from some exotic land joining arms for the world overthrow of capitalism. These voluntary citizen-police brush me off, apologize curtly, and wander off to keep Moscow’s streets swept clean of decadent behavior.

I deposit the bread and two kilos of fresh cherries in Prabhupada’s room and tell him of the afternoon’s adventures. He chuckles at my gang/dokument story, but says we must be very careful now—we are in a police state. He suggests bicarbonate of soda paste for the swelling slap mark on my cheek and tells me not to attend the yoga meeting tonight. “These are police. They want to trap us.” Then he enjoys the protein-rich bread and some cherries, a fruit rarely seen in India.

DAY THREE: TUESDAY

We take our second early-morning walk through Red Square, and I get a good photo of Prabhupada standing in front of the Moscow Manege on nearby Mokhovaya Street, once used as an indoor riding range to train up to two thousand cavalry officers at a time.

At 9:30, Captain Lal picks us up and we head over to Professor Kotovsky s place, about two miles east of the hotel, around Red Square and past the Lubyanka. Lubyanka? Wow, that’s KGB headquarters. Isn’t that where they torture spies? I tell Prabhupada about a book I read years ago called One Day in the Life of Ivan Denisovitch, written by a guy named Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn, which describes the horrible things that went on inside this modest building. Captain Lal points out, “There’s the statue of Dzerzhinsky. He started the KGB.”

We pull in front of 2 Armyanskiy Pereutok, the Institute of Oriental Studies, an outlying satellite of the USSR Academy of Sciences. The two-story, once-magnificent edifice looks a couple of hundred years old at least. We open the shoulder-high iron gate, mount steps under a white-columned portico, and open the front door. It’s quiet in here, antique and musty, with big rooms and hallways. I shout, “Hello?” We wait long minutes until we hear a door slam deep in the interior and see three older gentlemen approaching in ill-fitting suits.

They greet us cordially, hands are shaken, and names exchanged, and Professor Kotovsky leads us back to his office. Prabhupada is calm, but I m super excited—this is a rare occasion, years in the making—and I take a photo once everyone’s seated. One guy jumps up and motions no photographs. (It’s so dark in here they probably wouldn’t come out anyway.) Captain Lal says he has to leave to pilot his plane back to Delhi, and he asks Kotovsky to kindly call a taxi for Swamiji when we are finished. These guys are awkward, unsure of themselves, so I sit and wait for Prabhupada to take the lead. He does.

“So we have come so far to see you to discuss that modern sociology is targeting the state or the people as the owner of a certain state, but our Vedic conception is ishavasyam idam sarvam: ‘Everything is owned by Isha, the Supreme Controller.’” I quietly turn on the Hitachi cassette recorder in my book bag and, like any good spy, slide it unseen beneath the table.

Prabhupada continues, “The other day I was reading in that paper, Moscow News, there was a congress, communist congress, and the president declared that ‘We are ready to get others’ experience to improve.’ So I think the Vedic concept of socialism or communism will much improve the idea of communism. Just like we are thinking in terms of human beings, the socialistic state, that ‘Nobody should starve. Everyone must have his food.’ And in the Vedic conception of grihastha, householder, it is recommended there that a householder shall see that even a lizard or a snake living in that house should not starve. And what to speak of others? The grihastha, before taking his lunch, he is recommended to stand on the road and declare that ‘If anybody is still hungry, please come. Food is ready.’ Then, if there is no response, then the proprietor takes his lunch. In this way there are so many good concepts about this socialistic idea of communism. So I thought that these ideas might have been distributed to some of your thoughtful men. Therefore I was anxious to speak with you.”

Kotovsky says, “Yes, here in our country, we have great interest in the history of old, old god.”

Prabhupada: According to ancient Vedas and Bhagavad-gita, society is naturally divided into four classes, or ashrams—intelligent class, administrative or warrior class, mercantile class, and working class.

Kotovsky: But all of this has changed now.

Prabhupada: Yes, but we want to revive the best from the original idea. What is missing now is the aim of human life: selfrealization. Modern civilization means sense gratification due to misidentification of self with this body. But self means spirit. 1 am not speaking about religion; this is science and philosophy.

Kotovsky: So forget about Vedas. Our approach is, we don’t believe in anything without argument.

Prabhupada: Argument is allowed, but not with a challenging spirit. Just like cow dung. You are familiar? You are thinking it is dirty, but Vedas say it is pure, and science also says it is disinfectant, so why don’t you accept? You are pursuing Lenin consciousness; we are trying to understand Krishna consciousness. Both are historical personalities. Both are telling us to surrender. Our life is by surrender, is it not?

Kotovsky: To some extent you surrender—to the society, for instance, to the whole people. It seems to me that surrender is to be accompanied by revolt. I see the history of mankind as a revolt against some kind of surrender.

Prabhupada: It is full stop when you surrender to Krishna. Other surrender, yes, you have to change by revolution.

Kotovsky: Ah, ha, ha, yes. The final surrendering.

Fearless Prabhupada—he’s jumped right in, but it’s almost like teaching music to the deaf. These guys have been steeped in Marxist dogma since birth. Their entire academic careers have relied on kowtowing to the party line. Any deviation and it’s off to the gulag for you, professor. It appears that Kotovsky is trying to digest what Prabhupada is saying, but only to convert this Indian swami to his way of thinking. Agreement by the two sides seems impossible. The professors are restless, and gradually the debate breaks down.

Prabhupada surprises me by pulling out a few pages of typed manuscript, a chapter from the First Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam that he must have brought with him. He hands the pages to Kotovsky and requests that he read them. I ask the professor if he can arrange any speaking programs for the swami, and he replies, no, he is leaving early tomorrow morning for his holidays. He suggests we try the Indian Embassy.

Prabhupada: We shall arrange.

Kotovsky: Hmm, hmm, that you can. That you can...

When we ask the professor to call us a taxi, he laughs and says that that might be difficult—we would most likely have to wait an hour, and even then it might not come. He suggests we walk back to the hotel and draws us a map.

We walk the three kilometers back to the National Hotel. As we pass Lubyanka I wonder how many eyes are watching us from the rows of blank windows. Prabhupada remarks, “So, either there is no sufficient taxi, they do not get profit, or there is no sufficient demand.”

We get back to the hotel about noon, and after lunch I ask the man at reception where I can change American dollars. Rishi Kumar had given me a hundred dollars cash before we left Bombay, and yesterday’s taxi incident has convinced me that we need some rubles, even though we pay for our food with coupons. The man directs me to a nearby bank. As I approach the bank entrance, a young Indian boy stops me on the sidewalk, says his name is Madhukar, and tells me he’s from Jaipur. Do I want to change foreign money with him at a much better rate? The bank is offering ninety kopecks to the dollar ($1 = 0.9 ruble)—a rate I knew was artificially fixed by the government and far from the dollar’s real worth. Madhukar offers me twice the value—ten dollars would buy me twenty rubles. But I don’t want to jeopardize our already delicate mission, so I politely decline. Then his friend comes over, a nice-looking Russian lad, and I ask him his name.

“I am Anatoly. Do you have any jeans for sale?”

I laugh, look down at my dhoti, and say, “I don’t wear jeans.” “Do you have any Beatles records?”

“Hmm. No, but I’m traveling with the Beatles’ guru, from India. He’s in his room right now at the National Hotel. Would you like to meet him?”

They wait outside the bank, and when I finish my business they join me, excited to meet the Beatles’ guru. As we walk back to the hotel I learn that Anatoly Pinyaev is a philology student, studying the science of languages at Moscow University. He speaks very good English. Madhukar’s dad works at the Indian Embassy. I say, “Look, this swami you’re about to meet is the coolest person ever. He knows everything, and if you listen, you’ll learn the greatest secrets of eternity.”

Prabhupada’s face lights up like the sun when he sees these two boys enter his room behind me. He seems to know them and they know him, and there are no awkward moments. He feeds them cherries, and we chat all afternoon. I have never seen Prabhupada act with such charm and power: he is uncoiling the lines of destiny and gently reeling these boys in. Madhukar already knows much about Hinduism and, though he appreciates what Prabhupada is saying and the work he is doing around the world, I can see he has his own agenda. Anatoly, however—here is an evolved spirit in a famished land hungry for the truth.

Anatoly asks, “What is the origin of Sanskrit?” So, first, Prabhupada leads him through the quagmire of Max Muller and other Orientalists who have fabricated a distorted history of Aryan migrations and the Sanskrit language. Quoting from the Vedas often, Prabhupada demonstrates that Vedic literature is the world’s oldest linguistic record, and his mastery of it is self-evident. They discuss how Sanskrit is the mother of all tongues and has now been absorbed into other languages. Anatoly is enthralled. This is truth far beyond what any textbook or professor has ever taught him.

About 6 p.m., Madhukar excuses himself to go home, but Anatoly is reluctant to leave. When I mention that I need to find wheat flour and rice, Anatoly volunteers to guide me to the right shop. As it’s getting late, he says we’d better go now. Prabhupada asks if Anatoly might be free tomorrow and would like to come back. “Yes, I will be here by mid-morning,” he says.

We hit the streets and, playing to my curiosity, Anatoly leads me into a Russian department store. There are big tables as far as the eye can see, each heaped with hundreds of the same item: dinnerware here, table lamps there, men’s pants here, shirts over there—but all in the same style and color. The prices seem very cheap to me. The soles on Anatoly’s shoes are heavily worn, so I buy him a new pair. He is thankful, of course, but in that Soviet, egalitarian way—like every man deserves to have good shoes.

We move on to the grain shop, and there I buy a kilo of hard, BB-sized North Korean rice—all they had—and a kilo of coarsely ground wheat flour. Once he is sure I can find my way back to the hotel, Anatoly modestly takes his leave. He’s quiet and thoughtful as he walks away, but I can see the fire of discovery in his eyes.

When I return to Prabhupada’s room, I show him the rice, and he turns to Aravinda and tells him to cook it all into sweet rice. “That way we shall have food for three days without much botheration.”

Later, when Aravinda comes back from the kitchen, he says, “Prabhupada, I can’t find enough containers to put all this sweet rice in. I’ve filled your cooker, your glass, and your drinking water lota. There’s not much left over, but I don’t want to use the hotel’s pots.”

“Just bring my lota from the bathroom.”

“But that will be unclean!”

“Never mind. It is a sin to waste prasadam. Sometimes we have to make adjustment.”

Aravinda also makes up some chapatis, and we dine in regal style that night, Prabhupada’s face bright with triumph: he has planted a Krishna conscious seed on Russian soil.

DAY FOUR: WEDNESDAY

We got to Red Square a little later than usual this morning. When Prabhupada again saw people lined up before Lenin’s tomb, he recounted yesterdays meeting with Professor Kotovsky: “As we are worshiping Krishna, they are worshiping Lenin. So I asked Professor Kotovsky: ‘Where is the difference in principle? You are also worshiping somebody. So you have not been able to stop worshiping. How you can be godless? You have made your own God, that’s all.’ I did not say to him also that ‘This is your false God,’ but I said that ‘You are worshiping Lenin; we are worshiping Krishna. Where is the difference in philosophy? You have to worship somebody. Now it is my selection whether I shall worship Lenin or Krishna. That is a different thing. But the principle of worshiping is there in you and in me.’ He could not answer. What he’ll answer? Everywhere this is going on.”

The Red-Square scene was already less interesting for Prabhupada, but I shot a couple more good photos. On the way back, he wanted to go down into the Tverskaya Metro subway station, across from the National Hotel. We walked around the spacious subterranean aisles and platform—everything was clean and well-decorated and the air was fresh—and Prabhupada was impressed. He wrote to Tamal Krishna in Calcutta: “The underground trains are far better than American or English. The underground streets are very neat and clean. The surface streets are also daily washed. But there is some difficulty in collecting vegetarian foodstuffs.” I took a couple of photos of Prabhupada on the street in front of the hotel, surrounded by busy Muscovites and electric trolley cars.

“Prabhupada, do you see that guy standing over there in the blue suit, with the white shirt and necktie? He’s holding a newspaper.”

“Ye-e-e-sssss.” (Like, so what?)

“Don’t look, but I see him every day everywhere I go. Do you think he’s a KGB man?”

“Yes, he is following us. We are very dangerous persons.”

Anatoly arrives at the hotel at about noon, and his discussion with Srila Prabhupada intensifies. We sit in the tiny room and eat sweet rice as Prabhupada explains to him the very basics of spiritual knowledge: God is a person, the Supreme Personality; His name is Krishna. All energies, spiritual and material, emanate from Him. Spirit is eternal; matter is temporary. You exist so Krishna can enjoy your association and service. You are a small spark of His spirit with the same qualities He has—you are eternal and full of bliss and knowledge—but because you wanted to enjoy separately, Krishna gives you a material body and the material world as your playground. Your original position is with Krishna, so use your life to go back home, back to Godhead. Here is how you do that.

I can see the pieces clicking together in Anatoly’s mind. Aided by his training in logic, he seems to understand everything Prabhupada tells him, accepting it as the absolute truth. It’s becoming more and more obvious: Anatoly is Krishna’s man—and the reason Prabhupada came to the Soviet Union. The hours fly by as Prabhupada changes this boy’s heart, planting love for Krishna within it. This is the most powerful exhibition I have ever seen of Prabhupada’s mystic potency.

**\* \* \***

We had only one day left in Russia after this one, and I really wanted to visit Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn. When I mentioned this to Anatoly, he said, “I have heard he is here, in a dacha outside of Moscow. I will take you there.” I asked Prabhupada if it would be all right for me to go for a few hours to meet this man. “He’s a world-famous Russian author—he just won the Nobel Prize, even though his government objected. He spent many years in Soviet prisons, and I think he’d really like to hear about your Krishna consciousness movement.” Prabhupada thought it was a good idea.

So Anatoly and I rode the metro train about fifteen miles to an enclave west of downtown Moscow called Zhukovka, where Anatoly said that many artists, musicians, scientists, and government ministers had rustic homes in the woods called dachas. I knew this trip was risky—that it violated the terms of our tourist mandate—but we were on a roll, and I knew Krishna would help.

We got off at Zhukovka station and asked around for Solzhenitsyn, wandering up and down the bucolic lanes of this Peter-and-the-Wolf fairytale village. Everyone was suspicious, but I guess they figured that we were just too weird not to be friends of his, so someone finally directed us to the home of Mstislav Rostropovich, a world-famous classical cellist. A beautiful woman in her mid-forties answered the door. She looked distraught and very nervous, darting glances at my shaved head and sadhu robes, while she and Anatoly spoke quietly for some time. Then Anatoly turned to me and said, “This is Galina Vishnevskaya, a famous soprano opera singer, and she is Mrs. Rostropovich. She says that Mr. Solzhenitsyn was living in that woodshed back there (he pointed to a pine forest behind the house), but that KGB has come last week and he has fled.”

Galina burst into tears at this point, and I could only imagine the pain these great artists had to endure for their genius. “But if you have got some message for him?” she says.

I reach into my book bag and pull out the smuggled Easy Journey to Other Planets and hand it to her. She examines the brightly colored cover depicting a gaunt sage dreaming Radha-Krishna in a lotus balloon above his head. A radiant smile comes over her face, and she says in perfect English, “Thank you very much. I shall see that he gets it.”

On the way back to Moscow, I chuckled to think that Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn would appreciate the poetic genius in some of Prabhupada s Easy Journey statements, such as:

A Russian fiction writer is now contributing suggestions to the rest of the world that scientific progress can help man to live forever. Of course, he does not believe in a Supreme Being who is the creator. Yet we welcome his suggestion because we know that actual progress in scientific knowledge will certainly take men to the spiritual sky and inform the scientist that there is a supreme creator who has full potencies beyond all materialistic scientific conceptions.

If a small toy satellite has a creator in Russia or America, it is reasonable that the gigantic satellites have their creator in the spiritual sky. If a toy satellite requires so many scientific brains for its manufacture and its orbiting, what kind of subtle and perfect brain created galaxies of stars and maintains them in their orbits?

The Russians are unaware that in the Srimad-Bhagavatam the socialist philosophy is most perfectly described. The Bhagavatam instructs that whatever wealth exists—all natural resources (agricultural, mining, etc.)—is created by the ultimate creator, and therefore every living being has a right to take part of them.

If a prisoner thinks, “I am in this cell—let me request the warden to change my cell, and I will be happy,” that is a mistaken idea. One cannot be happy so long as he is within the prison walls. We are trying to be happy by changing cells—from capitalism to communism. The aim should be to become free from this “ism” and that “ism.” One has to change completely from this “ism” of materialism; then he can become happy. That is the program of Krishna consciousness.

I nudged my new Russian friend seated beside me and said, “Hey, Anatoly, how do you say rhinoceros in Russian—you know, the big animal with the horn on its nose?”

“Nosorog, or you can say hocopor”

**\* \* \***

It’s after nine o’clock and the sun still shining as Anatoly and I climb stairs in the National Hotel, tired but elated by our trip to Zhukovka. As we reach the second floor, we hear a woman screaming Russian curses—“Durak!”—we look down the hall and a steel pot flies out of the maids’ kitchen door and slams into the opposite wall, followed by Aravinda, running toward us, arms thrown over his head. “Mudak!” Blam! Another pot sails out and clatters in the hall.

I yell, “What’s going on, man!” He stops beside us, out of range and smiling now. He thinks it’s funny. “That lady’s mad at me because I used all her sugar in the sweet rice. I guess that stuff’s pretty precious over here.”

Prabhupada greets us warmly, hears our latest stories. We carry on talking late into the night. Finally, Anatoly rolls himself up in a blanket on the floor of my room. He lives with his mother somewhere across Moscow—too far to travel at such an hour. He says he’ll skip classes tomorrow so he can stay with Prabhupada as long as possible. He hasn’t said much about himself, but we can see that Krishna has sent the perfect person to hear and absorb advanced spiritual knowledge. Anatoly is serious, learned, and has an instinct for transcendental life. He is accustomed, like most Soviets, to austerity, and knows the inner rewards earned by sacrifice.

DAY FIVE: THURSDAY

Anatoly joins us for Prabhupada’s morning walk. He won’t let me take his photo in case the KGB gets hold of it. When we return to the hotel he asks Prabhupada more immediate questions, like, “How should I relate to the fearful situation in Russia? What should I do?” His questions take a more practical turn, getting down to brass tacks: how do I learn more? How do I help you? Where do we go from here?

Prabhupada explains everything, puts it all into perspective: you are an eternal spirit soul; the world around you is a temporary state of affairs, no better and no worse than any other time or place; you are especially dear to Krishna, so He has given you this unique chance to serve Him in this difficult condition; you are very fortunate. I am here. I will always support you. My disciples are here—thousands of devotees all over the world—they will support you.

I give Anatoly my bead bag and beads. (“Don’t worry, Hl get more in Paris.”) Prabhupada patiently teaches him how to chant on them, chanting with him, round after round until he gets the knack. He instructs Anatoly to chant before a picture of Radha and Krishna. “Krishna lives in your heart. He will give you direction how to do everything, from within.” I give Malati’s Bhagavadgita to this amazing budding Krishna devotee along with the photos that were inside it, explaining, “This is baby Krishna, this is Chaitanya, this is Bhaktivinode Thakur.” Prabhupada shows him how to apply tilak, and we give him all the tilak we have. Aravinda brings flour up from the kitchen, and Prabhupada shows the Russian boy how to mix it with water, how to form the dough into little balls, and then how to roll the balls into chapatis using an empty milk bottle. Aravinda takes Anatoly down to the kitchen and shows him how to cook the chapatis on an open flame. They puff, you see?

We think about ways to send or bring Prabhupada’s books into Russia—books will be critical to preaching here. Anatoly is quiet for a while, then says, “Prabhupada, it will be much easier if I have a foreign wife.” Wow, I’m thinking, that’s a great idea! Prabhupada says, “That is no problem. I shall send to you.”

After our lunch of sweet rice and chapatis, Madhukar arrives at the hotel with his father, Mr. Tandon, to meet Srila Prabhupada. Prabhupada is very cordial. They talk of familiar Indian places and people (with a billion people in India, it’s surprising how everyone seems to know everyone else). They speak mostly in English, but sometimes in Hindi. Prabhupada asks Mr. Tandon if the embassy will be able to arrange some speaking programs for him. He says no, they cannot get mixed up in religious matters. Then the Swami asks, “Will you help me import my books into Russia?” Mr. Tandon says it will be difficult to import books legally, but he adds, “It will be better to bring them by diplomatic pouch.” One by one, plans fall into place. I take a photo of Mr. Tandon—it’s the last one on the roll.

We have to be up early tomorrow morning to catch our Aeroflot flight to Paris, so late that evening Anatoly says his goodbyes to Srila Prabhupada and departs, with the future of Russia resting on his shoulders. I stay up very late, typing a long letter dictated by Prabhupada:

My Dear Professor Kotovsky,

Please accept my greetings. It was a great pleasure to talk with you on Tuesday and I hope you will kindly read the chapter of Srimad-Bhagavatam which I left with you. From your book “Soviet Studies of India” I understand that academician Mr. A. P. Baranrikov completed a great translation, working the matter of Ramayana into Russian. Srimad-Bhagavatam is the ripe, mature fruit of the Vedic knowledge, and Ramayana is but a partial representative of Srimad-Bhagavatam. I have already given you a sample copy of my translation of Srimad-Bhagavatam (one chapter only) and there are many hundreds of chapters like that. I think some of you should translate this great book of knowledge into Russian and it will be a great contribution. I am fully prepared to cooperate with you. India has very many authorized transcendental literatures for the benefit of the whole world. I am glad to note on page 72 of your book that “They (the Soviet scholars) regard the ancient literary heritage of India not as a petrified miracle of bygone times but as a living and growing tradition that exerts a fruitful influence on present-day literature and remains an inexhaustible source of literary and cultural development of contemporary India.”

The Russian people as a growing nation, and having a good feeling upon Indias culture, may take advantage of this treasure house of transcendental literatures, not only for the benefit of the Russian people but for the whole world. Whatever is done by a great nation or a great man is followed by ordinary persons, so it is my mission to distribute the treasure house of Indias transcendental knowledge to the whole world, and your cooperation in this connection will be a great asset. You wanted to see the manuscripts of my lectures, therefore I am sending herewith an Introduction to the lectures and if you so desire I shall be glad to send essays on these subjects:

1\.    Vedic Conceptions of Socialism and Communism

2\.    Scientific Values of Classless Society

3\.    Knowledge by Authoritative Tradition

I hope this is meeting you in good health.

Your ever well-wisher,

A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

\[Epilogue: Srila Prabhupada never heard from Professor Kotovsky again. Anatoly was initiated as Ananta Shanti das, and from this tiny beginning—and despite jails, asylums, torture, even government-sponsored killings suffered by those he brought to

Krishna consciousness—Ananda Shanti single-handedly created an underground cadre of Hare Krishna devotees that gradually spread throughout the USSR. By the late 1970s, the KGB had set up a special directorate just to quash the Krishna movement. But the Soviet empire collapsed almost exactly twenty years after Prabhupada’s brief visit, as he predicted it would. Prabhupada never returned to Russia. He didn’t have to. For him, five days was enough to radically change the consciousness of a great nation. I am the witness.\]

**\* \* \***

We left the National Hotel early to catch our 10:10 a.m. Aeroflot flight, scheduled to arrive 12:05 p.m. at Le Bourget Airport in Paris. Once we were in the air, Prabhupada asked me if I had anyone in mind who could become Anatoly’s wife. I said I was thinking of Mondakini dasi, a French devotee living at Bury Place in London. Prabhupada said, “I was thinking the same thing.”

He had just bagged another mega-rhino, and he was glowing with accomplishment. Chasing rhinos with the Swami was all I ever wanted to do for the rest of my life.

Suddenly the plane was rocked by heavy turbulence that continued for long minutes as we sat chanting and gripping our seats. Somewhere over Poland, the Ilyushin-62’s heating system collapsed and the plane descended thousands of feet to preserve cabin heat while attendants ran around handing passengers one thin blanket each. Aravinda and I gave our blankets to Prabhupada. We could see our breath as we talked—it must have been near freezing onboard—and we became very concerned for him. О Krishna, please don’t let Prabhupada suffer like this!

I keep looking over at Prabhupada snuggled in blankets with only the tip of his nose showing, clicking his beads beneath the blankets, hearing muffled sibilants when he says Krishna’s name—and I feel so protective, so responsible, like a father for his son. It dawns on me that this secretary’s service is not just about taking dictation and typing letters. There is really only one consideration for this job: doing whatever it takes to keep Srila Prabhupada happy.

Prabhupada is happiest when he’s spreading Krishna consciousness in the biggest way possible. He has all good qualities in full, but Prabhupada has this one quality that attracts me most: he is a visionary. Kind of a dreamer myself, I have attached myself to this one of his many aspects, and I renew my vow to use every ounce of my puny strength to advance his dreams. It’s all so new. I’m in the clouds with Srila Prabhupada now, with no other responsibility than to protect him, follow his quick mind, look ahead, and try to increase his happiness at every moment.

**\* \* \***

During the rapid descent, my sinuses clogged and I was seized by a terrible migraine. I was still reeling in pain when we landed in Paris just after noon on June 25. To top it off, French customs officials, suspicious of our travels in cold-war Russia, detained us for an hour before we finally staggered outside to greet the clamor and great joy of about thirty devotees. The Paris devotees had been struggling mightily against France’s established, bourgeois indifference, but they had finally managed to move out of their squalid downtown quarters and into a nice rented house-temple at 26 Rue D'Etienne d’Orves in the leafy suburb of Fontenay-aux-Roses six miles south from the Eiffel Tower.

That afternoon, Prabhupada held a press conference at the India Tourism Office on Boulevard Haussmann, near the Louvre. In the evening he spoke at the famous Olympia music hall on Boulevard des Capucines. The next day we flew to Los Angeles (gaining a day by flying west), stayed the night in L.A., then on June 27, hopped up to San Francisco for their fifth annual Ratha-yatra festival.

The 1971 San Francisco Ratha-yatra is a huge affair, with three big rath cars, hundreds of devotees, and ten thousand celebrants-all this just four summers since our humble beginnings on the back of a rented truck in 1967 s Summer of Love. After so many years in the UK and India, I’m excited to be back in the land where everything works, my own hometown, surrounded by so many eager, competent devotees. This would also be my first Ratha-yatra with Srila Prabhupada physically present.

The opening ceremonies were held on a stage erected at the intersection of Haight and Masonic, and a perky young mayoral candidate named Dianne Feinstein, president of San Franciscos Board of Supervisors, praised the Swami and his Krishna movement. (She had destiny written on her face, and although she lost the mayoral race to Joseph Alioto, Feinstein later reigned as mayor for ten years and has served as a US senator from California for the past twenty-six years.)

Prabhupada followed her remarks with his famous speech, in which he said, “When our eyes are anointed with love of God, we can see Him everywhere.... \[A devotee\] can see the Lord every moment within his heart and everywhere, wherever he goes.”

Many have described how Prabhupada sat kinglike on Lord Jagannath’s car that day, then how he got down to dance with his devotees. But no words can convey the mass ecstasy that swept over us all. The procession, preceded by a thirty-foot figure of Lord Chaitanya, His golden arms raised in rapture to a perfect blue sky, His head ringed in white roses, made its way ponderously through Haight-Ashbury, wound down through Golden Gate Park, and arrived at the Pacific Ocean. Every time I looked at Prabhupada he was grinning from ear to ear, the lightning-bolt veins at his temples filled and throbbing, and I thought he might burst from joy.

On June 29, we fly back down to Los Angeles, Prabhupada’s Western world headquarters, now called “New Dwaraka.”

Prabhupada had left the US for India nearly a year earlier. He has now returned to the Western world, the source of the manpower and money that is fueling his global expansion, radiant with triumph and success. During his absence, the Krishna movement has grown so rapidly in the US that most of his disciples have never seen him before. Now they are crying, almost mad with delight.

GBC, temple presidents, and the spiritual leaders of the US zones are gathered in L.A.—Karandhar, Keshava, Vishnujan Swami, Jayananda, and others from across the country whom I’d never met. There are new ISKCON temples in Amherst, Massachusetts; in Austin, Dallas, and Houston, Texas; Atlanta, Georgia; Baltimore, Maryland; Boulder and Denver, Colorado; Chicago, Illinois; Detroit, Michigan; Gainesville, Tallahassee, and Miami, Florida; Laguna Beach, San Diego, San Jose, and Santa Barbara, California; Maui, Hawaii; New Orleans, Louisiana; Philadelphia and Pittsburg, Pennsylvania; Salt Lake City, Utah; St. Louis, Missouri; Washington DC; and Wilmington, Delaware. Things have been well managed in the US over the past year, but like any booming new corporation, ISKCON is fraught with growing pains. Prabhupada meets with his leaders to fix their problems and plan for the future. Top priority: send sixty qualified devotees to India to assist with the work there.

Between July 2 and July 13, Prabhupada initiates dozens of devotees. Seeing so many beautiful, single young boys and girls at his daily lectures, he cautions us and explains the real meaning of love:

If I have got an instinct to love others, so why God will not have this instinct to love others? If I have got attraction for the opposite sex, why God should not have? Why He should not be attracted by Radharani? Very simple truth. But the difference is: here, everything is false. False means the attraction is not real attraction. But there the attraction is real. Here I am attracted with a boy, with a girl—after six months, finished. Because there are so many defects.

Therefore the attraction does not exist. Just like you see one mannequin in the dress shop, very nice girl standing, but it is a false. Similarly, this body made of material elements is not our real body. False. Similarly, the girl’s body is false. Therefore because false, the so-called love and attraction is also false. Therefore our so-called love breaks. There is no love here. It cannot be. There cannot be any love. That tendency is there, but due to this material contamination, lust is going on in rhe name of love. Actually it is lust.

Prabhupadas L.A. quarters above the temple room were open around the clock for devotees who wished to visit, catch up, ask questions, seek advice. Prabhupadas open-door policy created a new problem, and I had to somehow insulate Srila Prabhupada from the constant flow of devotees seeking admittance to his rooms. I tried my best to schedule appointments, but it was practically useless; Prabhupada would hear someone outside and ask me, “Oh, who is there?”

“Well, a couple of devotees from Pittsburg, Prabhupada. They’re flying back today and wanted to see you.”

“Let them in!”

People flowed in and out of Srila Prabhupada’s room almost nonstop. He seemed to love it, though, so I dropped the gestapo act and admitted everyone freely.

In the early mornings, Prabhupada dictated letter after letter to catch up with his correspondence. During these days in L.A., he wrote fifty-two letters in fifteen days.

Aravinda was slow to transfer Prabhupada’s words from Dictaphone to typewriter, so we devised a new system to streamline the letter chain: I would discuss the points in each letter with Srila Prabhupada, write out his replies in clear longhand, and give the finished copy to Aravinda to type. The typed draft was then given back to Prabhupada to go over, add anything, and sign. I was beginning to see how very important these letters were. When a devotee received a letter from Srila Prabhupada, he or she would share it with the devotees at his or her temple. A single letter could raise enthusiasm and dictate all local affairs until the next letter arrived. So I began to ask Prabhupada more personal and detailed questions about each point in the letters, often pressing him to elaborate, expand, and strengthen his replies. The letters became longer, more comprehensive, grammatically correct, and easier to read.

After long years in London and India, it just felt great to be back in America among old friends in the land of instant hot water, good food, and smooth-flowing traffic. In some ways, I was just along for the ride. Prabhupada’s big leaders were here and were taking care of his food, transport, lodging, and programs. My sole duty was up close and personal: to coordinate and implement Srila Prabhupada’s immediate desires and to keep an eye on everything around him, especially on what was next in the busy days and weeks ahead. Up to now, Prabhupada’s travels had been rather random, depending largely on where he was needed most and who sent him tickets. But now that temples had been established across the globe, Prabhupada began to see his travel as a straight line circling the planet from west to east, with side trips north and south from that center line.

We hashed out Srila Prabhupada’s upcoming itinerary and sent letters to organize plane tickets for the weeks to come. Prabhupada requested three round-the-world airline tickets, one for himself, one for me, and one for Aravinda: L.A.—NY—London—Paris-Bombay—Calcutta—Tokyo—Honolulu—L.A. These tickets cost about $1,000 apiece in the summer of 1971. Tickets arrived for Prabhupada to visit Detroit as well, so he fixed our eastbound departure for July 16. Los Angeles-Detroit would to be the initial leg of his second round-the-world transcendental journey.

I phoned my folks in Oregon, and they offered to send me a plane ticket from L.A. to Salem if I would come up and visit them for a few days. I asked Srila Prabhupada if I could skip the Detroit leg and meet him in New York on July 20, and he approved. It was high summer in the fruitful Willamette Valley, usually my favorite time of year there, but as I wandered around my parents’ home and farm in dhoti and flip-flops (“Why don’t you wear some shoes, Sammy?”), there was no nostalgia, no lingering sentiments. All I could think about was Prabhupada. I worried whether he was being tended to well, and was much relieved to get on the plane to New York City.

**\* \* \***

It was hot and sticky in Brooklyn. The ISKCON temple at 439 Henry Street was packed to the rafters with two hundred sweltering devotees. Most of Prabhupada’s time was used to initiate devotees in nine separate ceremonies—120 new disciples. The temple room windows were open, there were a few weak fans (no AC), and with the sacred fire burning it was well over a hundred degrees in there. But Prabhupada was tireless, resolute. He seemed a little more solemn here on the East Coast than he had in California. He presided at every ceremony, lectured in the temple morning and night, and even took short side trips up to Boston and down to Atlanta and Gainesville, where he initiated even more disciples. His stamina was beyond understanding.

One afternoon, a new devotee named Pratyatosa das offered to buy Srila Prabhupada the latest four-track Sony tape recorder, and he described the new technology to him:

Prabhupada: Computer?

Pratyatosa: Yeah, they could be used in many, many ways in the Society. I think the most important use is for helping in transcribing and editing and composing. A computer can do composing automatically.

Prabhupada: Automatically?

Pratyatosa and other devotees went on to explain computer language, paper tape punches, photon printing machines, the Honeywell nationwide network, and on and on. When they finished, Prabhupada gave his final opinion on the dawning digital electronic age: “But as soon as an electronic is a little sick, the whole thing stops.”

Nevertheless, Prabhupada’s secretarial team acquired a SmithCorona portable electric typewriter with interchangeable ribbon cartridges, and both Aravinda and I were pleased by how much easier it was to type on this electronic marvel. We also bought a bulky 110/220 transformer, which we would need in Europe, India, and other foreign lands to convert power for the new machine.

Shortly after our arrival in Brooklyn, a devotee told me that George Harrison was in New York and would soon be performing at Madison Square Garden. Wow! Good news! Since I’d left London for India in October 1970, communication with George had been sparse, and I missed him very much. He was my dear godbrother, and had done so much to spread Krishna consciousness in the world. I had written to him almost every week from India, in tiny script on both sides of flimsy aerograms, describing cobras and tigers and yogis floating by, cardamom and excrement and the smells of jasmine, dust, and monsoon rain—shrill noises and wondrous rhapsodies, jeweled maharajas and leprous beggars, demons and humble saints and, of course, Srila Prabhupada—what he had said, what he was doing, how like a commanding general he had become, leading his tiny army against the massive entrenched forces of impersonalism, politics, and greed that had usurped modern India. “Once again, George, Krishna’s name resounds throughout the land of His birth and glorious pastimes.”

I phoned Terry Doran at Friar Park in the UK, who told me that George and Pattie were staying at the Park Lane Hotel, and he gave me their room number. “Yes, George and Ravi are having a benefit for the Bangladesh victims—you know, East Pakistan, the war, the cyclones? The people there are starving. He’s trying to get Bobby to play, and Eric. You should give him a call.”

Over the phone George says, “Come on over. It’s a bit hectic, but we could use some prasadam over here.” So I schlepp bags of prasadam from the temple on the subway from Bergen Street to 59th in Manhattan, anxious to meet my friend. What’s he cooking up this time? The Park Lane Hotel, at the south end of Central

Park, appears elegant and exclusive. The liveried doorman opens the huge glass door and welcomes this Hare Krishna monk as if I belong there, and the tension lifts. I call Mal Evans, Georges roadie, from the lobby phone, and he guides me up to the command center. George is leaping around, flushed with excitement, and phones are ringing all over the place.

“Hey, George, what’s this all about? People are running everywhere, man! It looks like you’ve got the whole floor up here. Are these all your people?”

“Shyamsundar! Hare Krishna! Hey, what a great surprise. It’s really good to see you.”

“Well, Prabhupada just happened to be passing through New York. He’s over at the Brooklyn temple, and I—” Chris O’Dell waves from across the room. “Hare Krishna, Chris! How’re ya doin’! Anyway, George, I heard you were in town. What’s this all about?”

“Were puttin’ on a charity concert. It was Ravi’s idea. For the Bangladesh refugees. You know, they’ve been hit by cyclones, war. They’re in real bad shape over there. We’ve got Madison Square Garden booked for August 1. Two shows. Will you be around?”

“Yeah, were heading over to London on the second.”

“Great, I’ll get you a seat. I’ve got some reserved down in the front somewhere.” He shouts across the room: “Chris, don’t forget those lighting people. Give ’em a ring! Sorry. Yeah, hey it’s good to see you. I really love all your long letters from India,” he laughs.

“It’s been a year or so, huh?”

“I’m trying to get John to play, and Eric, even Bobby, but he hasn’t done a concert in years so he’s a bit spooked. I was thinking to have a kirtan in the show, get the whole crowd chanting Hare Krishna, but I don’t know. That may not fit in now. We’ll see.”

“Wow, hey, I brought you guys some prasadam. What else can I do to help?”

“Just keep bringing that prasad! Gotta run. Haribol!”

I came back the following afternoon with huge sacks of prasadam, paper plates, and plastic forks. George’s floor at the Park

Lane is swarming with people in a hurry. Total chaos. I looked around for George and heard a guitar being played in a nearby room. I opened the door and there’s Bob Dylan in a chair strumming “Like a Rolling Stone” on an acoustic guitar, trying to remember the lyrics. From time to time he stops and glances at his notes scattered on the bed. He stumbles over a phrase, “You used to ride ... You used to ride on the ...”

“Chrome horse with your diplomat,” I say, plunk down a plate of prasadam on the bed, and leave.

George had been at this for a month. No one had ever put together a charity concert of this proportion. Just to figure out the legal structure and who’d be getting the money—all that, plus gather the best musicians in the world on one stage—“You wouldn’t believe the headaches,” George says. “Krishna is pulling the strings, Shyamsundar, that’s for certain.”

Then George says, “Wait a minute, I want to show you something.” He takes me over to a closet, pulls out a white cowboy-cut suit jacket, and says, “This is my concert coat. See, I’ve had a tailor embroider the Hare Krishna mantra inside the collar and round the cuffs, OM signs here on both lapels. The writing is very small, but I’ll know it’s there! And they made a funky white tie with OM on it, but I’m not gonna wear it. Here, you take it!” He laughs, like, yeah, you’re really going to wear this, hands me the tie, and I shrug and stuff it in my bead-bag.

“But you know, it looks like we won’t be able to have a kirtan on the stage. Nobody seems to like the idea but me. We’ve got Ravi and his crew, and that’s already too much Far East stuff. Later, maybe I’ll have another special concert for the Krishnas. But I’m a little worried. We haven’t had much time for rehearsals.”

“That’s all right, George. You’ll be singing ‘My Sweet Lord,’ and that’s got the mantra. What you’re doing is the highest form of service to Krishna: spreading His name to millions of people. Krishna will help, you’ll see. The concert will be perfect!”

Madison Square Garden, August 1, 1971, Concert for Bangladesh, 2 p.m. matinee show: George walked onstage, and after twenty thousand fans gave him a five-minute standing ovation, he looked down at Bhavananda and me in our front-row-center seats, grinned, and greeted us with a soft “Hare Krishna.” What followed was one of the great rock music spectacles of our time.

George sang “Awaiting On You All” (“Try chanting the names of the Lord and you’ll be free”) and “Hear Me Lord.” During the ten-minute version of “My Sweet Lord,” Ringo, Billy Preston, Bob Dylan, Eric Clapton, and Leon Russell joined in the chorus: “Ha-re Krish-na, Ha-re Krish-na, Krish-na Krish-na ...”

Back at Henry Street, I described the concert to Srila Prabhupada. He was a little disappointed that the organizers wouldn’t allow us to have a kirtan onstage, but he was proud of George and all he was doing to spread Krishna’s name.

**\* \* \***

On the afternoon of Monday, August 2, we flew from Kennedy Airport to London Heathrow. The London devotees had been looking for larger premises for a year, so far without success. As 7 Bury Place filled beyond capacity, small groups of devotees had spun off to start new ISKCON centers in Birmingham, Cardiff, Edinburgh, Manchester, and Amsterdam. Over the past one year in Great Britain, the Hare Krishnas had become a recognized part of national life, familiar to British citizens from Devonshire to Inverness. They were seen on Oxford Street every day. Footballers chanted Hare Krishna between halves at Arsenal games. These strange people were the Beatles’ friends, so you had to take them seriously. If you took a tourist poll, “What are the three items you most associate with London?” the answer would be, “Big Ben, black taxis, and the Hare Krishnas.”

Prabhupada had been away from his beloved England for one year and eight months. In addition to the forty-plus Bury Place residents, devotees from France, Germany, and other parts of the

UK descended on London as soon as they heard that Prabhupada was coming. We arrived at Heathrow around midnight to a tumultuous welcome from a hundred devotees, many of whom had never seen Prabhupada before. Malati had flown in from India a few days earlier. At the airport I held my little Saraswati again and smothered her with affection. She giggled, happy to see her father. “You’re three years old now, Saraswati! Are you helping your mother?” “Yes, and I have a new doll.”

I was so happy to see the British devotees! Their manic energy demonstrated the same spirit we had planted on these shores exactly three years before, a “just go for it, Krishna will provide” mood. Mukunda and Janaki had left Britain for Washington State a few months back, turning the London management over to eighteen-year-old Irish-born Tribhuvanath das. He was unschooled and inexperienced in management, but doing a good job under the circumstances. Income from Radha Krishna Temple records and concerts had run dry, and collections from street sankirtan and book sales were barely enough to meet the cost of living in downtown London. Whatever funds the temple could gather had just been spent on the third annual UK Ratha-yatra, which was celebrated on July 4. Money was scarce, but spirits were high, and that was the main thing. Money would somehow come, by Krishna’s grace, whenever it was needed. The UK was my GBC zone of responsibility and, hearing of its ups and downs over the past few months, I had been worried. But now Prabhupada was here, and his presence would surely steady this ship and set it back on course.

Prabhupada settled into his second-floor room at Bury Place and greeted senior disciples such as Dhananjaya, Kulashekar, Jai Hari, Tribhuvanath, and Ranchor. Aspiring new devotees also gathered in his room, including Mr. Arnold, a middle-aged man who worked at Midland Bank. Prabhupada caught up on the news and began to sort out the temple’s problems as if they were his own—like how to raise half a million pounds to buy a building in downtown London.

Nodding to me, Prabhupada says, “So you will raise one-fourth million.”

“Okay,” I say, and begin laughing.

“And Mr. Arnold will help from the bank with one-fourth million.”

Mr. Arnold replies, “I don’t think I can get my bank to loan it, my lord, but certainly we’ll go and see the bank,” and the room erupts with laughter.

Yeah, we’re in the deep end all right, way over our heads. We have no money, but we throw these sums around like they’re peanuts. With Prabhupada among us, anything and everything really is possible.

And that was the joy and the lightness: the laughter in all of this, Prabhupada just tickled my funny bone with so many things he said and did. I found myself cracking up in his conversations with others, and even in his lectures. Sometimes, together, we acted almost like the American TV duo Johnny Carson and Ed McMahon—Ed laughed at nearly everything Johnny said. Srila Prabhupada often gave me that sideways glance, raising his eyebrows, indicating, “You like the show, eh? Am I doing all right?” I was like Prabhupada’s audience meter, when I’d send him a laugh, he’d up the energy and lay you out in the aisles. And then he’d come in with the kicker: “So long I am here, let us try something tangible, not simply fairytale proposals.”

I ask, “Srila Prabhupada, what is laughter?”

He says, “It is the difference between what you expect and what really happens.”

“Well, then, Prabhupada, why do you always make me laugh?” “Perhaps you expect too much from me.”

**\* \* \***

I’d become accustomed to Prabhupada’s furious pace, but here in London his pace seemed to double. He launched into a vigorous lecture routine, speaking in the temple morning and night and at outside programs in Hampstead Town Hall, the Hindu Centre, and St. Pancras Town Hall. Prabhupada’s audiences in London were more intellectual and challenging than elsewhere, and there were many well-informed Indians present, so the question-and-answer periods that followed his lectures were often longer than the lecture itself.

On August 3, he spoke from Srimad-Bhagavatam:

So our proposition is that we are somehow or other in an envelopment of so many mistakes and therefore we are suffering. Suffering means due to ignorance. Just like a man does not know the law of the country. A civil instance: just like here in London the car is driven from the left side. In America the car is driven by the right side. So suppose one comes from America, he’s driving the car from the right side, and the police arrest. “Why do you arrest me, sir?” “Because you are driving on the right side.” “That I know.

I do not know that you have to drive left side.” “That does not mean you are free from criminal charges. Come to the court.” So this criminality has happened on account of ignorance. So any criminal person wrongly guided means ignorance. Therefore we have to develop real knowledge. The real knowledge is that God is one, God is great, we are part and parcel of God, and therefore.we have to serve God. This is knowledge. Thank you very much.

Twenty-eight very thoughtful questions followed this lecture, and Prabhupada delivered perfect, concise answers to each. For example:

Guest: When the living entity comes to the material world, what is the state of the spiritual body? And then how does this spiritual body begin to develop?

Prabhupada: Spiritual body is there already. You simply cover yourself with another material coating. Just like you are trying to go to the moon planet. Your body is there, but you coat your body just suitable to the moon planet. And that dress costs $40,000 or something like that, \[laughter\] So after spending $40,000 for each man and going for so many years, they have collected a little dust. That is their success.

Srila Prabhupada was always thinking about his plans for expansion in India, and he wanted to revisit Mr. Apa Pant, the Indian High Commissioner for the UK who, at their last London meeting in November 1969, had promised Swamiji his help. Ара B. Pant was the son of the Maharaja of Aundh, a princely state near Pune, in Maharashtra.

The temple didn’t own a car, so on the morning of August 4 a hippie with a flower-painted VW Beetle volunteered to drive Prabhupada and his entourage to India House in nearby Aldwych. Mr Pant’s aristocratic bearing and love for Krishna were obvious. Prabhupada spoke to him about Rupa Goswami and gave him a copy of The Nectar of Devotion. There was no business talk for about half an hour, but then Prabhupada said, “The last time I was here, you said ‘Swamiji, whatever you want I will do if I can.’ Now I have one request.” Then Prabhupada asked two things of Mr. Pant: to help obtain missionary visas for his foreign devotees traveling to India, and to invite Indira Gandhi to the groundbreaking ceremony for his proposed Mayapur temple project.

Tribhuvanath: I was fascinated. Prabhupada was an ambassador from the Goswamis, and when he met the Indian Ambassador he did it on his ground, not on the Indian ambassadors ground. Prabhupada didn’t behave like a diplomat or in any way try to conform to a mundane idea of negotiations.

On August 5, Prabhupada held a press conference in the temple room at Bury Place. As usual, Prabhupada wowed and charmed the reporters with his astounding replies to their questions. As his secretary, I sensed another new duty: to act as translator or middleman at these interviews, prepared to nudge conversations along so that both sides understood each other better.

Interviewer: But if somebody else were to say that God had told him something else, would you equally believe him?

Shyamasundar: It’s not that we don’t accept other religious processes.

Prabhupada: No, we believe other process. Just like there are steps. If you want to go to the topmost story, so you go by steps. So some of them have gone fifty steps, some of them have gone hundred steps, but to complete the required steps is a thousand steps.

Christianity was always a hot topic in Britain, and Prabhupada was frequently asked how Christ related to Krishna. On the morning of August 7, a few devotees were in his room at Bury Place, and Prabhupada said, “Christo—this is a Greek word. But krista is a perverted representation of krishna. In India also, still, if one’s name is Krishna, we call Krista—still. My younger brother’s name is Krishna, so we were calling him by the name Krista. And from this word krista, the Christ has come. So this Christian religion has got a link with Krishna. And Jesus Christ went to India. He remained there for twelve years. He resided in Jagannath temple. But there was some misunderstanding; therefore he left Jagannath temple. He went to a Buddhist temple in Katmandu. So at that time, when he was staying in India, he received one letter, his father and mother died, so he returned home. So Jesus Christ remained for considerable years in India.”

I said, “Yeah. There’s a whole period of his life where there’s nothing said in the Bible about Christ, about his movements.”

“Yes,” said our all-knowing Srila Prabhupada.

On the afternoon of August 7, the Radha Krishna Temple band, riding the last wave of last year’s hit record Govinda, appeared at the Harmony Farm Festival at Tunbridge Wells along with Stockridge, Brinsley Schwarz, and the Pink Fairies. I didn’t go, but I was told the Festival people paid for the devotees’ petrol and gave them £15 cash.

A Londoner named Patrick Fitzgerald ran a printing business in Holborn, and we often used his services to print flyers and posters. Fitzgerald was a member of Mensa, the worldwide society that at that time required its members to have an IQ over 200. When Patrick heard that Prabhupada was in town, he came to Bury Place on August 9 to ask the Swami some questions.

Patrick: There’s one philosophical point I would like you to elucidate. If you could just explain to me how this fits in: the monistic or the unitary view that Krishna is everything, all and in all, and then at the same time that there is this world of illusion which is somehow distinct from Krishna. Could you just explain this point to me?

Prabhupada: This is very easy to understand. Just like you are recording my speeches in the tape recorder. When you play back, it will speak just like I am speaking, but I am not there. Is it not a fact?

Prabhupada’s daily conversations with intelligent Brits at Bury Place were so unique, so important—I had never seen him so mentally nimble—that I began to record them. This record of Prabhupada’s quick brain at work was unprecedented and priceless. We began to see that inviting intelligent persons to Prabhupada’s room for discussions enlivened him more than anything else.

George was still in America putting together the film of the Bangla Desh concert. He had asked me to check on Eric Clapton as soon as I got back to England, to “give him a little Krishna love,” deeply worried about Eric’s addiction to hard drugs. So on August 10 I asked Prabhupada for an afternoon off and took the bus out to Eric’s estate, Hurtwood Edge, in Surrey, about forty miles southwest of London. Eric’s long-time girlfriend, Alice, answered the door, and we sat for a couple of hours waiting for Eric to get himself together and join us. Alice was the pretty daughter of David Ormsby-Gore, the fifth Baron Harlech, a former ambassador to the US and a well-known patron of the arts. She asked me many intelligent questions about Krishna philosophy, and we were deeply engrossed in discussion when suddenly, through the front door, clattered Ginger Baker and two of Eric’s other musician friends. Eric came downstairs-not really glad to see me-but he greeted me with a soft “Hare Krishna.” Before long they’d set up instruments and amps and were jamming. They played one song that moved me very much, and from the words Eric sang it might have been called “Sunshine of Your Love.”

“Wow, Eric,” I said, “that’s a beautiful song!”

“What? You’ve never heard it before?!” They laughed and joked that they were meeting the only person on the planet who’d never heard their song.

It was after midnight when at last I said that I’d better get going. No one knew if the buses to London ran that late, so Alice volunteered to drive me home. We squeezed into one of Eric’s Ferraris, just one of maybe half a dozen sleek cars lined up in his garage, and roared off into the night. I was witlessly scared as Alice hit 120 mph down the one-lane, tunnel-like country roads. They were paved paths, really, and so old they’d sunken into the earth, overtopped by hedges and oaks. I threw my mind into neutral and relaxed, praying that Krishna would save me for another day. Alice fired question after question about the Supreme Personality of Godhead as we raced toward sure oblivion. Finally, we topped a rise near Tooting Bee and I saw the dawn sun lightly touching St. Paul’s dome on the northern skyline. I was glad to be out of that haunted house and almost at the feet of my spiritual master in the sunshine of his love.

**\* \* \***

I was seeing firsthand just how many balls the Master Juggler can spin in the air, each one important to his overall harmony of action. Prabhupada was satisfied that the US operations were going well, thanks to good managers and steady income streams from public sankirtan, so he was free to plunge into the London affairs, such as finding and paying for a bigger building, launching programs to increase book sales, and rearranging managers.

Dayananda arrived in London from the US—he had pioneered temples in Los Angeles and Florida—and took over the post of treasurer. His management experience soon began to have a positive effect.

Despite all that was going on in London, Prabhupadas attention was never far from India. He corresponded almost daily with his leaders there and was especially focused on getting something going in Mayapur. Tamai Krishna had recently acquired nine bighas (3 acres) of land in Mayapur from the Sek brothers, Muslim farmers, for Rs. 14,500/- ($1933)—about $640 per acre—and he and Jayapataka were negotiating for more. One day, Prabhupada asked me if any of the London devotees were building engineers. “Well, Naranarayan is here,” I said. “He knows how to build things. And Ranchor—I believe he has some architectural training and knows how to make plans and elevations.”

Prabhupada immediately called these two devotees to his room and began sketching out his Mayapur master plan for these eager boys to develop. Each day for a week Naranarayan, Ranchor, and the German artist, Vasudeva, accompanied Srila Prabhupada on his early morning walks as he pointed to features on surrounding buildings that he would like to replicate in Mayapur. One day, he aimed his cane at the magnificent eight-story Victorian hotel across Russell Square and said, “Just see that—‘Hotel Russell.’ What is the height?”

After some muttered discussion, the boys agreed it was about a hundred feet with its gingerbread towers.

“We shall make thrice as big,” Prabhupada announced.

“Wow, Prabhupada, that’s over three hundred feet!”

“Yes.”

On a sheet of plywood, the architects began to build a scale model of the Mayapur plan, following Prabhupada’s daily comments and advice—the temple here, guesthouses over there, prasadam hall, kitchens. On August 14, Prabhupada wrote to Tamal Krishna in Calcutta: “I have already engaged Nara Narayan, Vasudeva, and Ranchor to prepare a grand scale plan for the Mayapur land and as soon as it is prepared, Naranarayan will go there.”

And Prabhupada didn’t forget about Russia, either. Mondakini devi dasi, a young, dark-haired beauty from France, was a very surrendered devotee. Her self-effacing sweetness, saintly behavior, and determination were special; she was an angel among angels. Mondakini was serving as head pujari for Radha-Londonishvara but was getting restless. She wanted to go out and preach somewhere. When she heard that Srila Prabhupada had been to Russia, she said to her friends, “That is a place I would never go!” A few days after Prabhupada arrived in London he called her to his room and asked her if she had considered marriage. When she said, “No,” he said, “Oh, you have not found anyone good enough for you?”

“I never want to get married,” she said.

Then Srila Prabhupada smiled very brightly and asked in a casual way, “Mondakini, would you like to go to Russia and marry this boy, Anatoly? You can help develop the preaching in Russia.”

“Yes, Srila Prabhupada!” she said immediately, swallowing her fears in swift commitment to an almost impossible mission. She began to learn Russian from another devotee, Sudurjaya, who knew the language, and soon she would leave for Paris to obtain her visa for Moscow.

Prabhupada maintained his dynamic pace. He walked for an hour each morning in Russell Square, Regents Park, or in his favorite spot, St. James’s Park—the spires of Buckingham Palace and Whitehall visible through towering willows. He lectured, wrote letters, met and counseled devotees throughout the day, greeted any guest who came to his door, and then translated all night in the solitary glow of his table lamp, with little rest. After the terrific heat of New York City, it was rainy and unseasonably cold in London. And, after so much punishing travel, Prabhupada’s health started to run down.

In the very early morning of August 11, I’m sleeping on the lower bunk in a small room next to Prabhupada’s room when I hear my name called out by my spiritual master—Shyamasundar!—as if in a dream. I fly up, slam my head on the bunk above, and dash to

Prabhupada’s door. I open it, slowly, and Prabhupada—small, grayfaced, and waiflike—falls forward into my arms. Stunned, I pick him up with both arms like a doll—he doesn’t weigh anything, like feathers—and I lay him on his back on his bed. His eyes are closed and he’s shivering. I rush to the gas fireplace and turn the heat as high as it will go, then cover him with every blanket and shawl I can find.

“No hospital,” Prabhupada whispers, just as I think to call an ambulance. Confused—bewildered by this dreamlike event-I sit by his bed and chant Hare Krishna intensely but softly so as not to disturb him. An hour passes. The shivering subsides. The mound of blankets on Prabhupada’s bed rises and falls to a slower, more regular rhythm. Then he moves the blankets from his face and says, “Just make a paste, black pepper, little water,” and runs one hand over his forehead and temples. I softly tread upstairs and wake the girls, who rush to the kitchen while I return to Prabhupada’s side. A few minutes later, Malati hands me the paste through Prabhupada’s door. I whisper to her, “Tell them not to use kartals and mridanga at morning arati” I apply the black paste gently—it’s cold to the touch and must feel good on his fevered brow. The long night’s vigil begins.

When daylight touches his blue curtains at 5:30 a.m. I hear a snuffle and Prabhupada slowly rises, sits up in his bed, swings his legs to the floor and says, “I did not hear kartals. There is no morning arati?” The sparkle has returned to his eyes, and I’m so relieved I laugh. Then, looking at his watch, he says, “It is time for morning walk. Are you coming?” All as if nothing had happened. I’m overwhelmed with gratitude—and more convinced than ever by this mystic display of Prabhupada’s inconceivable transcendental energy.

But he just wouldn’t slow down. He had me run out to a tobacconist to buy him a small round tin of Hedges mentholated snuff. It must be Hedges, he says, no other brand—and for the next few days there were little brown snail trails running from his nostrils, relieving congestion. I made an effort to keep visitors away and held back any letters that required him to make big decisions until he felt better.

On August 14, Prabhupada wrote to Madhudvisa: “London climate is very much unsuitable for me. It is always damp, dark, and cold. So very soon either I will have to go back to N.Y. or towards India. Shyamasundar is contemplating that we go to Switzerland. What is the climatic condition in Bombay now?”

He wrote to Tamai Krishna on the same day: “I am getting older and the 76th anniversary of my birth observed by my disciples means that I am getting older. Naturally my health does not allow me to work very hard but still I am working as hard as possible to advance this mission and I request all my disciples to give their full cooperation in this great task.”

August 15 is Prabhupadas birthday; he’s 75 years old (in India they count time in the womb as the first year) and busier than ever. That morning he dictated twenty-four letters to disciples around the world, thanking them for remembering him on his appearance day: “I beg to thank you very much for your kind feelings of appreciation of my humble service unto you. You are all helping me in pushing forward this mission of Lord Caitanya Mahaprabhu, coming down by disciplic succession to my Guru Maharaja. So whatever you have spoken, it is simply due to them. I am simply the via media to receive them, on behalf of my Guru Maharaja, His Divine Grace Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Goswami Maharaja Prabhupada.”

Over the next two days, Prabhupada initiated many new disciples in the temple room, and in classes he spoke from Srimad-Bhagavatam, describing Krishna’s cosmic management.

Just like in our institution, International Society for Krishna Consciousness, although I am the head, still, I have got so many assistants, the GBC members. They are assisting me, departmental. Somebody is here, somebody is there. Similarly, God has departmental management, and because it is such a huge affair, there are 330,000,000 officers of Krishna. These 330,000,000 assistants are within one universe, and there are innumerable universes.

And for each and every universe, there must be similarly 330,000,000 or like that. They are called demigods, managers. Demigods means exactly under the position of God.

On August 18, Srila Prabhupada receives a letter from Apa Pant regarding his request that Indira Gandhi attend the groundbreaking ceremonies at Mayapur: “Her Secretary has written to say that the Prime Minister regrets very much that she will be unable to do so on account of her very heavy schedule of engagements.” (OK, that strikes India off the must-go-there-now list.)

Later that day, Prabhupada was interviewed for BBC Radio. He played a one-hour verbal tennis match in his room with two very sharp British journalists, who fired their questions and received startling answers:

Journalist: What is your view of predominant Western civilization, sir?

Prabhupada: This predominance is dwindling. Where is your British Empire gone? There was Roman Empire, there was Mogul Empire, there was Carthaginian Empire, there was Egyptian Empire, Greece, Napoleon, Hitler and so on. They come and go. So this kind of empirical—imperial—onslaught, they will come and stay for hundred or two hundred years, and create some problems. So they will come and go, create some disturbances and go. Nobody will stay.
