Journalist: Could you accept the idea of God in another manner than your church?

Prabhupada: If you refuse to accept Krishna as God, then you present your God. Give me His address, name, and occupation. Can you give me?

Journalist: No.

Prabhupada: Then you take from me. I can give. Why you are refusing?

After three weeks of frantic activity in London, things finally began to settle down. There were fewer outside engagements. Prabhupada held court in his Bury Place room; daily conversations on very high subjects kept him enlivened, and his health quickly recovered. He especially enjoyed the often heated philosophical discussions he was having with the Mensa guy, Pat Fitzgerald: British rationalism vs. the Vedas. After one of these sessions, Srila Prabhupada turned to me and said, “So you have studied philosophy in college?”

“Yes, Prabhupada. German philosophy was my major.”

“We have to be prepared to discuss philosophy with this intelligent class of men—their philosophy. So we shall discuss. You will come to me every morning at 8 a.m. You will be the Western philosopher and I will debate you. We shall publish as a book. Is that all right?” Then he quoted a verse from Sixth Canto of Srimad Bhagawatam: “Acting from within the cores of the hearts of all philosophers, who propagate various views, He \[Krishna\] causes them to forget their own souls while sometimes agreeing and sometimes disagreeing among themselves. Thus He creates within this material world a situation in which they are unable to come to a conclusion.”

I told him I would have to bone up a little, that I was a little rusty on particular philosophers, but he could see I was excited. Yes, I thought, this will be a wonderful intellectual exercise, one-on-one with my spiritual master. But am I sharp enough for this? I ran to a bookstore on Oxford Street and bought a Barnes & Noble Outline Series book called History of Philosophy, and started reading. A few days later I felt ready. We would start with Socrates, regarded as the great-grandfather of Western philosophy. I turned on the tape recorder:

Shyamasundar: Socrates says, “Know thyself,” gnothi seauton. He thought that we could arrive at the truth by meditating— approaching a subject from every mental angle using the maieutic method, or Socratic dialectic, until there was nothing left but the truth.

Prabhupada: He was a muni, a great thinker. However, the real truth comes to such a muni by that process after many, many births.... If we take the instructions of Krishna directly and surrender unto Him, we save time and many, many births.

Prabhupada respected that Socrates reached Brahman realization without a teacher, and said that this in itself was proof of reincarnation. He liked that Socrates was searching for absolutes, eternal principles, and agreed that good morals or ethics are an automatic result of right thinking.

Shyamasundar: Generally speaking, Socrates was more concerned with God as a moral reality than as a personal conception.

Prabhupada: Moral reality is necessarily personal. If a man is moral, we say that he is honest. If he follows no moral principles, we say that he is dishonest. Thus morality and immorality refer to a person. How can we deny personal morality?

Shyamasundar: Socrates defines “right” as that which is beneficial to others and “wrong” as that which does harm to others.

Prabhupada: That is a general definition, but we should know what is beneficial to others. Krishna consciousness is beneficial, and anything else is not beneficial.

**\* \* \***

Over the next few days, there were many discussions about where Prabhupada should go next. The options seemed to be India (but no definite programs were set up there yet), the US (but things were already running smoothly there), Switzerland (for health), and Africa. One Indian gentleman in London, Mr. Pandiya, had offered Prabhupada his nice house in Mombasa, Kenya, on the Indian Ocean, where Prabhupada could repose and translate.

Brahmananda Swami had been in Kenya for two months, so Prabhupada requested him to go to Mombasa and give his opinion of the place. Brahmananda phoned and said, “This is the most beautiful place in the world!” So on August 27, Prabhupada wrote to Gurudas in Delhi: “So far London temple is concerned, things are going on here very nicely under the supervision of Dayananda Prabhu who is appointed treasurer and Tribhuvanatha as president. I may go to Africa and then come back to India.”

August 29 was Radha’s birthday, Radhasthami, and Prabhupada spoke in the temple:

Alone, you cannot enjoy. When you are in the circles of friend or family or other associates, you feel pleasure, ananda. Just like I am speaking. The speaking is very pleasing when there are many persons here. I can speak here at night, dead of night, nobody here. That is not pleasure. Ananda means there must be others. So because Krishna, the Supreme Enjoyer, is anandamaya, embodiment of bliss, therefore eko bahu syam, He has become many. We are also Krishna’s part and parcel, to give pleasure to Krishna. And the chief pleasure potency is Radharani.

While Prabhupada spoke, the girls in the small kitchen downstairs were preparing a huge cauldron of Radharani’s favorite dish, plum pudding. Little Saraswati came running down the kitchen stairs, tripped—and landed right in the middle of the simmering chutney!

Malati: And she went headfirst into the pot. She got burned pretty badly, and we had to take her to the hospital. The devotees go, “Oh, now we can’t offer the chutney!” When Prabhupada found out about it, he sent for me, very alarmed, and said, “I heard Saraswati was injured. Is she all right?” She got a little bum, but she was all right—and Prabhupada said it was OK to offer the chutney.

Discussing Western philosophers became an important part of each morning at Bury Place. After Socrates we moved on to his disciple Plato, who lived from 428-348 BC.

Shyamasundar: The senses perceive the changing phenomenal world, but according to Plato, the noumenal world is perceived by the mind. It is this noumenal world that is absolute, eidos, or ideal, permanent and universal. Would you say that ultimate reality is ideal in this Platonic sense?

Prabhupada: Not ideal—factual. Param satyam dhimahi: “We offer our obeisances unto the Absolute Truth.” This relative world is a perverted reflection of the absolute world. It is just like a shadow. A tree reflected in the water may appear to be exactly like the tree itself, but it is a perverted reflection. The actual tree is there.

Prabhupada agreed with Plato’s concept of ideal love, that Platonic love is love between souls, and that perfection within the world of the senses can never be achieved. He admired Plato’s division of society into guardians (philosopher-kings), who rule by their intelligence, warriors, who protect with their courage, and artisans, who satisfy society’s appetites.

Shyamasundar: Plato’s word for God is demiurge, which in Greek means “master builder” or “architect.”

Prabhupada: In Sanskrit this is called shristi-karta, but this conception is secondary. Lord Brahma is shristi-karta, and Brahma is inspired by Krishna. The original master, Krishna, is not shristi-karta because He does not do anything directly.

Plato reasoned that the soul must be eternal and that it must have existed previously in an ideal world where it learned about eternal principles. Plato perceives man’s soul as in a marginal, intermediary position between two worlds; it belongs to the ideal world but now has a material body. Therefore we must mold our lives in such a way' as to regain our original perfection.

Prabhupada agreed with most of this and appreciated Plato’s reasoning, but again, he stressed that without direct knowledge of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, all such speculations were basically useless.

Bhavananda arrived in London from Los Angeles and added his considerable artistic talents to the Mayapur design team, and on September 5, Prabhupada took his Mayapur planners to Westminster Abbey to view the architecture and get ideas for his project. Westminster Abbey stands in regal splendor adjacent to the Houses of Parliament and Big Ben, and it has been the wedding, coronation, and burial site for British monarchs since 1066. Great architects and craftsmen have expanded the cathedral’s size and decoration over the past one thousand years, and at more than 530’ in length and 225’ high, Westminster Abbey is the iconic symbol of Britain’s Christian spiritual heritage.

We walk around the great church, noting how clever buttresses allow such dramatic interior height. We tread over the graves of kings and poets buried in the Abbey floor. “Just see,” the Swami says, “I am walking on Darwin’s body. Now he has become an ape.”

We’re impressed by how much Prabhupada knows of British history as he explains the fate of Oliver Cromwell and reels off facts about this king and that. We pass down the long nave leading to the altar through the middle of a Christian ceremony: a hundred voices lift from the choir on both sides and echo off the hundred-foot arched Gothic dome above, invoking a feeling of heavenly longing despite the hushed roar and foot traffic from dozens of tourists. In this great cathedral I’m seeing Srila Prabhupada in the light of history as he marches along the transept tapping his bamboo cane on the flagstone floors of time.

When we returned from Westminster Abbey, a wonderful surprise greeted Prabhupada at Bury Place: Dr. Weir, head of the London Mensa Society, was there with Pat Fitzgerald; they’d come to talk high philosophy with His Divine Grace. What followed was the ultimate classic debate between Western deductive empiricism and Eastern inductive ontology, the ascending process vs. the descending process, rational vs. sublime. I was amazed to see how easily Prabhupada answered the statements and questions put forth by these two highly learned men, how he took control, kept them off balance, and gently made his own hard-hitting points. (Perhaps our recent philosophy book sessions had helped

Prabhupada prepare for discussions like this—or, better put, he was always prepared; he was just preparing ms.)

The Mensa men used terms like “postulates,” “categories,” “dichotomies,” “axioms,” “tautological,” “infinite regression,” and Prabhupada stuck to Krishna’s words in the Bhagavad-gita to clarify and defeat their conclusions. They used Latin terms like causa causum. Prabhupada came back with summum bonum. They quoted Thomas Carlyle, and Prabhupada rebuffed with William Cowper. They referred to Christ, Socrates, Theocritus, Blake, Freud, Jung, Buddha, and Perry Mason, and Prabhupada caught every allusion (well, maybe not Perry Mason, but he knew he was a TV lawyer). He dazzled these hardened sophists not only with his vast knowledge but the quick aptness of his replies:

Prabhupada: Now, so long I accept this material body these problems are there: birth, death, old age, and disease. Then if I don’t accept this material body, then these problems are solved immediately.

Dr. Weir: This is what they call “solution by denial.” Prabhupada: It is not called solution. It is a fact.

Prabhupada: Buddha philosophy is more or less based on this voidism: make everything null and void. No more activities. No more love. We say, “Just like you cannot see properly because your eyes are diseased, so cure the disease and then you see properly.” And the other says, “All right, pluck them out. The disease is in the eyes. Pluck them out.” That is not very good proposition. We say, “You take treatment to make the eyes to see properly. Remedy the problem.” Sarvopadhi vinirmuktam tat-paratvena...

Dr. Weir: I must say that’s very interesting.

Prabhupada: Then what makes the difference between animals and man?

Dr. Weir: Because of this feeling of wanting to know.

Prabhupada: Yes, that is scientific spirituality. That is advancement more than the animals. Otherwise, if your science simply remains on the platform of eating, sleeping, mating, and defending, you remain animal. But when that scientific research goes to the spiritual thing, that is the special prerogative of the human.

Prabhupada: And one who has not developed God consciousness, he cannot have any good qualities because his business is mental speculation. Mano-rathenasati dhavato. By simply mental speculation, he’ll be trapped up in this material world.

Dr. Weir: Mental speculation alone is sterile.

Prabhupada: Mental speculation, mano-rathena. Mano is “mind,” ratha means “chariot.” One who is driving on the chariot of mind. The chariot of mind will take him away.

Dr. Weir: It would be very interesting if you would care to take our IQ test.

Prabhupada: Our activities are above intelligence. Spiritual. Our process of test is how far he is advanced in God consciousness. That is our test. Harav abhaktasya kuto mahad-gunah.

After an hour-long battle between two mental giants and the perfect sage, Srila Prabhupada went down to the temple to lecture to his devotees from Srimad-Bhagavatam for another hour.

**\* \* \***

Bhavananda and Naranarayan wanted to leave for India immediately to put in motion the Mayapur plan they had been designing. Prabhupada asked them to go via Nairobi in order to check on conditions there and advise him whether or not he should go. They left the next day, and a few days later they phoned from Nairobi to report that Brahmananda had indeed established a solid footing in Kenya, that the Mombasa house was very nice, and the climate was perfect.

In his room that night, Prabhupada and I went over his travel options, and he asked, “Where do you think I should go?”

I knew he would ask me this at some point, so I’d been thinking: which place would best support Srila Prabhupada’s childlike glee and love of adventure? There was really no other choice. “Africa sounds pretty nice, Srila Prabhupada.”

He said, “Yes, I was thinking the same thing. Make arrangements with Brahmananda. We shall go to Africa.”

So on the night of September 9, 1971, we leave the misty isles of Albion and fly into the heart of Africa. On the plane I reflect on everything that’s happened these past three months. What does being Prabhupada’s secretary—more now, his right-hand man—what does all this mean? It’s more than just typing or taking dictation, the passive role accepted by Prabhupada’s previous secretaries. I’m too active for that. I see myself more as a great warrior’s aide-de-camp, a lieutenant helping spark Srila Prabhupada’s wishes into action. I’m responsible not just for coordinating events as they happen around him, but also for making things happen. My job is to excite Srila Prabhupada, to look ahead and hasten the future. He has begun to ask for my opinions—on management, events, his schedule. I feel more like a collaborator, someone to bounce his ideas off, a sounding board. This is a new direction for the post called the Swami’s secretary. Am I overstepping my bounds? I don’t know. Sometimes I push the envelope, but Prabhupada seems to appreciate dancing on the edge—in fact, he’s the perfect living example of the go-for-it attitude. No matter what else happens, Prabhupada has become so dear to me I can scarcely believe my good fortune.

**\* \* \***

Brahmananda is a big guy, tall, weighing in at maybe 250, and New-York-City tough. I once saw him pick up a drunk who had passed out on the temple room floor, throw him over his shoulder, and lay him gently on the sidewalk on Second Avenue. One of Prabhupada’s first disciples, Brahmanada negotiated Prabhupada’s Bhagavad-gita As It Is publishing deal with Macmillan, handled most of the early East Coast temple business, and was in every way Prabhupada’s strong, front-line sergeant. In late 1970, Brahmananda hitchhiked across Turkey, Iran, and Afghanistan, chanting Hare Krishna in the saffron robes of a Vaishnava sannyasi. He sweated it out in a Turkish jail. People spit at him and threw stones as he preached openly across West Pakistan while war with India loomed. When Big В reappeared in India in June, Prabhupada was greatly relieved and dispatched him to Africa with two other devotees, Jagannivas and Chayavana, to carry on his daring missionary work. If anyone could tame the Dark Continent and plant the seed of Krishna consciousness there, it was Brahmananda.

On the morning of September 10, as we descended into Nairobi International Airport—“Look, Aravinda, out there! Giraffes on the runway!”—the big Boeing lurched and the captain announced, “Ladies and gentlemen, we’ll be swinging around for reapproach. There seems to be wildlife on the landing strip. Please keep your seatbelts fastened.”

London-Nairobi was the fourth leg on Prabhupadas transcendental tour and the first time I would see him preach in the wilderness. Africa seemed a land so raw and strange—and yet innocent and beckoning. I was excited and curious to see how Prabhupada would tackle this unique challenge.

Brahmananda met us at the airport and took us to his Indian friend’s home to rest. She was a middle-aged widow named Shakti Mati who, with her four sons, owned a business called Sai Nursery on the outskirts of Nairobi, not far from the airport. Sai Nursery raised exotic tropical flowers, trees, and bushes for landscaping. The next morning Srila Prabhupada took his walk around the nursery and commented that it was “like Vrindavan.”

Later that day we hopped a one-hour flight southeast to Mombasa, on Kenya’s east coast. The art deco Pandiya family home was large, with glass-brick rounded edges, and the front and sides of the house covered with fluorescent magenta bougainvillea. A big veranda looked out on the turquoise Indian Ocean, wide, stark-white beaches, and palm and frangipani trees. The house was named Swarga Loka

(“Heavenly Planet”), and was painted a saffron color because some other swami used to stay there.

We were just four degrees south of the equator, so sunlight was direct and intense, but a steady sea breeze and open doors and windows kept the temperature perfect. Srila Prabhupada walked around the place, obviously pleased. “Our Brahmananda has said this is the most beautiful place in the world, and I think it is so.”

Except for his two servants—and a few members of the Pandiya family, who didn’t intrude much—Prabhupada was alone in a peaceful place for the first time in at least a year. There were no outside engagements and no one coming to see him. He walked on Nyali Beach each morning, worked on his current book, wrote a few letters, ate the Pandiya’s fine Gujarati cooking, relaxed, and healed. The cough he’d carried with him from London quickly disappeared.

Srila Prabhupada was translating Srimad-Bhagavatam all day and most of the night now. “Translating,” of course, meant much more than just converting the four- or eight-line Sanskrit shlokas into meaningful English. He was creating his significant purports to go along with each verse. These purports ranged from a few sentences to several pages each. I had the chance during this longer pause in our journey to observe Prabhupada at work in the quiet of his room as he spoke directly into his dictation machine. And this is what amazed me: he reeled off translation and purport so effortlessly, rarely stopping to look up a word in his Sanskrit dictionary or consult one of the Vaishnava commentaries he’d brought with him. He spoke with fluid grace, never faltering, like giving a lecture in slow motion, each word enunciated, then saying “comma,” “full stop,” or “new paragraph.” Thus the many pages he produced per day would find their way into print a few months later with very little editing. Each purport was perfect, clear, and to the point. Very few questions came back from his editors in New York and Boston, not because the mails were slow but because what Prabhupada spoke was so flawless. The words flowed through Prabhupada like they must have flowed through Vyasa, the Goswamis, and all great Vedic transcendentalists before him. He was tuned to Krishna’s wavelength. In one of his purports Srila Prabhupada said, “The secret in a devotee’s writing is that when he writes about the pastimes of the Lord, the Lord helps him; he does not write himself. As stated in Bhagavad-gita, dadami buddhi-yogam tam yena mam upayanti te. Since a devotee writes in service to the Lord, the Lord from within gives him so much intelligence that he sits down near the Lord and goes on writing books.”

In the past three years, Prabhupada had published eight books of absolute knowledge, making clear the deepest of all subject matter: the complete history and explanation of everything. No ordinary human being could do this. And yet “translating” was just a part-time job for Srila Prabhupada.

There was time now, too, to dive into our Western philosophy discussions for the book Prabhupada was planning to call Thus They Talked. In London we’d covered the Greek philosophers Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle. In Mombasa we plunged into the early Theists, philosophers who had lived and taught in the five centuries following Christ’s birth.

We tackled Plotinus (205-270 AD) first. Plotinus called God the Nous, the Soul, or the One, but said that we limit God by attributing qualities to Him. “Nonsense,” said Srila Prabhupada, but he did like one of Plotinus’s statements:

If the \[individual\] souls remain in the intelligible realm with the Soul, they are beyond harm, and share in the Soul’s governance. They are like kings who live with the high King. But there comes a point at which they come down from this state, to one of individuality. They wish to become independent. When a soul remains for long in this withdrawal in estrangement from the whole, it becomes a thing fragmented, isolated, and weak.

I soon realized that these hours-long philosophy discussions were devouring tape pretty fast. We had a limited number of tape reels, and we couldn’t buy new ones in Africa. I had to start transcribing the philosophy sessions so I could reuse the tapes. Aravinda was busy typing Prabhupada’s Bhagavatam translations and letters, so between us, the little Smith Corona electric typewriter was in constant use.

**\* \* \***

Kenya was a former British colony, and over the past century the Brits had brought thousands of Indian families, mostly Gujaratis, to Kenya to harvest sugarcane and build railroads. Gradually the Indians had monopolized commerce in Kenyan society, and native Africans harbored a long-standing grudge against these immigrant shopkeepers and moneylenders. Indian society was isolated, living in separate communities. The British had been violently thrown out of Kenya only seven years before Prabhupada’s visit, and there was a growing fear among Indian Kenyans that they might be next. As a result, many Indian families were moving to London. Indian leaders in Kenya were struggling to bridge the growing cultural gap for the families who remained.

After a few long, sedate days at Swarga Loka, translating and talking philosophy, Prabhupada began to get antsy—he wanted some action. Brahmananda appealed to local Indian leaders to find public venues where Prabhupada could speak. They arranged a lecture at a run-down Radha-Krishna temple in Mombasa for the evening of October 13. The temple was located in the Kenya Railways Housing Estate, now a densely-packed African neighborhood. Not many people attended—mostly local Indians, a handful of Africans—but as always, Prabhupada spoke as if to thousands:

So what is the opportunity of understanding the self? The whole night is engaged either in sleeping or in sex life, and the whole day every day you have to get money in order to purchase things. That’s all. But after all, this human form of life is so important, I have to know myself, but I do not get time. If this meeting would have been a political leader’s meeting giving all kinds of false hope, millions of people would have come. But because it is a meeting for understanding atma-tattva, self-realization, nobody is here. So our Krishna consciousness movement is being pushed in unfavorable circumstance. Nobody is interested unless he is very, very intelligent man. We cannot get immediately members of the society like the political parties. We have to canvass from door to door, “My dear sir, why you are forgetting yourself? Kindly try to understand. Here is a book, Bhagavad-gita. Try to understand.”

Afterward in his room, Prabhupada told us what he had observed: “You must go out and preach to the Africans! Indian people already know everything; you cannot change them. But they will help you with money and contacts. You present Krishna consciousness to the Africans. That is your purpose.” He recommended that Brahmananda fly some African-American devotees over from the US to help him.

On September 15, Srila Prabhupada wrote to Madhudvisa:

Now I am preparing one book answering all types of philosophical arguments. Shyamasundar every morning is putting questions from different angles of vision beginning from Socrates, Plato, Aristotle, etc. and we are trying to answer. It will require some time but I wish that all our preachers should be well versed with all philosophical ideas and after studying all philosophical points of view we will put Krishna Conscious philosophy on the top.

And so we continued with the early Theists. We discussed Origen (185-254 AD), the first major Western thinker to ascribe a personality to God: “Ultimate reality may be defined as the relationships of persons with the infinite Person Himself.” Unlike previous Western philosophers, Origen prescribes a clear method for achieving reunification with God: through Christ. If individual souls do not achieve reunion with God in one lifetime, they are reincarnated again and again.

So far, Prabhupada has agreed with much of what the Greeks , and early Theists have said, correcting them from the Vedic standpoint and filling in gaps in their knowledge. He somewhat respects that they came to their conclusions through self-analysis, that they did not have the advantage of a parampara system dating back to Krishna Himself like our own. But all notions of agreement and respect go out the window when we tackle our next philosopher.

Augustine (354-430 AD) gave philosophical shape to the early Christian Church and was later canonized. He broke with previous thinkers by proposing that the soul is born when the body is born and then becomes immortal.

Prabhupada: If the soul is created, how is it immortal? How can the soul sometimes not be eternal?

Shyamasundar: Augustine claims that after this one lifetime, you go either to heaven or to hell, eternal salvation or eternal damnation.

Prabhupada: No. Spiritual consciousness can be revived at any moment.

Shyamasundar: Augustine says, “God is not the soul of all things but the maker of all souls.”

Prabhupada: Then how is God all-pervading? Certainly God has the potency of omnipresence. This cannot be denied.

Shyamasundar: According to Augustine, mind, reason, and the soul are one and the same.

Prabhupada: No. The mind and intelligence differ according to body, but the soul remains the same.

Shyamasundar: Augustine says, “Let these Platonists stop threatening us with reincarnation as punishment for our souls. Reincarnation is ridiculous.”

For the first time since we’d begun having these philosophy discussions, Prabhupada became furious. “No. Reincarnation is scientific fact!” he shouted, quoting Vedic verses to confirm it. The kicker came when Augustine said that only human bodies have a soul.

Shyamasundar: Augustine says, "We do not apply ‘Thou shalt not kill’ to plants nor irrational animals that fly, swim, walk, or creep. By the Creator’s wise ordinance, they are meant for our use, dead or alive.”

Prabhupada: These men, there is no clear philosophy in Bible, so they make up anything, using big words, and every rascal follows! The Bible says, “Thou shalt not kill” without qualification.

I’m beginning to see through Prabhupada’s eyes how vast civilizations rise and fall on the philosophy of one man, and how important it is, once having found the true philosophy, to propagate that truth in a rational manner to all humankind.

**\* \* \***

Prabhupada asked the Mombasa Hindu community to donate the Radha-Krishna temple he’d visited. He promised them we would repair, occupy, and revive it. But after ten days of negotiations they said no, they could not give. Prabhupada was disgusted. “They cannot do themselves, but they will not give.”

By October 20 Prabhupada had had enough of the quiet life and we returned to Nairobi so he could jump into action. We' stayed with various Indian Life Members, living for a week at a time in the homes of Mr. Krishen Behal, G. N. Shah, Harjibhai Patel, and Mr. Damji Devji. In this way, Prabhupada set the example for Brahmananda on how a sannyasi should move around and not stay in one place too long, strengthening him for the rigors of preaching in Africa. Wherever we stayed, Prabhupada liked to walk in the mornings at the Nairobi Arboretum, near the city center.

Nairobi, nicknamed “Green City in the Sun,” lays practically on the equator, and the sun’s direct ultraviolet rays are transformed by nearly six thousand feet of altitude into the perfect climate for lush plant growth. Nairobi Arboretum was created by the British in 1903 as a place to research tree species that might be friendly to the climate, and the park is home to 350 species of trees, 100 species of birds, and troops of graceful ver-vet monkeys. In the early mornings—equatorial mist rising from damp green meadows into the flowering crowns of exotic trees, fantasy-colored birds singing, squawking, and booming-we strolled around the Arboretum with Srila Prabhupada. It was like watching the dawn of time in primordial paradise. When the mist burned off you could see snow-covered Mt. Kenya to the north and Kilimanjaro to the south. At nearly twenty thousand feet, these are Africa’s highest peaks.

Brahmananda’s crew had been making Life Members for a few months, focusing on the Hindu community. The devotees had recently been allowed to use the Kutchi Gujarati Hindu Union, a dilapidated building on Duke Street, downtown, in a mostly African neighborhood. Statues of demigods, Sita-Rama, and Laxmi-Narayan were mounted along the walls in the temple’s dark interior.

The devotees had been holding quiet kirtans in the temple for a few weeks now and had attracted a couple of African boys, but not much was happening. One day, Prabhupada instructed Brahmananda to open the doors and windows, go out on the street in front of the building, and have a boisterous kirtan. Soon the hall filled with curious Africans, and Prabhupada lectured to the crowd while someone translated his words into Swahili. From that day on, Krishna consciousness truly began to take root in Nairobi.

Then Prabhupada really put us on the spot: “It is not sufficient, some few people coming to the temple. You must go out, have sankirtan in the public places.” But as we jaywalked across Ngong Forest Road and threaded our way through the Kibera slum toward Kamukunji Park to conduct the first public sankirtan in Africa, even stalwart Brahmananda looked scared. Me—I was shaking like a leaf! Just yesterday I had seen a guy in a breechcloth walking down the street with a zebra hide over his shoulder, fresh blood running down his back, This was a savage country, only recently seized back from the British by the murderous Mau Mau. In the 1960s, Jomo Kenyatta had launched his successful campaign to oust the Brits from this same Kamukunji Park, and here we were, heading for the very Freedom Tree under which he had rallied his people to revolt. Would this work?

There were seven of us: Brahmananda, Bhavananda, Naranarayan, Jagannivas, Chayavana, Aravinda, and me—seven white men acting out under the very symbol of the black man’s freedom from white rule. Oh, boy.

We planted our sign with a painted maha-mantra under the famous huge, spreading acacia tree. The air was painfully bright, midday ultraviolet searing our shaved skulls as Big B tentatively whomped the mridanga. I slammed some kartals together, and our troop lost itself in a blissful half-hour kirtan, twirling all over the place, African-style. When we opened our eyes, there were maybe a hundred people gathered around us, some of them dancing. Most had big smiles, lots of teeth showing. We were ecstatic. Brahmananda raised his danda in the air, like, “See, I’ve got a spear too!” and gave a short lecture in English with a little Swahili (Jambo! Jambo!) thrown in, and then asked for questions. One woman yelled out, “Do you have the book?”

“Yes!” B shouted back, and he whipped out a Bhagavad-gita and thrust it into the air. “We got the book'.” The crowd surged forward, surrounded Brahamananda, and passed the Bhagavadgita around from hand to hand. This is what they were waiting to see—if we had the gospel.

With great relief we returned to the temple and informed Srila Prabhupada that, yes, the public loved the sankirtan and many had danced and wanted to know more. Prabhupada gave us a big smile. “Yes, they are innocent people. They will enjoy our singing and dancing and eating prasadam. Now you must give them books. Why there are no books? Get more books here immediately.”

Brahmananda: Yeah, the Christian missionaries started all that. They were very effective in bringing Christianity. Their first program was to translate the Bible into the local language. And many times that was the first thing written in these people’s languages. The Christians did that all over Africa.

On October 5 Prabhupada wrote to Tamai Krishna in Calcutta: “The books are very urgently required here because we have made about 35 members and none of them have been supplied with any books. Every day they are asking for books and this is not very good for us.”

That night, Prabhupada appeared on Voice of Kenyas current affairs TV talk show, “Mambo Leo.” The host was a little antagonistic, and when Prabhupada held up a picture of Lord Chaitanya, the guy said, “He cannot be God! This is a human being.”

“Why God cannot come as human being?” Prabhupada shot back.

October 6 was a very big night. Two African-American devotees, Bhutabhavana and Hari Kripa, had arrived from New York, and Vishal had arrived from London. When we stepped onstage that evening at the University of Nairobi’s Taifla Hall—ten guys, both black and white, in bright robes, singing the holy names and dancing, arms upraised, with their elderly master from India, who was draped in garlands—the crowd of two thousand students went wild. You could hear a pin drop during Prabhupadas powerful lecture.

We therefore invite the most intelligent men in the world to understand this Krishna conscious philosophy and try to distribute it all over the world. We have now come to these African countries, and I invite all intelligent Africans to come and understand this philosophy and distribute it. You are trying to develop yourselves, so please develop spiritually, for spiritual development is sound development. Don’t imitate the Americans and Europeans, who are living like cats and dogs. Such civilizations built on the consciousness of sense gratification cannot stand. The atomic bomb is already there, and as soon as the next war breaks out, all their skyscrapers and everything else will be finished. Try to understand this from the real viewpoint of human life, the spiritual viewpoint. This is what this Krishna consciousness movement is about. We therefore request you to try to understand this philosophy. Thank you very much.

After Prabhupada’s lecture, Chayavana showed a movie of Hare Krishna people chanting around the globe while Brahmananda narrated, New York style, his voice booming.

The next day, the Daily Nation reported:

ISKCON SWAMI EXPLAINS PHILOSOPHY

Impeccable showmanship Was matched with unimpeachable philosophy of Gita at a jampacked Taifla Hall in the University of Nairobi yesterday evening when about 2000 people and undergraduates listened to Acharya Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada and his disciples, who presented the “Hare Krishna Revolution.”

Everyone was fired up by this University of Nairobi event, so I suggested we plan a weeklong event like we’d held at Cross Maidan in Bombay. Brahmananda was dismayed by the large scope of such a project—everything was happening so quickly since Prabhupada had come less than a month earlier—but I was gradually talking him into it.

To show Brahmananda how you do it, I proposed to Prabhupada that we have a one-night party at Mr. Krishen Behal’s nice home in the European quarter and invite all the biggest people in Nairobi. Prabhupada agreed. I opened the phone book to the section called

“Government of Kenya,” and wrote down about a hundred names and addresses—Minister of this, Minister of that. On the printed invitation, we presented a list of invitees just as we had done in London in January 1969 with our “Guess Who’s Coming to Dinner?” event. On our morning walks with Prabhupada through , the European quarter, where most of the government ministers lived, we dropped the invitations into their mailboxes.

Brahmananda: What happened was that one of the names Shyamasundar said would be there was a minister who had subsequently become like an enemy of the state. He was a traitor. I mean, it was a big scandal. I think they hanged him or something But that had happened after the phone book was printed. We didn’t know that, so we had his name on the invitation—that this man is coming—and everyone knew he was dead, so they kinda knew what we’d written was bogus.

Nevertheless, a number of Kenyan government ministers showed up. We laughed a lot and they schmoozed with Prabhupada and appreciated what he was doing. We used the themes of brotherhood and unity in our approach to these African leaders. I told Brahmananda, “If you really want Prabhupada to stay here, you gotta have a big program.” The idea for a weeklong festival was planted and connections made that night would pay off in a big way later.

By the second week of October, outside programs had tapered off and the Nairobi devotees were now going out every day on street sankirtan. The devotees in India were preparing programs for Prabhupada to attend, but there was no rush for us to get there, so Prabhupada returned to translating the Bhagavatam and to our daily philosophy book sessions and the small, intimate room conversations he liked so much. Brahmananda and I looked around Nairobi for intelligent people, both African and Indian—doctors, real estate agents, anyone—and brought them to Prabhupada’s room. Night after night he answered their questions about God, the soul, and anything else, in long, tape- recorded sessions. Just watching and listening to him, it became clear to me that Prabhupada was a shaktyavesa avatar, endowed by Krishna with special powers to transform the lives of everyone who met him.

From Nairobi, and for the next year and a half, I began to write newsletters, often once a week, mailing them to Karandhar in L.A. In them I would describe for devotees around the world what Prabhupada was doing at that particular time. Karandhar included these reports in his regular BBT newsletters and sent them out to all ISKCON temples.

The pace has been lightning fast, and His Divine Grace is opening up yet another vast theater of operations. The African people are thronging with curiosity and serious questions ... Prabhupada, after finishing one late-night preaching marathon, asked for food and remarked, “You see, I am hungry. Keep me talking—that is my life. Don’t let me stop talking.”

And I continued my letters to George Harrison, too, like this one, written late one stormy night from a wealthy Life Member’s huge home in the suburbs:

Onset of seasonal rains are announced tonight, heavy clouds rolling over us spitting lightning, so I’ll hasten while the mood is on—pattering drops interrupt cricket static in the garden outside, flame-trees whipping with wind, guavas plopping, thunder rumbles, far-off jackals cackle at a joke—the black velvet African night descends on this great, still house—I can almost imagine brushing shoulders with a bandoliered Bogart as he races down the hall to lions in the kraal, or shouting across to Lauren Bacall if she’d like another pink gin as breeze shifts the organdy drapes and slams a door somewhere in this vast dark interior—wherein sits Srila Prabhupada at a desk in a tiny pool of light speaking words of wisdom, let it be, oh let it be ...

**\* \* \***

By now we were discussing the Scholastics, thirteenth-century medieval Christian philosophers, and particularly Thomas Aquinas (1225-1274). St. Thomas Aquinas’s main work, Summa Theologica, was a compendium of the theological teachings of the Catholic Church.

Shyamasundar: Aquinas says, “Before God created us creatures, He must have been by Himself, alone.”

Prabhupada: That is a material idea. It is the material world that is created, not the spiritual world. God is always in the spiritual world with countless servants, living entities, everlastingly.

Shyamasundar: Aquinas says, “The most proper name for God should be He who is.”

Prabhupada: Why? If God is active and has created the entire universe, what is wrong with addressing Him according to His activities and attributes? One of His attributes is attraction. God attracts everything. The word Krishna means “all attractive.” What then is wrong with addressing God as Krishna?

Shyamasundar: Aquinas fosters the idea that God cannot be approached directly but only through His son, Jesus.

Prabhupada: That’s all right, but why not see God now, talk with Him? They have no information. Poor fund of knowledge.

Shyamasundar: Aquinas says, “Divine scripture contains many meanings in one text, so that it may be appropriate for each man to marvel at the fact that he can find the truth that he has conceived in his own mind expressed in divine scripture.”

Prabhupada goes ballistic on this one: “This is why so much division is there in Christianity, interpreting. If one is perfect, why should he try to change the words of God? And if one is imperfect, what is the value of his change? If the words can be changed, they are not perfect. As soon as you interpret, all authority is gone. Simply hodgepodge.”

From the medieval Scholastics we moved on to Renaissance thinkers, the empiricists, scientists, guys like Francis Bacon, Thomas Hobbes, and Sir Isaac Newton. We were spending an hour a day, one or two sessions per philosopher, and we were fast running out of blank tape reels again. I was transcribing as fast as I could manage by hunt-and-peck on the small electric typewriter.

After the Renaissance thinkers we started on Rationalist philosophers like Rene Descartes, Baruch Spinoza, John Locke, George Berkeley, and David Hume. By mid-October, we finally got to my favorites, the German Idealists. I had majored in German Philosophy at Reed College, thinking maybe these guys had a handle on absolute truth—at least their didactic language and complex thought processes had convinced a lot of people for two hundred years that they knew what they were talking about, even if few could actually understand them. After a year of trying to get my head around Sein und Zeit, I wasn’t convinced that these men knew that much after all, and I decided to become a poet instead of a philosopher. A few years later, Swami Bhaktivedanta came into my life and answered every question I had about being and time—immediately, simply, and to my full satisfaction. Now here we are in the heart of Africa debating Kant, Hegel, and Schopenhauer. And Prabhupada so easily dissects their obtuse ideas, much to my delight.

Since there weren’t many outside programs, we spent more and more time on the philosophy book, sailing into the British Utilitarians Jeremy Bentham and John Stuart Mill, then on to the Evolutionists. Charles Darwin was by far Prabhupada’s favorite fall guy, and he spent three sessions defeating and dismissing Darwin with faultless logic and humor. It was all I could do to play the straight-laced British naturalist because Prabhupada had me rolling on the floor with laughter time and again.

“That missing link...” he said. “We can give him the missing link.”

Finally, word came from Jayapataka in India that a weeklong pan-dal program had been scheduled in Calcutta starting on October 28. This would be followed by a ten-day pandal in New Delhi. So I went down to the East African Airways office at Saddler House on Koinange Street and, using our prepaid round-the-world tickets, booked three seats from Nairobi to Bombay, then Bombay to Calcutta. Bhavananda and Naranarayan would travel with us, so I presented their tickets and booked two more seats.

On October 10, Prabhupada wrote to Tamai Krishna in Calcutta: “I am starting for Bombay on the 19th October at 10:00 p.m. reaching Bombay at about 8:30 a.m. on 20th morning, flight #E.C. 820.1 shall stay there until the 23rd, four days, and then I shall start for Calcutta, starting J. A.L. at 6 am in the morning and reaching Calcutta at 8:30 am on the 24th October, 1971.”

Little did we know.

Leaving Kenya on the night of October 19, the plane stopped in Kampala, Uganda, a nation now ruled by the harsh dictator Idi Amin. Civil war had been raging in Uganda for nine years, and Entebbe Airport reflected the country’s state of abject poverty. There was a shortage of jet fuel, it was late at night, and we had to wait several hours until the EAA flight to Bombay could be refueled. We had no food, so I ran all over the empty airport and paid a large amount of dollars for a loaf of white bread and some lemon soda. Srila Prabhupada ate it with relish as dawn rose over Africa.

**\* \* \***

We are exhausted, but I can’t sleep on the plane to Bombay, thinking about the last six weeks in Africa, how cool it is to see Prabhupada in action in a totally unknown environment. He is the pragmatic master of sudden chance, always finding fine balance between risk and impossibility. He knows what to do in every situation. I’m spellbound by his physical beauty, whether he’s just awakened with heavy eyelids and a little gray stubble on his chin or dressed to the nines for TV. I’m in love with his words as he takes on a BBC moderator or Thomas Aquinas. Prabhupada’s actions are perfect. He is at once the charming Diplomat to the Court of the Black King; the mighty Corporate Manager in hornrimmed glasses firing memos to his fifty branches; he is the Great Sage mounting the dais to bring us wisdom. Prabhupada is at once a simple man (“Just bring one glass of water”) and a Game Master thinking four moves ahead. He is inscrutable, complex, and also the modest maiden dressing for the ball: “Shall I wear cotton tonight, Aravinda, or silk?”

It takes my steady, full awareness every moment of every day to keep up with Prabhupada. My secretarial duties alone are nearly overwhelming: typing letters, manuscripts, and tapes, sending telegrams, making phone calls, making sure we have fresh tape reels and cassettes, batteries, typewriter ribbons, paper, and carbon paper for copies, or fixing a broken dictation machine or tape recorder, dealing with ever-changing voltage in foreign lands. Then there is the philosophy book. And making sure Prabhupada is steadily engaged in high discussions, that his outside programs are well-prepared, and monitoring the flow of traffic in and out of his rooms. On top of this, there are travel arrangements: correspond with devotees at the next destination, prepare itineraries, arrange tickets and documents. I’m straining every ounce of my energy and will, trying to keep up with the World’s Smartest Man. Even now on the plane, I’m thinking, What have I forgotten?

Yet despite the strain, every day with Prabhupada is like a jolly camping trip, a romp in the park. We laugh constantly. I live for that tinkle of his little bell through the door. He trusts me, depends on me, in a way. My wife and child are being cared for and waiting for me in India. I am content. We now had a major African rhino in the bag. But am I getting complacent?

I was about to be sorely tested.

## Four

The first quality of a pure devotee, as described in shastra, is that he is “kind to everyone,” or merciful. That  Prabhupada consented to be with us in this material world was his greatest act of mercy. From time to time, though, Prabhupada’s mercy had a rather sharp edge to it, like when he criticized politicians or a bad philosopher. Even with his disciples Prabhupada sometimes addressed stubborn or ill-thought behavior with a rebuke or harsh words: “Why you have nor sent the books?” or “Now you have become guru?” I cringed whenever he got angry with another devotee, dreading the day his wrath might fall on me. Still, I came to see these short-lived outbursts as merciful acts that fit the time and circumstance and in all cases were deserved by the devotees at whom they were aimed. What’s more, such persons were tough enough to accept Prabhupada’s reprimands and remedy themselves. Some of these guys were Vietnam vets with tough skins, and they needed strong treatment. I wasn’t built that way. Had Prabhupada said an angry word to me, I would have wilted and walked away, my life ended right then and there.

After a long night in the sky, East African Airways flight 820 from Nairobi descended into Bombay. When we stepped onto the tarmac at Santa Cruz airport on the early morning of October 20, 1971, we could hear the kartals, drums, and loud kirtan from a hundred Bombay devotees somewhere inside the terminal. Relaxed and smiling, our little party—Prabhupada, Aravinda, Bhavananda, Naranarayan, and me—headed for customs; it had been four months since we’d left Bombay for Moscow, and we were happy to be back on the soil of Mother India.

A long line formed before the health inspectors desk—uh oh, what’s this? I watched each passenger hand over a yellow card. When our turn came, the official in white with a peaked cap said, “Yellow fever certificates, please.”

“Uh, I don’t think—the airline people in Nairobi didn’t say anything about...” I stammered.

“You must have valid certificate before you can enter India.’’

I made a show of rummaging around in my briefcase, trying to think.

Knowing we didn’t have this document, I said, “But this is Bhaktivedanta Swami. You can hear outside—the people of Bombay are waiting for him!”

“Even Indira Gandhi comes here without certificate, she is not allowed,” the inspector replied.

“What do we do?”

“Either you can get back on the flight and return to your port of origin, or you can go into our quarantine facility here, for six days.”

“But there hasn’t been any yellow fever in the world for years!” I protested.

“Here is the law.” He handed me a piece of paper, and I showed it to Prabhupada. It said, “Any person, Foreigner or Indian (excluding infants below six months), arriving by air or sea without a vaccination certificate of yellow fever will be kept in quarantine isolation for a period up to 6 days if He arrives in India within 6 days of departure from an infected area.”

Kenya was on the list of yellow fever endemic countries, and none of us had the certificate. Totally flustered and contrite, I glanced at Srila Prabhupada, expecting his well-deserved anger at my having overlooked this vital document. He’s smiling? Not exactly smiling, but calm and accepting. He said to the official, “We shall stay in your facility.”

I was so embarrassed, distraught, a total failure, weak with desperation. “No, no, this cannot be happening!” Prabhupada sensed all this, of course, and said t;o me softly, “We need this time to rest, anyway. Krishna has arranged.” Such is the spiritual master’s mercy.

I asked an official to inform the devotees outside what was happening, and the five of us were escorted by bus to a fenced-off, single-story, tin-roofed concrete bungalow a short ride from the terminal. The building was sealed with mosquito-proof screens and partitioned into six rooms. Prabhupada took the front room with the window, and Aravinda, Bhavananda, Naranarayan, and I spread out into the cubicles down the hall. Our prison for the next six days.

OK, take a deep breath. The Calcutta pandal program begins on October 28. We’ll get out of here on October 26, so we can still make it. But all the Bombay programs must be canceled. We are not allowed to leave the premises, but we are allowed a few visitors for two hours a day—as long as they pass through an isolation chamber by the entrance, get sprayed with insecticide, and step into an ammonia disinfectant footbath before entering.

Madhudvisa, Rishi Kumar, Sada Jiwat Lal, and a few other Bombay devotees and supporters came to visit, so Prabhupada was able to catch up on local affairs. Prasadam was sent over about noon every day, and in the evenings Aravinda cooked for all of us using Prabhupada’s three-tiered cooker over a one-ring kerosene camp stove.

Most hours of the day and night we were alone with Prabhupada. He spent his time translating and catching up on correspondence. Except for periodic low-flying aircraft, the place was quiet and peaceful.

Aside from his translation work, answering a few letters, and our morning philosophy book sessions, Prabhupada didn’t have much to do. Much of each day—and late into the night—I sat in his room alone or with one or two others, conversing about everything under the sun, romping with Prabhupada through far-flung fields of thought and endeavor. Here, in quarantine limbo, we talked about Krishna, the cosmos, the imminent war in Pakistan, and various people we had each known. We didn’t talk so much about our pasts but more about what the future might hold. I even explained to Prabhupada the art of American baseball, showing him how you hold and pitch a ball.

Prabhupada may have been from another century, but his friends were young, and we were gradually learning each other’s language. His mature take on spirit, our young experience with matter—they blended in perfect harmony. I never felt more comfortable in anyone else’s company. Not that I took him for granted. No. That was part of the perfect pitch: Prabhupada surprised me at every turn, kept me off balance until I could leap right or left or reach behind my back and field a one-hop grounder off a well-hit fastball. I think I surprised him too, asking him some off-the-wall question or broaching a topic that would stretch his imagination, sharpen his delivery. I couldn’t think of ever doing anything else but being by Prabhupada’s side, taking care of his every wish. These daily talks with Prabhupada, often one-on-one, became my reason for existence, and more than ever, nothing was impossible.

**\* \* \***

We discovered that most of the Nairobi philosophy session tapes were missing—Aravinda couldn’t find them in his luggage! This was a major blow. I had transcribed eight of the Greeks and early Scholastics, and I found tapes for Leibnitz, Hume, Kant, and half of Fichte, but about twenty-two hours of taped discussions covering fifteen Western philosophers had disappeared. I immediately asked someone to call Brahmananda, and he looked around wherever we had stayed in Nairobi, but couldn’t find them. The Renaissance thinkers, Descartes, Pascal, the British Empiricists, German heavies like Hegel and Nietzsche—gone! Darwin—gone!

But since we’d just finished Darwin, we decided to continue on with the Evolutionists, hoping the missing tapes would eventually be found. If not, Prabhupada said we’d do them over later. From within our prison walls at the Bombay airport, we explored the late nineteenth-century philosophy that claims humankind descends from apes:

Shyamasundar: The first one, Thomas Huxley, says that man can take nature into his own hands and mold his own evolution.

Prabhupada: That is nonsense.

Shyamasundar: Henri Bergson thinks that what he calls the Vital Force is guiding everyone, thus creating its own evolution. We should just drift in the course of things and the Vital Force will determine our history and future. He uses the analogy of the seasons: if we simply place ourselves in the seasons, they will take us toward something, toward springtime, for example.

Prabhupada: That’s all right, but you cannot compare. The analogy is mistaken. The season is matter, material changes. But the evolution is not matter. There is spirit soul. He is making his evolution. So he has got independence. The living entity has got the right to accept or reject. So if he takes, he makes his progress, accepting the right path, then he comes to the goal. But if he rejects, he’ll not reach the goal. That depends on him.

Shyamasundar: Samuel Alexander is the philosopher of “emergent evolution.” He says that the mind is creating a constantly improving world. His idea is that, in the future, the race of man, because of an improved mental life, will be replaced by a race of superconscious beings on earth.

Prabhupada: People think that by nature’s way they will be promoted, that once they have attained the status of man they cannot be degraded. But if one can go up, he can also go down. The rich man can become poor again. These evolutionists and others think that everything goes up, progresses. They don’t even have the common sense to look around them.

On top of losing all those reels of tape, the Uher reel-to-reel tape recorder broke down one day. I sent it into Bombay with Madhudvisa to be repaired, but he returned with bad news: no parts are available in India. No one had considered just how much Srila Prabhupada talked—and I wanted to record everything he said, as much as possible. Lectures, conversations, walks—it seemed like Prabhupada talked for about eighteen hours a day plus two more at night when he translated books. I burned through tapes at a record pace and, without the Uher, had to resort to our little Japanese cassette player. Even cassette tapes were hard to find in India, and they cost over $4 apiece. The Bombay devotees found me a handful of cassettes, but at one or two hours per tape I ran through them quickly. Quarantine, lost tapes, broken tape recorder—nothing seemed to rattle Prabhupada. He told the story of how in 1948, after years of labor, his twelve-hundred-page Bhagavad-gita As It Is manuscript had been stolen from his home in Calcutta, and how he had simply sat down and started over.

Prabhupada vibrated with dynamic vitality, excitement, and adventure, yet surrounding him there was an ineffable air, an aura of softness. You felt it as soon as you got within a foot or two of Prabhupada’s physical body. It was cooler and softer there, like a cushion. It was like entering an air-conditioned room, totally calm in a halo around his body.

Another quality of a self-realized, pure devotee is silence. Sometimes Prabhupada would—I swear—talk to me without opening his mouth. I’d look up and he’d just be looking at me, and I would have heard a full conversation in my head. This happened sometimes when I was composing his letters. I always had the feeling that something else was guiding my hand. I could hear his words ringing in my ears. I wrote with my eyes closed, not thinking too much about what keys to peck on the typewriter or even what words to say, and everything came out all right.

Four days into our quarantine, on October 24, just before dawn, I heard the tinkle of Prabhupada’s bell. When I ran into his room he said, “I have got this yellow fever certificate. It is in my room in Calcutta, in my almirah \[wardrobe\], third shelf down, with other papers. You phone to Jayapataka—he has the key—and tell him to bring here to me in Bombay.” So that night, Jayapataka arrived with Prabhupada’s yellow fever inoculation document, and the next day, on October 25, Prabhupada left for Calcutta, one day before his servants could escape their enforced confinement.

Now, you can look at this incident in different ways, but I think Prabhupada knew all along that he had the certificate and he wanted to rest before plunging into months of heavy preaching programs in India. Prabhupada was faultless, without error. You could say, “Oh, he forgot” or “He made a mistake.” But if you look closer, it wasn’t a mistake. It was just another event in his lila, his inconceivable playtime with us.

**\* \* \***

Bhavananda, Naranarayan, Aravinda, and I fly a thousand miles east to Calcutta on October 27, one day before the ten-day pandal program is set to begin in Deshapriya Park. From the air we see endless rice paddies, straw-roofed villages, the vast Ganges delta—there s the ocean, the Bay of Bengal—and then the giant city below: one of the most densely populated regions on earth.

Calcutta is as big as Bombay—seven million people—spread on a north-south axis along the eastern bank of the Hooghly River, a deltaic branch of the Ganges where it meets the sea in the northeast corner of India.

This is Prabhupada’s hometown, and after hearing so much about Calcutta from him and reading about it in books I’m curious to see this city for the first time. Driving in from Dum Dum Airport, Calcutta looks like a crowded, more colorful version of London, filled with run-down Victorian buildings and monuments.

The British first came to this city in 1690, and Calcutta was the capital of British India for 240 years before the seat of government shifted to New Delhi in 1931. There are more than twenty universities and colleges in Calcutta, including prestigious Scottish Churches College, where Prabhupada studied in 1920. Freestyle intellectual exchange is a prominent pastime in Bengali society, and Calcutta was a thriving metropolis when Bombay was still a malaria-ridden swamp.

Bengalis have always been the revolutionaries and out-of-the-box thinkers of India. In the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries (when Prabhupada was a young man), Calcutta was the home of the Bengali Renaissance, an intellectual movement combining the positive aspects of Indian culture and history with an appreciation for Western modernism. Great artists, like Rabindranath Tagore and Satyajit Ray, roamed the streets. Jagadish Chandra Bose proved that plants have consciousness, and he pioneered radio science and microwaves; S. N. Bose, a quantum physicist, worked with Einstein on the concept of atomic subparticles, one of which is named (the boson) after him. During this time, the Indian nationalist movement took birth in Calcutta, and firebrands like Surendranath Banerjee and Subhas Chandra Bose (Prabhupada’s classmate at Scottish Churches College) gave form to the “Quit-India” politics, later adopted by Mahatma Gandhi, which eventually led to India’s independence from Britain.

It is Bengal’s spiritual contributions, however, that have outshone all of these material, intellectual achievements. Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, who is Radha and Krishna combined, appeared in 1486 in Mayapur, a small village a hundred miles north of presentday Calcutta. Sri Chaitanya introduced the chanting of the Hare Krishna mantra as the surest way for liberation in the Age of Kali and instructed that the teachings of bhakti-yoga be spread throughout the world. (It seems no accident to Krishna devotees that the British appeared in this region two hundred years later to give the Lord’s followers the language by which they have now delivered His message around the globe.)

The Bengali Renaissance spawned several iconic spiritual thinkers, such as Sri Aurobindo, Ramakrishna, Vivekananda Swami, and Paramahamsa Yogananda, some of whom traveled to the West in the 1920s and ’30s, creating an image for Americans of what a swami looks like, how he acts, and what he says. But in 1965, another Bengali swami came to America: A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami. His timing was perfect. For the first time in history, ancient Vedic science in its purest form took deep root in American soil, and in just six years the Swami’s movement spread across the globe. Bhaktivedanta Swami imparted India’s ancient spiritual science to a thousand disciples, radically changing their lives. Now, in late October 1971, he returned to Calcutta, where it had all begun.

We taxied into Calcutta and were happy to be reunited with Prabhupada. Two days’ absence from him had seemed like two years.

The ISKCON temple at 3 Albert Road was well-located in central Calcutta, just east of the Victoria Memorial. This was the once-posh Park Street district, bounded by Shakespeare Street (north), Circus Avenue (south), Camac Street (west), and Hungerford Street (east). There was a nice lake across the street from the temple, and on our first early-morning walk with Prabhupada we watched huge fruit bats with six-foot wingspans flap slowly across the dawning sky and disappear into the tall palms ringing the lake. Srila Prabhupada told us about vampire bats, too, which alighted on cattle at night to suck their blood. One devotee piped up that, yes, he wondered about the big sores on his toes when he awoke from sleep out on the balcony, and Prabhupada told him to cover his feet under the mosquito net.

Prabhupada: In Calcutta, there are many good manufacturers of musical instruments. Bengal is famous for music and hair.

Kaushalya: And hair.

Prabhupada: Yes.

Hamsaduta: Hair?

Prabhupada: There is oil in the hair of Bengali women. They have got hair like this. My sister had so big bunch, \[laughter\] Black hair and very long. So Bengal is famous for hair and music.

Srila Prabhupada was in good hands on his home turf, with Jayapataka, Giriraj, and other senior devotees taking care of everything. The Albert Road devotees were rushing around, preparing for the pandal at Deshapriya Park. There wasn’t much time for philosophy book sessions, but we managed to squeeze in a few.

After the Evolutionists, we tackled the Dialectic Materialists, the philosophies of communism. Calcutta was a hotbed of Marxist activity, and we saw the hammer and sickle on almost every wall and lamppost. Striking workers and agitators marched in the streets. All over Calcutta, radical Bengalis were trying to instigate the class struggle between the bourgeoisie and the proletariat. It was the perfect time and place to discuss Karl Marx, Vladimir Lenin, and Mao Tse-tung.

Shyamasundar: So Karl Marx made a manifesto called The Communist Manifesto, which lists ten points for social reform. The first one is the abolition of private property—all property becomes public.

Prabhupada: The whole tendency of the individual spirit soul is that I want profit from my activity. He may propose all these nice things according to his philosophy, but he cannot change the mind of the people. Therefore all such proposals will be futile. Simply waste of time, that’s all.

Shyamasundar: His idea is that the minds of people can be changed.

Prabhupada: But not in that way, by force. That is not possible. You cannot change the mind even of a child by force, and what to speak of elderly man, educated man. Is it not so?

Shyamasundar: Marx says that all property, all capital, communications, transportation—everything should be centralized in the hands of the state.

Prabhupada: These basic ideas cannot change: that I want some property, I want some adoration, and I want some position. This will never change. These rascals, they do not know the basic principles of human psychology. We have seen ourselves: Russia is not happy and they are simply waiting for another revolution. They may theorize so many things. So far as we are concerned, foreigners in Moscow, we could not get even food to our satisfaction. There are no vegetables, no fruits, no rice. Practically we could have starved. Their utopia—it is not possible.

Shyamasundar: Vladimir Lenin studied history, and he said that history develops in leaps, or revolutions, progressing toward communism. So Lenin wants to make a leap to the dictatorship of the proletariat, and this he calls “the final stage of history.”

Prabhupada: No. We can say, and they may note it also, that after this, the Bolshevik Revolution, there will be many other revolutions, because so long people will live on the mental plane there will be only revolution. That’s all. Our proposition is, “Give up this mental concoction. Come to the right point. And that is spiritual platform.” If one comes to that spiritual platform, svamin krtartho ’smi varam na yace: “No more revolution. I am completely satisfied because I have now seen You.” Krishna consciousness is the final revolution.

Shyamasundar: Mao Tse-tung stresses the contradiction between capitalism and communism—that this contradiction involves conflicts and eventual revolution. Without conflict there can be no progress. Mao thinks whoever wins in a battle of ideas must have the right idea.

Prabhupada: No. That is based on “might is right.” But we do not accept this theory. We say, “right is might,” not “might is right.”

Shyamasundar: His idea is that political power comes from the barrel of a gun.

Prabhupada: Because he is rude. He cannot conceive that there may be, among the sober gentlemen, the gun of reasoning. And for these crude rascals it is an argumentum ad baculum. Of course, the gun is sometimes needed when the other party is completely animal. But if both parties are animals, then what decision can be made? You see? Therefore our conclusion is taken from shaastra. Shaastra \[with a long “a”\] means scripture, authorized decisions. “Then you do not come to reason? Then let us turn to weapon.” Shastra \[with short “a”\] means “weapon.” So this shaastra, scripture, and shastra, gun, both these words come from shas-dhatu, the same root. Shas means control. So conflict there is, but the mediator is shas, control. Otherwise, there is no end of struggle if you don’t accept an authorized mediator, a controller.

Shyamasundar: Mao says people need constant ideological brainwashing to maintain this state of collective activity-slogans, repeated so often that the people will accept them as truth. This truth will ultimately control. World communism means there will be no more conflict.

Prabhupada: What is the value of your slogan if you are yourself controlled? Our point is that if you are yourself blind, how you can lead other blind men? First of all, why the whole world will accept this communism unless it is perfect? If it is imperfect, how do you expect that the whole world will accept it? Perhaps you have not understood your philosophy; therefore you are so much optimistic. Therefore it will fail. Anything imperfect will fail. That is my proposition. You make however much propaganda, it will fail. By philosophy, you are supposed to give something which will not create any conflict. But by conflict, by crushing, by subduing, if you want to establish your peace, then what is the value of this peace?

For the Deshapriya Park pandal, Jayapataka was taking extra precautions with security. Just six months earlier, at Prabhupadas first Calcutta pandal at Chowringhee Maidan, pro-Mao Naxalite students had interrupted the program, forcing Prabhupada to leave the stage when chairs started flying and to make a quick exit through a flap at the rear of the tent.

The violent Naxalite faction of the Indian Communist Party, who followed the tenets of China’s Mao Tse-tung, had split away from the Marxist-Leninists in 1967 in the small Bengali village of Naxalbari after several demonstrators were killed in a confrontation between landlords and tenant farmers. Their ideological leader, Charu Majumdar, had declared that the Naxalites should assassinate individual “class enemies,” such as landlords, businessmen, teachers, police officers, and politicians, and that revolutionary warfare was to take place not only in rural areas but everywhere and spontaneously. Earlier in 1971, the Naxalites gained a strong presence among the radical sections of students in Calcutta, and—who knew?—maybe swamis were on their hit list too.

In July 1971, just three months earlier, Indira Gandhi had taken advantage of President’s Rule to mobilize the Indian army against the Naxalites, launching a colossal combined army and police counter-insurgency operation named “Operation Steeplechase,” killing hundreds of Naxalites and imprisoning more than twenty thousand suspects and cadres, including senior leaders.

So the Naxalite threat was diminished, but it was still there and violence was in the air. The civil war in neighboring East Pakistan (East Bengal) raged on (Dacca is just two hundred miles northeast of Calcutta), and war refugees were flooding into West Bengal. Besides this, there was the rumored threat that other Bengali religionists, envious of Prabhupada’s success, were plotting in the background. Calcutta was one of those places—maybe like Istanbul or Zanzibar—that inspired intrigue, and you just never knew who was plotting what. So there was a real worry that Prabhupada might be in danger and that his lectures in Deshapriya Park might spark violence.

From Albert Road, Deshapriya Park is a two-mile drive south down Sarat Bose Road. Each morning and evening a Life Member drove Prabhupada to the elaborate pandal, where fifteen thousand citizens waited to hear him speak. “Why you are spending so lavishly for this pandal—you could be feeding people!” was the usual gripe from the Bengali commies in the crowd. Prabhupada answered, “My disciples have made like this. They want you to come and hear, so they have to trick you.”

Bob Cohen, an American Peace Corps volunteer working in nearby Bihar, attended one of Prabhupada’s pandal lectures. He describes his adventure:

Finally I began to look for a taxi. It was now evening rush hour. I ran up to a taxi that had stopped at a traffic light, although it was too dark to see if there were passengers inside or not. “Take me to Free School Street,” I said, jumping in. I glanced over to my side, and there in the corner, amid piles of books and magazines, was a shaven-headed devotee in orange robes.

“Hare Krishna!” I shouted excitedly. “Hare Krishna,” he replied, somewhat surprised. “I’ve been looking for you people all day,” I said. “I saw a poster advertising a festival in Deshapriya Park, but I can’t find the place.”

“It’s just nearby,” he said. “We’re two minutes away. I’m going there now.”

We pulled up in front of what looked like a large park. The entire park was covered with a huge circus tent, and under its canopy crowded almost fifteen thousand people. At the far end of the pavilion was a big stage, and at its center was a simple, raised dais. There sat the founder and spiritual master of the Hare Krishna movement, His Divine Grace Srila Prabhupada, regally wearing scores of flower garlands offered by his disciples. He sat silently as one of his disciples introduced him to the crowd. When the disciple explained that Srila Prabhupada had traveled alone at the age of seventy to boldly preach Krishna consciousness in America, the audience responded with a standing ovation.

When the program was over and Srila Prabhupada was being driven away in his car, I ran up to the devotee who had spoken. “Can I come?” I begged. “I’d like to meet the guru.”

“Sure, jump in,” he replied, smiling.

We’ll catch up with Bob Cohen later.

The days passed peacefully—no Naxalite problems—but attendance at the ten-day Deshapriya pandal gradually fell off, and by the final day, November 6, there were maybe two hundred people in the huge tent. Prabhupada was upset that the organizers had advertised the event in Bengali, even though he was speaking in English. Many attendees couldn’t understand what he was saying.

During these days at Albert Road, between his lectures at Deshapriya Park, Prabhupada was mostly concerned about his Mayapur project, located a hundred miles north of Calcutta. The Calcutta crew had purchased a total of eleven bighas (3.6 acres) of land in Mayapur over the past eight months and had put up some fencing, dug a well, and built one hut on the property.

(Gurukripa told me a great story about that hut: a few months earlier, during the monsoon season, the Ganges River had inundated our Mayapur land to a depth of ten feet. He and Sudama Vipra (both Vietnam vets) took refuge in the ceiling of the bamboo-and-thatch hut, along with a ten-foot python that had wrapped itself around a rafter. The humans and the giant serpent stared at one another until an unspoken agreement was reached: you stay in your space and we’ll stay in ours. Each day for three days the two devotees dove down, swam through the underwater door, and climbed onto the roof to await rescue. At night they returned to the rafters, the pitch black, and their uncertain truce with the python.)

We were scheduled to leave Calcutta for New Delhi on November 10, and time was short. Prabhupada wanted to start big construction at Mayapur immediately according to the master plan that Naranarayan and Bhavananda had brought with them from London. Every day these boys, along with Jayapataka and Achyutananda, met in Prabhupadas room to go over the plan. Prabhupada instructed them to buy more land, build a huge guesthouse, and start stockpiling steel and cement. And every day he asked them, “Why you are taking so long?”

The Albert Road temple was much like Bury Place in London—a great location but too little space. The search for bigger premises had been going on for months, and the day before we were to leave for Delhi, the devotees took Prabhupada to see a run-down, once aristocratic house in the Ballygunge district. In the grand, cobwebbed entrance hall there was a hollow elephants foot by the door, where gentlemen used to keep their umbrellas, and on the wall opposite, the elephant’s moth-eaten head, with one yellow eye and most of its trunk eaten away by time. Calcutta seemed a city worn out by time and politics.

But observing the intelligent passion in the Calcutta men who came to visit Prabhupada, and meeting his look-alike sister, Pishima—even his son Vrindavan came to visit—I began to appreciate Prabhupada on another level. Life in Bengal has its roots in spiritual antiquity. Calcutta’s culture is based on philosophy and thought, not business and commerce, like Bombay. Now, a lot of Prabhupada’s expressions, his worldview, and his brand of humor made better sense to me. Bengalis are a people of action; they like to get things done. Calcutta is a cosmopolitan city but a small village at the same time. Everyone seems to know one another’s families and gotra, or line of ancestral descent. The heavy heat, the draw to languor, the guarding of every rupee won or spent, finding comfort in a small straw hut, the constant aroma of mustard oil and spices in the air—all these are part of Prabhupada’s Bengali tradition.

**\* \* \***

The largest and perhaps most entangled government bureaucracy in the world is ruled from New Delhi, with more than six hundred million lives in these officials’ hands. Early on the morning of November 10, 1971, we flew Indian Airlines from Calcutta to Delhi, arriving at Palam Airport at 8:30 a.m. to a VIP reception on the tarmac beside the parked plane. Sri Hans Raj Gupta, mayor of New Delhi, along with a dozen government bigwigs, met Prabhupada as he stepped off the gangway. Flashbulbs popped, and Gurudas and Tamal Krishna beamed as they garlanded Prabhupada. They’d done a great job organizing this reception by Delhi’s leading politicians.

Srila Prabhupada always seemed to be near the center of earth-shaking times and places, such as the “Human Be-in” and “Year of the Guru” in San Francisco, the Beatles in London, and the Cold War in Moscow.
