Now, here in New Delhi, Prabhupada again stood in the midst of serious world events: India was on the brink of war with Pakistan, and New Delhi was command central.

Bhaktivedanta Swami had lived off and on in Delhi for almost ten years during the late ’50s and early ’60s. It was here that he had struggled alone to print and distribute his Back to Godhead magazine and three-volume Srimad-Bhagavatam, Canto One. In those days he often walked across Delhi because he didn’t have the half-rupee for bus fare. Now, six years later, he was returning in triumph as India’s spiritual ambassador to the world.

Prabhupada is led to a temporary raised dais, decorated with pictures and an array of his books, just a short walk from the plane, in the open winter sunshine. A hundred people are gathered to greet him. He climbs up, sits down and, one by one, devotees in dhotis and dignitaries dressed in suits and Nehru jackets slip a garland over his head and receive a brief blessing. Kshirodakshayi hands him a copy of the first Hindi BTG, and Prabhupada praises the color printing and layout. The sun is in his eyes. Prabhupada is grave, considering the impact of this moment, while Mayor Gupta and others extol his accomplishments, everyone wagging their heads in the Indian sign of agreement. Prabhupada then speaks to these government leaders in words they will most appreciate:

By the grace of my Guru Maharaja and by your blessings, I went to the Western countries and had such a very, very good response. I went there empty-handed with forty rupees in my pocket and free return ticket by the Scindia Steam Navigation Company. And for one year I had no place to live, I had no money to eat. Still, I was going here and there. After struggling for one year, in 1966 I incorporated this International Society for Krishna Consciousness. We now have sixty-five branches all over the world, and in each branch there are maximum two hundred devotees like these, and minimum twenty-five devotees, at least. At Los Angeles we are spending $20,000 per month, which is in Indian exchange two lakhs of rupees. Similarly, we are spending $10,000 in New York. In this way we have to spend seven lakhs of rupees per month for maintaining all these men, and we have got big, big publications, like this \[gesturing to the row of books displayed below him on the dais\]. When I went to the Western country I brought only three volumes of Srimad-Bhagavatam, which I published here in Delhi while living in the temple of our Sri Krishna Sharma. He gave me shelter there, and I was publishing these books, and actually Lalaji \[Mayor Gupta\] also contributed something for my publication. So in this way I went there with three books. Now we have got over three dozen books, all beautifully published.... My request to you all ladies and gentlemen, please try to understand the potency of this Krishna consciousness movement, and make your country proud.

While Prabhupada speaks, I scan the crowd. So many devotees are here! This is the first time since the Kumbha Mela ten months earlier that most of the India devotees have gathered in one place. I see Gurudas, Yamuna, Tamal, Madri, Tejiyas, Kshirodakshayi, Bhanu, and Subal Swami from Delhi; Madhudvisa, Rishi Kumar,

Dinanath, Gargamuni, and Vishal from Bombay; Jayapataka, Achyutananda, Bhavananda, Palika, Naranarayan, Yadubara, Sudama Vipra, and Gurukripa from Calcutta; and there’s Bali Mardan fresh in from Malaysia.

And here’s Malati—and Saraswati! I hug my daughter, lift her into the front seat of the car with me, and we follow Prabhupada’s car to the home of Ram Niwas Dandaria, where Prabhupada will stay. Hans Raj Gupta sits in the lead car with Swamiji. We can see them yakking back and forth, joking and laughing like old classmates. When we reach the house, there are newspaper reporters waiting, and Mayor “Lalaji” looks on while Prabhupada sets them straight:

Prabhupada: In India, everyone is born Krishna conscious.

And now they are trying to forget Krishna consciousness.

Reporter: Oh, we are. I see.

Prabhupada: Krishna says, mattah parataram nanyat kinchid asti: “There is no superior personality than Me.” And we accept it.

Reporter: Can we see Him, just as we see Lalaji here?

Prabhupada: And why not? Just like Lalaji, now he is known here. We can see. But if he goes in the streets, nobody knows him. Is it not? One who does not know Lalaji, he meets Lalaji on the street, but he does not see him. Why?

Reporter: Question of eyes and knowing, sir.

Prabhupada: Yes. Then he has to prepare his eyes to see Lalaji.

Reporter: So, sir, how do we prepare eyes to see?

Prabhupada: That is—that is the question.

Suddenly, an air-raid siren wails in the distance, warning residents all over Delhi of an impending Pakistani air attack—the signal for a citywide blackout. Shutters slam shut, and only a tiny pool of desk light illumines the group:

Reporter: Sir, this is the real, present ugly reality here. We are being threatened. This siren is a, shall I say, a very ugly reality before us.

Prabhupada: You are already in ugly reality always, twenty-four hours. Material reality. Suppose there is no blackout. Still, if you go in the street, is there any guarantee that you will go home?

Reporter: No, no.

Prabhupada: Then you are always in ugly reality. Why do you say this blackout? This is one of the features of that ugly, material reality. That’s all.

Reporter: I know, sir, but this is collective, national danger here. Have you anything to offer to us as a, as a—

Prabhupada: This Krishna consciousness. Our only remedy is this Krishna consciousness. You take to this and you will be happy. Krishna’s devotee is not afraid of anything or anyone. He will fight, but only under Krishna’s direction. This is the only remedy.

**\* \* \***

For months, Prabhupada had been corresponding with Gurudas and Yamuna to arrange a pandal in Delhi and invite big politicians. At his urging, they had met twice with Prime Minister Indira Gandhi at her home, and this top-down strategy looked like it was paying off. Gurudas and Yamuna had earned the confidence of some of Delhi’s biggest movers and shakers, and now, with the help of Tamai Krishna and Tejiyas, had put together a public festival of epic proportions in the heart of India’s capital.

King George V laid the foundation stone for the city of New Delhi in 1911, and twenty years later, Lord Irwin inaugurated this elegant capital of the British Raj. The city was designed with wide streets radiating from a central hub called Connaught Circus. ISKCON had booked the Life Insurance Corporation (LIC) grounds on Connaught Inner Ring for its spectacular, ten-day pandal show. It was like hitting the bulls-eye of India.

Bhagavata Dharma Discourses at the LIC grounds, starring His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami and his American and European disciples, will run from November 12 through

November 21, 1971. Chief guests for each of the ten nights were listed in all the newspapers. It was an all-star cast of Delhi’s finest:

Hans Raj Gupta, mayor of Delhi

Harivansh Rai Bachchan, renowned Hindi poet (and father of Amitabh, rising Bollywood star)

Sri Shyama Charan Gupta, Chairman of the Delhi Municipal Council

Sri C. B. Agarwal, orator

Justices Bipin Chandra Misra and S. I. Rangarajan, magistrates, Delhi High Court

Dr. Atma Ram, scientist, Director-General of the Council of Scientific and Industrial Research

Col. B. R. Mohan, ex-mayor of Delhi and owner of Mohan Meakin Breweries

Sri L. N. Saklani, industrialist

Sri Aditya Nath Jha, Lieutenant-Governor of Delhi Jagjivan Ram, Indian Defense Minister

The Honorable James George, Canadian High Commissioner

New, four-foot-tall white marble Radha and Krishna Deities have been brought from nearby Jaipur and installed center stage in the elaborate pandal in Delhi’s heart. During the day, thousands of citizens circulate through the pandal to watch bare-chested Nanda Kumar perform elegant aratis while forty or fifty foreign devotees (and some Indian boys, too) chant Krishna’s name with wild abandon. But it is the evening programs that capture the greatest crowds. November weather in Delhi is generally cold, with nights falling into the low forties. Still, for ten consecutive evenings over ten thousand people gathered at the LIC grounds, heavily bundled in sweaters and shawls, to see and hear the powerful sadhu, their countryman, A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami, who has created all of this.

Night after night, Srila Prabhupada, dressed in gleaming saffron silk, sits on an ornate vyasasan, his head held high in the spotlight while a different chief guest introduces him ro the people of Delhi. Some of the chief guests read prepared speeches and others speak impromptu, but all of them extol Prabhupadas achievements, especially his work in spreading Indias culture in the West. While they speak, Prabhupada looks straight ahead, once in a while rolling his tongue between his gums and teeth as if to say, “I take no credit.”

Politicians, industrialists, poets—each chief guest represents a different area of Indian accomplishment. One night, Dr. Atma Ram, one of the nations leading scientists, introduces Bhaktivedanta Swami, and this is Prabhupadas response:

Just like Dr. Atma Ram is scientist because he has acquired the qualities of a scientist and he is working in his laboratory for scientific research; therefore he is scientist. This is the scientific process; the first process is, that you must hear. As you go to college and hear the professor, then you become advanced in that particular type of knowledge. Similarly, to know the science of God, you must first of all hear from the expert. Otherwise, how you can know who is God?

I was invited to speak in Massachusetts Technology Institute. So my first challenge was that “Where is your technology by which you can distinguish a dead man and a living man?” If you have got scientific knowledge, and if you say that “this dead man is a lump of matter, chemicals,” then you just replace those chemicals and make him alive again. That is scientific knowledge—not theory.

With each successive lecture, Srila Prabhupada makes a deeper impression on this high-class audience, the men and women who govern India, convincing them with his vast knowledge of shastra, his enlightened grasp of contemporary culture and international affairs, and his astute read on politics. The Swami says knowledge is India’s greatest asset, “and now you, as India’s leaders, you should give it to the world.” Before and after each lecture, there is a surge from the sophisticated crowd to touch the feet of this unexpected saint, the new hero of India. Just when you thought Srila Prabhupada couldn’t get any greater, he exceeds all models of greatness.

On the night of November 20, Prabhupada lectures on education:

Don’t spoil your children. The modern educational system is without any knowledge of Bhagavan. I may tell you frankly—not so much in India, but everywhere else—these countries are practically slaughterhouses. In our country, it is a different thing; at least we have got the Vedic culture at home if it is not in the schools. But in other countries, because there is no bhagavata-dharma culture, the students, although they are provided with ample opportunity for education, the nicest educational system, nice buildings, nice facilities, everything nice, unfortunately the products are coming out frustrated, confused young men, and some of them are called hippies.

So in this city of Delhi—it is a great city and very important city. There are very important men here. They should kindly consider this proposal that there must be a very organized school to understand this bhagavata-dharma, just to teach their boys, and their life will be successful. It is the duty of the father and mother to see that “My son, this is your last attempt of coming into this material world.”

As if on cue, a tall American hippie boy with long blond hair, tattoos, and wearing a flamboyant vest, storms out of the crowd and halts before the stage, shouting, “Have you realized yourself within yourself?”

“Yes,” Prabhupada says. He tells the boy to come onstage, and gives him a seat and a microphone.

The boy challenges, “You answer my question. I asked first!”

Prabhupada gets a little angry and says, “I am not your servant!” and has the boy removed. The crowd is shocked, and many get up and leave.

Later, we understood the lesson from this enigmatic display of Prabhupadas power: bhagavat-dharma, perfect knowledge, is effectively transmitted from master to student in a submissive, not challenging, mood.

Saraswati is a star in every show. She dances around onstage, followed by Prabhupadas eyes. He laughs when she jumps in the air, enticing the audience to higher excitement. She wears tiny Rajasthani outfits, bold-colored blouses and skirts with mirrors and spangles.

On one side of the pandal is the very popular Questions and Answers booth, where a senior devotee sits on a high vyasasan and answers the public s questions. All day long and into the night, three or four hundred visitors stand around this booth and throw questions at Tamai Krishna or Gurudas or Rishi Kumar, sometimes until well after midnight. Opposite this booth are a photo display of ISKCON’s activities around the world and a large-scale model of the Mayapur scheme.

The Minister of Defense, Mr. Jagjivan Ram, scheduled as chief guest on the night of November 21 cannot make it—it seems he has to prepare for war—so Gurudas entices Indias Finance Minister, Sri Yashwantrao Chavan, to come instead, along with Aditya Nath Jah, the Lieutenant-Governor of Delhi. Both give laudatory speeches. Then Prabhupada speaks, enlarging on his theme for national spiritual education:

Krishna says in the Bhagavad-gita that the leading people must be ideal. Yad yad acharati sresthah. Sresthah means “leading personalities.” Tat tad evetaro janah: and his actions are followed by the ordinary men. The present condition of India, I may say, is spiritually so much fallen because our leaders were disinterested in the spiritual matter.... I am very glad that the Finance Minister and the Governor both agreed to this point, and I shall request you, all respectable citizens of New Delhi: try to establish this institution. We have got perfect direction from Krishna, so there will be no mistake. Even if you think that our state is secular, this will be perfect secular. It is the duty of the state to see that people are advancing in Krishna consciousness; otherwise they will never be happy. People must be taught—educated-to become Krishna conscious, and the state should see to it. That is the instruction of the great sages and saintly persons. It is the duty of the governor and the king to understand this science of Krishna consciousness. Otherwise how you will be able to give benefit to the citizens?

On another night, the Canadian High Commissioner, Mr. James George, dressed in a sharp, white linen suit, with his elegant wife beside him onstage, introduces Srila Prabhupada:

For several years I’ve been wanting to meet this swami and see what it was about him that was affecting so many of our young people in Canada and North America. I was very happy this evening to come in response to your kind invitation, and especially to meet Swamiji. I think, as he himself said to me a short time ago, there is something really happening. Whether he is doing it or it is being done through him, as he said, it is beside the point. Hut there is something happening, and everyone who doubts that should be here tonight to see this. It is happening, not only here in Delhi, but it’s happening in Toronto and Cleveland and Los Angeles and New York and all sort of places. What is it? I don’t know how he would answer that question. For me, at a deeper level what is happening is the awakening of a search.

Prabhupada replies without missing a beat:

So you are here, our Honorable High Commissioner of Canada is present, His Excellency. So I request that you have come to our country: please try to understand this philosophy. And so far I have traveled in your country, I have traveled in Canada also, as you have already mentioned, and we have got five branches—in Montreal, Toronto, Vancouver, Hamilton, and Ottawa. So the boys are struggling. They are distributing literature, their books, and I am getting very encouraging reports. Many young men, they are coming. It is a very scientific movement. So I request you to, I mean to say, inform your government to give these boys who are struggling to inject this Krishna consciousness movement in your country all facilities.

One day during the pandal, Madhukar Tandon, on holiday from his school in Moscow and visiting his family in Delhi, came to see Prabhupada to chalk out ways to help his friend Anatoly (now Ananta Shanti das) in the USSR, especially by getting books to him by diplomatic pouch through Madhukar’s father at the Indian embassy in Moscow.

As the days passed, many devotees were falling sick—Yamuna, Tejiyas, Aravinda, and a dozen others—coming down with dysentery, jaundice, or bad colds due to freezing weather, the long hours, poor and irregular diets, and germs carried by the huddled masses. But the show went on.

Prabhupada often meets important Delhi men in his room at Mr. Dandaria’s house. Colonel B. R. Mohan, a previous mayor of Delhi and now Director of Mohan Meaken Breweries, couldn’t make it to the pandal on the night he was supposed to speak, but he meets Prabhupada on a sober morning, lamenting his sinful activities, practically crying in Prabhupada’s lap that his company is India’s largest producer of beer and rum. Prabhupada tells him, “Suppose you are selling liquor. The thing is bad, but because people want it, government gives license that you can sell for the drunkard but under these rules and regulations, not freely. Similarly, the living entities, all the conditioned souls who have come here in this material world, their real purpose is how to enjoy this material world. A living entity is not enjoyer; he is servitor. But when he wants to enjoy, he is sent into this material world. Krishna bhuliya jiva bhoga vancha kare. When we forget our constitutional position and we try to enjoy this material world, Krishna allows, ‘All right, you go there and enjoy, but these are the restrictions, and you will suffer.’”

**\* \* \***

Aravinda, Prabhupada’s servant, typist, and our close traveling companion for the past five months, was having some problems. He was starting to challenge Prabhupada’s statements and sulk at Prabhupada’s slightest chastisement of him. The lost philosophy tapes were a constant topic of discussion. Seeing Aravinda’s growing anger and frustration, I could sense what was coming. One night, late into the night, Prabhupada and Aravinda met alone, and the next morning Aravinda announced that he was leaving. Prabhupada unhappily accepted his decision and gave him train fare to Calcutta with a promise for plane fare back to the US, probably hoping he would come to his senses after taking a break.

Back in the 1950s, when Prabhupada was based in Delhi, he sometimes stayed with the S. K. Joshi family in Kamala Nagar. Joshi’s small son would often sit on the Swami’s lap. Now a grown lad, Joshi’s son was initiated by Srila Prabhupada as Rohininandan das, and Prabhupada began to train him to assume Aravinda’s duties. Nanda Kumar, who had been Prabhupada’s servant for a short time in L.A. in early 1970, was also available, but his pujari duties at the pandal were more important to Prabhupada. So this young Indian boy moved in with us as Prabhupada’s personal servant.

As a result of Aravinda’s departure, the job of typing letters was passed to me—now I was both composing and typing the mail, and my days and nights were even busier. Letters became much more involved and personal as I realized their value to the devotees. I carefully went over each letter with Srila Prabhupada, taking notes as he gave detailed, exhaustive replies. The post of secretary becomes a conduit, the transparent via medium to deepen and expand messages between Srila Prabhupada and each devotee who writes to him. His movement was growing so fast—now with more than sixty centers and hundreds of devotees—and so too grew the volume of letters arriving on Prabhupada’s desk. There were as many as ten a day.

With Prabhupada’s busy pandal schedule, plus meetings in his room with devotees and citizens, and with my added burden of typing letters, we scarcely had time for philosophy debates these days. Still, we managed to squeeze in the American pragmatists William James and John Dewey.

Shyamasundar: Today we are discussing American philosopher William James. His philosophy is called pragmatism, or that which can be practically applied. His central thesis is that thoughts should produce habits of action. Tired of theoretical philosophy, he wanted to see that philosophy had practical application.

Prabhupada: So philosophy without practical application is called mental speculation. It has no value. We agree to that. Philosophy must be practically applied in life. That is real philosophy.

Shyamasundar: He says that terms such as “God” and “matter” and “absolute”—terms like that must have cash value or practical worth. He says, “You must bring out of each work its practical cash value.”

Prabhupada: Yes. Therefore our Krishna consciousness movement daily brings cash value without any business, without any labor. What do you think?

Shyamasundar: So when we use the word “God,” it has cash value?

Prabhupada: Cash value. We are going to everyone. We are simply showing some book and taking money. We are giving books worth $200 and taking $1,100. So our Krishna consciousness movement has cash value. Outsiders, they are surprised: “How these people live in such a nice house, eat so nicely, and have so many cars, and they have no anxieties, although they do not work, they have no business?” So what can be more cash value than this?

Shyamasundar: John Dewey’s idea is that the problems of philosophy are rooted in social conditions. By changing social structures through education, then the problems of philosophy will be solved. Deweys greatest faith was in the educational system, that the educational system should reflect the real welfare of the community.

Prabhupada: Yes. The best educational system is Krishna consciousness: tan manye adhitam uttamam. Because people are being educated without Krishna consciousness, education is becoming valueless. This is education: Krishna is one, and everything else is zero; so if one is there first, zeros are added—ten, hundred, thousand—increases its value. Take out the one and it is all zero. Thousands of zeros will not carry any weight. So they are all zero without Krishna.

Shyamasundar: Dewey says that we must rest content with a faith and a commitment that helps us to face the future resolutely, reconstructing our environment to obtain more satisfactory adjustments. This is really modern Western philosophy in a nutshell.

Prabhupada: Why not take directly the words of God?

During one of his Delhi pandal lectures, Prabhupada said: “We are discussing and meeting all Western philosophers from the angle of vision of Srimad-Bhagavatam. So we are never defeated. We come out victorious in every case, and it defeats all other philosophy.” And on November 17 he wrote to Brihaspati in Philadelphia: “Philosophy is the highest, but even higher than philosophy is practice of philosophy.... Soon I shall be printing my latest book, commentaries on your Western philosophers, and with this instruction you will be able to defeat all of the mental speculators and so-called scientists who are simply misleading everyone.” Our morning philosophy discussions are taking on increasing importance for Prabhupadas mission, and I try to stay sharp.

On the evening of Thursday, November 25, Gurudas takes us to the Delhi home of Dr. Karan Singh, the hereditary king of Kashmir and Indias current Minister of Tourism and Civil Aviation. Karan Singh s home is truly posh (a British acronym for “Port Out, Starboard Home,” meaning “high class”—the shady side of a ship steaming between England and India). Malati and Saraswati are here too, along with an English lady, the guest of Dr. Singh. We gather around the indoor swimming pool, relaxing amid potted palms, muted lighting, and the soft sound of a sitar from hidden speakers. Mrs. Karan Singh enters. She is Yasho Rajya Lakshmi, a princess of Nepal, and we are struck by her radiant beauty and penetrating, gold-green eyes. I switch on the tape recorder: this is going to be good.

Prabhupada starts right in:

Therefore, simply for decoration of the body, that is not human civilization. Civilized activity is going on the basis of keeping this body in comfort. Yasyatma-buddhih kunape tri-dhatuke. Everywhere attempt is being made how to keep this body comfortable. The karmis, they are trying to elevate themselves to the heavenly planets, they are trying even to go to the moon planet. But wherever you go, you cannot be any way comfortable. Therefore,

Bhagavad-gita says that janma-mrtyu-jara-vyadhi-duhkha-dosanudarsanam. Intelligent person will see that “However comfortable I may be, I have to meet death.” Whatever you create, it will be taken away.

Dr. Karan Singh is himself a keen and learned Vedic scholar, and they banter back and forth on a very high level. Prabhupada appreciates the Maharaja’s humble demeanor and says, “I am very glad to see you are original kshatriya, a raja-rishi \[philosopher-king\].” They swat Sanskrit back and forth like tennis balls for many minutes, the Maharaja even completing shlokas that Srila Prabhupada begins. Prabhupada has read one of Dr. Karan Singh’s books, and he quotes from it: “Every statement of Hinduism in the context of the nuclear age assumes tremendous importance.” Then he adds, “And we are giving shape to your philosophy.”

Karan Singh: So one of your followers is George Harrison of the Beatles?

Prabhupada: He gave me nineteen thousand dollars.

Karan Singh: Did he!”

Their conversation cracks us up. The Maharaja’s spontaneous, aristocratic, but almost childlike responses are refreshing, and everyone is having a hilarious time.

Prabhupada: In the Western countries, they are getting money and misusing on wine and women. Just like a child, you give hundred rupees, he will spoil it. They say in Bengal, “The monkey is decorated with pearl necklace.” Or another, “The farmer, what does he know about the taste of liquor?” These are very instructive.

Karan Singh: That is from the days before country liquor.

Prabhupada: If you give one bottle of John Walker —

Karan Singh: \[laughs\] Johnnie.

Prabhupada: \[laughs\] Johnnie Walker? What is it?

English woman: I’m so surprised you know the name of that liquor.

Prabhupada: Yes, I am used to everything, \[laughter\]

Dr. Singh has just returned from Russia, where he was sent as an envoy to the Eastern Bloc nations to explain Indias position on the impending war between India and Pakistan.

Prabhupada: I have sown the seed in Moscow. I have got one student.

Karan Singh: I look forward to the day when you. chant Hare Krishna in the Red Square.

It was a perfect evening, with Prabhupada working behind the scenes as Krishna’s amazing diplomat.

A few days earlier, some guests in Prabhupada’s room had volunteered to go to Vrindaban, just eighty miles south of Delhi, to scout accommodations for the Swami and his disciples. Now they had reported back, having arranged everything there. On November 25 evening Prabhupada wrote in his letter to Satsvarupa: “You will be pleased to note that I am taking a party of devotees to Vrindaban tomorrow for parikrama. We shall remain there for a few days.”

The Delhi pandal program had been an overwhelming success, attended by nearly a million people—another huge rhino for His Divine Grace. Over twelve thousand copies of the new Hindi BTGs had been distributed. Highlights of the pandal program appeared on TV news even in America and Europe. The Los Angeles Times printed this article on November 21, 1971:

U.S. KRISHNA CHANTERS

TELL IT TO THE HINDUS

NEW DELHI—The Hare Krishnas have put on an old-fashioned, Gita-thumping revival here in bustling Connaught Circus in an attempt to sink some roots in their spiritual motherland. Indians in general are skeptical of the Hare Krishnas. Many Indians think of them as American hippies in Hindu clothing. However, the Indians learned during the 10-day festival that the American gurus know their Vedic scriptures. Inside the vast multicolored tent a few days ago, a thin, elfish-looking young American in a saffron robe sat cross-legged on a stage and answered questions from about 400 Indian listeners ... And so it went for more than an hour. The Hare Krishna guru spoke with a New York City accent right off the East River, but he knew his Sanskrit and the Indians were no doubt impressed.

Prabhupada’s triumph at the pandal event in New Delhi was like completing a full circle: in the early ’60s, the Swami had struggled in Delhi alone and penniless to print the few volumes of an ancient scripture that he hoped, beyond reason, to someday bear with him to America; now, just a few years later, he had returned to India’s capital as acharya of a worldwide spiritual organization that distributed thousands of his books every day.

And now Prabhupada would take us to Vrindaban, Krishna’s home, the place where Bhaktivedanta Swami had hatched the Hare Krishna movement.

**\* \* \***

chintamani-prakara-sadmasu kalpa-vriksha-  
lakshavriteshu surabhir abhipalayantam  
lakshmi-sahasra-shata-sambhrama-sevyamanam  
govindam adi-purusam tam aham bhajami

I worship Govinda, the primeval Lord, the first progenitor, who is tending the cows, yielding all desires, in abodes built with spiritual gems and surrounded by millions of purpose trees. He is always served with great reverence and affection by hundreds of thousands of Lakshmis, or gopis.

Vrindavan! The place where Krishna lived five thousand years ago and, by all accounts, where you could still see Him if you have the eyes. Prabhupada had lived here himself and had been telling us about this magic village for the past five years.

In the very early morning of November 26, 1971, heading south from Delhi on Highway 2, Prabhupada pointed to a mile-long ancient stone wall in the distance—it looked maybe eighty or a hundred feet high, with turrets, arched gateways, slots on top for shooting arrows—and he said, “That is Indraprastha fort, built by the Pandava dynasty, where Krishna used to visit.”

What?

“Yes, it is still existing,” Prabhupada said.

There were four of us in the car, an Ambassador with a crazy driver whom we had to tell over and over, “Slow down! Bus! Behind!” He apparently didn’t speak English. “Wait for the bus behind us, you schmuck!” Gurudas yelled, and whacked him on the shoulder. The bus behind us held nearly forty devotees, and we were all on our way to see Vrindaban for the first time, with Srila Prabhupada as our master guide.

A few other devotees had been to Vrindaban over the years, but it was Jaya Govinda’s photos and description in the August 1969 Back. To Godhead that had captured my imagination:

The Supreme Lord is described as eternally youthful. He is not burdened by the maintenance of all the planets, but, by His omnipotence, He maintains them effortlessly and at the same time enjoys childlike sports as a cowherd boy, in eternal Vrindaban. He has many companions and lovers there, amid beautiful fields and surroundings described as “all-conscious.” That spiritual realm, the Supreme Lord Sri Krishna and all His eternal associates, the enactment of His eternal pastimes—all this is sometimes made manifest in the material world.

Thus the Vrindaban which we find as a small village in north central India today, and pictured on these pages, is in reality the very Vrindaban of the spiritual sky, the supreme abode of the Lord. At this location the Lord reveals His eternal pastimes, although He has not left that original locality in the spiritual sky. This is done by the Lord’s omnipotence, and it is as though a dimensional window has opened, making the spiritual realm visible to beings of this mundane world.

The BTG article included photos of temples, bathing ghats, giant banyans, and the ancient paths where Krishna walked, covered by the patina of centuries. And we were almost there! Prabhupada pointed and said, “Vrindavan is just in those trees.” Suddenly the driver pulled off the road, stopped the motor, and got out, opened the hood—and was engulfed in a cloud of steam. Prabhupada exchanged a few words in Hindi with him, then motioned for us to get out, this car has broken down. The bus pulled in behind us, and Prabhupada herded us aboard. So we entered Vrindavan all together in our giant chariot, coughing diesel smoke and resounding with loud kirtan, Srila Prabhupada and his forty pioneers, to explore the homes and hearts of Vrindavan and its legendary inhabitants.

The Delhi festival was finished, but there were matters to be watched carefully in Bombay and Mayapur—so we’d probably only stay in Vrindaban for three or four days. There was never much of a plan to these trips, usually just some vague arrangements. In this case, Srila Prabhupada, his immediate entourage, and the few female devotees with us would be housed at Saraf Bhavan, a kind of guesthouse for upper-class pilgrims; the rest of the devotees went to a dharmasala (hostel) nearby.

Right away Prabhupada took us sightseeing. Our first stop was the Radha-Damodar temple. The tiny courtyard and rooms where Swamiji had sat for so many years designing the Hare Krishna movement seemed disappointingly small and drab at first, but when we saw the love in Srila Prabhupada’s eyes and felt his connection with the place, we began to make the leap: look behind what you observe in Vrindaban, through the layers and deeper dimensions where faith will take you—and there find the holy of holies. Is that a flute I hear? How many ecstatic dawns did Rupa Goswami see creep into this courtyard? On how many hundreds of freezingsweltering nights did Prabhupada crouch by dim candlelight to extract our language from the Srimad-Bhagavatam? At Radha-Damodar we feel the pangs of Prabhupadas lonely meditations and the rising ironic chuckle in his throat as he writes, in 1958:

I am sitting alone in Vrndavana-dhama.

In this mood I am getting many realizations ...

Everyone has abandoned me, seeing me penniless-

Wife, relatives, friends, brothers, everyone.

This is misery, but it gives me a laugh. I sit alone and laugh...

Who will give me news of them, tell me who?

All that is left of this family life is a list of names.

As secretary, the cassette recorder and handheld mic are my best friends because they allow me to walk or sit beside Srila Prabhupada wherever he goes. My excuse is to record every word, as he points with his cane and says, “This is the samadhi of Jiva Goswami” or “Here is where Rupa Goswami wrote his books.” Hah! Gesturing with his right index finger poking through his saffron silk bead bag he looks fifty years younger at Brahmanda Ghat as he assumes the part of Krishna’s mother—his eyes big as saucers—and tells us: “Here Krishna used to eat dirt while playing, and His friend complained to Krishnas mother, ‘Oh, your son is eating dirt.’ ‘Krishna, You are eating dirt?’ ‘No, they are enemies; they are telling lies. This morning we had some quarrel, so they have become My enemies and are telling lies.’ \[laughter\] And they charged again, ‘No, He has taken dirt. He has taken. We have seen.’ ‘All right, open Your mouth. I want to see.’ And as soon as Krishna opened His mouth, she saw the whole universe within.”

Srila Prabhupada tells us the eternal stories of each place we pass, dead matter coming to life with his fluid animations. We are his kids being taken by their father to his childhood haunts, showing us where this or that actually occurred, from tales we’d heard by the fireplace and, before, could only imagine.

Saraswati s caught a shuddering cough from the cold nights and sleeping rough in Delhi, but she follows—or leads—Srila Prabhupada around Vrindaban, sometimes holding his hand.

We walk the golden mica sands that bank the Yamuna River, a straggling band of cheering white geese, fresh with the glow of youth and a lifetime of good food and education, led by our peerless Pied Piper. “This is where Krishna stole the gopis’ clothes,” he tells us. “And here is where Krishna killed the Keshi demon.” Dr. O.B.L. Kapoor joins us, too. He has been Prabhupada’s friend and godbrother since 1932. He is so intelligent, learned, gentle, and familiar, and we develop an immediate fondness for him.

Early on the second morning, fog burns off the Yamuna River in the first hazy sun. “Can we take bath here, Srila Prabhupada?” Prabhupada, sitting on the steps at Keshi Ghat next to his pal Dr. Kapoor watches our tentative entry into the freezing Yamuna water, then laughs as we splash and whoop and a few bold Americans swim out into the sacred current. Even the women overcome their shyness, strip to their slips, and jump in. Prabhupada squats on the steps, dhoti up around his knees, and smiles at Saraswati running back and forth at his feet. She badly wants to jump in the river, but she’s still coughing, so I tell her no. “Come on, Prabhupada, come on in!” He can’t resist any longer. He removes his marigold garlands, takes off his sweater, hands someone his watch, unbuttons and removes his kurta—and wades a few steps out until water is up to his waist. He pinches his nose, then—swppp!—ducks straight down and under the water. Splash! He reemerges straight-up, wiping water from his eyes. As Prabhupada dries off with a towel on the steps, we steal glances at his golden full-body beauty: he’s happy as a lark.

Then off in the bus to Govardhan for a swim in Bindu Sarovara pond, at the base of Govardhan Hill. Then on again, to Barsana, Radharani’s birthplace, where two men carry Srila Prabhupada up many concrete stairs to Her palace on a palanquin—“Make way! The Maharaja returns!” He’s laughing like a little kid.

Prabhupada: Yes, to ride on this palanquin is very comfortable, \[laughter\] at the cost of others, \[laughs\] \[indistinct yelling in background; mynah birds singing\]

Tamal Krishna: We should construct one, Prabhupada, for the remainder of the trip.

Prabhupada: It is better than any other vehicle. Formerly, kings were being carried by palanquin. They did not like carriage. Palanquin was transformed to carriage first in England—palanquin-carriage, drawn by horse, \[looking around\] Just see, how much fine work in marble stone. This is the spot—

Shyamasundar: This is the spot?

Prabhupada:—where Radharani was born.

Shyamasundar: Oh, wow. Right there?

And then over to Nandagram, Krishna’s hilltop childhood home:

Prabhupada: Nanda Maharaja left Gokula and came here because the demons were disturbing Krishna. They decided to change.

\[a local guide explains something about the temple in Hindi\]

Tamal Krishna: What did he say, Srila Prabhupada?

Prabhupada: \[to guide\] You explain in English. Explain in English.

\[curtain opens: obeisances\]

Guide: The statue, this one, with the turban, that is the deity of Nanda Maharaja. And on this side in yellow sari, that is Yasoda Mata. You see? And in between Yasoda Mata and Nanda Baba there are Krishna and Balarama, both brothers. Krishna is on the side of Yasoda Mata, and Balarama is on the side of Nanda Baba. And in that corner on the left-hand side you see Radharani, there in that corner. And in the extreme right, there are these two, they are friends of Lord Krishna—Sudama and Madhumangala.

Prabhupada: \[to Tarnal Krishna\] You just give them one rupee. Put here.

Leaving Nandagram, Prabhupada points at Radha’s Barsana palace in the distance and tells us how Radharani would sneak out on moonlit nights while Krishna climbed down from this side and They’d meet in the middle—“Just see, down there, in that forest between.”

As we tour the environs of Vrindaban, we are seeing and meeting the Vrajavasis, the citizens of Vraja, living cultural descendants from Krishna’s time, with their reverence, their lives patterned by love for Krishna, whose Deity stands in every home and in each of five thousand temples around town.

We chant and dance down Vrindaban’s ancient streets, barely wide enough for a bicycle rickshaw—ching chingl—as we step aside to avoid open sewers. Krishna’s name is echoed by the time-darkened walls. Hariboll shout the shopkeepers and pilgrims we meet. We see majestic temples packed between brick-and-stucco homes, walled compounds, arcades and verandas, pavilions and alcoves. Iron bars or carved stone screens protect windows from marauding, pink-faced macaque monkeys. A maze of plumbing runs down the sides of buildings and tangles of wire top every utility pole, where electricity pirates grab their volts. (One power company worker tells us that only about 60 percent of electricity in Vrindaban is actually paid for.) Everything seems run-down, deteriorated. Parts of town look almost abandoned. But from this day forward, Srila Prabhupada and his pack of angels begin to breathe new life into the holy precincts of Vrindaban.

**\* \* \***

Srila Prabhupada is in a negotiating mood. He returns to his room at Saraf Bhavan and, while his disciples continue sightseeing around

Vrindaban, Prabhupad greets a parade of officials, godbrothers, and elevated Vrajavasis, such as Krishnadas Babaji and Gourachand Goswami. In master salesman mode, Swamiji narrates to them his accomplishments in the West and asks the assembled luminaries for one thing only: land.

Mr. Kashiram Saraf and his wife, Geetabai, tell Prabhupada they have an empty four-acre property in Raman Reti and that they’re considering donating it to a deserving spiritual group. Raman Reti means “charming sands,” and it’s the place where Krishna used to play with His cowherd friends. Sounds great, so we go to look at it. Saraf Bhavan is across from the main post office in the center of Vrindaban, and the Raman Reti land is about one and a half miles away, out in rice paddies and undeveloped forest at the junction of Chhatikara Road and the parikrama path. It’s nice land, Gurudas and I agree, “But Prabhupada, it’s so far from town, and these people walk, everywhere. Who will come all the way out here?”

Nonetheless, Prabhupada likes this land and expresses his interest in it. But the offer is not firm, and Prabhupada doesn’t want to seem overanxious.

In Vrindaban, Srila Prabhupada is more relaxed and intimate than I have ever seen him: greeting and preaching (maybe bragging a bit) to old friends in his quarters or on the street, during meals and massages—yak, yak, yak in the pleasant winter sun. He is so elegant, graceful, friendly, carefree, wise, practical, and self-confident during this exhibition to his extended family in Vrindaban. What a contrast to Delhi, where he was the politician-king. Here, he is the old friend, the favorite uncle, the patriarch, the worshipable village elder. We have never seen this side of him, but we quickly understand it: Vrindaban dwellers are grounded in magic, they are accustomed to the miraculous, and they see Prabhupada and his disciples as the recurrence of an ancient sacred pastime.

Prabhupada charms, he cajoles, he argues from logic. It has been a natural progression for him: from native son to first worldwide preacher of Vaishnavism. The historic success of his mission is visible, and Vrindaban must make room for him. There is a lingering atmosphere of reserve and suspicion among the caste goswamis in Vrindaban—maybe a tinge of envy? “We have been content here for five thousand years. Our forefathers own this land and culture. Are these followers of yours genuine? Do they qualify to settle down among us? Look how they wander around like loosed gorillas. Will they raise holy hell?”

After two or three days, the obvious devotional demeanor of these fresh, overseas Krishna devotees—plus Srila Prabhupada’s keen arguments and diplomacy—wins out, and on the evening of November 29, 1971, Vrindaban’s mayor, city officials, and priests from the main temples gather to salute Srila Prabhupada in a very moving public ceremony. Mayor Vishvambhar Goswami, head sevaite of the Radha-Ramana temple, delivers “An Address of Welcome to the Most Exalted, Unlimitedly Decorated Sri Srimad Bhaktivedantaji Maharaja”:

О most revered Svamiji,

In this holy land of Vrndavana, we the inhabitants of Vrndavana, who are known as Vrajabasis, hereby welcome you and offer you this humble address.

О chief of understanding transcendental mellowness, We know that for many years you sat in the Radha-Damodara Temple and worshiped Her Majesty Srimati Radharani in a meditative mood, and thus you now have the transcendental vision to deliver the entire world. As proof of your perfection, we can see these foreign devotees before us, and we feel very proud to see how you have transformed them into such pure devotees.

О great preacher of Vedic culture,

Formerly, a great many svamis went to foreign countries, but now you have wonderfully preached the sankirtan movement and the sublime philosophy of the bhakti cult Jn the Western countries, and that is the only means for giving peace and prosperity to all people of the world in this age. For preaching religion and culture, your holy name will remain ever dazzling.

О most revered Svamiji,

To speak frankly, we feel a very intimate relationship with you, and we feel perfect satisfaction at this time to be privileged to present to you this address of welcome. We are certainly sure that through your preaching alone the transcendental message of Vrindabana will spread all over the world. May you be crowned with success in these noble activities.

We beg to remain Yours most obediently,

Chairman of the Vrndavana Municipality, Its Members and Secretaries, and the Citizens of Vrindaban-dhama.

The next day, Prabhupada writes a formal letter to the Vrindaban municipal authorities:

I am so much obliged to the devotee-residents of Vrindaban for their giving me the civic reception. We want to open a centre of magnificent proportions in Vrindaban City, as was discussed with Your Honor by my secretary, Sriman Ksirodakasayi das Adhikari. I therefore request you to give us the one-acre piece of land near the Dak Bungalow for this purpose.

Dak Bungalow is near the center of Vrindaban, so Prabhupada is trying for this municipal land, maybe to appease our argument for a more central temple, but he keeps his options open.

Most of the devotees return to Delhi by bus on the morning of December 1. Prabhupada postpones his own return in order to spend one night in his old rooms at the Radha-Damodar temple, thus fulfilling the legal terms of his residency there. He’s been negotiating with Gourachand Goswami, hereditary head priest at Radha-Damodar, to retain these rooms, but Gourachand is disputing the terms and rental fee. Prabhupada threatens to sue him, and his possession of rooms at Radha-Damodar temple remains another unresolved issue. Prabhupada appoints Kshirodakshayi to remain in Vrindaban to continue negotiations with Gourachand and the Sarafs.

Kshirodakshayi, formerly Kedar Nath Gupta, is married with three sons. He owns Friendly Store, a small Indian grocery at 85 Dudden Hill Lane in Northwest London. He had met the London devotees in early 1969, and he and his entire family had become Prabhupada’s first initiated British Indian disciples. Now he’s in Vrindaban, having left the store in his family’s care and come to India to take charge of the Hindi edition of BTG and to assist in opening centers in Delhi and Vrindaban. But Kshirodakshayi has trouble working with Gurudas and Western devotees in general, so Prabhupada guides him in a personal, separate way. Kshirodakshayi gets things done behind the scenes, working with his fellow Indians.

It’s far from a done deal, but a large and permanent ISKCON presence in Vrindaban is inevitable. It will happen; there will be land and a great temple built. It will be a struggle all the way—thousands of years of entrenched interests and the untested abilities of young Westerners ensures there will be hardship, but it will happen.

The pieces are beginning to fall together: Surat, then Kumbha Mela, now Vrindaban. Prabhupada’s descriptions of India are no longer dreams for us; our movements ancient Indian roots are real. The Radha-Damodar temple, the samadhis of the Six Goswamis, the sparkling mica sands of Krishnas pastimes—we’re seeing them, feeling them—and were right at home. Vrindaban belongs to us, too.

On the eve of his departure for Delhi, Prabhupada writes to Rupanuga in New York:

The officials and residents of Vrindaban have greeted us very nicely, and they are simply astounded to see our samkirtan party chanting with great jubilation through the city streets. The Mayor has publicly proclaimed that I have done something wonderful, and practically speaking, they realize that before I went to the western countries no one there knew about Vrindaban. Now hundreds of visitors and hippies from your country will come here to see Krishnas place. The Vrindaban devotees have understood that Vrindaban is now world-famous due to my preaching work, so they are all very much appreciating their hometown Swamiji.

I also write—composing my regular newsletter describing Prabhupada’s latest activities for devotees around the world—and mail it off to Karandhar in L.A.:

And then, in late November you really did it—all on for the magical mystery tour! You put us on a cluttered bus, squeezed in with us to point the way—He’s coming to take you away, take you ... and dropped us HERE: THE ROOMS at Radha-Damodar, just as if you left them yesterday, where the seed of Hare Krishna movement was planted, nourished and sprang full-blossomed from your head—through your pen, through your legs, through your heart, through your (Krishna! what a) beautiful mouth—across a million miles and a thousand ages—to our eager eyes and ears ... HERE: We feel the pangs of your lonely meditations, and weep with joy at the wellspring of your inspiration.

For six action-packed days you danced with us through the long-silent byways of Vrindaban town, awoke the Vrajavasis with thunderous sankirtan, delighted in showers of rose petals and ululations and Haribol’s! and humbly accepted their headmans welcoming speech: “We are certainly sure that through your preaching alone the transcendental message of Vrindaban will spread all over the world... Your holy name will remain ever dazzling.”

Richard Slavin was in Vrindaban too. We had last seen him at the Cross Maidan festival in Bombay eight months earlier. Since then, he had shaved off his matted dreadlocks and he was glowing and happy to see us. He had been sleeping rough on the banks of the Yamuna for about six months, still looking for the perfect guru. Richie, as we called him, hung out with us every day and asked a hundred questions, like, “Why has the Swami brought you guys to India?” and “What’s Prabhupada really like?” As Prabhupada was leaving Vrindaban Richie said to him, “Srila Prabhupada, I wish to offer you my life.” Prabhupada replied, “Yes, you are home.” Soon, Richard Slavin would become Radhanath das and, ultimately, Radhanath Swami.

Radhanath Swami: At that time I was with Prabhupada every day from mangala-arati through the evening. I understood then that this is the goal of life, to have this type of love with Vaishnavas in sharing the mission of the spiritual master and pure love for Radha and Krishna... Never before in the history of Vrindaban did people from all over the world, from outside of India, come in a spirit of devotion to worship Sri Sri Radha and Krishna.

**\* \* \***

The Ambassador car we’d arrived in had been repaired and, without incident, we raced the eighty miles back to Delhi in late afternoon December 2 to beat the dusk-to-dawn citywide blackout. We reunited with the other devotees at the Birla Temple ashram on Mandir Marg, in the heart of downtown New Delhi.

On March 26, 1971, eight months earlier, during our Cross Maidan festival in Bombay, East Pakistan had seceded from West Pakistan and declared itself the sovereign nation of Bangladesh. On March 27 Indira Gandhi had pledged her governments full support to the Bangladeshi rebels’ struggle for independence. By the beginning of December, millions of refugees were pouring into India, placing an intolerable stress on Indias already overburdened economy. The drums of battle were now beating in Delhi: war between India and Pakistan was inevitable.

Tensions were high. Nixon was backing Pakistan; Brezhnev was supporting India. Many Delhi residents thought we were CIA agents. But we carried on chanting, dancing, and spreading Krishna’s message without a care in the world.

On the morning of December 3, Gurudas takes us (by Ambassador car) to visit Mr. Kenneth Keating, US Ambassador to India, at his office in the vast American Embassy on Panchsheel Marg. His wife, Anne, is there too, and the Keatings are super-nice people, informal but respectful, and they hit it off with Prabhupada right away.

Prabhupada: Spirit soul is individual. He is passing through the evolutionary process. Evolutionary process means as he is desiring, and nature is supplying a particular type of body.

Mrs. Keating: That’s very interesting. I believe in the transmigration of the soul.

Prabhupada: Yes. It is a fact.

Kenneth Keating: What is your definition of God?

Prabhupada: The definition of God—first of all, God is the root of everything, the source of everything. Now you take anything and find out where is the original cause, then you come to God. Take anything on this table. Your self, your body, everything you take—if you go on searching, searching, searching, what is, where is the origin, then you come to God. That is the perfect definition of God. Janmady asya yatah: the origin or source of everything. What do you think?

Kenneth Keating: Oh, I’m very impressed.

Prabhupada presents them with a copy of Krsna book, and they marvel at the paintings inside. They joke with shy Saraswati, and Prabhupada tells them about his American disciples—the Vietnam vets, sons of rich men, college grads, hippies disillusioned with the American way of life. The Keatings are struck by the devotees’ sincerity and they want to know more.

Prabhupada: You are a small sample of God. Just like you take a drop of ocean water and you taste it, then you can understand immediately the whole ocean is salty. Similarly, if you analyze your characteristic, then the same characteristic is there in God. Just like you want to love someone. Everyone wants to love someone. Therefore it can be concluded that God has got the propensity to love.

Mrs. Keating: Yes. God is love.

Prabhupada: Yes. Krishna means love, so when you want to love somebody and somebody wants to love you, the both of us must be a person.

Mrs. Keating: Both must be?

Prabhupada: Person. You don’t love the air. You love your husband. He is a person. And he loves you. You are a person. Therefore God must be person. This is the conclusion. Because I am person, I am a sample of God, so God must be person. What do you think? Without God being person, how we can reciprocate love?

Half an hour later, Keating announces that another ambassador (from Pakistan? Bangladesh?) is waiting to see him, so he must “move from the sublime to the mundane.”

“I appreciate very much your coming in,” he says to Prabhupada, and Prabhupada, not missing a beat, replies, “Now, I am also coming to the mundane point of view. The next time I go back to USA, I wish to see the president, if you can help me?”

“Well I, I can write a letter...”

That very evening, December 3, 1971, Pakistan’s air force attacked eight Indian airports, including the base at nearby Agra. In a broadcast to the nation Prime Minister Indira Gandhi declared war. “We must be prepared for a long period of hardship and sacrifice,” she said, and by midnight India launched integrated ground, sea, and air strikes in both West and East Pakistan. Kenneth Keating got very busy very fast trying to broker a peace.

Prabhupada exposed the insidious economics imposed by superpowers on third-world countries such as India to guests in his room at Birla Ashram:

These big nations, they do not leave the control. They sell things, and they catch some leaders, who take loan from them, then they take it: 80 percent for themselves and 20 percent for you.... In India also, that is being done. America is loaning, and those who are in charge of the government, they’re purchasing weapons from them. These Britishers and Americans, they’re constantly engaging Pakistan in fighting spirit and supplying them ammunition. Their business is going on, taking loan from them and paying to them, and they remain debtor perpetually.

One guest who came to see Prabhupada was Dr. Lokesh Chandra. Dr. Chandra was a prominent and widely traveled Indian scholar. He had introduced Gurudas and Yamuna to Indira Gandhi some months before. His studies in Indology demonstrated that in ancient times Vedic culture had spread from India into all parts of Asia and Europe, and he had collected artifacts proving that many old cultures were Vedic-based. These artifacts were displayed in his museum, the International Academy of Indian Culture, in Hauz Khas, a leafy southern suburb of Delhi. Prabhupada visited the Academy on December 4, and Dr. Chandra was excited to guide him through its many displays.

Dr. Chandra: These are the gods and goddesses found in monasteries in Japan. This is Aditya, Skanda, Saraswati, hmm? This is Narayana, Vishnu, Ganesh. There are twelve thousand monasteries of Mantrayana in Japan, and in them you will find Sanskrit texts and Indian gods and goddesses.

Prabhupada: That means \[turning to his disciples\]-I told you several times—this Aryan culture was all over the world.

Srila Prabhupada had been telling us for years about Bharat-varsha, the ancient, worldwide kingdom of India. He was constantly citing evidence from Srimad-Bhagavatam how various tribes had left India and settled in Europe and Asia—the Huns to Hungary, the worshipers of Skanda to Scandinavia (the Sanskrit navya means “accessible by boat, navigable, navy, sailor”)—and how through language you could trace words in Latin, German, English, even Chinese, back to their Sanskrit roots. The Greek word kristos, he said, means “the anointed one” and is a cognate of the Sanskrit krishna. Seeing Dr. Chandra’s collection was yet more proof of Prabhupada’s all-encompassing knowledge.

Dr. Chandra next led us through an extraordinary exhibition of Ramayana artifacts from Indonesia and Burma, amply establishing this ancient Vedic epic in these cultures. As we strolled around the place, Prabhupada was very impressed. Dr. Chandra then described the intricate political maneuvers that had led to Ms. Gandhi helping the Hare Krishna people obtain long-term visas:

Prabhupada: This museum—government is not helping?

Dr. Chandra: No. The government wants to help, but I do not want their help—no interference. The Prime Minister would very much like to help me \[laughs\] for political reasons. My father was president of the Jana Sangh \[Indian political party\].

Prabhupada: I see.

Dr. Chandra: So I came over to her side in December last, and she was very insistent, so I had to come. So she wants to help me, but I have told her I want a political position and not money. \[Pointing to some Balinese statues\] This is Vishnu, and that is Krishna with a bow and arrow. They \[ISKCON devotees\] have been to Mrs. Gandhi with me \[laughs\]. And they could stay in India only on the intervention of Madam Gandhi, not otherwise. Because the Maharashtra government had recommended that they should be turned out. The Home Secretary—once he met me at a dinner and related the whole story. So he went to the Prime Minister and asked her what to do. She said, “All right, tell them that they should observe time and allow them to stay.”

Prabhupada: Ha.

\[Two years later Dr. Chandra would become a Member of Parliament, a position he held for twelve years.\]

On our return trip to the Birla Ashram, we saw the sights: the Houses of Parliament, the President’s palace, grand government buildings, temples—and we talked about construction, marble, cement, and sandstone. We passed a man driving a motor scooter, his wife seated behind, and Prabhupada said, “This is the fashion. Take the wife to the backside, and as soon as the sari is entangled in the spokes \[laughs\]... Very dangerous.”

The war with Pakistan was rapidly escalating. The next day, we shifted en masse from Birla Ashram to a dharmasala in Kamala Nagar, a neighborhood in Old Delhi five miles from the government sector and out of the firing line—less visible to suspicious eyes. The dharmasala was a block of plain concrete buildings around three sides of a central square, behind a high wall and solid iron gates. We occupied rooms in different buildings and met in a larger, common room for kirtan and worship, where Prabhupada lectured to us morning and evening while we sat out the war. Two armed soldiers stood outside our gates. We were free to come and go, but someone was watching our every move. There were even occasional demonstrations outside the gates, with locals shouting Yankee Go Home!-type slogans. But we ignored them and went about our business. On December 8, Prabhupada wrote to Hamsaduta in Germany: “Due to war in India, our programs here have been reduced, and there is every night blackout, so our future plans here are not settled up at present.”

Along with war and misgivings that we were CIA, another major incident interrupted and colored our lives. On December 9, India’s famous actor-director Dev Anand released his film Hare Rama, Hare Krishna. It quickly became the most watched movie in the country. The film depicts the Hare Krishnas as Western hippies who smoke hashish and don Hindu robes to escape the cultural turmoil in their own countries. Overnight, Asha Bosle’s single record from the film, Dum Maro Dum became the #1 song on the Indian Hit Parade. The chorus Dum Maro Dum, Hare Krishna, Hare Ram, (“Take another puff of hash and chant Hare Krishna”) now greeted us wherever we went. A second song from the film has Dev Anand singing Ram Ka Naam Badnaam Ha Karo, or “Don’t give Rama a bad name.”

When we brought this news to Prabhupada, he said, “No publicity is bad publicity. At least they are chanting Hare Krishna.” \[Both the film Hare Rama, Hare Krishna and the song Dum Maro Dum stayed at #1 until May of 1972.\]

**\* \* \***

Prabhupada took morning strolls around the old-city neighborhood, and most citizens greeted him warmly. As long as we were with Prabhupada, we were safe. We bought newspapers and followed tank and infantry battles at Chhamb, Basantar, Hilli, Longewala. We saw the growing certainty of India’s victory. Prabhupada spoke from Srimad-Bhagavatam in the common room morning and evening, and these sessions became more like conversations, discussions about war and peace, death and the science of changing bodies.

Tamal Krishna: Material scientists may argue that “We can see that Saraswati is changing from the time she was a little girl, and now she is a little older, we have seen her both times, but at the time of death we have not seen the next body of anyone.”

Prabhupada: But why do you believe so much your rascal eyes?

That is the answer. Do you think that your eyes are perfect? There are many types of seeing. Not that simply with glaring eyesight you can see. You can see what is Saraswati. You are seeing the body. What is Saraswati? Do you know? So what you are seeing? You are seeing the body. So what is the power of your seeing? There is another body, sukhavati, subtle body. Can you see the mind? But everyone has got mind. Can you see intelligence? But everyone has got intelligence. So what is the power of your seeing? Why you are so much proud of your seeing, your nonsense seeing?

He also spoke about personal spiritual advancement:

Devotee: But if you have that material desire, does that slow up your advancement?

Prabhupada: Krishna is so kind, because you desire, He’ll give you. “Oh, you wanted this? All right, you take!” That is the benefit of Krishna consciousness. You get both these things, material and spiritual. But then you come to the point, “For this paltry happiness I requested Krishna. What a fool I was.” Therefore Dhruva Maharaja regretted that “I was such a fool that I came to ask Krishna for my father’s property. What a nonsense I was.” You will get—whatever you want—but once you have got Krishna, your all other desires will vanish. Krishna is so kind.

Devotee: The spiritual master, his duty is to take all of his disciples back to home, back to Godhead. Is he responsible for all of these people who are attempting to serve him?

Prabhupada: That is nice question. You have to please your spiritual master. If you please him, then he is responsible. But yasya prasadan na gatih kuto ’pi: if you displease your spiritual master, then you are nowhere. How do you adjust these things? So, if you take it in this way, that my spiritual master has taken responsibility, so whatever nonsense I do, it doesn’t matter... But that is not the fact. If one takes responsibility for you, you must abide by his order. It is reciprocal. If you please your spiritual master, then God will be pleased. If you don’t please him, then you are nowhere.

The dusk-to-dawn citywide curfew and blackout was still in effect in the nation’s capital. Prabhupada’s room is on the second floor facing the street, so Yamuna and Kaushalya cut out squares of black paper to tape over his windows. “Ah ha,” Prabhupada says, “This is first-class intelligence.” He goes on to explain, “First-class intelligence means you see a need and do it. Second-class intelligence is I tell you and you do it nicely. Third-class means I tell you and you run out and come back and say, ‘What did you say?”’

Each night, Prabhupada and I meet behind those blacked-out windows and converse by candlelight and catch up on letters. On December 10, Prabhupada writes to Bhagavan in Detroit:

Yes, actually there is now a skyscraper framework \[ISKCON\]. Now you have to decorate and cover it nicely. To construct the form is the difficult portion of the adventure, but when it is there, it is not difficult to finish it—simply it requires a little taste. I am always thankful to Krishna that I have somehow got so many wonderful boys like yourself to assist me in this way. Now it is practically in your hands to finish and push on what I have started. I am now getting all respect and honor, so now you must preserve that standard and not dishonor me. Thank you very much for helping me.

And to Rupanuga in New York:

Regarding the philosophy book, some tapes were lost and we have to do them over again. And due to our moving about the work has slowed down. Shyamasundar informs me that each tape will require a lot of editing work, and that all of the tapes must be finished before editing can be completed, because each philosopher must be seen in his relationship with all of the others. As soon as this work is finished I shall let you know.

Besides the philosophy tapes lost en route from Africa to India, I discovered that several sessions had been partially or completely recorded over, erased, in my haste to catch everything Prabhupada was saying. I wrote to Karandhar, asking him to send as many blank cassettes as he could by mail or, better yet, with devotees traveling to India. No matter how many he sends, there was always a chronic shortage. Labeling the cassettes properly was a nightmare, too. Prabhupada was moving around so much that I was always hurriedly throwing a few cassettes into my book bag, hoping there would be enough blank space on one of them to catch his next random nectarine pronouncements. If I set a cassette down in a public place, it was like dropping a hundred-rupee note: someone would steal it. Some of the tapes were damaged by heat, or the spools got jammed when I rewound them, and many tapes were spoiled. So I decided to type all night to transcribe each day’s tapes and thus preserve precious space for the next day’s talks.

In addition to the shortage of cassette tapes, there was a constant battle to find AA batteries. I had a few rechargeables, but, with India’s fluctuating voltage and frequent power outages, charging them was a constant challenge. Shipping completed tapes abroad meant long hours at the main post office, filling out forms and worrying about theft. So I held onto tapes, hoping large packages had more certainty to arrive than small bundles. I shipped these packages of tapes to Karandhar in L.A. or to Jayadvaita in NYC. Or I gave them to reliable devotees returning to America.

None of us had anticipated just how much Prabhupada could talk—and how important it was to catch his every word. I often had to make judgment calls: will Prabhupada’s description of Krishna stealing the gopis’ clothes (live, from the banks of the Yamuna River) be more important than his morning lecture on a verse from Srimad-Bhagavatam? Looking back, it’s a wonder we managed to record as much as we did.

During the long inactive days of war, Prabhupada and I went back to work on the philosophy book. We started with the Existentialists.

Shyamasundar: Soren Kierkegaard says that existence is prior to essence. In other words, that by existing we come to our essence. We realize ourselves by understanding the experience of our existence.

Prabhupada: Yes. That is our theory, that we are struggling or transmigrating from different species of life, and when we come to the perfectional stage of living condition, human form of life, so then we understand what is the aim of life. So as spirit soul I am existing, and then, at my perfectional stage, I learn what is the essence of life. Essence of life is Krishna consciousness. Therefore existence is first, and then to understand the essence.

Shyamasundar: Kierkegaard’s idea is that passion is truth.

Prabhupada: That’s all right. Passion means activities. But where your activity will end? What is the purpose of the activity? You start your car, but if you do not know where to go, then what is the use of starting your car? Simply spoiling your energy and spoiling the petrol. “I do not know where I am going, that doesn’t matter. Anyway, I start my car, never mind if I meet with accident.” Is that a very good proposition?

Shyamasundar: This is the philosophy of existentialism: it is not what is done or the purpose behind the doing, but how it is done.

Prabhupada: Unless you know the goal, the fixed-up point, then your energy may be misused. The passion, the energy, will be misguided. Why not take to Krishna consciousness immediately, no in-between existence? That is perfectional stage. Why should he speculate and be misguided by your so-called, existentional person?

Shyamasundar: Jean-Paul Sartre says that “Material in itself is nonconscious, inert, fixed, opaque, uncreated, devoid of potency, lacking becoming, and without any reason for existing; therefore it is superfluous.” In other words, existence doesn’t have any meaning.

Prabhupada: This kind of philosophy is stated in the Bhagavadgita as asuric philosophy, demonic philosophy, because the demons, they do not believe in any superior cause. They take everything as accidental. There is no actual purpose. So they have no brain to go farther. So what they superficially see, they take it. They do not find out the farther cause. Many modern scientists also explain that simply “It is nature, nature.” But Nature is not independent. Nature is phenomena; but the noumena is God, Krishna.

Shyamasundar: Sartre’s idea is that because a man or a living entity has no “thingness,” he is always changing one thing to another. Therefore life, the living entity, is no-thingness, nothingness.

Prabhupada: No. He has his identity; he is the actual thing. But in the present circumstances, because he is conditioned by matter, therefore he is changing, and when he becomes free from this condition he will have no change. He is actual fact, so he cannot be nothing.

Shyamasundar: Sartre says that the structure of man’s essence, his consciousness, is freedom. Man is continually free to change as he chooses. He says that we are “condemned to be free.”

Prabhupada: Yes. Anyone who is in this material world, he is condemned. But the next question will be, If one is condemned, then he can be blessed also. The other side of condemnation is blessing. So he does not know what is the blessing side. That he takes as nothing. That is nonsense.

Shyamasundar: Sartre says that we cannot escape this situation of freedom and that we are therefore responsible for our activities. Everyone is free to determine what is his future.

Prabhupada: But you have put yourself under the control of material nature; therefore, according to the modes of the material nature, your position is there, you cannot control. Just like you have freedom to move, but if you are thrown into the ocean, your freedom is choked up. Therefore your duty is how to get yourself released from the condition where your freedom is choked up.

Shyamasundar: His idea is that because we have to choose for ourselves, everything is in our hands. In every situation of life we can be either a coward or a hero. Therefore we must choose to be a hero.

Prabhupada: That heroism is just like insect heroism. There is blazing fire, and all kinds of ants and insects are falling in. One insect is very heroically following on the path: Put, put, put, put. That means if you go to hell you must go like a hero. So what is the use of such heroism? This philosophy has been taken up by the hippies.

The philosophy discussions often carried over into Prabhupadas letters, and on December 12, Prabhupada wrote to Danavir, in Portland:

Also it is nice that you are infiltrating into the schools and colleges. These are the best customers for our philosophy. Give them nice philosophy, let them challenge us with any mundane philosophy and we shall very scholarly defeat them. The difference is that we have got absolute authority from the Source of Knowledge, Krishna, while your western mundane philosophers are simply speculating on the mental platform, which is always changing. Therefore, a philosopher is not a philosopher unless he refutes his predecessor and produces something new. This kind of knowledge is useless. Actually, no one has got any philosophy nowadays, everyone is acting according to his own whims. Therefore all the young boys and girls in your country—and all over the world—are fed up with this lack of philosophy and they have taken to the philosophy of hopelessness, this existentional: Everything is empty, therefore let me enjoy. Actually, philosophy means practical application—if it is mere theory then it has no value. Krishna philosophy has worked in the same way for the last 5,000 years at least, taking historical calculation. So which philosophy is better, 50- or 100-year old Darwin philosophy, or eternal Krishna philosophy?

On December 16, thirteen days after the war began, Pakistan sued for peace with India, and the war ended. That afternoon we pulled up stakes and flew to Bombay.

**\* \* \***

The first thing Prabhupada did in Bombay was to issue a press release. On December 17, the Times of India printed the following article by a staff reporter:

AMERICANS DON’T BACK PRESIDENT NIXON

Swami Bhaktivedanta of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness stated in Bombay on Wednesday: “It is regretted that due to President Nixons anti-Indian feeling, some of our Indian brothers are developing an anti-American feeling.” He said that all his students were Vaishnavas and had nothing to do with politics. “Besides, it should be noted, as we are getting information that the American public is not in accord of President Nixon against India,” he added.

After two weeks of dark, cold, wartime sequestration, the warm winter sunshine at Akash Ganga and the busy commercial life of Bombay were a welcome relief. Srila Prabhupada hit the ground running. The seventh floor at Akash Ganga was already overflowing, and when Prabhupada came to town, dozens of visitors appeared at our door at all hours of the day and night, creating a circuslike atmosphere at the posh Bombay address. The landlord, Mr. Billimoria (Prabhupada called him “Botliwala”), was litigating to get us out of there. ISKCON had to vacate soon. (At least now, though, the Pakistan Consul General on the floor below us was closed, and there were no more riots in the parking lot.)

Mornings, we carried on with the Existentialists.

Shyamasundar: Martin Heidegger says that we understand our being through anxiety.

Prabhupada: Yes. Just like we have got our own land in Mayapur, so we can go and live there. There is no question of Botliwala. But we won’t go there. We have accepted this apartment. Therefore we must change it. Is it not? We are paying three thousand rupees. Still anxiety, because we know at any moment he can be a trouble and kick us out. So asad-grahat: it is not your place you have accepted. Similarly, because you have accepted this material body, then there must be anxiety.

**\* \* \***

Srila Prabhupada sat in his room and took stock of recent events, replied to a flood of letters that had caught up to him, and began composing and conducting the prelude, the overture to his Indian symphony, in allegro—bright and fast—putting shape to his plan for three huge temples: one in Bombay, one in Vrindavan, and one in Mayapur.

Over the past few months, Tushta Krishna and his wife, Krishna Tulasi devi, in the course of making Life Members, have befriended a Mr. A. B. Nair and his wife, Chandrabai. Nair was an important man in Bombay. He was editor-in-chief of the Free Press Journal, one of Bombay’s largest English-language newspapers. He was also a politician and owned much land around the city. Among his holdings was a five-acre parcel in Juhu, a swank suburb north of Bombay, a couple of blocks from Juhu Beach. In November, Mr. Nair had casually offered this Juhu land to ISKCON for a nominal price. Srila Prabhupada knew the land—it was adjacent to Sumati Morarjee’s Scindia Colony grounds, where Prabhupada had sometimes stayed before leaving for America in

1965\. The first thing Prabhupada wanted to do in Bombay was to meet Mr. Nair, inspect the land, and strike a deal.

On December 21, Prabhupada and three devotees rendezvoused with Nair at Juhu and walked the property. It seemed so far away to me—about an hour’s drive from downtown Bombay. We motored north from Akash Ganga through crowded ghettos reeking of fish, and then the homes got nicer, enclosed by compound walls, interspersed with rice paddies and coconut groves. We passed the old Juhu Aerodrome and entered the holiday enclave of Juhu. There were a couple of new resort hotels, green and peaceful, but we were far from the city center.

The Juhu land is spacious and thickly covered in tall coconut palms, but dominating the property is a three-story apartment complex, several buildings filled with tenants. OK, there’s plenty of room to build around them, and this is India—you sort of take what you get here and learn to live with it.
