There is an open forty-foot well in the center of the property, and people walking down two flights of cement stairs to fetch fresh water from a pool. Laundry is drying all over the place. The land has a rural yet big-city feel to it: kids playing cricket, Hindi pop music from a radio somewhere, occasional taxi horns outside the compound—but birds singing, possible cobras, sea breezes—and watch out for falling coconuts.

Srila Prabhupada inspects the grounds in a serious but light, holiday mood, disguising his pleasure at what he was seeing, bantering with his disciples and Mr. Nair, who points to an apartment and says, “Mahatma Gandhi used to stay here.” Prabhupada is the suave strategist, wheels in his head churning out points for negotiation. Mr. Nair is obviously pleased to show us the place-his mind-wheels cranking out how to rip off this deep-pockets Swami and his American disciples, the sons of bankers and millionaires.

After the tour, we go to the Nair’s house in Theosophical Colony, about a mile back down Juhu Tara Road toward Bombay, for lunch and negotiations. Prabhupada compliments the food, tells a few jokes, and then settles down to business.

Here’s what they worked out: the total price will be Rs. 1,400,000, with Rs. 200,000 down and the balance over time. Prabhupada then negotiated how the down payment would be made: Rs. 50,000 now and three more payments of 50,000 to follow. With the second payment, we could move onto the land. Nair’s solicitors would draw up the transfer documents. The property was in Mrs. Chandrabhai Nair’s name, so she would have to sign everything on her husband’s behalf.

On December 22, Prabhupada wrote to Yamuna in Vrindaban:

Here in Bombay we have got good prospects to purchase very large land in Juhu for even a cheap price, just in the middle of a neighborhood. We shall build our camp there & begin constructing temple immediately, & later on we shall develop large hotel & school.. So in general we shall make our headquarters in Bombay, & build up Vrindaban and Mayapur, that’s all. I will take care of everything, you just give me two life-members daily in India, & I shall do the rest.

Prabhupada then fired off letters to Gargamuni and Kshirodakshayi in Vrindaban, urging them to acquire land in Vrindaban at once and to finalize the contract for his rooms at Radha-Damodar. Hurry, hurry, hurry. Then he wrote to Jayapataka in Calcutta, expressing his strong desire to hold a “grand festival” in Mayapur “from middle of February to first March,” just two months from now. He wanted cottages built in Mayapur by then, and a pandal. There had recently been a robbery at our Mayapur camp, so Prabhupada added, “If you want to keep gun, I have no objection.”

On December 22,1 wrote to Jayadvaita, Prabhupada’s editor in New York, describing the frustrations and delays that complicate my efforts to record Prabhupada’s words:

Shortly after entering India of course the Uher blew up and cannot be fixed here. So for recording all translating and lectures, etc., I have switched to common cassette tape In a few days I’ll be sending you registered to the hilt all the existing reel-type tapes we have of Prabhupadas words (including Bhagavatam translations) up to the breakdown of Uher, plus I’ll send all cassette-type tapes as well. On the cassettes, as I never know until the moment Prabhupada opens his lotus mouth if he’ll speak in English, Bengali, or Hindi, there are sometimes mixed languages on the same tape. So kindly retape all the Hindi speeches onto single tapes and reship to me for turning over to our Hindi BTG crew here in India. On each tape I’ll try to catalogue as much pertinent information as possible about individual speeches, etc. Because cassettes are scarce in India, some lectures are sandwiched in and around other items, so will leave it to you to sort out. As for the philosophy tapes, most of the good stuff was lost, including Darwin, some biggies, and the rest I am slowly transcribing and editing here, each in the light of his predecessors and followers. These philosophy book sessions are extraordinarily long—each more than 1 hour—so must be pared down to preserve the essence, and that essence should be clear and nonrepetitive for practical use as an all-attractive textbook for truth... Today we finished Wittgenstein.

**\* \* \***

From the seventh floor of a skyscraper in downtown Bombay, with planes flying overhead and traffic roaring in the street below, I sat with the small wise Indian sage seated before me and discussed the philosophies of Austrian and German professors who lived in the early-twentieth-century world of war and tumult, trying to make sense of it all.

Shyamasundar: Wittgenstein proposes that metaphysical or mystical ideas, even though they are not expressed in words, can be felt or appreciated without knowing whether or not they are true. For example, when you say, “Everything is Brahman,” are you also willing to include another set of propositions which show how to experience Brahman—how one can experience this fact, “Everything is Brahman”?

Prabhupada: That is not very difficult. Just like this International Society. Originally I started, so in any center I am there. My photograph is there, I am there, accepting Bhaktivedanta Swami. So personally I am not there, but I still am there by my expansion of energy. So similarly, Krishna is the original Brahman. Whatever we see, we perceive, experience—it’s all Krishna’s expansion of energy. Indirect perception is there. Just like we accept that everything has got some cause. So I am a person; the cause is my personal father, and his father is also person. Similarly, the ultimate father, the original father, although I have not seen, I cannot sense-perceive, still, I must conclude that He is a person.

Shyamasundar: But that statement, “It can be seen that my father had a father had a father,” can be seen to be true.

Prabhupada: No. That is not seen; that is perceived. Perceived. Anubhuta. In Sanskrit, anubhuta. I can think of my father having father. That is perception. Even if it cannot be shown, it is a fact. Not that because it cannot be shown, it is not a fact.

Shyamasundar: Today we are discussing one German philosopher named Edmund Husserl, who started a school of philosophy known as phenomenology. The definition of phenomenology is “a descriptive analysis of inner experience or subjective processes, or the intuitive study of essences.” Their method begins with the things themselves—they say “to the things themselves”—or in other words, they begin from phenomenon.

Prabhupada: Just like one can analyze in this way: I am sitting on this comfortable seat cushion. Cushion is a thing. Why I am sitting here? Because it is giving comfort to my body. Then I come to the study of body. Why I am maintaining this body? Because I am the soul; I am living in this body. Then ultimately I love my self, my soul. I love this seat because it gives shelter to my body. I love this body because it gives shelter to my soul. I love this soul because it is part and parcel of Krishna. Therefore ultimately I love Krishna, is it not? That is pure consciousness.

Shyamasundar: Husserl says that the phenomena, or the things, are the ways or manifestations in which objects present themselves through their appearances. For example, a “picture.” “Picture” is a permanent, changeless idea. Even it may have many appearances that come and go, but the idea of “picture” is permanent or changeless, is it not?

Prabhupada: Yes. That is confirming our theory of spiritual world as permanent. Just like sometimes there are dolls, show dolls; that is phenomena. But the idea behind the dolls—that is permanent. A beautiful girl is not phenomenon; that is fact. This is a crude example. Similarly, this material world is phenomenon. That is explained by Sridhar Swami that because the spiritual is true, fact, therefore the phenomenal expression of the spiritual world amidst matter appears to be true. This material world, phenomenal world, is not fact, but because it is representation of a fact, therefore it appears as fact. That is phenomenology.

After the session, I say to Prabhupada, “That’s it. Husserl was the last contemporary philosopher. We’re finished, except to make up the philosophers on those missing tapes.” I tell him about the many untranscribed philosophy tapes, the backlog I’ve been trying to type even when there’s so much else to do. He says, never mind, you finish typing those first, we will do the make-up philosophers later—maybe in Los Angeles.

As 1971 drew to a close, the future looked jam-packed: In addition to the big projects underway in Juhu, Vrindaban, and Mayapur, Kaushalya and Srimati were in Jaipur trying to organize a pandal program for January, and Giriraj was in Madras planning a program for February. Furthermore, Prabhupada had written to devotees in Malaysia, Australia, and Hong Kong, promising to be with them by April. The already amazing pace was now even quicker.

And London—Prabhupada never forgot about London and the long search for a large temple there. On the evening of December 28 he called me to his room.

Prabhupada: I have got a promise from R. D. Birla that he will give half a million pounds to build a Hindu temple in London. But problem is this Reserve Bank of India. They will not allow rupees to go out. Now we have met this Yashwantrao Chawan at Delhi program, Finance Minister, and he has appreciated. “I will help you,” he told me. You are GBC for British zone. What do you think?

Shyamasundar: I think we should strike while the iron is hot, Srila Prabhupada.

Prabhupada: Yes, I want you to go there, New Delhi. You meet Gurudas there. You both see Mr. Chawan and ask him to allow—lift the restrictions, change rupees to pounds sterling. Make arrangements.

I stayed up all that night to type nine letters, orders for Prabhupada’s troops around the globe, and then hopped an early-morning train to New Delhi. From what I understood, this “promise” from R. K. Birla was pretty flimsy, equivocal, and not written in stone, but we didn’t look at things that way. Whatever Prabhupada and Krishna wanted would happen, so we charged headlong into the future, delighting in these daredevil adventures, come-what-may actors in Krishna’s unfolding display.

India’s strict foreign currency controls—at a time when the only rupees spent abroad were for oil and weapons—made it highly unlikely we would succeed on this particular mission. Besides, it was almost New Year’s Eve, and certainly the government offices would be closed for the holidays. Plus India needed its currency reserves to rebuild after a costly war. But, hey, maybe it was

Krishna’s plan, another miracle in the making? Tamal Krishna would take over as secretary while I was gone.

For the train ride to Delhi I bought some magazines in order to catch up on world news. The cover of Time featured “The Bloody Birth of Bangladesh,” with a civilian in an orange sweater holding an AK-47 in his upraised arm and shouting victory: “Jai Bangla!” The war, the uneasy peace—and what’s this? George Harrison’s Bangla Desh triple record album is at the top of music charts around the world! In the close synchronicity that seemed to shape events around Srila Prabhupada, the album had been released on December 20, just four days after the war ended, and had already sold millions of copies. Now the whole world was hearing “My Sweet Lord,” “Awaiting On You All,” and Billy Preston’s “That’s the Way God Planned It.” Good news.

**\* \* \***

I was in New Delhi with Gurudas, laughing with him again. Like me, Gurudas had a relationship of spontaneous laughter with Srila Prabhupada, and when he and I were together we laughed a lot too. When I was with Gurudas, it seemed there was nothing we couldn’t pull off for Prabhupada—and have fun doing it. In the taxi from the train depot we remembered some of our major pranks: the Avalon Ballroom, the Beatles, crashing the High Commissioner’s party in London, swapping one-liners with the King of Kashmir. We missed Mukunda, our fellow laugh-riot friend. Most devotees were just so serious all the time. You could joke around with Rishi Kumar (“You need to go on sankirtan more, Rishi, you’re so ugly you stop traffic!”), and you could even get a chuckle from ever-so-serious Giriraj if you made a statement so outrageous that he knew it wasn’t true. Even Tamal Krishna had a laugh in him if you told him flat-out what a jerk he was being. Yamuna, of course, was always good for a laugh if you said something a little off-color to bring that blush to her cheeks. But with Gurudas, our special bond was chasing rhinos together with gut-splitting laughter.

Gurudas told me the story of how he knows Finance Minister Yashantrao Chawan—how he met him through Jagjivan Ram, the Minister of Defense. Back in October, Gurudas needed a really big name to headline the Delhi pandal, so he made a plan to approach Jagjivan Ram at his home at 6 Hastings Road in Kailash Colony. When he and Tamal Krishna approached the gate, they noticed only one guard, so they quickly hatched a plan: Tamal would distract the guard while Gurudas made a beeline for the front door. It worked, and Gurudas found himself seated across from the surprised Defense Minister of India in the man’s living room. On the wall above Jagjivan Ram’s head was a huge portrait of a saintly old woman, tilak on her forehead, her hand in a bead bag, obviously his mother. They talked about her: “Yes, my mother, Vasanti Devi, she’s a great Vaishnavi, still living.” When Gurudas requested Mr. Ram to be a guest speaker at the upcoming pandal, he prevaricated, stuttered, and tried to refuse. So Gurudas said, “If you won’t agree to introduce Prabhupada at our pandal I will tell your mother!” That got him, and the Defense Minister said, “Oh, please don’t tell my mother. I shall appear!”

Of course, Jagjivan Ram had a good excuse not to appear—war was about to break out with Pakistan—so he persuaded his colleague, Finance Minister Yashantrao Chawan, to come in his stead. On the night of November 21, Mr. Chawan sat backstage with Prabhupada and yakked with him in Hindi for fifteen minutes. They seemed to hit it off. Onstage, the Minister introduced His Divine Grace in glowing terms, and I guess Prabhupada saw this as a chance to ask him a favor. So here we were, five weeks later, ready to call it in.

The problem was: the Delhi government offices were closed for New Year holidays. So Gurudas and I waited until January 5 and then made our way to the Finance Ministry on Vijay Chowk Road, adjacent to Rashtrapati Bhavan (India’s White House). We sat in the waiting room for hours. Late in the afternoon, we were told to come back tomorrow, when the Minister would be in his office. Next day, the same thing. We sat all day, chanted a lot of rounds, “But he is definitely coming in on Friday, so please kindly return tomorrow,” said the secretary.

OK, we’re getting the runaround, but maybe the guy’s busy, so we’ll give it one more shot. Finally, on Friday afternoon we see a flurry of activity in rooms down the hall, so we boldly stride down the hall, past outstretched arms trying to stop us, and into the big office at the end, surprising Chawan as he’s settling into his chair.

“Hare Krishna!” we exclaim and all but prostrate ourselves at his feet. We hand him books and prasadam and slip a now two-day-old garland around his neck. He recovers well, and we sit down for a chat. He says he will see what he can do about releasing R. D. Birlas foreign exchange money, but we can see he really doesn’t care much about our request. He says that the Reserve Bank of India must decide—passing the buck—and they are more interested in buying weapons at this point, so he doesn’t give us much hope. He asks Gurudas to leave his phone number, indicating a scratchpad on his desk. Gurudas notices that the pad is made from recycled documents cut into fours and stapled together. Gurudas says, “Aha, you are the perfect Finance Minister, saving the nation’s money by reusing paper!” The Finance Minister takes this as a great compliment. We have a good laugh together, then leave, but Gurudas and I are certain that as soon as we exit his door he will put our request at the bottom of the pile of papers on his desk, filed away for eternity.

**\* \* \***

January 7, 1972—I’ve been away from Prabhupada now for ten days—the longest separation in months. On the train back to Bombay that night, I have the chance to weigh all that has happened over the past few months: Prabhupada and I have traveled together to twenty different cities on four continents in six months, camping under a couple dozen different roofs. “Keep me talking,” he said, and I had tried to keep him talking—not just the boring stuff, but always about something exciting, new—unexpected topics like, “What is virtue, Srila Prabhupada? What is salvation? What is it like on the moon?” I’ve recorded hundreds of hours of Prabhupada talking. His little bell tinkling, even in my sleep, has become my wake-up call to happiness, transcending all other previous ideas of happiness. I cannot see enough of Prabhupada—the physical beauty of his face, his gorgeous face—captures my eyes from morning till night, and I feast on his endless allure. He trusts me; he depends on me. How can I possibly deserve such mercy? He is in my care, I think, forcing me to rise beyond all other considerations. I’m his audience. He seems energized by my laugh. He tries things out, floats ideas. I’m his sounding board, his straight man. “What do you think, Shyamasundar?” He catches my glance from across the stage, raises an eyebrow, like, “How am I doing?” His smile, his laugh—especially his laugh—are all I live for.

I never felt that Prabhupada was an ordinary man. It was just the opposite. He was so obviously a spiritually empowered resident of another world, so far outside the ordinary, that I often felt unworthy to be in his presence, dejected by my own failings and insufficiencies. I often thought, “I will never be able to perfect this Krishna conscious process like he has. How can I even aspire to such perfection?” But then he’d ask, “What is your opinion?” or make a funny face—and again I’d feel like his young-old friend, his Charlie McCarthy, a soul mate on this hilarious adventure together.

As 1971 ends, from the calm eye inside Prabhupada’s hurricane I’m so proud of his vast expansion, his dreams and plans realized in such short time. I’m proud of my godbrothers and godsisters too, so dedicated and effective, so strong in carrying out Srila Prabhupada’s schemes. How wonderful is the mysterious personal interference of Krishna for his pure devotee!

I’m seeing the world through Prabhupada’s eyes: you plan, you point, then you ease off to let Krishna spin the action around you. You watch for magic, the hints, the clues: in this person, that letter, the butterfly that lands on your finger—then you risk it ail and leap into Krishna’s delightful, real-time dream.

I can’t sleep on the train back to Bombay. Tomorrow I’ll rejoin my spiritual master for what I expect will be the most exciting year of my life: 1972.

## Five

To understand India you have to appreciate the history of its kings, the maharajas, and the special pact they had with their subjects. In ancient times, India was ruled by pious kings schooled and advised by brahmin priests according to scriptural precepts. Society itself was organized by occupation and natural proclivity, like a human body, with power passing from the head (the words of God, via the priestly class), to the heart (the royal class), through the arms (the warriors), to the belly and legs (the producers and workers). Life was peaceful, efficient. If any citizen had a problem or dispute, he or she could approach the king for a fair settlement. The king was the ultimate responsible authority. Even if there was drought or pestilence, the king was accountable and would be expected to rectify all discrepancies and restore harmony in the kingdom. For this the people loved their king, honoring him almost like a deity. If a diamond was found in a farmer’s field it went to the king for his glorification. Otherwise, this glittering object had no value.

The vast material wealth of ancient Indias rulers—even up to modern times—can only be roughly calculated by today’s standards. For example, Aurangzeb was the sixth Mogul emperor of India, and Mir Jumla, just one of Aurangzeb’s generals, was said to possess 20 maunds (1645 pounds, or 3.8 million carats) of diamonds from the legendary Golconda mines of South India—and this was after he had turned over the lion’s share to his emperor. The Regent Diamond, 410 carats when mined at Golconda, wound up in the hands of Napoleon Bonaparte, who later pawned this single stone to the Hapsburgs to finance his invasion of Italy. Osman Ali Khan, the Nizam of Hyderabad, was declared by Forbes magazine to be the fifth richest man in history. When he abdicated his throne in 1952, the Nizam fled India to Istanbul with cargo planes of treasure worth an estimated $210.8 billion. He was in such a hurry he left behind the 185-carat Jacob diamond, a gem the size of a golf ball, which he’d been using as a paperweight on his desk. The Koh-i-Noor diamond, pride of the British Crown-at 106 carats, a mere trinket by comparison—was said to be equal in value to one day’s food for all the people in the world. The kings of Jaipur, a small city-state in Rajasthan, particularly liked emeralds, and in the 1500s, when the Spanish looted the New World, the greatest of Colombia’s emeralds came to adorn the shoulders of Man Singh and other Rajput rulers, worn like garlands with giant pearls between. Once stories of this great wealth began to circulate in medieval Europe and the Middle East, the race was on to find and conquer India.

The Indian kings were honored by their people, and in turn the kings honored God—Krishna in one of His many forms—above themselves, and built temples around their kingdoms to glorify the Deity and give facility for public worship. According to Vedic histories, millennia passed in relative peace and tranquillity. About five thousand years ago, as the iron age of Kali advanced, this system began to break down. One by one, with the gradual degeneration of humankind’s finer sentiments, kings were corrupted and kingdoms fell.

Now, 1,972 years into the Christian era, there are few godly kings left in the land of Bharat (India). The present-day royal house of Jaipur is one of the last vestiges of a pious ruling family.

I arrived back in Bombay from Delhi on January 8,1972, only to learn that Srila Prabhupada had been invited to Jaipur to speak from January 15-21 at Radha-Govindaji temple on the palace grounds. Kaushalya and Srimati had been in Jaipur for a month or so, buying marble Radha-Krishna Deities to ship to ISKCON temples around the world. Their obvious love for Krishna and Prabhupada had deeply impressed the local citizens, especially the priests at Radha-Govindaji temple, who had arranged Prabhupada’s visit from Bombay and sent him plane tickets.

I caught up on this and other news when I arrived in Bombay: Tushta Krishna, Tulasi Krishna, and Sudama Vipra have left to start a temple in New Zealand; we still have no land in Vrindaban, but Prabhupada is pushing for it; due to the recent India-Pakistan war, foreign devotees are not allowed in Nadia district on the border with Bangladesh—and so the Mayapur project is on hold. I also hear that Prabhupada might go to Nairobi after Jaipur. Let’s see what happens. And the best news? Fifty new cassette tapes have arrived from Sudama in Tokyo!

We left Bombay for Jaipur on January 12. On the one-and-a-half-hour flight, Prabhupada told me the fabulous story of the Govindaji Deity:

Five thousand years ago, Krishna’s grandson Vajranabha installed the black marble Deity of Govinda—Krishna in His aspect as a cowherd boy—in a temple in Vrindaban. Over time, the exact site of Vrindaban was lost, so in 1517, Lord Chaitanya assigned Rupa Goswami to relocate and excavate the original village where Krishna was born and raised. Rupa unearthed the ancient Govinda Deity, and in the late 1500s, Man Singh, then king of Jaipur, erected an elaborate, seven-tiered temple in Vrindaban where Govindaji was reunited with a much smaller brass murti of Radha. At the time, Man Singh’s temple was the most impressive example of Hindu architecture in northern India.

Emperor Aurangzeb's palace was located in Agra, just forty-five miles southeast of Vrindaban. One night in 1670, he saw from the ramparts of his palace a light glowing in the distance. He was told that this was a beacon atop the Radha-Govindaji temple in Vrindaban. He became furious that something stood higher than his own palace and ordered his army to dismantle the temple. The soldiers tore off the temple’s top three tiers, and the truncated temple stands today, unused.

Radha-Govindaji escaped Vrindaban just ahead of Aurangzeb’s troops, and for the next forty-four years, the Deities were hidden in various places. In 1714, Maharaja Jai Singh, great-grandson of Man Singh, brought Them to Jaipur, a hundred and fifty miles southwest of Vrindaban, for safekeeping. In 1735, the king built a temple for Govindaji within the Jaipur palace compound, which is still in active use today. The temple was placed eight hundred feet down slope and directly in front of the palace, so the Maharaja could see the Deity from his windows. Jai Singh installed Govindaji as King of Jaipur and accepted for himself the position of chief minister. To this day, the Jaipur royal seal reads, sri govinda-deva carana savai jai singh sarana: “Lord Govinda, at whose feet Jai Singh takes refuge.”

Prabhupada explained further: “Temples within palaces are especially for the royal families, but in many of these palace temples the public is also allowed to visit. The temple of the King of Jaipur is situated within the palace, but the public is allowed to assemble. If one goes there, he will see that the temple is always crowded with at least five hundred devotees. After the arati ceremonies, they sit down together and sing the glories of the Lord with musical instruments and thus enjoy life.”

Yes, we are seeing it now: as a few of us walk behind Prabhupada and his escorts, loaded with luggage, through gates and long colonnades into the Jaipur palace grounds, ahead we see hundreds of Krishna bhaktas, the ordinary citizens of Jaipur, dancing and chanting Krishna’s names in the famous temple before us. Kaushalya tells us, “Seven times a day, starting from early morning, they blow a siren in town, ring the temple bells, and thousands of people drop whatever they’re doing and run through the streets of Jaipur shouting ‘Govinda!’ It’s a Krishna conscious city—can you believe it?” Prabhupada makes a beeline for the temple, and the masses part to let us in.

The large kirtan hall at Govind Devji temple is open on three sides, with columns supporting the flat roof. The marble-domed Deity chamber is on the north side of the temple, and within it Radharani and Govindaji stand, elaborately dressed, being showered with flowers and scented water by a team of pujaris as the assembled devotees chant Their names: Hey Rad-ha Go-vin-da! Hey Rad-ha, Go-vin-da! The echoing transcendental sound lifts us into another, more magical world. Those eyes! Radha’s and Krishna’s eyes are large and petal-shaped, outlined in red, with small black pupils in huge white pools that follow you like magnets. Even from a distance they penetrate and sear your soul with unusual power. There’s a wide marble hallway for perambulating around the Deities, and we follow Prabhupada around the altar three times, amazed to see men, women, and children touching their heads to the walls, shouting and mumbling prayers and cries for deliverance. Their personal connection to God and His consort is palpable, and humbling. After circumambulation, we return to fall supine before Radha-Govinda, deeply moved, and the crowd goes wild seeing Prabhupada and his genuine Western bhaktas.

Prabhupada is housed in an elevated round, stone guesthouse, beneath towering banyan trees and a huge overhead water tank just a hundred feet from the temple. To enter, you walk up concrete stairs to a wide veranda circling the place. Nanda Kumar, Rohininandan, and I will stay here with Prabhupada. (Rohininandan will train Nanda Kumar as Prabhupada’s servant and then return to Delhi to assist Kshirodakshayi with the Hindi BTG.) Malati will come every day with Saraswati to cook. Prabhupada cautions us about monkeys—there are pink-faced macaques everywhere. These monkeys are aggressive, showing their fangs and hissing at us. They come onto the veranda and steal anything they can, even entering the house if we leave the door open. So Prabhupada tells us all to carry stout sticks, arming us for continual encounters with these disturbing creatures.

The day after our arrival, our temple hosts stage a grand parade through the streets of Jaipur with Prabhupada seated on an open, decorated four-wheeled wagon pulled by two huge white Brahma bulls, their horns spreading more than three feet. Prabhupada mounts the cart in front of his bungalow and laughs as his chariot barely squeezes through the palace gates and out into the Pink City.

In 1727, Maharaj Jai Singh began his construction of Jaipur (“City of Victory”) according to the Vastu shastra, or Vedic spatial principles. The original walled city is two and a half kilometers square, aligned north, south, east, and west. The walls are twenty feet high and nine feet thick, entered through seven gates made large enough for elephants to pass. The main boulevards are 111 feet wide, bisected at right angles by smaller streets for commerce. All walls and buildings are made from or faced with solid cleaved bricks of pinkish-red sandstone. In 1853, the entire city was painted pink for a state visit from Prince Albert, and it remains pink to this day.

Rohininandan holds an elaborately embroidered umbrella over Prabhupada’s head as he cruises slowly around the city walls, followed by a couple of hundred loud-singing and dancing devotees, halting in traffic as the cart is met by crowds of enthusiastic citizens, lurching forward again as police do their work. Prabhupada is sitting erect, draped with garlands, and he looks handsome and self-content. As we pass the fabled pink walls, I notice many posters plastered on them to advertise the latest films, like Gambler, with Dev Anand in Clark Gable moustache smoking a cigar, the story of a criminal don and a card sharp—this is the same guy who just came out with Hare Ram, Hare Krishna. And look at that one—a 5' x 3' poster for Go-Go-Go World, the 1966 Italian shockumentary featuring depraved scenes from around the world; a busty woman in a short negligee proclaims, “Girls! Girls! Girls!” This is all new stuff for provincial India, as Kali-yuga hastens to destroy this pious society.

Prabhupada must have noticed the posters too—he never misses anything—because that evening he comments in his room:

Just like there are so many sex literatures—very attractive. It is selling like anything. But we are not interested in those rascal literatures. Tad vayasam tirtham: such literature is considered as the place of enjoyment of the crows. Vayasam means crow. The crows take enjoyment in the garbage. You have seen? Just like vultures—they come together to take pleasure in a corpse, dead body. But a white swan, raja-hamsa, he goes to a place where there is nice water, lilies and lotus and nice trees. So we have to become swans, raja-hamsa, paramahamsa. Birds of the same feather flock together, is it not? If you keep your feathers crowlike, then you cannot mix with the swans. That is not possible. This is the test. There are classes of men like crows, and there are classes of men like swans, paramahamsa.

Devotees arrive from Delhi, Bombay, and Calcutta and, in a flurry of all-night, last-minute activity they erect the pandal just to the side of and behind the Govind Devji temple. As usual, Prabhupada speaks in the early mornings and again at 7 p.m., but this time he speaks in Hindi, as not many people here speak English. It’s January, and at these morning and evening hours it’s very cold in Jaipur. Still, thousands of devotees bundled in shawls pack the house. Prabhupada is uniquely appreciated in Jaipur by people who are already full-fledged Vaishnavas. Almost every citizen comes to these speaking engagements, and Prabhupada is very happy here. The Maharani of Jaipur, Queen Gayatri Devi herself, introduces

Prabhupada on opening night. We don’t understand what she says, but it sounds good.

That night, sitting in his room, Prabhupada tells us:

There was monarchy, the kings were very responsible. Therefore, the kings were known as rajarshi. Raja rishi means the king is supervising administration of the state, still his character was just like a rishi, a great sage. That was the qualification of the king. At the present moment, practically all monarchical state is abolished. Just like in England, the queen, she is powerless. Actually, the queen has no power. People have taken away the power. It is the same here in Jaipur. Actually, they are the owner of the state, Jaipur state, the old state, at least five hundred years old, but now they have no power. The people have taken away the power. But according to Vedic civilization, this peoples government, democracy, is not sanctioned. In the Kali-yuga, anybody, by hook and crook, if he captures the royal throne, he becomes king. There is struggle: who will capture the power? We see practically in political held, people are interested for capturing the power, but they are not interested in really helping the citizens. So they put their manifesto before the election that we shall serve you in this way and that way, but when they get power they do not benefit the citizens—simply keep their position of power.

Later that night, Queen Gayatri’s emissary arrived at the bungalow and invited Srila Prabhupada to a private discussion with Her Highness at the palace tomorrow morning. We were about to see the wisdom of Prabhupada’s words spelled out in actual events.

Maharani Gayatri Devi, now fifty-two years old, was herself the daughter of kings. Her father was second in line to the king of Cooch Behar, in West Bengal, and her mother was a Maratha princess from Baroda. In 1939, Gayatri Devi became the third—and favorite—wife of Jaipur king Sawai Man Singh Bahadur (nicknamed Jai). She was a stunning beauty, voted by Vogue magazine as one of the world’s ten most beautiful women. After India’s independence in 1947, the Maharajas remained a powerful force in Indian society, and both Jai and his wife entered politics. In 1962, Gayatri Devi was elected to the Lok Sabha (India’s House of Lords), a post she held for fifteen years. She represented the Swatantra Party, in direct opposition to Indira Gandhi’s Congress Party. As Prabhupada and I walked from his bungalow and through magnificent gardens to meet the queen, I double-checked that my tape recorder was loaded with a new cassette and fresh batteries. This should be a good one.

The huge, seven-story City Palace sits atop a slight rise at the south end of the sixty-acre grounds; Govind Devji temple is at the north end. As we walked the distance between, Prabhupada enjoyed the fountains and rectangular gardens, abloom with winter flowers and strutting peacocks. He pointed off to his left at Jantar Mantar, the astronomical observatory built by Jai Singh in 1727. The sundial there, he said, measures time within two-second accuracy. “These kings, they used to send their laundry by ship to Paris for washing.” As we climbed up the stairs to the large palace veranda, we saw two ornate silver pots, each more than five feet tall. A sign at their base said that these were the largest silver objects in the world, weighing 760 pounds apiece and each holding five hundred gallons of water. They had been used by the Jaipur king on his state visit to London in 1901. “You see,” Prabhupada said, “he used to bring his own supply of drinking water, from the Ganges.”

We were met by a servant, who led us up many stairs and down long hallways and into a majestic room. The queen greeted Prabhupada at the door in a flurry of what I guessed was Bengali. She looked at me, puzzled, and Prabhupada told her, in English, that I was his secretary, that a swami should never be alone with such a beautiful woman—and we all laughed, breaking the ice.

The two of them sat at opposite ends of a couch, and I sat at a table maybe twenty feet away, reached into my book bag, and switched on the tape recorder. While they chatted in Bengali, I studied the Canaletto painting on the wall and looked out over the gardens below, pretending to ignore them. But as they spoke I saw that Gayatri became increasingly agitated, then sad, and at times even burst into tears. Prabhupada treated her so gently, like a daughter who had suffered a loss, and I heard Sanskrit quotes interspersed with his fatherly commiseration and advice.

An hour later we left the Maharani and, as we walked back through the halls of the palace, Prabhupada stopped to look at old black-and-white photos on the walls, depicting days of royal glory past. One showed two Englishmen in pith helmets holding rifles and standing behind two huge dead tigers, labeled, “Duke of Edinburgh and Sir Christopher Bonham-Carter; Queen Elizabeth II visit, January 1961.” The photo next to it showed “Jai at age eleven with his first tiger.” Prabhupada remarked, “Even, say, twenty-five years ago, the Maharaja of Jaipur, he used to go into the forest every year and he would fight with a tiger simply with a sword. He would fight with a tiger in the jungle, and he was so expert, he would kill. And then the tiger would be brought in procession, in royal procession. Because the king used to say, ‘This tiger is the king of forest. I am also king. So after his death, there must be a royal reception.’ So kshatriya, or warrior-king, means they used to kill—practice. Unless they practice, how they can kill? And now our president, they practice only smoking, that’s all. And when there is fight, he is in the safety chamber, and others are, the poor boys, they are called by the draft board to go and fight. This is not kshatriya. Kshatriya means when there is fight, the king must come forward first. The other party’s king also. The king will fight with king, and the soldiers will fight soldiers according to position. And when the king is killed, then victory is owned. It doesn’t require that all the soldiers were killed. No.”

As we returned through the gardens to Govind Devji temple, I said, “Prabhupada, I recorded your conversation with Gayatri Devi, but what was that all about?” He rounded on me and said, “You must destroy that tape immediately!” Ohhh, OK—and I punched rewind. As we walked on, the story gradually came out: “She has many enemies. Her husband, the king—he was killed two years back in accident, polo accident, and she misses him very much. And now she has no money to pay such big expenses. She is a strong lady, very great devotee, but these politics—there will be much trouble. She has offered me land to build one center here, somewhere behind Govind Devji. Just you discuss and search out one plot.”

In his room, Prabhupada elaborated on the story, saying that Jai’s death (in June 1970 at Cirencester Polo Grounds in Gloucestershire, England) was highly suspicious. And then on December 28, 1971, little more than two weeks ago, a bill was passed by both houses of Parliament to amend the Indian constitution, abolishing the ancient privileges, titles, and privy purse of India’s royal families. No wonder she was so upset!

“Just like government at the present moment, they are allowing charity still, but most of the portion of the income they take away by income tax so that one may not have any power to give in charity. So at the present moment, the government does not declare that charity is illegal, but that time is coming very soon, very soon. As soon as there will be communist government. Our Indira Gandhi is cooperating with the Russians, and as soon as she is under the control of the Russians, gradually communism will be introduced. People are afraid of this attempt by Indira Gandhi.”

(Three years later, in 1975, Indira Gandhi declared a national emergency under the Maintenance of Internal Security Act and jailed political leaders opposed to the Congress Party. In a draconian act of revenge, Gayatri Devi was indicted on a trumped-up charge of tax evasion and sent to the notorious Tihar Prison in Delhi, where twelve thousand prisoners jammed a facility built for five thousand. When she emerged five months later, her political will was broken and she retired.)

The next morning, after the early pandal program, Prabhupada took his entourage for a walk to inspect the buildings Gayatri Devi had offered for an ISKCON temple. Behind Govind Devji temple, there were acres of parkland called Jai Niwas, with gazebos, roses, pomegranate trees, pools, and fountains. Straddling the gardens to the north was a large old building called Moti Mahal. With columns and pagodas, it looked like a small version of the main palace, but it was run-down, unoccupied. Prabhupada loved it, but readily agreed that it would be very expensive to refurbish. He delegated Kaushalya, Devananda Swami, and Rohininandan to open negotiations with the queen’s people to see' if we could get it. “Krishna is the greatest king. If He wants, money will be there.”

(A month or so later, despite devotees’ efforts to make a deal for the Jaipur royal buildings, political forces prevailed and ultimately, the queen’s people withdrew her offer of temple land.)

Kings and monkeys: these are the two main topics of discussion in Srila Prabhupada’s quarters at Jaipur. One morning I heard screaming and ran out to see my little Saraswati about to be attacked by a snarling bull monkey twice her size, menacing her with his two-inch fangs. I grabbed a stick and chased him off, but Saraswati was traumatized for days. These monkeys got into everything, stole clothes and soap and pots and pans—anything left outside and anything they could reach through the barred windows.

Devotee: Ahhhh!

Prabhupada: What is that?

Devotee: A monkey. No one is guarding.

Prabhupada: Somebody must guard at all times. So monkeys, they are now taking advantage that these people have got some eatables. Therefore, Darwin’s theory is from monkey. That’s a fact. From the monkey, cow, and lion, the next birth is human life. So vimrishya loka-vyasanam kripaya ucuh sma satrinah. People, they are now disturbed both ways. One way, the king is a rascal. Another way is that, taking advantage of the bad king, the thieves and rogues, they are also very powerful. Actually, at the present moment, people are disturbed by unnecessary taxes, at the same time bad elements. So without proper king, without nice state, everyone is unhappy. And that is going on nowadays. In the Kali-yuga, it will go on continual, and more and more people will be unhappy.

Another major topic of conversation in Jaipur was whether or not Tamal Krishna should take sannyasa. He had married a California girl, Madri dasi, in France in March 1970. Madri had become very much a part of the ISKCON India scene and was loved and respected by all of us, but especially by her female counterparts. The Jaipur program had been arranged by women, managed by women, and with the queen’s presence, the female force was behind all our activities here. The women were in charge of obtaining Radha-Krishna murtis for the entire Society and were being tutored by Govind Devji priests in the arts of Deity worship. So imagine the stir when Tamal Krishna showed up from Bombay, leaving his wife behind, and petitioned Srila Prabhupada to grant him sannyasa. TK even packed saffron-dyed clothes and a danda. The women freaked out, and in emotion-charged meetings with Prabhupada they voiced their displeasure with this cold-hearted move. Tamal was relentless and met with Prabhupada morning and night, debating with him the logic behind his desire to renounce married life. Tamal’s determination prevailed and, on our final day in Jaipur, Prabhupada initiated him in a Vedic fire ceremony as Tamal Krishna Goswami.

On January 22, we flew back to Bombay.

**\* \* \***

Prabhupada is upset that no money has yet been paid to Mr. Nair for the Juhu land and that negotiations have stalled. He’s also troubled that nothing has been done in Vrindaban and that foreign devotees are still prohibited from entering the post-war zone around Mayapur. He will fly to Nairobi tomorrow for a big program there, and he hints that if the India affairs are not in order soon, he will stay in Africa, maybe fly from there to Australia and the US, bypassing India altogether. Prabhupada writes to Jayapataka: “I am going to Africa on the morning of 24th instant, and it is quite likely that I shall not be returning to India for some time, perhaps several months. I especially wanted to stay in India for this Mayapur celebration, but if there is no scope, or if our men are not able to do anything, then I can return to USA via Africa.”

The prelude to Prabhupada’s India Temple Symphony is now in crescendo, building suspense for the first movement. Srila Prabhupada sometimes had this strategy, and I’d seen it before: when events were stalled or leaders too complacent, he would go elsewhere, hinting that he may not return. Devotees everywhere lived for Prabhupada’s personal presence, and they’d double their efforts to please him, entice him back to where the action was.

**\* \* \***

Madhudvisa is having personal problems in Bombay. Perhaps bigmoney politics and the supercharged pace of life here have eroded his pioneer enthusiasm. Prabhupada decides to take Madhudvisa with him to Kenya to swap places with Brahmananda. Madhudvisa will manage Africa affairs, and Brahmananda will spearhead the Juhu project. There are only two airline tickets, so Madhudvisa will go with Prabhupada as his secretary, carrying two full-sized Radha-Krishna deities onboard, and I will stay behind in Bombay to work with our lawyer, Mr. Diwanji, on the languishing Juhu legal matters.

After a week in Nairobi, where Prabhupada headlined a five-day World Hare Krishna Movement Festival in the city’s biggest sports stadium, on February 4 Prabhupada returns to Bombay with Brahmananda. Mr. Diwanji has worked with Nairs’ lawyers to draft the Juhu paperwork, and on February 8, the sales agreement is signed by Prabhupada and Mrs. Chandrabhai Nair. Prabhupada has given the Nairs Rs. 50,000/- in down payment (“earnest money”) on the Juhu land. He will have to pay another Rs. 150,000/- thirty days after completion of the conveyance deed, and the total balance will be twelve lakhs of rupees at the rate of three lakhs per annum for four years. There are eight rupees to the US dollar, so the total price for the Juhu land is $175,000. Not bad for nearly five acres of prime, near-beach property in Bombay. Prabhupada is the ultimate negotiator.

He immediately assigns Brahmananda to clear the land and prepare accommodations for the devotees to live there. Prabhupada also directs Brahmananda to erect a pandal so that he may return in March to lay the temples cornerstone and install Deities for his most magnificent project yet. He calls it “Hare Krishna City” because in addition to a huge marble temple there will be a hotel, school, and high-rise apartment complex. Many devotees still think the land is too far from downtown Bombay, but Prabhupada is so keen on the place that we jump into action to help him.

A few days later, Madhudvisa Swami returns to Juhu from Nairobi, sick and dispirited from severe jaundice. Bad news from Africa: the devotees have looked all over Nairobi, but the philosophy book tapes are lost forever. We’ll have to go back to the Renaissance and redo about fourteen philosophers.

But good news from India: the government has lifted the travel ban in Nadia district, so the Mayapur festival can go ahead as scheduled in the last week of February. The pieces are beginning to come together.

Meanwhile, Giriraj has been in Madras since late November with Achyutananda, Bhavananda, Dinanath, and Bhanu, and together they have arranged a weeklong program for Prabhupada there beginning on February 11. On the morning of February 10, we leave by train for Madras.

**\* \* \***

About an hour after the Madras Mail chugged out of Bombay’s Victoria Station—and after fifteen or twenty devotees cleared Prabhupada’s first-class AC sleeper cabin to find their own seats in second-class coach—the conductor appeared and punched our tickets. The door’s slammed and latched against the noisy corridor, and I’m suddenly a little shy and awkward to be alone with Prabhupada for this long journey in such cramped quarters. Rohininandan has been dispatched to Delhi to help with the Hindi edition of BTG, so Nanda Kumar is traveling with us as Prabhupada’s servant, but he’s riding in coach with the others. Sure, I’d been alone with Prabhupada much of the past seven months, but I always had my own space somewhere, a room or hallway separate from him where I typed letters or transcribed tapes or read or slept. But now here we are, together in this small cabin crammed with luggage and boxes of food—he’s stuck with me for the next thirty hours. Where should I sit? How do I act? What if I... pass wind?

First thing Prabhupada does, he starts arranging the gear: “Just pull down both beds”—one above the other, folding down from one wall—“Put that box under, then that one, now take out from suitcase one fresh dhoti, my chaadar, big one, brown” ... “Now fasten suitcase, put under” ... “Your case, put here, and prasadatn, keep here.” In no time, Prabhupada has the little cubicle organized, and then he sits on a fold-down seat by the window, looking forward with the train’s progress, watching the countryside flow by, chanting on beads. “You may sit on the bed,” he says. It’s sort of like traveling with my dad.

After a while he says, “Achaa, there are any letters to reply?” “Yes, Prabhupada, something from Govinda dasi in Hawaii, Hayagriva in Ohio, and letters from Hamsaduta and Himavati in Germany.” These are some of his dearest disciples, so he delves deeply into their points, one by one, telling both Govinda dasi and

Hayagriva that he has just “purchased a large, twenty-thousand-square-yard plot of land in the wealthy and most beautiful neighborhood of Bombay.” To Govinda dasi he writes that Bombay is “very much like your Hawaii,” and to Hayagriva, “Part of the space will be used for a school and college of this Krishna Consciousness, especially to attract the bright young Indian boys and girls, so I think that you shall have to come here in future to take charge of such institution of learning.”

It’s clear that gossip about Tamai Krishna taking sannyasa has made the rounds, and Himavati is worried that her husband, Hamsaduta, may also seek a separation. Prabhupada responds to her: “And you should not worry, he’ll never be given Sannyas without your sanction. There is no question of giving him Sannyas, so why you are asking?”

When the train stops at stations, Prabhupada slides the window open, taking in the frantic sounds of travelers and hawkers selling snacks. He calls now and then for a coconut dab or roasted chickpeas wrapped in old newspaper, and the sellers are immediately reverent to see a swami, female vendors pulling saris over their faces. Prabhupada bargains with them to get their lowest price, and I shove a few crumpled rupee notes or some coins through the window. “Hare Krishna!” they shout—even in these remote villages Bhaktivedanta Swami is famous.

We chat back and forth on many topics as devotees drift in and out of our cabin, and then Prabhupada tells us the history of Madras. In 1640, the British East India Company built Fort St. George on the Coromandel Coast, establishing their first toehold on the southeast rim of India. From there, the British gradually conquered all of India. Prabhupada tells us about Lord Clive, whose ferocious, double-dealing tactics primarily advanced the British cause. “India welcomed everyone, but the result was they were occupied. The English people were welcomed. Lord Clive was welcomed. But he intrigued to occupy India. And his statue is worshiped in London. But what was his credit? He made an intrigue—he illegally entered India and made an occupation. That is Western civilization.”

Prabhupada has visited Madras in the past, and he tells us that the English language is still widely spoken in Madras, and that it is the major cultural, economic, and educational center of South India. Now, Giriraj has set up meetings with several intelligent, high profile Madrasi leaders, and Prabhupada is anxious to speak with them.

After a red-orange sunset, the windows darken, and Prabhupada asks me to read to him from Krsna book—the story of Krishna and Balaram entering Mathura. I read about young Krishna breaking King Kamsa’s bow “exactly as an elephant breaks a stalk of sugarcane in the field”—and watch Srila Prabhupada smile, his eyes closed in pleasure. Then Krishna is wrestling the giant elephant, Kuvalayapida, becoming “decorated with drops of perspiration and sprinkled with the blood of the elephant” as He rips out and hoists the ivory tusk onto His shoulder in victory. We chat about this and that, and then it’s growing late. Prabhupada says he will now do some translating work and asks me to bring his books and Dictaphone and adjust his light. “You may go up-top bed—and sleep. Is it all right?”

I extinguish all but the reading light above his tiny, folddown escritoire, then climb up, spread a chaadar over my reclined body and, drowsing, watch Srila Prabhupada below. He puts on his glasses, arranges his books, presses buttons on his Dictaphone, plays back yesterday’s work, and starts to translate. He speaks slowly and clearly into his microphone, enunciating each word for his faraway editors as eternal wisdom flows from the ageless Author in an uninterrupted, continuous stream—rail tracks clackety-clack—zzzzwht! from a tiny rewind—ding, ding, ding, receding, a deserted crossing—and I fall into sleep. A far-off moan from the locomotive breaks my slumber. The light is out and I scoot over to peer over the edge of my bed, down to Prabhupada’s bunk, expecting to see him sleeping. I’ve never seen him actually asleep—this may be a first. He’s on his side in the starlight, legs drawn up, and just then his right eye opens and stares into mine above, with no expression on his face, as if to say, “Thought you’d catch me sleeping, huh?”

On February 11, Prabhupada is met at Madras Central by Giriraj and a huge crowd of local well-wishers and then rushed into the back seat of a flower-decorated 1959 black Chevrolet Impala convertible for a grand parade through the city’s streets. A marching band and elephant lead the way. It’s like one of those old Charles Lindberg ticker-tape parades down Fifth Avenue—confetti and rose blossoms rain from the skies, Prabhupada waving to the crowd.

On February 12, Prabhupada goes to the home of Sri Chakravarti Rajagopalachari. At ninety-three, this gentleman is one of India’s elder statesmen. He is deeply religious, a father of the nation. He had been governor of West Bengal, Chief Minister of Madras state, and when Vice-President Richard Nixon visited “Rajalalji” in 1954, he called him “the wisest man in India.”

Sixteen years ago, in his March 1956 issue of Back to Godhead, Srila Prabhupada had critiqued the recent controversial division of India into fourteen states and three union territories by the States Reorganization Commission and writes:

In his recent speeches, Sri Rajagopalachari the veteran politician of India said: “It is good to be a political and national Leader and to take office and work hard for it.

I have seen it is better to be able to leave it and enjoy the company of the sages of our land and to help them to speak to men and women again” We think that the above assertion from the lips of Sri Rajagopalachari will give some food to the politicians and national leaders of India for pausing upon their leadership over the innocent people in general.

Mahatma Gandhi praised C. Rajagopalachari as “the keeper of my conscience”; he was founder of the Swatantra Party, Gayatri Devi’s friend and mentor, a man of sharp wit and seasoned insight. Rajaji and Prabhupada sit down in adjacent armchairs and discuss the global spread of Krishna consciousness.

Rajaji: Who is the chief of your group? You \[pointing to Madhudvisa Swami\] or him?

Prabhupada: \[laughter\] As you like.

Rajaji: He holds a big stick, so I’m asking.

Madhudvisa: Prabhupada is the chief. He has given me the stick.

Rajaji: Now you have undertaken a very great task. I am not competent to discuss or comment upon it.

Prabhupada: No. I think you are the best man to comment upon it.

Rajaji: True. Still, I am not presumptuous enough to comment on one who has actually taken up the work. That is the difference between thinking and doing. Thinking is easy. Doing requires inspiration. And you have taken it up.

Rajaji: But I think any religious movement like yours-people think they can buy up the grace of God by giving some money, right? So you won’t find any difficulty in getting money, because there is an air of sin in the world, and the sinful people think they can compromise by giving money. Let them give. But that is the smallest point. Another point is, I suppose in each institution, in each center, there are men as well as women ... It’s very important, because all religious organizations have floundered on scandal, and you will have to be very careful about that. This is what an old man has a right to tell you.

Prabhupada: Yes.

Rajaji: That’s one thing. But these are minor matters. On the philosophy of action, which you have taken, of converting the people to worship of Sri Krishna, in Bhagavatam there is lots of danger so far as Sri Krishna goes. The Bhagavatam deals with a chapter \[Tenth Canto\] of His life which can mislead people.

Prabhupada: Yes. That we are very cautious.

Sri C. Rajagopalachari is as sharp as a tack, shifting quickly between jokes and serious philosophy, and both his advice and openhearted endorsement of Prabhupada’s global preaching effort give Prabhupada a visible lift.

While in Madras, Prabhupada speaks from the seventh chapter of the Bhagavad-gita for three consecutive nights in a large hall called Rajiswari Kalayana Mandapam (on Edward Elliots Road, now called Dr. Radhakrishnan Road) to about five thousand English-speaking citizens each night. Madras is practically the capital of the Mayavada—or impersonal—Hinduism, so Prabhupada speaks strongly about Krishna as a person—a historical person, author of the Bhagavad-gita—and he rails against interpreting Krishna in any other way.

One of the strongest advocates of Mayavada thinking is Dr. Sarvepalli Radhakrishnan, now eighty-three years old and a resident of Madras. This former president of India had written a popular interpretation of the Bhagavad-gita, wherein he famously states: “It is not the personal Krishna to whom we have to give ourselves up utterly but the Unborn, Beginningless, Eternal who speaks through Krishna.” Prabhupada had been speaking out against Radhakrishnan’s interpretation of Bhagavad-gita for years. In an article called “Scholars Deluded,” published in the November 1958 issue of BTG, Prabhupada wrote:

We have all respects for Dr. Radhakrishnan because he is not only the second citizen of India, the great world renowned philosopher and a scholar in the Hindu philosophy but also he is truthful Brahmin with considerable knowledge in the Impersonal Brahman. There is a popular saying that there is no harm in creating some antagonism with a great scholar but there is harm even by creating a friendly relation with a fool... So we are not afraid of Dr. Radhakrishnan when we put forward the critical review of his great scholarly exposition of the Bhagwat Gita.

In the Bhagwat Gita the Personality of Godhead Sri Krishna says surrender unto Me only. But Dr. Radhakrishnan says surrender not to the person Krishna. Is it not the opposite view to suit the particular type of philosophy followed by Dr. Radhakrishnan? This indirect interpretations of the Mayavadi school for all kinds of revealed scriptures are properly condemned.

Prabhupada was always saying stuff to us like, “Dr. Radhakrishnan states, ‘It is not to Krishna the person, but something else within Krishna.’ He is proud of his becoming a great philosopher, but he does not know that there is no within and without Krishna. And he is trying to comment on Bhagavad-gita. He does not know what is Krishna. He’s making a distinction which does not exist.”

Now here we were, about to meet the man himself. Somehow Giriraj had arranged a meeting with Dr. Radhakrishnan at his home, and when we arrived there his middle-aged daughter warned us that he was not in very good condition. Still, she allowed the meeting to proceed.

Prabhupada greeted the old man with all courtesy, and then quickly launched into his argument that Krishna is a person, the Supreme Personality, and that to interpret His aham (“I am”) in any other way is a flagrant error. When Dr. Radhakrishnan couldn’t respond—his eyes and wizened smile were vacant of all expression—Prabhupada recognized his advanced decrepitude, and we soon thanked his daughter and prepared to make our exit. When Prabhupada took his hand to say goodbye, Dr. Radhakrishnan weakly spoke his first words of the entire meeting: “Swamiji, you please pray for me to God.”

Later, back in his room, Prabhupada said, “Suppose I have written one book, Bhagavad-gita. So I have got some intention. So why should you interpret my intention with your intention? But because it is popular, you are taking advantage of my book and expressing your views. How much cheating! How much cheater you are! Therefore he is suffering, Dr. Radhakrishnan. He has lost his brain. Void. Simply sitting down like this. He cannot ask whether he is hungry or not. His daughter is always attending, giving him some food. Then he is eating. Otherwise, he does not say. Lost everything. He has mentioned Bhagavad-gita as a mental speculation in his Indian Religion book. So he is such a great offender he must suffer. But it is good for him. Because he is a gentleman, therefore his offenses are being compensated in this life.”

In Madras, seeing Prabhupadas unrelenting defense of Krishna as a person, we understood on a deeper level the prayer we said daily to our spiritual master:

namas te saraswate deve gaura-vani-pracharine  
nirvishesha-shunyavadi-paschatya-desha-tarine

“Our respectful obeisances are unto you, О spiritual master, servant of Saraswati Goswami. You are kindly preaching the message of Lord Chaitanya and delivering the world, which is filled with impersonalism and voidism.”

Prabhupadas presence was felt throughout this vast city, and each day Madras’s largest English-language newspaper, The Hindu, summarized his lecture from the previous evening in its pages.

**\* \* \***

Mr. Kuppuswami Veeraswami, the High Court Chief Justice of Madras, fell in love with Saraswati—now going on four-and he gave her a six-inch, solid silver murti of Krishna playing His flute. One evening, the Chief Justice came to visit Prabhupada, and Prabhupada told him that he thought the silver murti was too valuable for a small child. He had taken it away from her, he said, and would replace it with another, less expensive murti. Just then Saraswati ran by in the hall, and Prabhupada yelled to her, “Saraswati, come here. Your friend is waiting!” There was a long pause. Then Saraswati stuck her head around the door. “Where are you going so fast?” Prabhupada asked. “I have to pass stool,” she said, and hurried back down the hall.

Prabhupada doubled over with laughter. When she came back a few minutes later, Prabhupada said, “Here is your friend. He has given you Krishna.”

Saraswati said, “But Krishna is gone! Someone took Him!”

“Where is Krishna?” asked Prabhupada. “Is He under the cushion?”

“No.”

“Is He on the shelf?”

“No.”

“Who has Krishna?”

She’s crying by now, and she looks at each person in the room, then at Prabhupada. “You have Krishna!” She rushes forward and searches the folds of his dhoti. He reaches behind and pulls out a doll-like Krishna and holds it up: “Here is Krishna!”

She howls with relief and joy, grabs Krishna, and darts from the room.

Prabhupada is laughing so hard tears run down his cheeks, and he talks to us that evening about transcendental love in the mood of separation, using Saraswati’s example: “Krishna, where are You?”

Next morning, laughing, he asks Malati, “Where is your all-attractive daughter?”

Malati: She is in all-attractive maya.

Prabhupada: Actually, she is all-attractive \[chuckling\]. Everyone loves her.

Satsvarupa had established a school for ISKCON kids in Dallas, Texas, and had sent Prabhupada a tape of the children lecturing and chanting mantras. Each morning for several days, Saraswati sat in Prabhupada’s room and they listened to the tape together. Prabhupada wrote Satsvarupa on February 16: “Saraswati and I have enjoyed the tape of the children lecturing very much.” How special this is, I thought, that “my” child is having such a wonderful life.

It’s important to remember in all of this that we seldom knew from day to day how long we would be in one place and when or where we were going next. As Prabhupadas secretary, I tried constantly to firm up dates, times, and places, and to fix travel arrangements as far in advance as possible. But with Prabhupada this was a near impossible task. Sometimes I had our tickets in hand, but there was still this floating feeling that things might change; I had only a vague idea what would happen today, what to speak of tomorrow or a week from now. I always had to leave space around Srila Prabhupada for serendipity, last-minute changes, adapting to Krishna’s plan. Who knew? Maybe in ten minutes a guy would walk in or a letter arrive, a telegram, a phone call—and Prabhupada would say, “Let us go. Make arrangements.”

For example, the Mayapur program is fixed, and Prabhupada is scheduled to be in Calcutta around February 20. But on February 15 Prabhupada gets some really good news from Vrindaban, so he writes to Gargamuni: “Now I have just received a cable from Kshirodakashayi Prabhu that Mr. Saraf has given us the land. We shall gladly accept the offer, so kindly inform Kshirodakashayi to draw up all the legal papers and gift deed for handing the land over to our Society, and he can bring those papers along with a plan of the plot showing length and width dimensions and we shall settle up our Vrindaban program when he shall meet me in Mayapur.”

So now it looks like we’ll go to Vrindaban right after Mayapur, but nothing is for sure. In Bombay, Brahmananda and others are clearing the Juhu land, and Prabhupada hopes to go there before leaving for Australia in March or April—but again, nothing is certain. Communications are slow.

For months, Prabhupada had been corresponding with his godbrother В. V. Puri Goswami, promising that he would visit his ashram in Visakhapatnam in the near future. On February

15,    Prabhupada gets a phone call from Puri Maharaja. He has arranged some programs in Visakhapatnam, so now Prabhupada tells me that he and all the devotees will travel by train from Madras to Visakhapatnam, leaving tomorrow. We’ll stop there a few days and then proceed to Calcutta. “Make arrangements.”

**\* \* \***

Visakhapatnam (“Vizag”) is a mid-sized town on the east coast of India, about halfway between Madras and Calcutta. The five-hundred-mile train journey north from Madras to Vizag takes fourteen hours. Once again, I found myself in a tiny cabin with Srila Prabhupada as we made this nightlong journey. Prabhupada talked and joked and played tapes until around 10:30 p.m., when I switched out the lights and we lay down to rest. About midnight, Prabhupada sat upright and called my name: “Shyamasundar, take dictation.”

Over the past few years, a team of ISKCON artists in Boston and New York had been expertly illustrating Prabhupadas books, creating wonderful, large oil paintings that were photographically reduced to fit a book page. This team was headed by Jadurani dasi, and she often wrote Prabhupada for instructions regarding the themes to be painted. That night, en route to Vizag, Prabhupada dictated responses to Jadurani’s latest queries about scenes from Third Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam.

(1)    Varaha and Hiranyaksa were fighting on the water. Brahma was observing from the sky. The earth planet is being rescued from Garbhodaksayi, and from the earthly planet many rocks are coming while being balanced on the tusks of Varaha. Yes, the demigods are present in the sky around.

(2)    Lord Brahma throwing off bodies is just like if there is some dirt on my body and I throw it off, a little disgustedly.

Just like if our body becomes dirty many insects gather, and we remove these insects with one hand and throw them away.

(3)    The demigods were in the clouds, and they were throwing down flowers just like water pours down from clouds.

(4)    Kapiladeva’s mother was questioning in the palatial house.

(5)    This cosmic manifestation is different and non-different from the Lord, because it is coming out from His bodily effulgence; for example, from sunlight all the planets are being created, similarly, the effulgence emanating from the body of the Lord is creating so many universes.

(6)    When S. Manu & his family attended arati many people and servants were there, because he was emperor or king.

(7)    1st Boar Incarnation is not different from Varaha. Lord Brahma was seeing from his plane.

(8)    In Ch. 31, the progress from subtle to gross elements is just like sometimes a rocket is thrown in the sky, bursts, and so many lights & illuminations come. Exactly how, that is artist’s work.

(9)    Baby in the womb prays to four-handed Narayana. Actually, the baby remains with hands folded as if praying until he is born. Narayana is inside.

(10) Time mixes with the material energy just like a flower bud: in due course of time the bud fructifies and comes into flower, or like a time bomb: in due course it bursts out.

(11) Greed is potential, lust is an active outburst.

(12) Agitation of time is just like a water pot put on the fire, and after some time becomes boiled; when it comes to this boiling point, that is agitation. Or, with chemicals, if alkaline and acid mixed, there is effervescence or agitation.

(13)    Devahuti’s house is the palace or flying city that Kardama Muni created by mystic powers.

(14)    Garbhodakasayi Vishnu lays on the water.

(15)    Krishna had a chariot driver.

Later that night, in my weekly newsletter to devotees, I wrote: “Prabhupada’s train of thought is so lucid and coherent, I can only conclude that while we think Srila Prabhupada is sleeping he is not sleeping but is reflecting on ways to serve Krishna more and more.”

В. V. Puri Maharaja’s Sri Krishna Chaitanya Ashram was wonderfully situated just off Beach Road in the Pedda Waltair district of Visakhapatnam, on a sandy bluff overlooking the Bay of Bengal. The February weather was perfect and reminded us of Southern California. The absence of traffic, cool sea breezes, and sparkling sunlight were welcome relief. The eight-room ashram was spacious and filled with good vibes.

Prabhupada was surrounded by a few admiring old friends and fifteen of his loving children in this sacred and beautiful natural place. We had experienced so many exciting adventures with Prabhupada, dashing from place to place in such rapid order—now it feels like a holiday, a vacation from crowded cities, reclined at Prabhupada’s feet while he sprawls in his easy chair on the front porch and laughs and kids with his buddies, watching hummingbirds and seagulls play, noon sun shimmering on a chartreuse sea.

While the old men schmooze, we can’t resist and, with Prabhupada’s permission—“Yes, you go! Chaitanya and his friends would sport in the waves!”—we run off to the beach to catch some waves (although Nanda Kumar and other surfer types wouldn’t really call them waves) in the warm, gentle ocean below the ashram.

Each morning, Prabhupada leads his throng of merry pranksters along the beach while we pick up shells and skip flat rocks. He tells us of Lord Chaitanya, how much He loved this coast, how the Lord and His devotees would swim and play in this sea in great jubilation. Nanda Kumar asks Prabhupada, “Isn’t this really just sense gratification, this playing around in the ocean?” Prabhupada replies, “The sun is there—Krishna is the light of the sun. Ocean is there—Krishna is the taste of water. You are surrounded by Krishna. How can you forget Krishna? He is all around you.”

After our second day in Vizag, Prabhupada told me, “Just delay a little. Postpone the trip to Calcutta. We shall stay here a few more days.” And the days stretched out, glorious days with sunburned skin, saltwater swims, sand in our clothes, and Krishnas name on our tongues. Thirsty days relieved by cool coconut water and the balm of Prabhupada’s laughter as he lounged in his chair on the shady front porch, sucking on a loose lower front tooth, one arm raised and folded behind his head, eyes closed in mirth, snorting with glee and smiling so big you could see all his teeth, and above, the lotus-pink crescent of his gums.

We caught up on a few letters.

To Jayadvaita in New York: “I think Pradyumna is feeling morose about his wife. That is not good so he will not be able to work. Therefore better let him come and live with me here in India and I shall train him properly in Sanskrit language. He may come immediately, in time for Mayapur festival beginning by 25th this month. In this way, he can fly directly from New York to Calcutta and live with me.”

To Satadhanya in Tokyo: “So far personal association with the Guru is concerned, I was only with my Guru Maharaja four or five times, but I have never left his association, not even for a moment. Because I am following his instructions, I have never felt any separation. There are some of my Godbrothers here in India who had constant personal association with Guru Maharaja, but who are neglecting his orders. This is just like the bug who is sitting on the lap of the king. He may be very puffed-up by his position, but all he can succeed in doing is biting the king. Personal association is not so important as association through service.” That point really hit home, and I couldn’t sleep that night, seeing myself as the biting bug in Prabhupadas lap.

Bhakti Vaibhava Puri Goswami was seventeen years Prabhupada’s junior. He had accepted sannyasa in the Gaudiya sampradaya in 1966, and had been Prabhupada’s avid fan and supporter for years. In early June 1971, Prabhupada had made a brief, two-day visit to Puri Maharaja’s ashram in nearby Rajahmundry, and the two continued to correspond frequently.

Puri Maharaja advertised Prabhupada’s daily lectures at his ashram and in other venues around Visakhapatnam, such as the Gaudiya Math and the Ladies’ Club. At the Gaudiya Math, Srila Prabhupada was speaking to his godbrothers, some of whom were inimical to what he was doing, and he said, in barely veiled admonishment:

In this material world, everyone is trying to be predom-inator. One nation is trying to become predominator of other nations. One brother is trying to be predominator of other brothers. The human form of life means different parties, different mentalities, trying to predominate over each other. This is tnaya. One blind man is trying to lead other blind men. There cannot be any benefit. So everyone should give up the spirit of predominating. They should know the ultimate goal of life is Vishnu, Krishna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, and we shall all surrender unto Him. Then there can be peace and prosperity.

One day, we went sightseeing at the famous Simhachalam (Lord Nrsingha) temple, on a mountaintop a few miles northwest of town, a place Lord Chaitanya visited in the early 1500s. But all too soon the party was over and it was back to business. Late on the afternoon of February 22, we found ourselves waiting for the overnight train to Calcutta.

Puri Maharaja will travel to Mayapur with us. It’s hot on the train platform, there’s no drinking water, so I climb a nearby coconut tree (showing off for Prabhupada—yes, sometimes he’s my audience) and fetch down a huge coconut. Someone has a pocketknife, so we hack off the top of the coconut and hand it to Prabhupada. He drinks from it and hands it to Puri Maharaj, who drinks and hands it back to me. I drink, then pass it to the next devotee, and in this way the entire party drinks dab water to their full satisfaction from one infinite coconut.

We arrive at the Albert Road temple in Calcutta on “23rd instant,” and Prabhupada is happy to see Pradyumna, who has flown in from New York to join Nanda Kumar and me on Prabhupada’s permanent roving staff. Pradyumna will transcribe and compile Prabhupada’s translation work; he will provide the Sanskrit-English synonyms and make sure the editors in New York receive a continuous stream of finished work. This is a big relief for me. We are now three adjutants to our commander-in-chief and will travel together as his flying wedge, his aides-de-camp, for the next one year.

The first thing Prabhupada does in Calcutta is send a check and a letter to our Bombay lawyer, Mr. Diwanji, paying Mr. Nair his second installment of fifty thousand rupees so that the devotees can move onto the Juhu land:

Please accept my greetings. I have heard from my disciples in Bombay that Mr. A. B. Nair is a little anxious to settle the financial side of our Sales Agreement for the Juhu property, so I am enclosing in his wife’s name a check no. CT/A-T 492829 drawn on the Central Bank of India, Head Office, Fort, Bombay-1, for the sum of Rs. 50,000/-(Rupees Fifty-thousand only), to bring to one lakh of rupees the amount we have paid Mrs. Nair to date as per our Sales Agreement. Now you may please hand over the check to Mrs. Nair, and you can assure Mr. Nair that the balance one lakh I shall pay him immediately upon my return to Bombay during the first week of March.

In the West, acquiring a temple was a pretty straightforward business. Someone owned the land or building and you bought or rented or leased it from them. Then you got permits and moved into the premises, remodeling it to suit your purposes, or you constructed a building on the property. Although we were young and inexperienced in these things, the devotees in America and Europe somehow managed to procure facilities fairly well, quickly opening temples all over the Western world. Srila Prabhupada more or less stayed out of all this, only occasionally helping with money or advice.

But here in India it was just the opposite. From day one, getting land and building temples in India was Prabhupada’s responsibility. We didn’t have a clue how to buy and develop property here, so Prabhupada took command, overseeing every aspect of real estate acquisition and construction. And man, was he on top of it.

In India, there were obstacles—thousands of years of obstacles—to overcome.
