Land had been passed down for so many hundreds of generations in India that clear title to almost any piece of property was ambiguous at best. Antiquated caste and religious customs, entrenched traditions, and eons of cheating and sleight of hand made even the simplest dealings impossibly difficult. For hundreds of years, foreigners doing business in India have faced these problems. In 1676 one French traveler to India named Jean-Baptiste Tavernier, in his report to King Louis XIV, wrote an entire chapter called “Concerning the Frauds which can be practiced in manufactures, either by the roguery of the workers or the knavery of the brokers and buyers.” He stated that at the end of each day, an Indian businessman will assemble his kinsmen, go over accounts, and plan future strategy “if possible to defraud rather than be defrauded.” Things hadn’t changed much in three hundred years.

Srila Prabhupada had been trying to get a foothold in Mayapur since 1967. Here, along the final miles of the Jalangi River, the land was verdant, produced year-round lush crops, and thus carried great value. Back when food was wealth, the British had seized this area of India first, and the agricultural wealth of Bengal—rice, silk, indigo, jute, and cotton—powered the British industrial empire. And before the Brits, the Moguls had hurried to this rich land at the Ganges Delta, displacing its tenants and populating the region with their own descendants. Grain and taxes from the Bengal region flooded Akbar’s and Aurangzeb s coffers, financing Muslim rule over the rest of India.

Lord Chaitanya was born in Mayapur in 1486, and Mayapur became the cradle of Gaudiya Vaishnava culture. Over the centuries, however, Vaishnavism became corrupted, breaking into several sects often at each other’s throats, and by now much of the agrarian population around Mayapur was Muslim. Remnant Vaishnavas were dug in, and they jealously protected their tiny hold over land and people around them. The Naxalite movement had its roots in this part of West Bengal, and there was frequent communist agitation between landowners and tenants. A flood of refugees from the recent war in Bangladesh, just twenty miles to the east, inundated the region. Constant threat from local armed gangs created a volatile, dangerous situation around Mayapur.

In his divine wisdom, Srila Prabhupada had sent a few eager, recently converted hippies into this difficult situation armed only with a deep faith in their spiritual master. After four years and many trips to Mayapur by Jayapataka, Achyutananda, Tamal Krishna, and others, ISKCON had finally acquired a few acres of holy Mayapur soil.

The ISKCON land is located half a mile north of the confluence of the Hooghly and Jalangi branches of the deltaic Ganges and a mile south of the Yoga Pitha, the temple built on Lord

Chaitanya’s birth site. The entire area is open field and rice paddy, only a few lone trees scattered across the flat landscape. During the sweltering summer of 1971, the devotees built a fence around the property, constructed a brick, bamboo, and straw-roofed cottage, and dug a well. A monsoon flood in the fall of ’71 nearly ruined the place, but the devotees rallied and rebuilt. With this intrusion by mostly foreign devotees, local tempers flared, bandits came in the night, gunshots were fired, and police were called in. The position in Mayapur was shaky at best.

But Srila Prabhupada was undaunted, relentless in his desire to establish a magnificent ISKCON temple at the heart of Lord Chaitanya’s birthplace, the center of Gaudiya Vaishnavism. Hostile neighbors, floods, bandits—none of this mattered to Prabhupada. Naranarayan and other devotee architects had designed and built a scale model of the proposed temple complex, and for months Prabhupada had been exhorting his troops to lay the cornerstone at Mayapur. Devotees were negotiating for more land, and building materials were being stockpiled on the property. The gun smoke had barely cleared, and the scene was finally set for the grand event. The first movement of Prabhupada’s three-part India Temple Symphony is about to begin. On the morning of February 25, driving slowly up the one-lane, badly paved Bhaktisiddhanta Road—after miles of uninterrupted farmland, seeing only water buffalo, a few men on bicycles, and the occasional two-wheeled oxcart—we suddenly see the 12' x 18' lavishly painted billboard beside the road:

ISKCON

INTERNATIONAL CENTRE AT LORD CHAITANYA’S

BIRTHPLACE

SRIDHAM MAYAPUR

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY FOR KRISHNA CONSCIOUSNESS

FOUNDER-ACHARYA

HIS DIVINE GRACE A. C. BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI

and at the bottom, in the biggest letters on the whole sign, the single word: PRABHUPADA.

With six-foot neon tube lights on all sides, the billboard features a painted vision of Prabhupada’s dream: a huge white temple rising hundreds of feet from the flat, green farmlands. Jet-age American-style advertising meets ancient rural India.

**\* \* \***

We turned left through a rickety bamboo fence and stopped the car. On our left was ISKCON Mayapur headquarters, the only finished building on the property: a Bengali-style country cottage about fifty feet long, built of local bricks and with a high, tiled roof overlain with straw thatch. The edges of the thatch were trimmed to within five feet of the ground and hung out over a veranda along the north side of the building, supported by a dozen bamboo posts. With thick drooping thatch roof cut sharp at the corners, the cottage looked like something out of a Grimm’s fairytale.

Inside was one big room maybe forty feet by twenty feet, with a high, tapered ceiling and bamboo rafters. Reed mats cover a packed cow-dung floor. An L-shaped kitchen adjoined on the east side of the room, separated by palm-leaf woven screens. Electricity had been run in from the road, so there were lights and fans (on the rare occasions when power ran through the public grid). Behind the cottage was a hand pump set over a well, for water, and an outhouse. A cloth and rattan fence surrounded the yard and cottage, with a cute bamboo-and-lath arched entryway. There were palm- and bananaleaf decorations everywhere and colored rice powder mandalas on the pathways—Bhavananda’s touch for sure.

Prabhupada loves the place. He’s instantly at home.

He sits on the Western-style bed in one corner while dozens of devotees gather around him on the floor, everyone flush with success and anticipation. All of Prabhupada’s India-based, saffron-clothed sannyasis are here—Jayapataka, Achyutananda, Brahmananda,

Gargamuni, Tamai Krishna, Devananda, Madhudvisa, and Subal—making an impressive show for Prabhupada’s local Gaudiya godbrothers. So many dandas are in the room they have to lean them against the wall outside. Wall to wall devotees, mostly old friends—Gurudas, Gurukripa, Bhavananda, Yadubara, Giriraj, Rishi Kumar, Mahamsa, Radharamana, Rebatinandan, Madhavananda. And the girls—Yamuna, Srimati, Kaushalya, Palika, Visakha, Malati and Saraswati, and yes, Madri, too. Prabhupada and his adoring disciples talk for hours, catching up and going over plans for the next few days, finally breaking to the veranda for a late lunch.

After lunch, Prabhupada tours the pandal construction, just a hundred feet west from his front gate. Two large, widely separated white tents have been set up near the pandal as quarters for the men and women.

The pandal is the usual pole-and-decorated-canvas affair, about fifty feet by a hundred feet and open on three sides. Against the back wall stand Sri Sri Radha-Madhava on an elaborate altar, the same brass Deities who presided over our Kumbha Mela program at Allahabad a year earlier.

While workmen are busy finishing the pandal, Prabhupada leads the Mayapur organizers out into the field behind the pandal. He paces off the four corners of a huge, four-story residenceguesthouse, the first building he wants to erect here. He instructs devotees to pile clods of dirt to mark each corner. Then Prabhupada shows them where to dig a pit, about fifteen feet deep and five feet square just outside the pandal entrance. This is where he will lay the cornerstone.

Back in Prabhupada’s room we talk late into the night, and finally Prabhupada signals that it is time for sleep. There’s a lingering threat of bandits, Naxalites, and gangs sent by disgruntled godbrothers, so at their request, Prabhupada allows the sannyasis to sleep on the floor in his room. Nanda Kumar fixes the mosquito net over Prabhupada’s bed and the lights are doused. All through the night I am kept awake by the whine of mosquitos and loud snoring—Brahmananda’s especially—and I hear Prabhupada tossing and turning on his bed above me. Next morning, Prabhupada, announces that from now on, the men will sleep outside on the veranda, “except for Shyamasundar. He may sleep in here. He does not snore.”

Each morning, Prabhupada walks around the property, pointing to adjacent fields and saying stuff like, “The temple should go here—bigger than Westminster Abbey or St. Paul’s in London,” or “Apartments for five hundred men should go over there,” or “Here you should dig a big lake.” He’s expanding on his dream for Mayapur.

I didn’t have much sense of what Mayapur was or who the Mayapur Vaishnavas were in the larger picture of Krishna consciousness. I was content to be engaged by Prabhupada in the wonderful dynamic of spreading Krishna consciousness around the world as fast as possible. Initially, I wasn’t that impressed with Mayapur. OK, some empty rice Helds, lots of water, some rather sad-looking Vaishnavas loitering here and there. But as we walked around, Prabhupada was seized by a special joy at being here, and gradually I began to see the area through his eyes: the once-great seat of our special philosophy, the tattered remnants of former glory—Lord Chaitanya as Krishna Himself with His companions dancing through palm and banana groves.

**\* \* \***

The Hare Krishna Festival began on the afternoon of February 25, 1972. For five days leading up to the full moon on February 29, devotees held twenty-four-hour kirtans in the pandal, broadcast by huge loudspeakers, attracting thousands of pilgrims to a novel display of ISKCON’s presence in this sacred land. The rented amplifiers were so loud that Prabhupada had to ask devotees over and over to turn them down—you couldn’t hear a conversation in his room. Prabhupada attended the pandal daily, but he let his disciples do most of the speaking. Achyutananda preaching in Bengali was a main attraction for astounded locals. Every day, the devotees fed lunch to hundreds of people, who sat on the ground in rows eating rice, dal, and chutney with their fingers from stitched banyan-leaf plates.

Prabhupada met with a few of his godbrothers in his cottage each day and, with varying degrees of success, argued his case for a united worldwide preaching effort. He said things like, “I have started this—it is our Bhaktisiddhanta’s wish—now you join me and I will give you all facilities.” There was a lot of lip service but little real commitment, sort of a wait-and-see attitude. Prabhupada’s guru, Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati, was also called Prabhupada by his disciples, so many of our Prabhupada’s godbrothers objected to him adopting the same honorific. “But my students have given me that name, what can I do?” was Prabhupadas reply.

Each day, Bob Cohen, an American Peace Corps worker, visited Prabhupada in his room. Bob had met Swamiji during the Deshapriya Park pandal in Calcutta a few months earlier. Cohen sat with Prabhupada in a humble mood and asked him more than a hundred almost perfect spiritual questions, receiving perfect answers in return. I sensed right away the special nature of their exchange and recorded the hours-long sessions despite our dwindling supply of cassette tapes. After their last session I said to Prabhupada, “Those discussions were perfect!” He replied, “Yes, you transcribe them. We shall print one book, Perfect Questions, Perfect Answers.” Their conversation became a classic record of the guru’s instructions to a neophyte.

Bob: Why does someone approach a spiritual master?

Prabhupada: Because he knows things as they are.

Prabhupada: Now, this vast water in the Atlantic Ocean and Pacific Ocean, how much chemicals were required? How many tons?”

Bob: Many.

Prabhupada: So who supplied it?

Bob: This was supplied by God?

Prabhupada: Somebody must have supplied.

Prabhupada: Krishna says, “This earth, water, fire, air, ether, mind, intelligence, and ego—they are My eight separated energies.” Separated. Just like this milk. What is this milk? The separated energy of the cow. Is it not?

Bob: So this earth and all is made out of Krishna but is not Krishna?

Prabhupada: It is not Krishna. Or you can say, Krishna and not Krishna simultaneously. That is our philosophy. One and different. You cannot say completely different, because things without Krishna have no existence.

Bob: Is the real goal of life to know God?

Prabhupada: Yes. Go back to home, back to Godhead. That is real goal of life. Just like the water from cloud falls down as rain, and the actual goal is to flow down the river and again go to the sea. So we have come from God. Now we are embarrassed in this material life. Therefore the aim should be, how to get out of this embarrassment and go back to home, back to Godhead. This is real goal of life.

Bob: Let me ask one more question. What is being foolish?

Prabhupada: Foolish means who has no knowledge. That is foolish. Is it not foolish?

Bob: Yes.

Later, Bob said, “I realized I might have to surrender. So I went behind the pandal and just collapsed in the rice paddies and started crying. I said to myself, ‘I can’t give everything up.’ So the next morning, Prabhupada called for me again, asked if I had more questions. I said, ‘Thank you very much for what you’ve done, but I have to leave now. I have to get back to my Peace Corps teaching.’ And Prabhupada said, ‘Don’t talk L-E-A-V-E’—he spelled it out—‘talk L-I-V-E.’ I said to Prabhupada, ‘So you want me to stay longer?’ He said, ‘Yes.’ I said, ‘Well, if you tell me to, I will.’ In essence I had accepted him as my guru. Prabhupada timed it just perfectly well to capture me.”

And in Bob’s surrender, we observed a classic, real-life drama: a modern, educated young man, driven by his inquiring intelligence and in a humble mood, converts to Krishna consciousness, convinced by direct argument and logic delivered by a genuine sage, the pure living representative of the truth. Bob would later be initiated by Prabhupada as Brahmatirtha das.

One night, I woke to the sound of Prabhupada clapping, rose from the floor, and saw his moonlit form sitting up in bed, shrouded by the mosquito net. “Just come inside,” he said. “There is one mosquito. Just kill it.” I opened the net, climbed onto the bed, and chased the tiny insect around in the confined space while Prabhupada tracked it with a flashlight. Finally, I nailed it with a determined swak. “Thank you. Now you may sleep.” The sonorous sound of big men snoring droned from the front porch, uninterrupted.

Three days before the cornerstone-laying ceremony, Prabhupada sent Brahmananda back to Bombay to erect some tents for devotees to live at Juhu and to oversee the shifting of personnel and Deities from Akash Ganga to the new land. They were to build a pandal, install the Deities, and advertise a ten-day Bhagavata Dharma Discourse. Prabhupada would come there later, in March, to lay the foundation stone. After he left, nighttime decibel levels from the front porch were audibly less.

Prabhupada caught up with his mail. Having received many complaints from women about their vagabond husbands, Prabhupada redefined his policy on marriage in a letter to Harsharani dasi:

Henceforward, anyone proposing to marry must produce some outside income and live outside the temple, they must know this in advance and be prepared to carry such burden. Let them be married, but at their own risk. I cannot sanction anymore. My Guru Maharaja never allowed, but when I came to your country it was a special circumstance so I gave concession, but I am not so much inclined anymore, so I shall not sanction, but they may marry on their own risk of knowing that such arrangement is always troublesome.

To Krishnadas in Sweden he wrote, “I have heard that the Nobel Prize Institution is in Sweden. Why don’t you present them our books? Find out what to do, if we can win the Nobel Prize.” Prabhupada was so active on every front. I was constantly amazed by his universal knowledge on every subject matter.

On February 27 he defines “miracle” in a letter to Nandarani dasi, in London:

Actually, miracle means to see something I do not understand, so I am thinking, that is miracle. Just like if a small child sees me lift up one table, the child may think in that way, that it is miracle such big table could be lifted by me. So in a real sense miracle means fool, because if I have no knowledge then I am fool, so I say Oh, it is miracle. So-called transcendentalists who have no real stock of knowledge they are fond of these miracles, and for the most part, because the innocent public is generally foolish, therefore they accept these rascal magicians as saintly persons. But real knowledge means science or knowing everything about something. So this Krishna Consciousness is not a miracle, it is science, and because we are Krishna conscious, therefore we know everything, so practically we can understand how Krishna wanted to please His devotees and He appeared there so wonderfully, and we do not see anything miracle.

With this understanding, the February 1972 arrival in Mayapur of forty Western devotees, of Lord Chaitanya, with their full intent to build the largest temple in the world, was not a miracle but a destiny—another way of saying “Krishna wanted to please His devotees and He appeared there so wonderfully.” Prabhupada was fulfilling the wish of Bhaktivinoda Thakur, his grandfather-guru, who proclaimed in 1885:

When in England, France, Russia, Prussia, and America all fortunate persons by taking up drums and cymbals will take the name of Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu again and again in their own countries, and raise the waves of sankirtan, when will that day come!

Oh! When will the day come when the white-skinned Western people will speak the glory of Sri Chaitanya on one side and on the other and with this call spread their arms to embrace devotees from other countries in brotherhood, when will that day come!

Bhaktivinoda Thakur had revived Lord Chaitanya’s movement around Mayapur back in the late nineteenth century, constructing a large temple at the Yoga Pith, Chaitanya’s birth site. His son, Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati, Prabhupada’s guru, had carried on his father’s work in Mayapur, but after Bhaktisiddhanta’s death in 1937, petty jealousies between Vaishnava factions had pretty much stilled devotional fires in the area. Now it was Prabhupada’s turn to reignite them. We could see all around us evidence of the former grandeur of Gaudiya Vaishnava culture: here is the exact place where Chaitanya was born, this is the road He marched to the Kazi’s house, there’s the place where He led sankirtan. Now everything appeared crumbled, unkempt, swallowed by jungle. But the connection was still there, and Prabhupada urged us to lift the veil and see the special potency of this place, Sridham Mayapur.

One day, he led us across the Jalangi River to visit Bhaktivinoda’s old house, Svananda Sukhada Kunja, in the village of Godrumadvipa. Bhaktivinoda Thakur spent the final twenty years of his life there, supervising construction of the Yoga Pith temple and as a base for preaching widely around India. His mortal remains are buried there. We traveled in a train of bicycle rickshaws one mile south on Bhaktisiddhanta Road, along the river to Hular Ghat. From there we took boat taxis a half-mile upstream to the opposite bank.

The two-story concrete bungalow is well-shaded by palm and mango trees. It’s dilapidated, but some elderly granddisciples keep the place clean. Prabhupada shows us Bhaktivinoda Thakurs bed, his altar, and his plate and spoon. “Wow, there are his books, and chanting beads! And those are his sandals—and his woolen shawl!” We even saw his four-foot-tall Jost kerosene-powered cooling fan—you lit a wick and the hot air drove a little piston, spinning four twenty-inch metal blades. Pre-electric AC! Seeing these items, we feel a strong personal connection with our ancestral guru, thus strengthening our bond with Srila Prabhupada.

Standing on the upstairs balcony, Prabhupada describes Bhaktivinoda’s vision: One night, many decades ago and standing on this very balcony, Thakur Bhaktivinoda saw a bright light in the sky to the north above Mayapur. He later wrote that in that light was “the effulgence of one exceedingly wonderful temple which will broadcast the eternal service of Lord Gauranga throughout the world.” The site for his vision was across the Jalangi, on the very land where Prabhupada had just begun building his transcendental city.

After touring Bhaktivinoda’s home, we loitered by the river, waiting for boats to take us back across. Devotees asked Prabhupada if they could swim. Prabhupada said, “Why not?” And before you knew it, he’d removed his kurta and jumped in with us. Someone splashed Prabhupada and he splashed back—a bunch of kids splashing around in the sacred Ganges with Lord Chaitanya’s direct living spiritual descendant. It doesn’t get much better.

February 29 dawned, the full-moon day marking Sri Chaitanya’s birth. The cornerstone pit had been dug fifteen feet down into the rich, compact (kilometer thick!) flood-borne alluvial soil of the Ganges Delta. According to Vedic tradition, a small statue of Ananta Sesha should be placed in the foundation of every new structure, blessing it with strength and long life. Ananta Sesha is the multi-headed king of all serpents, an avatar of Vishnu, and He is said to hold all the planets of the universe on His hoods as He eternally sings the glories of Vishnu from each of His mouths. When Ananta Sesha uncoils, time moves forward and creation takes place. When He recoils, the universe ceases to exist, and He alone remains.

**\* \* \***

Puri Maharaja has brought with him from Vizag a gold, rubyeyed Ananta Sesha, about one and a half inches tall, and today we will bury Him in hallowed ground beneath the planned construction.

The pandal is packed. Prabhupada sits on a cushion before the ceremonial fire, surrounded by ten of his godbrothers and godnephews, his sister Pishima, his ISKCON sannyasis, devotees from all parts of India, and hundreds of curious pilgrims. Bhavananda chants sacred mantras and feeds the fire. Before the actual cornerstone-laying ceremony, Prabhupada initiates half a dozen new disciples, all Indian. Then he has everyone gather around the pit outside, and loud kirtan begins. No one knows quite what to do, so Prabhupada sends Puri Maharaja and other devotees, one by one, down the rickety bamboo ladder—rungs tied with jute twine—bearing coconuts, potted plants, and other items. The ancient ceremony calls for five kinds each of flowers, grains, leaves, metals, nectars, dyes, fruits, and jewels. After these objects have been placed in the pit, Prabhupada sends down about a dozen bricks, telling Naranarayan to go down with a trowel. A bucket of mortar is lowered on a rope. Prabhupada then himself climbs down and shows Naranarayan how to randomly stack and mortar the bricks, leaving a small hole in the center. Then Puri Maharaja hands down Ananta Sesha and the cornerstone, which are placed in the hole and bricked over. There, it’s done.

To loud kirtan and shouts of joy, Prabhupada directs everyone to toss coins and rupee notes into the pit as it is refilled with dirt, all of us taking turns with the shovels. Then we gather again in the pandal and Prabhupada asks various devotees to speak, starting with Malati. Asking a woman to speak at such an occasion flies in the face of age-old protocol, and some of Prabhupadas godbrothers look a little uptight. But most seem happy to see this incredible new chapter opening for their deep-felt faith.

Malati: And then he says, “Malati, you speak.” Everybody was floored. I was floored. So what could I say? I'm just looking around. Here are all these learned Vaishnavas—certainly more learned than I’d ever be. I fust stood up and spoke about Srila Prabhupada. I was not a pandit, but I had no hesitation about speaking on the glories of Srila Prabhupada. And it was actually the right thing to do at that time.

We all felt a special lift after the ceremony. This was another first—seeing Prabhupada with his godbrothers acting in harmony to spread Krishna consciousness. Another rhino surrenders at Prabhupada’s feet.

\[Mayapur didn’t look like much then, but from these humble beginnings a mighty temple grew. Now, forty-six years later, I have lived to see a million pilgrims a year flock to ISKCON’s Mayapur complex, spread out over seven hundred acres, and as we speak, the Temple of the Vedic Planetarium rises 370 feet over all—and, yes, it’s higher than St. Paul’s.\]

**\* \* \***

We drove back to Calcutta on the morning of March 1, and the next day Prabhupada announced, “We are going to Birnagar village today to visit Lalit Prasad, who is my spiritual masters younger brother and Bhaktivinoda’s son. You will drive and I will give you directions as we go.”

I wasn’t sure what to expect. Srila Prabhupada didn’t tell us much about where we were going or whom we would meet once we got there. He had me drive us north from Calcutta on the road leading back to Mayapur in our old Krishna-mobile, a huge 1948 American Dodge four-door sedan converted to right-hand drive for driving on the left side of the road. At a particular crossroads he told me to turn right onto a dusty, red-dirt road. “I think it is here. Just a few kilometers.”

The huge car was packed with devotees: Prabhupada in the front passenger seat, and Gurudas, Yadubara, Bhavananda, and Jayapataka in the back. A few miles up the dirt road we came to a concrete bridge spanning a deep gulley so eroded by the last monsoon that there was a three-foot gap between roadway and bridge on both ends. “Prabhupada,” I said, “we can’t go any farther by car. Can we walk from here to Birnagar?”

Prabhupada paused a moment, then turned in his seat and said, “You in back will all get out. Shyamasundar, you reverse the car. Then go very fast over the bridge and we will make it.”

Wow, a Steve McQueen moment! From a dead stop I revved the old motor as high as it would go and released the clutch. We leaped at high speed over the first gap, raced across the bridge and leaped over the second gap and braked to a screeching halt. I looked back through a cloud of red dust at the beaming devotees as they scrambled over the bridge to the car and piled in. Prabhupada had that deadpan look on his face which had become so familiar, as though nothing unusual had happened.

We continued up the road to Birnagar. Prabhupada asked me to slow down a couple of times while he searched for familiar landmarks. Through the dense jungle on both sides of the road I’m seeing stone walls and remnants of what must have once been a large city, reminding me of those old photos of Angkor Wat in

National Geographic magazine: banyan roots intertwined with crumbling buildings.

“There are many temples of Shiva here,” Prabhupada said. “The Ganges used to flow by this city. Once, it was the biggest center of learning in the world.”

A mood of peace and tranquility overcame us. It was like going back in time to an age when crowds of saints walked the earth. And it began to sink in: I am a living link in a singular ancient chain of masters. I could sense Sri Chaitanya walking through Birnagar s bamboo thickets and mango trees, by its grassy little ponds. Jungle birds interrupted the otherwise total quiet. Late-morning mist was burning off and everything was green, butterflies flitting and the air just starting to warm.

We arrived at Lalit Prasad’s place—an old, partially restored temple. I was blown away by Lalit Prasad as he sat lotus-legged on his rope bed in a tiny room. He was ninety-eight years old and toothless, lit up like a light bulb, bouncing up and down on his bed, so animated and yakking up a storm with Srila Prabhupada. Two or three elderly women, apparently Lalits disciples, carried drinks in and out of the room during the conversation, which was sometimes in English—“I am spreading Lord Chaitanya’s movement all over the world. Why don’t you join me?”—for the benefit of us wonderstruck Americans, and sometimes in Bengali for more intimate topics. At one point, a few of us were guided a short distance from Lalit Prasad’s headquarters to see a mango tree by a little pond that Lord Chaitanya Himself had planted.

After some time, Prabhupada went into another room to eat and rest, and I stayed with one or two other devotees to talk with Lalit Prasad for hours more, recording everything, fascinated by his every word. He showed us scrapbooks of old photos—he had been a professional photographer in Calcutta in his younger days—including photos of his father, mother, and brothers. He showed photos of Calcutta in the 1890s: the magnificent buildings, the royal visit by King George V in 1912. I asked Lalit Prasad if he ever saw tigers out here. He said, Yes, a few years back there was a famine and a tiger had entered his room—“just here!”-and looked at him, then ambled past his bed and into an adjacent storeroom—“over there!”—where it ate half a sack of rice, then strode out with a gruff thank-you.

We left in late afternoon, successfully jumping the bridge again. But that was not the end of our driving troubles. The lurch of bridge-jumping had shorted out the horn, an item which is absolutely necessary for driving in India. As we entered the main highway back to Calcutta, I had to yell out the window to warn away pedestrians and cows. Finally, Prabhupada said, “Just stop here. Jayapataka, bring my plate and one spoon from the boot.” We proceeded toward Calcutta and, seeming to know whenever the horn was required, Prabhupada hung his metal plate out the window and—with a look of glee in his eyes—banged on it furiously with his spoon. Clang! Clang! Clang! Move aside! The Vaishnava army is coming through. After some time, Prabhupada relinquished this task to someone in the back seat, having shown us once more a simple, practical solution to even the smallest of life’s problems.

Two days later I typed the following letter from Srila Prabhupada to Lalit Prasad:

My dear Uncle Prabhupada Lalita Prasad Thakura, Kindly accept my humble obeisances at your Lotus Feet. The whole day spent at your Temple on Thursday was so pleasant and happy, that all my disciples felt celestial blessings in your association. Personally, I am so much encouraged by you, that I appreciate this as the blessings of Bhaktivinoda Thakur through his living representative.

OK, what’s next? Were back in Calcutta, and there’s sort of a plan to go to Bombay later in March to inaugurate the Juhu land, but what’s happening in Vrindaban? Kshirodakshayi is in Delhi. He didn’t show up in Mayapur, so Prabhupada whips off a quick telegram: “To Kshirodakasayi: Proceed Vrindaban immediately. Await instructions. Bhaktivedanta Swami.”

The telegram was followed by a letter of instructions dated March 4:

I think I shall be coming to Delhi very soon to settle up our business in Vrindaban. Concerning the Saraf land, I want to sign the documents before going to Bombay within a few days from now, and if there is possibility to lay down the cornerstone there I can also do that before leaving from India. This will please me very much and give me great relief to know that we have done something solid in Vrindaban before I leave, and the credit is yours. So I am entrusting that you can arrange everything very nicely about getting the deed prepared for the Saraf land, and you may also prepare one nice foundation stone and we shall hold a public ceremony and lay the foundation stone.

Fat chance, I’m thinking. Nothing gets done that fast in India.

Four days later, Prabhupada gets a phone call from Kshirodakshayi: “You can come to Vrindaban! Sarafs have given you the land in Raman Reti!”

Kshirodakshayi, feeling the pressure, had confronted Mr. and Mrs. Saraf in Vrindaban, saying, “You must decide now. Prabhupada wants to come!” The Sarafs agreed to let Radha and Krishna decide. They asked Kshirodakshayi to write “Yes” on one slip of paper and “No” on another, put them in a bowl before their household Deities, and draw one out. The answer was “Yes,” of course.

Just ten days earlier, Prabhupada had conducted the first movement of his India Temple Symphony—the Mayapur Sonata—in allegro, to a full house in the middle of a rice paddy in rural Bengal. Now he would dig deeper into his theme and calmly conduct the second movement in a slower tempo, in adagio, on an empty plain on the outskirts of Vrindaban.

On March 10, Prabhupada and his little team hopped a flight from Calcutta to Delhi, and by noon we were in Vrindaban. Gurudas was there, and Kshirodaksayi, Gargamuni, Yamuna—not an army of devotees, just a few of his lieutenants ready to help their general get the job done quick.

Again we stayed on the second floor at Saraf Bhawan, six rooms in a row, opening off a long hallway with stairs at one end. Prabhupada occupied the third room down, and his small personal staff was quartered in the fourth room. What followed was one of the most amazing—and amusing—sitcoms ever: Swamiji’s 3-Ring Circus.

There were three major tasks to accomplish: (1) to sign the deed for the Raman Reti property; (2) to sign documents granting Prabhupada his rooms at the Radha-Damodar temple in perpetuity; and (3) to organize a cornerstone-laying ceremony for a new temple on the Raman Reti land. Prabhupada was scarcely settled in his room when the various characters in the sitcom began to arrive, one by one, from central casting.

Quiet on the set! Action!

SCENE ONE

Interior. Room Three.

Prabhupada sits with Gurudas, Kshirodakshayi, Yamuna; Kashiram and Geetabai Saraf, the gentle and pious land donors, also seated, are talking warmly with Prabhupada, having worked out with him the final language of the gift deed to the Raman Reti land.

Enter Shyama Devi, gray-haired female guru, who has a small following in Vrindaban and London. Prabhupada knows her. She's accompanied by a retired Member of Parliament (local guy, wearing moth-eaten black suit coat over his dhoti) and a policeman. Shyama Devi points to the Sarafs.

Shyama Devi \[in Hindi\]

But you have promised the Raman Reti land to me!

Long impassioned argument in Hindi follows, Shyama Devi accusing, the Sarafs defending. Prabhupada jumps in and, in English, requests the newcomers:

Prabhupada

You kindly just please step down the hall to room five. I will join you there in a moment.

Shyama Devi, furious, exits the room, indicating she’s going home; the MP and cop, apologetic, say they will wait in room five, and exit; the Sarafs remain seated, visibly upset by Shyama Devi’s intrusion. Prabhupada placates them.

Enter Gaurachand and Madanmohan Goswamis, hereditary proprietors of Radha-Damodar temple; they have come to demand more money and stricter terms for Prabhupada’s lease on the Radha-Damodar rooms. Prabhupada knows that an ancient family quarrel prevents these two men from speaking to each other.

Prabhupada

Please kindly wait for me in rooms one and two. I will come to see you each there.

SCENE TWO

Interior. Room Five.

Prabhupada sits, joking with and cajoling the MP and policeman, also seated.

Prabhupada \[shouting\]

Shyamasundar!

Enter Shyamasundar, fresh from room four, standing at attention.

Prabhupada

You may type the gift deed now, for signing. There will be no further problem.

Shyamasundar

Yes, Srila Prabhupada, I’ll get right on it. And by the way, Dr. Kapoor is here—and your other godbrother, Krishna das Babaji-he just showed up from Radha-kund.

Prabhupada

Good. Ask them to wait for me in room six, last room.

Shyamasundar exits.

SCENE THREE

Interior. Hallway.

Time-lapse shot: Prabhupada, Gurudas, and Shyamasundar shuffling between rooms one, two, three, four, five, and six, singly or together. Gurudas and Shyamasundar carry sheaves of documents in both hands. A brief stay in each room, then on to the next. Clock on the wall spins through two hours.

SCENE FOUR

Interior. Room Three.

Prabhupada sits with Shyamasundar, Yamuna, Kshirodakshayi, and Gurudas.

Shyamasundar

I’m typing the Radha-Damodar agreement, Prabhupada, with that new clause: “Rooms shall not to be used for commercial purposes.” But the electricity just went down. I can’t finish typing it.

Prabhupada

Yamuna, you know this—what it is? Calligraphy? You can make agreement in nice handwriting. Is that all right?

Yamuna

Yes, Srila Prabhupada!

Shyamasundar and Yamuna exit.

Kshirodakshayi

We shall have to purchase the government tax stamps for both deeds, Swamiji. 165 rupees. Can you give me one check?

Prabhupada

Government will not take check.

Kshirodakshayi

No, I will cash with my friend.

Gurudas

And I will have to go to Mathura, Prabhupada, after you sign with Gaurachand, to register the deed.

SCENE FIVE

Interior. Room Six.

Prabhupada sits with Dr. Kapoor, Krishnadas Babaji, Gurudas, and Shyamasundar.

Dr. Kapoor

So you have got how many temples now?

Prabhupada

I’m not sure.

Gurudas

You have got ninety-two temples now, Srila Prabhupada.

Prabhupada

Ninety-two?

Dr. Kapoor \[laughs\]

Ah, you don’t know yourself!

Prabhupada

Yes, actually Kapoorji, I do not know how things are going like this. I tell you frankly, I have no credit. But things are going on like this. It is something like—of course, I am not comparing exactly—just like Krishna could not understand about His potency, and therefore He became Radharani’s feature to understand Himself. Of course, I... This is just a comparison. Things are taking very wonderfully. Just see these boys and girls, how seriously they have taken.

Dr. Kapoor

This is just what strikes people. How can these people be so sincere and so earnest?

SCENE SIX:

Interior. Room Three. Night.

Prabhupada sits with all 'Vrindaban disciples. Everyone laughing.

Gurudas

You did it, Prabhupada!

Shyamasundar

Both deeds are signed!

Prabhupada

And tomorrow I shall have to go to Mathura, for notarizing?

Gurudas

Yes, along with the Sarafs. We have got one car coming at ten.

Prabhupada

Before that, we shall go to Raman Reti, make a plan for the foundation stone ceremony.

SCENE SEVEN

Exterior. Raman Reti land. Early morning.

Prabhupada walks the open land with all Vrindaban disciples. Gurudas and Shyamsundar each carry one end of a ten-foot rope. They measure off lengths and widths as Prabhupada describes each building and marks its boundaries in the sand with his cane.

Prabhupada

This will be main temple. We shall call it Krishna-Balaram temple. Put one residence building behind, here. Prasadam hall and kitchens are there, and school should be very large, one hundred meters minimum, over there. You dig one pit just here, same as Mayapur, and we shall install Ananta Sesha day after tomorrow. Is that all right?

All

Jaya Srila Prabhupada!

\[END\]

Gurudas later described this incident in his book, By His Example: “Prabhupada was like a great ringmaster in a celestial circus, with a dancing bear in one ring, a tightrope act in another, clowns in another, and lions in another. He tamed and charmed us all. And all the documents were drawn up, we got the rooms in Radha-Damodar for life, we got the land, Shyama Devi couldn’t do anything, and Krishna das Babaji chanted and laughed. It was a wonderful day at Mr. Saraf’s place.”

Except for maybe Shyama Devi, everyone was satisfied. Each thought, privately, that he’d gotten the best deal and that Prabhupada had especially favored him in the negotiation.

Many more scenes played out over the next two days: the elegant Radha-Damodar agreement in Yamuna’s fine, medieval script (which she’d learned from Lloyd Reynolds in our Reed College days); the dash to Mathura to notarize both deeds; Kshirodakshayi’s dash to Agra to buy a simple marble cornerstone; and opening the International Society for Krishna Consciousness account at Punjab Bank on Mathura Road. The few devotees in Vrindaban rushed to dig a pit, gather items for the installation, and advertise the affair. Prabhupada sat in room three, answered a hundred questions, and directed the whirl of activity around him.

On the morning of March 13, with a decorated cloth fence hastily set up around the pit, Prabhupada sits under an umbrella and presides over the cornerstone-laying ceremony, much as he did at Mayapur, but this time with only a few devotees and some old friends attending.

“Hey, Gurudas, there’s Muz Murray and his girlfriend, Jaya, from Gandalf’s Garden in London!” Gandalf’s Garden was a neo-mystical hippie community—a Merlin/Stonehenge thing. They had a head shop at World’s End in Chelsea and published a monthly magazine. They were old friends who had helped us spread Krishna consciousness in the UK, and we were glad to see them on this historic occasion in Vrindaban.

The five kinds of flowers, grains, leaves, metals, nectars, dyes, fruits, and jewels were duly laid in the pit, the fire was lit, and sacred mantras intoned. Ananta Sesha and the cornerstone were set in brick and mortar, and just two weeks after the Mayapur ceremony, Prabhupada laid the foundation for his second major temple complex in India.

The next morning, we got word that during the night some goondas—hired vandals—had dug up the pit at Raman Reti and had thrown garbage into it. They had even removed the ISKCON sign! Prabhupada hit the roof, and the police were dispatched to apprehend the guilty and guard the property. This overt desecration was possibly the work of Shyama Devi and her followers, but we never found out for sure.

As in Mayapur, doing anything constructive in Vrindaban was fraught with difficulties. Long-held grudges haunted the village, and a few envious, impoverished sadhus living in their tiny but established niches didn’t like these rich foreigners rocking the boat.

In my weekly newsletter, I reported to devotees all over the world:

Earlier in the day, before breakfast, Prabhupada sewed up the 4,800 sq. yd. gift plot in Raman Reti where Krishna used to sport with Friends in the forest, about 10 minutes’ walk from Radha-Damodar temple. Prabhupada surveyed the land (with the rope from his mosquito net), bargained, drew plans, drew up the deed, went to Mathura magistrate’s court, and signed, sealed, and delivered everything in almost an instant.

He has asked me to request you that AT LEAST FIFTY (50) MEN FROM THE U.S. MUST COME TO INDIA IMMEDIATELY!!! At last we have got a solid program in India: Huge projects at Mayapur, Vrindaban, and Bombay. All the lands are acquired, arrangements made, and everything done by Prabhupada. But only a few men and women are here to struggle with this immense task—the biggest by far within the Society—of developing these three places, and believe me, these three projects are more dear to Prabhupada than any yet contemplated.

In a letter to Meenaketan das in New York, Srila Prabhupada wrote:

We have acquired with Krishna’s grace a very large plot of land for raising up a very excellent center to revive the spiritual life of Vrindaban on behalf of Rupa and Jiva Goswamis, and also they have consented to give us at least eight very nice rooms more at the Radha-Damodara Temple, besides the two rooms and veranda already in my occupation, so it is a very big work and we require many men to come now to Vrindaban and assist, and in fact I have just requested Karandhara to send me fifty more men by end of March or as quickly as possible.

**\* \* \***

Early in the morning on March 17, three days after breaking ground for the Krishna-Balaram temple in Vrindaban, Prabhupada is on a flight from Delhi to Bombay. Prabhupada’s on a roll, a spectacular expression of energy and divine will. He had hit the ground running in Vrindaban, and now he’s blitzing Bombay, going for the trifecta at Juhu, the closing salvo, the third movement to his India Temple Symphony.

Arriving at Juhu, we can see that Brahmananda has done a good job. Nearly five acres of brush, weeds, and decades of accumulated litter have been cleared. The Bombay devotees have relocated to tents and palm-frond-and-bamboo huts spread around Camp Juhu. Srila Prabhupada will stay in the home of Mr. and Mrs. Acharya, tenants on the property; Nanda Kumar, Pradyumna, and I will camp in their living room. A blue-and-white-striped pandal has been erected near the main gate, and the Radha-Rasabihari Deities have been brought from Akash Ganga and installed there. Signs and banners on streets in the Juhu area declare, “Bhagavata Dharma Discourses: A Hare Krishna Festival, by His Divine Grace A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, March 18-27.”

The comfortable quarters downtown are abandoned, and the Bombay devotees are forced now to make the Juhu project work. This was Prabhupada’s strategy: there was still no signed conveyance deed for the Juhu land, but in India, once deities are in a place and people have taken up residence, it is almost impossible to remove them. You see this all over India, old Hanuman and Laxmi temples standing in the middle of sidewalks or roads, and traffic diverted around them. Possession is nine-tenths of the law.

Juhu was almost completely surrounded by tidal mangrove swamps and rice paddies. To the east there was a drainage ditch and slums—the perfect breeding grounds for rats, snakes, mosquitos, and disease. The hot season had begun in Bombay, temperatures in the nineties and low hundreds every day, and we had no AC—not even electric fans. Many Western devotees lay sick in their sweltering tents with amoebic dysentery, jaundice, boils, or fever. Malaria and typhoid were constant threats.

There were thirty-two families—nearly a hundred ten-ants—already living on the Juhu property in six three-story concrete apartment blocks and three single-story homes, built in the 1920s and ’30s. The recent arrival of so many Krishna devotees had really upset the tenants, many of whom were Christian or Muslim. They must have been thinking, “Who are these people? What are they doing on our land?” The noisy construction at all hours, the sudden influx of semi-naked white-skinned gorillas tearing up the place, and now loudspeakers, a stage show, hordes of strangers flocking through their gates. It was a situation ripe for confrontation.

In a small garden at the center of the Juhu apartment complex stood an eighteen-inch bronze statue of Mahatma Gandhi. It was much revered by our neighbors. One morning they awoke to discover it missing, and later in the day someone found it at the bottom of the well. The Western devotees were obvious suspects. The situation became explosive.

Brahmananda: This was like stabbing the tenants in their hearts. They went to the police, but there was no evidence as to who did it. But it took a lot of pacifying to quiet them down. Of course, we wanted them to leave, but their apartments were rent-controlled from twenty or thirty years earlier—they were paying like fifty rupees a month—so they would never leave.

Heat, disease, disgruntled neighbors, chaos, but the show must go on. On the afternoon of March 17, Prabhupada initiates several new devotees in the pandal, including some Indian boys and girls. One boy, Hans Keilman, is an architect from Holland—last year Prabhupada initiated his brother, Henk (Hari Krishna das), who was now serving in London. Prabhupada names Hans Surabhi das and immediately enlists him in drawing up blueprints for his grand building scheme at Hare Krishna Land.

Surabhi: Prabhupada said, “Don’t waste your time on making beautiful things for the wrong purpose.” And then immediately he hinted, “Krishna has sent you here to design His temples.” And then he asked the secretary. He said, “Give him some paper and some pencils so he can start designing.” Then he told Shyamasundar to give me photos of the Radha-Gopinath Mandir \[in Vrindaban\]. He wanted that type of temple to be built in Bombay.

That night Prabhupada speaks in the pandal to a small crowd of well-wishers and neighbors, announcing that he will speak, starting tomorrow night, for ten days, and he states his purpose for being in Bombay:

I was ordered by my spiritual master to go to the Western countries and spread this Krishna consciousness movement. So in 1965, in the month of September, I went to New York with forty rupees in my pocket. Blit gradu-ally—this is a long history—in a very short time this movement has spread all over the world by the cooperation of my American disciples, both boys and girls. Now we have got more than ninety branches all over the world, and we have come here in Bombay to open this center in this place where we are now sitting. So by your cooperation we wish to develop this center into a very large institution for spreading Krishna consciousness. So this is an attempt, this camp is an attempt to invite our countrymen.

You can see the neighbors mulling this over, talking quietly among themselves. Prabhupada’s charm (he kids with noisy children and gives them sweets), calm manner, and deep purpose are evident, and slowly the tide begins to turn. (“Oh, this swami, he is a little special.”) By the next night, March 18, opening night of the Bhagavata Dharma Discourses, the atmosphere is charged with expectation and a new willingness to listen. And Prabhupada delivers.

Simply by hearing. Just like we are discussing here Sriniad-Bhagavatam, Bhagavad-gita. If you kindly come here and if you continue to hear, then all perfection you will get. You don’t require to be a Sanskrit scholar or very educated j or scholar or rich. Ahaituky apratihata. All these material conditions, neither they can help you, nor they can stop you from your spiritual progress of life, it is so nice ... It does not matter what you are—you may be a very great scholar, Or you may be illiterate, you may be medical man or engineer or ordinary man—it doesn’t matter. Sthanesthi-tahsruti-gatam tanu-van-manobhih. You remain in your position; there is no need of change. San-mukharitam bhavadiya-vartam. We have to simply hear from the right source the message of Krishna. That will be sufficient. Simply by hearing.

Prabhupada speaks from Srimad-Bhagavatam, and its like going back five thousand years to the Naimisharanya forest, where Suta Goswami spoke to thousands of sages eager to hear the answer to their question, “What is the absolute and ultimate good for the people in general?” Here, in the palm forests of Juhu, for the first time since Madras more than a month earlier, Prabhupada has a worldly audience—cosmopolitan Bombayites, English-speaking, middle-class, a lot of teachers, government workers, retired people, well-educated—and Prabhupada elucidates, expands on Suta Goswami’s answer: “The supreme occupation \[dharma\] for all humanity is that by which men can attain to loving devotional service unto the transcendent Lord Krishna. Such devotional service must be unmotivated and uninterrupted to completely satisfy the self.”

After two weeks of nonstop business negotiations, I see Srila Prabhupada once more as the great philosopher and erudite sage. His flawless performance before a receptive audience of five hundred locals, night after night for ten days, unfolds the full picture of Krishna consciousness—Krishna, the individual soul, their relationship; human society, dharma—and everything is crystal clear. I’ve heard him speak these things a hundred times and I never tire of it. It’s ever fresh, like clouds parting. Each time he speaks I feel the inner excitement of seeing the truth as if for the first time, and I understand the impulse called enlightenment.

During the discourses, gradually, the mood at Juhu turns from unfriendly, almost antagonistic, to its opposite, and the community warms to Srila Prabhupada and his devotees: “Ahh, this is why they love and obey this amazing sadhu. This is why they are the way they are.”

On March 23, Prabhupada conducts another cornerstonelaying ceremony, his third in little more than three weeks. This one is the most colorful and best organized yet. It’s Rama-Navami, Lord Rama’s birthday, and the Bombay devotees are prepared for it. Strings of marigolds, champa (frangipani) flowers, and tuberoses hang from the pandal around Prabhupada’s vyasasan. The pit is much wider, allowing several devotees at a time down there, so the sacred ingredients are well placed at the bottom. In the heat of early afternoon—a low-key hour, out of sight from our 9-to-5 neighbors—Prabhupada oversees the proceedings in a jolly mood, sitting at the pit-edge, directing traffic: “Put grains here. Yogurt pot will go over there.”

“No, no,” he shouts down to me, a box of Animal Crackers in my hand. “Just take out two, three pieces, not whole box!” Some of the pious neighbors join in. The kids come home from school and gather around. Amplified kirtan resounds through the neighborhood. Ananta Sesha is bricked over, and a foot-square slab of white marble with gold-embossed engraved letters reading

THE CORNERSTONE OF

THE “ISKCON” BOMBAY CENTRE

IS LAID ON RAM-NAVAMI DAY, MARCH 23rd, 1972

is taken into the pit and mortared to the bricks. Everyone joins in throwing dirt back into the pit—gray-haired women, old men with canes, a skinny gang of schoolboys, the devotees. Everyone grabs a pot, a basket, whatever container they can find, and joins the workmen in a bucket brigade between pile and pit, filling the hole. Prabhupada beats on a gong and laughs and passes out sweets.

That night, Srila Prabhupada turns serious. He has just committed to three massive projects in Mayapur, Vrindaban, and now Bombay. The work ahead of us is vast. He calls us together and asks, “How you will manage?” Millions of dollars must be raised, huge construction must be planned and executed, and we are so few and so inexperienced. He is especially concerned about who will manage things in Juhu.

There are two main candidates: Brahmananda and Madhudvisa. But things are not going well in Africa in Brahmananda’s absence, and Prabhupadas not sure of Brahmananda’s ability to manage money. Big В has contracted the pandal builders for Rs. 40,000/-, which is way too much; Prabhupada pays them Rs. 4000/-, calls them thieves, and they go away—not happy, but satisfied. Madhudvisa is still unsettled, wants to travel, and tells Prabhupada he’d like to go to Australia to preach. Giriraj, who was an enthusiastic collector for the Cross Maidan Festival in Bombay last year and who is good with important people, is still in Calcutta. Recently, Giriraj wrote that he’d like to serve in Bombay, so Prabhupada writes back to him that night:

I am very glad to inform you that today we have laid down the cornerstone for our Bombay Center here in Juhu, and the ceremony was very wonderful with many important people attending ... So now I think you should join me in Bombay immediately as you are free, after training up a competent replacement there. We have also a few days back laid the cornerstone for our Vrindaban Temple, so the program now in India is immense task.... You shall come here at the soonest, latest by the 27th March 1972.

On the morning of March 24, the Times of India published an article headlined “KRISHNA TEMPLE”:

The cornerstone for the proposed Krishna Temple and multi-storeyed building to house Hare Krishna City under the auspices of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness was laid on Thursday at a Vedic ceremony at Juhu by the founder of the Hare Krishna movement, Swami Prabhupada. Hare Krishna City will be built on the 20,000-square-metre site acquired by the society. It will have a massive temple of Lord Krishna, a co-operative housing society, an international university for Vedic studies, gurukula for children, a goshala, a public library and residential blocks. Swami Prabhupada told reporters that a feature of the city would be the international guest house exclusively for the use of tourists who would like to study the spiritual aspects of life.

**\* \* \***

Brahmananda returns to Nairobi, Giriraj arrives in Juhu; the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust is set up and registered with Prabhupada, Giriraj, and me as trustees; Surabhi and Naranarayan are drawing blueprints and building a scale model of Hare Krishna Land. Each morning Prabhupada walks up and down the nearby beach with his Juhu team, empowering them for the “immense task” ahead.

Surabhi: I could feel yes, there was something that gave me the energy, something that allowed me to do what normally I wouldn't be able to do, and I could feel what it means to be empowered. And when Prabhupada tells you, “You are going to do this," and it actually happens, you believe for the rest of your life that nothing is impossible.

Someone tells me they have recently seen a newspaper article stating that George Harrison was in a bad traffic accident. I use the Acharyas’ phone and ring Pattie at Friar Park. She assures me they’re both OK and gets George on the line. In a burst of excited chatter (you never know when the line will go dead on overseas calls from India) we catch up. He says the Bangladesh album is still at the top of the charts, he’s writing more songs about Krishna, and I tell him about the three new India projects. He asks, “When is Prabhupada coming back to the UK?”

“I don’t know, George. We’re leaving for Australia and Hong Kong in a few days, working our way east, in your direction.” He says maybe he’ll see us in the US—he’s going to New York soon.

“But we’re never really that far apart, are we, George?” I say. He says, “Haribol!” and we get cut off.

I too get sick during the last week of March, with some kind of fever, and Nanda Kumar takes over my typing chores for a few days. Sweating it out in bed I think about everything that’s been happening. In less than one month Srila Prabhupada has done the impossible: he’s laid the cornerstones for his main three temples in India, despite all odds: Naxalite bandits, floods, cold, heat, lack of money, envious opponents, unsupportive godbrothers, caste goswamis, a communist-leaning central government under martial law with strict rationing of construction materials, our shaky legal standing, and a handful of young but worn-out devotees. The lands we have acquired are so old, with so many contending proprietors, and so large and in such vital locations—to even think of owning these properties is almost surreal. We are recent arrivals on Indian soil, with very little organization or social standing. And yet despite these obstacles, Prabhupada’s unrelenting desire to please Krishna has somehow prevailed. He did it! Seeing all this from the inside—typing his letters, making phone calls, huddled with him in late-night strategy sessions—I am in awe. This is Superman beyond anything yet, and he is just getting started.

I remember Prabhupada’s purport to Chaitanya-charitamrita, Adi 7.61, where he comments on Lord Chaitanya’s wondrous activities, so relevant now to what I am actually seeing:

To draw the attention of common men, sometimes saintly persons, acbaryas, and teachers exhibit extraordinary opulences. This is necessary to attract the attention of fools, but a saintly person should not misuse such power for personal sense gratification like false saints who declare themselves to be God. Even a magician can exhibit extraordinary feats that are not understandable to common men, but this does not mean that the magician is God. It is a most sinful activity to attract attention by exhibiting mystic powers and then to utilize this opportunity to declare oneself to be God. A real saintly person never declares himself to be God, but always places himself in the position of a servant of God. For a servant of God there is no need to exhibit mystic powers, and he does not like to do so, but on behalf of the Supreme Personality of Godhead a humble servant of God performs his activities in such a wonderful way that no common man can dare try to act like him. Yet a saintly person never takes credit for such actions because he knows very well that when wonderful things are done on his behalf by the grace of the Supreme Lord, all credit goes to the master and not to the servant.

By the end of March 1972, Prabhupada has consolidated his trusted devotee platoons for construction work on three major temples. Jayapataka, Bhavananda, Achyutananda, and Tamal Krishna will command the cadre in Mayapur; Gurudas, Yamuna, Kshirodakshayi, and Subal Swami will manage Vrindaban. Here at Juhu, Giriraj, Gargamuni, Naranarayan, and Surabhi will administer the work. Srila Prabhupada is free now to advance on another, more distant front.

On the evening of March 31, we leave India and fly to Austra ia and the Far Orient.

## Six

Nanda Kumar: Prabhupada, your disciples—will we develop at least half your energy to continually preach someday?

Prabhupada: Why not full or double? You may have double of my energy.

Nanda Kumar: That’s inconceivable.

Prabhupada: Just like my Guru Maharaja did not travel all over the world, so I have got double energy than him. So you must have triple energy, four times energy than me. Then actually you are my disciple. I am doing ten times the work of my Guru Maharaja. So if an ordinary man like me can do ten times, you are Americans, so you can do twenty times. Then you are successful.

Nanda Kumar: If we can do twenty times, it’s only because you gave us the energy.

Prabhupada: Yes. My blessings are there. You do it. Try.

Shyamasundar: Just like Tushta Krishna and his wife. They hitchhiked all the way across Australia, Srila Prabhupada.

Prabhupada: Just see.

Shyamasundar: They landed by ship in Western Australia, at Perth, where our plane first landed last night. And then they hitchhiked across Australia, 1,500 miles through the desert, here, to Sydney.

Prabhupada: Just see.

Shyamasundar: Tushta said sometimes a car would not come by for many hours, and they would be standing in the hot sun, no trees or anything.

Prabhupada: Oh, desert—big desert?

Shyamasundar: Australia has a huge desert, and somehow they made their way across.

Nanda Kumar: He said that aborigines live there.

Prabhupada: Oh, they may attack?

Shyamasundar: And kangaroos—they saw some kangaroos. Those big animals that jump.

Prabhupada: They live in the desert, the kangaroos?

Pradyumna: There are many strange animals in Australia, Prabhupada.

Prabhupada: Predators?

Pradyumna: No, but strange, very strange animals. The marsupials. Shyamasundar: Those animals that bear their young in a pouch. Prabhupada: That is kangaroo.

Nanda Kumar: And platypuses. The platypus animal.

Shyamasundar: Half-bird, half-mammal. They live out of water and under the water. They have a bill like a duck and they have webbed feet and lay eggs like a bird, yet —

Nanda Kumar: And they have fur and a tail like a beaver.

Prabhupada: So the Vedic literature says there are 8,400,000 species of life.

Nanda Kumar: This platypus has all the species in one.

Prabhupada: This is Krishna’s mercy. If somebody is thinking, “If only I had like this, like that, like this, like that,” then Krishna says, “All right you will have it all.” \[laughter\] So we are trying to take facilities from Krishna, beginning from “O God, give us our daily bread, then give me motorcar, then give me airplane, then give me this, give me that.” So Krishna says, “Yes, you take all. But don’t talk of happiness, please. Sarva-dharman parityajya mam ekam—just surrender to Me if you want true happiness. Otherwise, you take whatever you like.”

Shyamasundar: The devotees, they don’t care. They’ll go across the Australian desert hitchhiking, but Krishna gives them happiness. And today, Tushta Krishna and his wife, Tulsi Krishna, are leaving here for New Zealand to start a temple over there. They are very happy.

Prabhupada: Yes, they are going for Krishna. That is happiness. They forget the trouble. Don’t be doubtful about this in the least. You may do or not do, but at least you be convinced that this is the nicest thing we have got. And there is no comparison.

Shyamasundar: And the more we experience it, the more we become convinced.

Prabhupada: Yes.

Prabhupada just wouldn’t stop talking. Since we had left the Bombay airport on Friday evening, March 31, 1972, more than twenty-four hours earlier, he hadn’t slowed down a bit. Now he’s sitting late at night in an upstairs room in a borrowed white terrace house at 26 Renny Street in the Paddington district of Sydney, Australia, talking with Pradyumna, Nanda Kumar, and me, and with his ISKCON Australia leaders Upendra and Mohanananda, about every subject under the crescent moon, including the flowers in a vase on his desk.

Upendra: The smell, the flavor, only comes out at night.

Prabhupada: Rajnigandha. They are called rajnigandha. What do you call in English?

Upendra: Night Queen.

Prabhupada: Just see Krishna’s wonderful creation. They will say, “There is no brain to create such nice flower. It is automatic; it is nature.” What is this nature? These fools have no explanation. We have got our explanation: Krishna. His energy is working. Svabhaviki jnana-bala-kriya ca. His energy is so fine and quick that automatically the flower color has come, the beautiful form has come. Even in modern science you want to talk with a friend, telephone is there. “Yes, I am speaking.” Actually the man is a thousand miles away, but by scientific arrangement he has come before me and is talking. The process is there. Just like modern scientific method has minimized space and time. For coming to Australia, fifty years ago it would have taken six months. Now we come in six hours. The process of coming is there. Similarly, in manufacturing this flower, the process is there. So if this electric, mechanical energy can work so wonderful, how much wonderful energies are there in Krishna?

This leads to the topic of devotional service, the process of becoming mad after Krishna, and Prabhupada has me read aloud to him from The Nectar of Devotion, the opening paragraphs of the second chapter, “The First Stages of Devotion.” He becomes absorbed in the words, goes into a sort of trance, and listens with eyes closed as I read on and on. I keep looking over to see if he wants me to stop, but then I read, “The Padma Purana recommends that one always fix his mind on the form of Vishnu by meditation and not forget Him at any moment. And this state of consciousness is called samadhi, or trance.” Ah, I get it. Twenty-five minutes and seven pages later I break the spell and say, “Prabhupada, it is so clear.”

Then we talk about wrestling.

Shyamasundar: Whenever someone challenges you, Prabhupada, you always seem to know the trick, what their weak point is, the weakness in their challenge—and you go right there. Every time.

Prabhupada: That is the trick of wrestling, \[laughter\] If you are a wrestler, even you are not very strong you must know the trick how to defeat the strongest man.

Earlier today, Allen Ginsberg had come by the Sydney temple to see Srila Prabhupada, but when someone told him that Prabhupada had traveled all night and had arrived in Sydney only this morning, and that he was at the Renny Street place a few miles away, Allen said, “Let him rest. I’ll come back later.”

Prabhupada: You are actually trying to love Krishna. But because nobody is giving you Krishna, you are crying.

Shyamasundar: Prabhupada, that was the name of Allen

Ginsberg’s first book, Howl. Howl means a protest, like loud crying. “You have not given us the right information,” Ginsberg says to his elders. “Now what do we do? Simply howl.” His book was the message of our generation, and we all read that book, the howl-protest.

Prabhupada: Allen is searching. Therefore he comes. Whenever he finds opportunity he comes to me. He’s searching after Krishna. He likes me. \[laughter\] Simply his only objection is that I am very conservative. He said, “Swamiji, you are very conservative.” Because I forbid so many things. He cannot give up his bad habits. Otherwise he would have joined me. But he cannot.

We then talk about freeways and the laughable race of modern civilization to keep pace with its own inventions.

Prabhupada: Vicious civilization. Simply increasing, increasing, increasing. And the government taking tax; therefore he has to prepare roads. So in your country, the more the motorcar increasing, the more flying over, more bridges... Just like in Madras we saw—they are also imitating.

Shyamasundar: Little tiny... \[laughs\]

Prabhupada: Little ti... ” \[chuckles\]

Nanda Kumar: A little tiny flyover. Prabhupada: Yes, they are advancing. Shyamasundar: Only two lanes.

Prabhupada: And doing it for unlimited years. When they will be finished, there is no guarantee. Here in Australia they have contract, “Yes, within six months.” But in India there is no guarantee. But that show is going on, that “We are doing something.” It is imitation. There was no need, but they want to make advanced like the Americans. In Bombay also, they made one or two flyovers with great endeavor, and one flyover collapsed. That flyover between that Princess Street and Marine Drive, yes, collapsed. Because all the contractors are thieves. Instead of giving cement, they are giving clay.

Shyamasundar: Now their plan is to tunnel through under Malabar Hill and make a freeway, the West Shore Expressway, along Warden Road and Nepean Sea Road. They began the tunnel when we first came there a year and a half ago, and now I don’t think it’s more than fifty yards into the mountain.

Prabhupada: The tunnel is there? Where?

Shyamasundar: As you go up Nepean Sea Road from Akash Ganga, just past the Russian Consulate, then there is an open space—in there.

It’s getting so late, after midnight now, officially April 2, and Prabhupada hasn’t rested in about two days. His elbows are propped on a low table-desk, head in his hands in a sort of reverie. Prabhupada appears to tower over us, his stature magnified by the greatness of his words, his control over every situation, his fearless intensity, and his gigantic spirit. Our point of view begins at his feet, looking up as if he’s way over our heads. His movements are so graceful, light, and expressive, yet heavy with meaning. He is guru, heavy. He seems so dominant, so huge in our eyes, yet I just saw the details in his passport as we passed through Australian customs: Abhay Charanaravinda Bhaktivedanta Swami, whose father is listed as “Late Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati Goswami Maharaj Prabhupada” and whose visible distinguishing mark is “Vishnu tilaka on forehead.” He is just 1 m 60 cm (5 feet 3 inches) tall and maybe soaking wet he weighs 120 pounds. He’s in that special zone now, wired but tired yet kind of drifting. “Where was I? Oh, yes.” He’s in bliss and wants to keep talking. Now he gets a snack attack.

Prabhupada: Have you got puffed rice?

Mohanananda: In the shop. You have to get it from the shop. Prabhupada: But I don’t think you can get now.

Nanda Kumar: We could try.

Shyamasundar: Any stores open around here? Any supermarket?

Mohanananda: It’s after midnight, Easter Sunday. There’s one little shop. They always carry puffed rice. I’ll go see.”

Prabhupada: You have got peanuts?

Mohanananda: Peanuts and milk?

Prabhupada: No, puffed rice and peanuts, and milk separate. \[laughter\]

I always marvel at Prabhupadas boundless energy, but tonight I’m in total awe—we’ve been going at high speed for more than two days under very difficult conditions. We had left Bombay fully expecting to spend two days in Singapore-Bali Mardan had been preaching in Singapore and had stirred up interest among leaders of the Indian community there. A Mr. Jamnadas Bhajwani had booked a big auditorium in Singapore where Prabhupada would speak, and they had distributed hundreds of tickets. After the six-hour flight from Bombay, even at a very late hour we could hear kirtan somewhere outside the customs and immigration hall at Singapore’s Changi Airport.

I stepped up with Prabhupada to the official behind the counter and handed him our passports. The official said, “Is that your lot out there?” We smiled and said, “Yes.” He said, “I’m to inform you that your entry into Singapore has been denied. You must make arrangements to travel onward or return to your port of origin.”

What?! It was like a repeat of the Bombay quarantine scene: a deep pit opened in my stomach. I blustered and pleaded, “But good sir, so many arrangements have been made!” The officer was adamant, though, and soon two constables appeared and hustled us back to the vast international transit lounge.

Prabhupada looked tired, disgusted. I’d heard that Singapore was kind of a police state with its strict controls-spitting on the sidewalk or chewing bubblegum were offenses punishable by caning on your bare feet—and apparently there was a “no swamis allowed” law too. What to do? “You just try to book the next flight to Australia,” Prabhupada said.

I ran around the almost deserted airport and was finally able to ticket us on the next flight to Sydney, via Perth, which would leave in about four hours. I didn’t have a phone number for the

Sydney temple, so I sent them a telegram with our new arrival details, hoping someone would receive it in time. Prabhupada was not feeling well. After his superhuman push in India to establish three major temples in a very short span, this was a real letdown. We ate from the tiffins Nanda Kumar had packed, chanted, and chatted—and you can imagine Prabhupada’s diatribes about governments in the Age of Kali. I found a stall that had fresh-squeezed pineapple juice, and after a few glasses Prabhupada seemed to perk up.

Then followed another grueling, ten-hour flight with an hour layover in Perth. Our arrival in Sydney on the 'morning of April 1 was also a near disaster: Australian customs authorities confiscated the fragile clay mridanga Pradyumna had so carefully hand-carried. Seems the rawhide heads and bindings might carry bubonic plague or something. So the precious heart of our kir-tan was whisked off to a weeklong quarantine. Jeez, what next? And no devotees were at the airport to greet us—they must not have received the telegram. We climbed into a cab, exhausted by all our travails, and drove through bustling morning traffic to 83 Hereford Street, in the Glebe district of Sydney.

A swarm of devotees met us on the sidewalk out front—so fresh, so quick, so unprepared—and their bright-faced enthusiasm immediately lifted our mood. Upendra was there, our pioneer friend from San Francisco; Mohanananda, the energetic red-haired American temple president; brave Hanuman; Vegavan and Padmavati, who had been married onstage at Cross Maidan; Tirthapada from London—maybe thirty healthy, tall, vital Western devotees, known around the streets of Sydney as the “ecstasy crew,” greeted us, overjoyed to see their spiritual master two days earlier than expected.

Mohanananda suggested a tour of the grounds, and Prabhupada inspected the incense factory housed in a ramshackle shed and the small printing press in another shed, where Prabhupada examined the latest Australian Back to Godhead magazine. We looked at the temple store, where they sold incense and a few Indian clothes, the art studio, and last but not least, the London double-decker bus parked out back, “Hare Krishna Movement” painted in big red letters along its side. Prabhupada was most impressed by the bus, which had been modified for traveling sankirtana, and said, “I could live in this.” Then we went into the temple itself, where the devotees had gone all out to prepare an elaborate altar for Sri Sri Radha-Gopinatha, the Deities Prabhupada and Vegavan had brought here one year earlier.

The Krishna devotees had been in Australia for more than two years—since February 1970—and there were strong temples in Sydney and Melbourne. The Hare Krishna movement was now an established feature of the emerging youth scene in the land Down Under. Our Hare Krishna Mantra record had hit #4 on the Aussie singles charts in late ’69, and Georges All Things Must Pass was #1 for eight weeks in 1971. The hippie explosions in San Francisco and London were pretty much over by the close of 1970, but in April 1972, the hippie culture was just peaking in Australia, and Srila Prabhupada was still riding that wave of radical social change. It was so refreshing to be here!

It was autumn in Australia. The air was brisk, invigorating after the sweltering heat of Bombay. After months in difficult, ritualbound India, we were excited to be sailing again in uncharted waters with a limitless future for Krishna consciousness in this marvelous new land. Australia was like Britain—organized, busy, traditional, and clean—but boisterous with an American-like flair and daring and the pioneer spirit of “let’s get ’er done.” After a tough six months in India, our little party must have appeared like shell-shocked veterans from the Normandy invasion on our first R&R from the war zone, or maybe like polar explorers boarding the rescue ship, wide-eyed and gaunt from a slog across Antarctica. Even Prabhupada had bags under his eyes. It felt good to be in a place where everything worked, law and order prevailed, where you could buy goods wrapped in neat packages for a fixed price. After the day-to-day uncertainties of India, in Australia I didn’t have to worry so much whether Prabhupada had this or that or what would happen next. Fellow Westerners were taking care of everything now, so I could let go and talk and chant and dance with abandon.

After a short arrival lecture and kirtan at the Hereford Street temple, we shifted to the Renny Street apartment, where Upendra’s friend, a man who wanted to write a book about the Hare Krishna movement, was waiting to interview Prabhupada. No rest for the weary. The guy starts asking about our dress and our shaved heads, and Prabhupada is a bit testy: “Don’t spoil your time with biography; philosophy is more important,” he says. “Because we know our business, therefore we are describing our facts very nicely. You are an outsider, so you cannot place our pros and cons so nicely.” Then it was back to the temple, where Prabhupada lectured for two more hours before returning to Renney Street, where he conversed late into the night with his disciples. Mohanananda had gone for puffed rice, but the store was closed.

Somewhere around one in the morning on April 2, we take our leave from Prabhupada, but as I head down the hall for a welcome mattress on the floor, Prabhupada’s little bell signals me back to his room: "Accha, there are any mails for us?”

“Yes, Prabhupada, there’s a thick envelope from Rupanuga, letters from Gurudas, Hamsaduta, Kshirodakshayi, and a couple others—oh, and here’s one from Rebatinandan, from London.”

“Just open and read.”

The letter from Rupanuga is a blockbuster. It seems that nine GBC men had met for four days in New York City without informing Prabhupada and had passed all sorts of resolutions. Rupanuga had sent the minutes of the meeting. Prabhupada hits the ceiling. He has me read each point again and again. We discuss them in great detail as he dictates his reply.
