Forty-eight hours without sleep and he’s still sharp as a tack, but a little cranky. According to Rupanuga, Atreya Rishi—whom Prabhupada doesn’t really know well yet—has been selected secretary for the GBC and is to receive all GBC correspondence, including monthly reports. Furthermore, Atreya is appointed to the management committee, with Karandhar, to supervise all ISKCON business and implement decisions reached by the GBC. And Hamsaduta had left his duties in Germany to attend the New York meeting and now wants to stay in the USA.

“I do not favor these changes! Tell them to stop everything. Why they have done this without consulting me? This news has very much disturbed me. Sriman Atreya Rishi das may be very expert, but without my say he has been given so much power and this has upset my brain.”

Prabhupada dictates a telegram to be sent out to the nine ISKCON leaders:

YOUR MATERIAL LEGAL FORMULA WILL NOT HELP US ONLY OUR SPIRITUAL LIFE CAN HELP US I HAVE NOT APPROVAL FOR ANY THESE PLANS STOP HAMSADUTA MUST RETURN GERMANY IMMEDIATELY DON’T LEAVE AGAIN STOP ATREYA RISHI HAS NO AUTHORITY FROM ME TO MANAGE ANYTHING REMOVE HIM ACKNOWLEDGE CABLE URGENT 26 RENNY STREET PADDINGTON SYDNEY

Dawn lights the early sky over Sydney, and Prabhupada answers another half dozen letters before saying, “Now it is very late. I think we shall not take walk this morning. Just you go take a little rest.”

Times like these I hurt inside and feel a helpless sympathy for Prabhupada having to deal constantly with such stressful matters as he builds his worldwide society for Krishna. But I know the pain will pass, that Prabhupada is a paramahamsa, unaffected by events around him and, like the shadow of a passing cloud, moments later he will laugh again.

That Easter afternoon, Prabhupada lectured at the temple for the Sunday feast. At 10 a.m. the next morning, April 3, Prabhupada was interviewed and filmed by television reporters in his quarters on Renny Street. At 11:30 he gave an interview to The Daily Mirror. In the afternoon he spoke at Sydney University. That evening he spoke at the temple. Over the next two days Prabhupada appeared on two more TV shows and lectured in the temple morning and night. He might have slept four or five hours over the past five days—I don’t know, I never saw him actually asleep. Where did all his energy come from?

On April 5 we flew to Melbourne. Prabhupada gave a press conference at the airport. The next morning the newspapers announced: “Tears Greet the Swami,” “Swami Gets a Flower Welcome,” and ‘“Spiritual Master of the Universe’ Drops In By Jet.” On the morning of April 6, Prabhupada installed Radha-Krishna Deities in the temple at 14 Burnett Street in the beachside suburb of St. Kilda and then initiated a dozen new disciples, conducted a wedding, and gave Hanuman the sannyasa order of life.

**\* \* \***

That same afternoon, we head for a Cistercian monastery near the small town of Yarra Glen, forty miles northeast of Melbourne. Prabhupada usually wants me to drive him wherever we are. I guess he’s trusted my driving since the early days, when we hurtled down the 101 in San Francisco together. Each time we were somewhere different, though, I’d have to sit behind the wheel in some new weird car and try to figure out where the turn signals were, usually hitting the windshield wipers instead. Today, I’m driving a medium-sized Australian Holden sedan, Prabhupada in the passenger seat, heading north in rush-hour traffic on the “wrong” side of the road and trying to follow directions uttered by Upananda from the back seat in his Aussie twang. Eventually the traffic clears, and when we at last turn right up a narrow, winding, gravel road, we find ourselves transported a thousand years into the past.

The Cistercian Order of the Strict Observance, better known as the Trappists, is an austere order of Catholic monks that traces its roots back to A.D. 1098 The Trappists lead a regulated life of contemplation, prayer, and congregational chanting, with an emphasis on manual labor and self-sufficiency. In 1954, a group of Irish monks moved onto a thousand-acre tract in the rolling hills near Melbourne and called it Tarrawarra Abbey. It’s autumn now, harvest season on this side of the world, and as we motor up the mile-long drive lined with giant eucalyptus trees to the hilltop abbey, we’re struck by the bountiful countryside around us: sheaves of wheat stacked in the fields, huge baskets of grapes piled at the ends of rows in the vineyards, cows grazing in belly-high grass.

“What are those mountains in the distance, Upananda, behind the abbey?”

“Those are the Victorian Alps, part of the Great Dividing Range-they’re covered in snow in winter.”

The whole scene looks like an idyllic Romantic pastoral painting. We explain to Prabhupada what little we know about cloistered Christian monks, their place in Western history, and how in isolation they have preserved the strict principles of their faith, and Prabhupada says, “Yes, it is just like our New Vrindaban ashram.”

As we pull into the courtyard, the sense of returning to a distant time is augmented by the sight of thirty monks, black scapulars over white cassocks, gathered to greet us-one now coming forward to reverently place a garland of fresh-cut roses around Prabhupada’s neck and then bow, head to the ground, like the pope has come to visit. Most of the monks bow with him, and we know this is going to be a special occasion. Father Daniel guides Prabhupada to an elaborate, elevated chair in the timber-paneled hall, and the brotherhood assembles before him on simple wooden chairs.

As usual, Prabhupada is amazing. He pitches his words perfectly, finding the common ground. He notes the historical aspects of both our faith and theirs, saying that religion is sanatana-dharma, the “eternal occupation,” and that the origins of Krishna consciousness are “practically prehistoric,” establishing his precedence in ancient authority. And like you ascetic Catholics, he says, we understand the importance of parampara, the generational handing down of pure religious principles, unchanged, through disciplic succession.

We are in rare company. Prabhupada says, “Out of many millions of persons, one is interested to make his life successful. And out of millions of successful persons, one can very rarely understand what is God.” And this is our strongest bond, he says: “Our definition of religion is to accept the orders of God. That’s all. It doesn’t matter what religion you are following. You may be Christian, I may be Hindu, another may be Muhammadan, but the test of religion is how one has developed his God consciousness.”

The monks eat it up, seeing that our faith and practices are so similar to theirs, even down to the shaved heads and exotic robes. There is a feeling of instant brotherhood, cross-institutional familiarity. Prabhupada is the dynamic authority, delivering the gospel in a way they understand. These men are well versed in Latin, so Prabhupada’s liberal use of Sanskrit only solidifies their conviction that he is for real. Forefinger and thumb of his left hand resting on thigh and curled in a circle, his right hand flows from one powerful gesture to another: two fingers pointing, this way, that way, then clutching the air, then forming his palm in a cup, always moving, in mudras of emphasis. Sometimes he scratches his neck or raises his glasses, closes his eyes tight, then opens them wide and looks into their hearts—and the monks are transfixed.

After the lecture, Hanuman Swami opens the floor to questions, and eight monks, some with lilting Irish brogues, stand to make profound queries, like, “How do we know God?” and

“When one becomes sinless, would he then experience the indwelling of God?” Prabhupada answers all questions to their complete satisfaction.

We follow with kirtan, which quickly moves from the sublime to the boisterous as the monks push their chairs away and clap and dance with us, some laughing with glee despite themselves, their innocent faces glistening with holy rapture. Ba-boom-BOOM! Kirtan ends, the monks bow down while Prabhupada recites Sanskrit prayers, then rise as one, somewhat stunned. (I’m thinking, “See, it’s the same thing, but we have more fun!”)

Father Daniel raises his arms, hushes everyone, and says, “We would like to thank Swami Prabhupada for his kindness and extreme gentleness in coming to us. We’ve listened with respect to the words of life divine, and they have warmed my heart, anyway, and given me hope for a family of human brothers to which we belong. I think we are closer to one another now, to the devotees of the temple. It’s very beautiful coming here tonight, and 1 certainly feel that this little meeting, the human brothers under a common Father, is a particular inspiration for coming in contact with a God whom we’re all reaching out for but who has many faces. Thank you very much.”

After the meeting we gather in the rectory around a long wooden table laden with fresh vegetarian food, a banquet right out of the Middle Ages, with heaps of grapes, platters of baked potatoes, and loaves of fresh-baked bread. I speak with the monk seated next to me, and he tells me there are about four thousand Cistercian monks and nuns living in 170 similar retreats worldwide. “Yeah, we call them ashrams,” I say. “You’ll find such places all over India.” He describes the monks’ daily routine, which surprisingly is much like ours:

4 a.m.: Office of Vigils—reading from scripture

6 a.m.: Office of Lauds—Mass, taking the Eucharist, an hour of prayer, then silent breakfast

8 a.m.: Office of Terce, then work in the fields or buildings for three hours

11:15 a.m.: Office of Sext—followed by main daily meal, silent while someone reads aloud, nap

1:40 p.m.: Office of None followed by four hours of work

6 p.m.: Office of Vespers, then a light evening meal, conversation allowed

8 p.m.: Office of Compline, which is sung in unison in the darkness, including Psalm 91:

Yow set your love on me so I will save you;

Protect you for you know my name.

When you call I shall answer: “I am with you” I will save you in distress and give you glory.

Followed by a final hymn, “Salve Regina”:

Hail, holy Queen, Mother of Mercy!

Our life, our sweetness, and our hope ... O clemens, o pia, o dulcis Virgo Maria.

One of the monks points to a picture of Mother Mary hanging on the wall and says with reverence, “She is the object of our love.” I say, “Yeah, she looks like Krishna’s mother, Yasoda.”

After eating, Prabhupada is led to a room to meet Dom Kevin O’Farrell, the old abbot of the monastery, who had come here from Ireland in 1959. They appear to be about the same age, bur Prabhupada is actually twenty-three years older and, compared to the abbot, he seems like a young kid. The two kibitz like old friends, immediately intimate. Prabhupada swings back and forth in a creaking rocking chair, laughing and sharing love of God with this jovial Christian elder for nearly an hour. Then it’s time to leave. There is an emotional leave-taking, even a few tears at parting after such wonderful communion, and as I look back from the car at the monks lined up and waving, I’m reminded of the theme throughout George Harrisons castle at Friar Park: jolly friars wishing you the best. Man, would George love to see this place.

We drive away through the bucolic, now moonlit landscape, sated with joy by this phenomenal meeting across the centuries. Srila Prabhupada chats all the way back to Melbourne, saying, “This is pure Christianity, but still they are raising cows for slaughter. This is the only defect.” This meeting with an ancient order of Christian monks is another first, much more rewarding and instructional than our brief meeting with the Anglican archdeacon at Westminster Abbey in 1969. And we have chased down another major rhino with the Swami.

**\* \* \***

On April 7, Prabhupada spoke to two thousand people at Melbourne Town Hall, and the following day we flew back to Sydney. On April 9, Prabhupada initiated fifteen more boys and girls in the Hereford Street temple. Over the next four days Prabhupada caught up on correspondence and worked on his books at Renny Street, lecturing in the temple mornings and evenings. Then Tushta Krishna phoned and said they were ready to receive Srila Prabhupada in New Zealand. Flights from Sydney to Auckland were heavily booked, so on April 13 Prabhupada flew to Auckland alone. When I voiced my concern, Prabhupada said, “I came to you alone—Krishna is always with me, so there is no question of ‘alone.’”

Nanda Kumar, Pradyumna, and I followed him a few hours later. After three hours in the air, and as we descended over Auckland, we were impressed by the city’s strategic location on an isthmus between the Tasman Sea and the Pacific Ocean-a magnificent port city spread out, green, and very beautiful. We were much relieved to see Prabhupada lounging in his room at 155 Landscape Road in Auckland’s Mount Eden suburb. He was wrapped in a blanket against the crisp autumn cold, grinning and glad to see his aides-de-camp, his disheveled but eager entourage.

The small, burgundy prefabricated house smelled of fresh paint. Tushta Krishna and about fifteen devotees had done an amazing job finding and renting a place and then getting it ready for Prabhupada, all in less than two weeks. Eden was a suitable name for the area. As Prabhupada toured the verdant backyard, Tushta pointed out apple, papaya, and orange trees. Prabhupada said that this was an auspicious combination, the perfect climatic blend of temperate and tropical, fruits of the north and south together in one place. Nanda Kumar brought him milk and cheese, and Prabhupada said that New Zealand milk products were the best he had ever tasted.

Srila Prabhupada wanted to be in Tokyo no later than April 20 in order to meet with Dai Nippon regarding the printing of BTG and his books. He was especially concerned about the delivery of his unabridged Bhagavad-Gita As It Is, which had been promised for June but then delayed until August. Printing his books was now foremost in Prabhupada’s mind. As always, our travel itinerary was open to change, and this New Zealand leg had not been part of the original plan.

So on the morning of April 14, I rush into town to get visas for Japan and fix our tickets, which were originally booked from Sydney to Tokyo. I drop our passports at the Japan Consulate General on Shortland Street, then run to the Japanese Airlines office a few blocks over, on Albert Street. JAL honors our round-the-world tickets and rebooks us Auckland—Tokyo, leaving on April 19. But there is no direct flight, so we’ll have to overnight in Hong Kong. The airline offers to pay for our lodging at the Park Hotel plus transport to and from the airport, leaving Hong Kong for Tokyo on the following morning.

When I return to Landscape Road, Prabhupada says, “You just phone Bhurijan in Hong Kong. We shall see what he is doing-if he can arrange any speaking engagement—and then he may accompany me onward to Tokyo.” Since late 1970, Bhurijan and his wife, Jagattarini, had been struggling to maintain a small ISKCON temple in Hong Kong. (Jagattarini-Janne Wesley-was a budding Aussie actress, costarring with Mick Jagger in the iconic film Ned Kelly, before she met Srila Prabhupada on a trip to Hollywood and became a Hare Krishna devotee.) When I call Bhurijan, he is pleasantly surprised and says he will try to organize some programs.

**\* \* \***

Town halls seem to be the venue of choice in this part of the world, and on the night of April 14, Prabhupada speaks to a full house at Auckland Town Hall, in the city center, a twenty-minute drive from Landscape Road. Srila Prabhupada is energized by the presence of so many inquisitive and intelligent minds; after seven months in Africa and India, here is a Western audience again, and he gets a chance to use his favorite tools: logic and argument.

“The question may be raised that how I became Krishnas representative,” is Prabhupadas’s opening theme. “The answer is also there, ready. We have got a disciplic succession,” and one by one Prabhupada names the long list of his spiritual predecessors. “I am simply repeating Krishna’s words as they are. Therefore you are hearing the truth. According to Vedic system, amongst the learned scholars, if one presents Vedic evidences, then his position is strong. Just like in law court, two lawyers are arguing. One lawyer who quotes from law books various bona fide quotations, the judgment is given in his favor because that is authorized.

“There are three kinds of evidences. First evidence is direct perception—I see something personally. And then historical evidence, and then shabda-pramana. Shabda-pramana means evidence from the Vedas. Out of three kinds of evidences, the evidence received from Vedas, that is essential. Tad-vijnanartham sa gurum eva ’bhi-gacchet. This word abhigacchet—it is a form of verb which is used where the sense is ‘You must! You must!’ There is no exception. You cannot say that ‘I may go to a spiritual master or I may not go. I can study at home.’ No. Just like in modern age also, if you are actually interested to be recognized as educated, you must get your admission in a recognized school or college and take degree. If you study at home, you may be very great scholar, but if you say that you have not passed MA examination, nobody will care for you. Vijnana means science. Spiritual knowledge is also a science. It is not sentiment; it is science.”

Although I have heard Prabhupada’s lectures now almost daily for years, I am always astounded by the nuances and deep layers he weaves around each of his simple themes, as if I’m hearing them for the first time. In each lecture he comes up with some new statement that blows my mind. Setting the scene for Bhagavad-gita, the fight between opposing armies, Prabhupada says: “So dharma-kshetre kuru-kshetre, samaveta yuyutsavah. Yuyutsavah. This yuyutsavah word is now used in Japanese language also. That is an artificial fight, what is it called? Yuyutsavah?”

Shyamasundar: Jujitsu?

Tushta Krishna: Judo?

Prabhupada: Yes, it is called yuyutsavah. When two parties are fighting, they are called jujitsu. Yuyutsavah, ‘Desiring to fight, they prepared.’”

Srila Prabhupada was at his best, belting out the truth with obvious authority, practical examples, and perfect logic, saying just the right things. Of course, he always did this, but his lectures in New Zealand were particularly condensed and powerful because the educated audiences were ready for him, hungry to hear him.

The next day, Prabhupada spoke to the mostly Gujarati Hindu community at Gandhi Hall in Ponsonby, an inner-city suburb of Auckland. And then on Sunday afternoon, April 16, he spoke to maybe fifty artists at the Barry Lett Gallery on Victoria Street in downtown Auckland. Of the 1001 topics I’d heard Prabhupada discuss, I’d never really heard him talk about art before. Prabhupada’s theme, Krishna is the Greatest Artist, was a classic.

Just like you are an artist. You are painting one picture, one very nice flower. You have to take your brush, the color, and the palette, and you are taxing your brain how to make it beautiful. But you see one rose flower in the garden. Not only one rose flower, many millions of rose flowers, they are coming out very artistically painted. But when we ask, the answer is that “It is nature.” But if we go deep into the matter, what is this nature? Nature means a working instrument, that’s all, an energy, or shakti, power. Without power, how the rose flower is coming to beautiful shape from the bud? That power is Krishna’s power. But that is so subtle and working so swiftly that overnight we see that a beautiful flower has come out. Just like when you paint one picture, I can see—everyone can see—that you are working. Don’t think that it is coming out automatically. Nothing comes out automatically. It is coming out of the energy of the Supreme Lord, but the energies are so subtle and artistic that all of a sudden you see a nice flower.

In the garden you see different flowers are coming out. The same ground, the same water, but different flowers are coming out, different flavors, different colors, varieties. So the answer is bijo ’ham sarva-bhutani. Krishna says, “I am the seed” ...

Now, what are the potencies, what are the artistic arrangements, scientific arrangements, within that small seed that it grows a big banyan tree? Not only a big banyan tree, but also many millions of big fruits, and within each fruit there are millions of seeds, and each seed contains the potency of another tree.

How much credit we shall give to the person who has manufactured this arrangement? This is Krishna consciousness, to appreciate the greatest artist. You are all artists, but when we appreciate the greatest artist, Krishna, then our life is successful.... The greatest art is to understand

Krishna. If we actually want to be artist, then we shall try to understand or try to be intimately associated with the greatest artist, Krishna. For this purpose, this Society is established. This Society’s name is International Society for Krishna Consciousness. The members of this Society are trained to see in everything the display of Krishna’s artistic sense, the artistic hand of Krishna. Aham sarvasya prabhava, mattah sarvam pravartate. Krishna says in the Bhagavad-gita that “Whatever you see, everything is emanation from Me; everything comes out of My energy.” Those who have developed love of God, oh, they are enjoying the transcendental pleasure in every moment by seeing the artistic work of Krishna.

Prabhupada was the greatest artist, but he painted in words; he was the greatest poet, in my estimation. I read a lot, and always have. I majored in literature and philosophy in college and have always been critical of writers and speakers. There was never a time when Prabhupada gave a lecture or had a conversation or wrote something that didn’t just astound me. Each time I heard one of his lectures I realized how he was weaving it like a tapestry, like a fully professional poet weaves a sonnet. He is the wordsmith beyond compare.

After the art gallery engagement, Prabhupada, now back in his room, asked me to play the recording of his lecture. When it was finished I said, “Prabhupada, that was the perfect definition of art.” He said, “Yes, now you just transcribe and send it to Jayadvaita in New York. They should print as a small book.” \[Later, this Auckland lecture, “Krishna: The Supreme Artist,” was included in The Journey of Self-Discovery.\]

On the morning of April 17, Prabhupada held a press conference with half a dozen reporters in his room, then spoke at a lunchtime open-air meeting on the grounds of the University of Auckland. On April 18 he installed the Radha-Krishna Deities

Tushta Krishna and Krishna Tulasi devi had so laboriously brought from India, naming them Radha-Giridhari-“Krishna who lifts Govardhan Hill”—perhaps referring to the volcanic cone that dominates the suburb of Mount Eden.

These early Deity installations were always hurried, and because we were totally inexperienced at this sort of thing, festivities were often accident-prone and humorous. The Auckland installation was particularly chaotic, with disaster threatening constantly, and yet how sweet it all was, and how serene Srila Prabhupada looked when the fresh paint on the ceiling above the sacrificial fire started to burn and the smoke alarms went off. Prabhupada simply ignored the cramped space, lack of oxygen, and the poor preparation, and by noon, Radha-Giridhari stood in Their new home, smiling at us all.

After the installation, I rush to the Japan Consulate General to pay NZ$8.40 and pick up four sixty-day tourist visas. We’ll leave for Hong Kong and Tokyo tomorrow.

**\* \* \***

On the twelve-hour flight from Auckland to Hong Kong, I had time to reflect on my actions as Prabhupadas secretary. The post of personal secretary had evolved; so many new responsibilities had been added during my tenure. Over the past year or so, Prabhupada had become a global jagat-guru, a genuine parivrajakacharya, “teacher as homeless wanderer.” He was now teacher to the world on a highspeed mission to every continent, and I was the first secretary along for this wild ride. There was no previous model. Travel preparations and logistics were almost daily chores now, and the number of letters to answer had increased manyfold. I was a GBC member, one of Prabhupada’s twelve zonal managers, so Prabhupada also relied on me for executive advice as he made management decisions or hammered out policies. Sometimes I felt like a chief of staff whether I wanted to be one or not. Besides these official duties, I had become

Prabhupada’s philosopher opponent, his adoring fan, a cheerleader for his daring escapades, a sounding board for his visions, and the fall guy, too, if our plans went south—and through all this my love for him deepened more and more each day. I had never loved anyone like this before.

I always kept in mind something Prabhupada had said: “One should not come very near a superior. Both the Lord’s Deity form and the spiritual master should be seen from a distant place. This is called maryada. Otherwise, as it is said, ‘Familiarity breeds contempt.’ Sometimes coming too near the Deity or the spiritual master degrades the neophyte devotee. Personal servants of the Deity and the spiritual master should therefore always be very careful, for negligence may overcome them in their duty.”

Instinctively, I tended to hang in the background, letting prominent locals have Prabhupada’s attention. I knew somehow humility served me well. After all, this was the best job in the world and I never wanted to leave it. Just be cool, let others lead and do the talking, be considerate of all devotees, let everyone who needs to see him have access to Prabhupada (yet cull the dangerous or disruptive), stand back when devotees take photos, and just do your job of keeping Srila Prabhupada happy, smiling, and talking. Record everything, but blend into the woodwork.

**\* \* \***

China! To be with my Indian swami in China was like the realization of a childhood fantasy. Of course, in April 1972 no one could really go to China; Mainland China was a closed communist country. The British colony of Hong Kong was as close as you could get, but it was still China, the mysterious dragon lair of Fu Manchu.

Dropping low over skyscrapers, we touched down on a runway at Kai Tak airport—it seemed to be in the middle of town!—and stopped just short of the South China Sea. Right away you could sense the frantic energy, the tight space, and the urgent fight for survival that was China, as people pushed and shoved to get their luggage and rush to customs. We waited until everyone else had left the plane, and then slowly made our way through immigration, got our bags, and found the bus to the nearby Park Hotel on Chatham Road in downtown Kowloon. On the short ride to the hotel we gawked through bus windows at the crowded harbor. “Pradyumna, look at all those junks? We saw men with long pigtails pulling rickshaws. “And there’s a coolie—see that guy with the pole on his shoulders balancing baskets of fish? They really do walk chop-chop!” Hong Kong was a stacked-up version of London in double-time, with thousands of neon signs vertically arrayed above the trams and a mass of seething humanity. Srila Prabhupada was spellbound too, and he remarked how these people, condemned to this hellish place, will eat anything, even dogs and insects.

The Park Hotel was swank and Westernized. Bhurijan had booked the penthouse function room for Prabhupadas welcome. It was just one floor above our rooms, and as we entered the hall Prabhupada was met by a dazzling reception: about 130 people, mostly Indians with families but a smattering of Chinese admirers too. The room was decorated with flowers and ornaments. Bhurijan offered obeisances and then led Prabhupada to a cloth-covered couch, and the room erupted in kirtan as children and prominent Indian businessmen, mostly Sindhis from a province now in Pakistan, came forward one by one and slid fancy garlands over Prabhupada’s neck.

Prabhupada pitched his lecture to the children at first, citing Prahlad Maharaja and the ever-changing-bodies theme. Then he addressed the expatriate Indians, cautioning them not to be overly impressed by the material world and thus forget their spiritual roots: “Just like we have come from airport to this hotel. This city is very nice. It is all right. You decorate this city nice; you make your life very comfortable. But if you forget Krishna, then you are defeated. Then you are defeated. That is the instruction given by Krishna to Arjuna: mam anusmara yudhya ca, ‘Carry on your prescribed duty of fighting, but always think of Me.’”

Later, Prabhupada met with Bhurijan and some Hong Kong gentlemen in his hotel room and they talked late into the night. Coincidentally, in this huge metropolis, Bhurijan’s apartmenttemple was just a couple of minutes’ walk from the Park Hotel—practically across the street—so the plan for the next morning was to walk in a nearby park, then visit the temple on the way back to the hotel.

On the morning of April 20, Bhurijan led us a quarter mile from the Park Hotel up Cameron Road through the Tsim Sha Tsui district, dodging piles of refuse in this concrete jungle where four million people crammed into a few square miles. The shops were closed at this early hour as we crossed Nathan Road and entered a thirty-three-acre piece of paradise called Kowloon Park. The park was filled with flowering bushes and trees. Bhurijan said that the plants, even whole trees, were regularly changed so there would always be flowers. And what really blew our minds: hundreds of singing birds in ornate cages hung from lampposts and tree limbs and filled the air with sweet birdsong. Prabhupada remarked that this custom was a remnant of a higher civilization. He quoted Srimad-Bhagavatam about how all parts of the world were once permeated by Vedic culture: “Abhira-shumbha yavanah khasa-dayah. These are the Mongolian group, khasadayah, China and Japan. They were also in Vedic age. If you make a study, you will see so many Chinese words are from Sanskrit.”

“But Prabhupada, the Chinese language, it’s so different from Sanskrit.”

“No, they could not read, but they made sounds to imitate. So many words are there, Indian words. I have seen Chinese in Calcutta since early age, and if they do not know a Bengali name they make a sound, similar. This Buddhist culture is from India for thousands of years.”

We saw long high masonry walls totally covered in tree roots surrounding the park. “Look, Prabhupada, I think those are banyan tree roots.”

“Yes,” Bhurijan said, “those are called stone-wall trees-there’s no space in Hong Kong, so banyans grow on the walls.”

Prabhupada said, “Those who attempt to merge into the existence of the Supreme Lord will have to accept bodies like those of trees, doomed to stand in one place for thousands of years.”

The pace is quick as we move around the increasingly crowded park, dodging t’ai chi groups—the tap-tap of Prabhupadas cane on the path, his saffron dhoti fluffed by the near-ocean breeze. As we walk along, many thoughts pass through my head, like “Are his feet cold in those open-toed slippers?” and “His toenails, they look like pearls.” Sandalwood fragrance wafts in his wake, and Prabhupada looks purposeful, chin high, as he chants quietly, his tongue playing with that loose lower front tooth. He looks spiffy this morning, and pumped to be in Hong Kong.

We exit the park and start back down Cameron Road. Prabhupada comments on the ragged children and little old ladies rummaging through dumpsters for food. Many crows are also plundering the garbage, and Prabhupada contrasts them with the park’s beautiful singing birds. “Just see, these crows, they are after the rotten discarded fruits of knowledge, and devotees, they are like sweet-singing cuckoos eating mangoes in the tree of love of Godhead.”

We climb the narrow stairs one floor to 34 Cameron Street, Apartment 1/D, home of ISKCON Hong Kong. Prabhupada sits on the low vyasasan Bhurijan has prepared for him. Quite a crowd is gathered, mostly Sindhi families, and Prabhupada glances around the small room and glorifies the efforts of his disciples who have been so determined to bring Krishna to this pagan madhouse.

Prabhupada: So this is nice place. You have decorated nice. Everything is nice so far the little place is concerned. How many rooms you have got?

Bhurijan: This is it.

Prabhupada: Only one room.

Bhurijan: And this little room in the back.

Prabhupada: That’s very natural. It is costly here. But these children and their parents are also very much interested. So I think you should have a nice institution here to train the children in this country. And after their education, they may take to any line of livelihood. \[In other words, get with it, you Indian businessmen. Help our devotees and they will help you.\]

After kirtan and breakfast prasadam, a reporter arrives, and Prabhupada grants him a long interview, answering every question in detail. Bhurijan needs all the help he can get. Then it’s time to leave for the Kai Tak airport. Another lightning raid by our jagat-guru.

**\* \* \***

I had been in my mother’s womb for four months when the Japanese bombed Pearl Harbor and barely three years old when we Americans dropped the A-bomb on Nagasaki. At seven I paid five cents at the Hollywood Kid’s Club Saturday matinee and saw John Wayne hosing down the “yellow peril” in Sands of Iwo Jima. In my early childhood the Japanese were considered the enemy—no one wanted to be the slant-eyed kamikaze whenever we kids played war games. Over the next two decades, of course, the story changed dramatically. At first, Japan was the source for cheap toys labeled “Made in Occupied Japan”; by now they were the second largest economy in the world—and they were printing Srila Prabhupada’s books. Go figure.

In the late afternoon of April 20, after a four-hour flight from Hong Kong, we descended into Haneda airport, south of Tokyo, and as far as the eye could see there were factories, ships, commerce, and industry. Japan was a nation on the move. I looked forward to seeing my old friend Brent, now Sudama das, the light-skinned African-American with striking blue eyes who’d sometimes lived at Ken Kesey’s farm and sometimes with the Grateful Dead on Ashbury Street. When I became a devotee in 1967, Brent used to corner me at the Dead house and grill me about Krishna. He was initiated as Sudama in September 1968, a month after Malati and I and the others had left for London. In August 1969 I’d heard he’d gone to Tokyo with his wife and Bali Mardan to start an ISKCON center there. Sudama was an aspiring L.A. actor, supercool and intelligent. He had the moves. He’d been in Tokyo now for almost three years—that took guts!-and I could hardly wait to see him.

Emerging from customs we see Sudama and his wife Chintamani, Trivikram, and Kartikeya Swami. There’s Bhanu, over here from India, and look, Karandhar’s here from L.A.! A few Japanese boys and girls bow down, overcoming their reserved Oriental manners to shout “Hare Krishna!” and “Prabhupada!" All of them are ecstatic to see their globetrotting spiritual master.

We drive northwest twenty-eight congested miles to the tree-covered hills of Hachioji suburb, to Asakawa-cho, the neighborhood where Sudama has rented an old farm compound with a couple of small buildings, twenty-two miles west of the Tokyo city center. The temple room has sliding rice-paper doors that open out onto a concrete patio. We crowd into the temple room and spill out onto the patio as Prabhupada mounts his vyasasan and puts the Japan yatra into perspective: “So, you are doing your best to do this service as Vaishnavas in this remote village. Our Sudama Prabhu is trying to give service to Krishna. We can give service from anywhere, from any position. So kindly stick to this service attitude. We want recognition by Krishna and His devotee, that’s all. So this place, you are a little distant from the city, but we are not concerned with the city. We are concerned for distributing this knowledge, Krishna consciousness.

“So the literature distribution—I have learned that there is a train service from here. It takes only forty-three minutes to downtown Tokyo. So this place is very nice. It is expenditure according to our means. Krishna may give us a better place, but wherever Krishna is, that is best place. We are not in Japan; we are in Vaikuntha, this temple. And we are doing the service of Krishna, Vaikuntha service. So with this attitude go on preaching. I am very pleased that you are doing your best. That I want.”

After a wild kirtan, dancing and sweating in the cold spring air, we walk to a small hotel just down the hill where Sudama has rented two one-room apartments, one for Prabhupada and one for his servants; Bhurijan will bunk with the brahmacharis up at the ashram. It’s really cold; these rooms have thin, rice-paper walls, tatami mats on the wooden floors, and only a small charcoal brazier in one corner for heat. Maybe the Japanese are used to physical austerity—now I can see what they mean by Zen: blot out your mind and go into the void. But we are fresh from months in the tropics, traveling without sweaters or coats, and despite a couple of blankets, I’m soon frozen to the bone. I wake the next morning with a sore throat, but go with Prabhupada on his first morning walk in this new and exotic land. Cherryblossom season is over, but there are little buds on all the trees, pussy willows, a scattering of daffodils, and I’m feeling a touch of spring fever after two years away from spring. It looks like Oregon, with fir and pine trees everywhere, gray skies, rain threatening.

Up and down hilly roads near the temple, we see small altars erected in front of certain big rocks or by little waterfalls, with a few plastic flowers on them and Japanese script on wooden boards. Sudama explains that these are Shinto shrines. Shinto is the ancient Japanese animistic worship of spirits in natural objects. When Buddhism came to Japan more than two thousand years earlier, Shinto beliefs were absorbed into the transplanted philosophy, so you still see a kind of prehistoric reverence for special rocks, trees, and grottos where spirits dwell. Yes, Prabhupada says, “They are seeing that everything has personality.”

Sudama tells us about some of these demigod spirits, “like Hachiman, the god of warriors, whom I believe is really Hanuman, from the Ramayana,” and we begin to see the Vedic influence here too. One shrine has a small carved and decorated bull reclined before it, and Prabhupada says,“That is Nandi, Lord Shiva’s bull for riding.”

Karandhar arrived in Japan from L.A. two nights ago in order to negotiate with Dai Nippon, the printers of Prabhupadas books. I’m so happy to see my old friend, who is the stalwart backbone of Prabhupada’s movement on America’s West Coast. Sure, we are competitors, in a way, but transcendental competitors trying to outdo each other with big projects to catch Srila Prabhupada’s attention. Karandhar’s tough, Lee Marvin act has captured my heart. He has a wry sense of humor and a silent laugh that shows only with his dimples, not showing his teeth. Right away Karandhar and Prabhupada get down to business:

“Here are some gifts from the devotees in L.A.”

“Oh, what is this? Something sweet?”

“Yes, Prabhupada. And that is sandalwood pulp. And so many letters. And these are checks.”

“That’s nice. When you bring checks, I am very glad, {chuckles\] So how much you have deposited?”

“Well, I have saved up for Mayapur $30,000. But Tamai Krishna wrote a letter and said to send the money to India. So I wrote him and told him that he and Jayapataka have to sign a requisition, I think. I instructed...”

“$10,000 was transferred from my account. So that has been replaced or not?”

“I have it. I didn’t put it in the account because I thought you wanted me to send it to Mayapur. But I haven’t sent it yet. I still have it in another account.”

“No. The thing is that you were collecting for Mayapur account, so how much it has come?”

“$30,000.”

“$30,000. So $10,000 was given from my account. So this has to be replaced. And then whatever you have got for Mayapur account, that is to be transferred. So we took from bank $10,000 worth. So that is done. Now you transfer the $10,000 to my account and balance you keep ready for transferring. That also I will advise you how to do. Besides that, out of the $33,000, you deposited $18,000?”

“No. $18,600.I still owe $15,000.”

“That money is not yet done.”

“By June.”

“Oh, by June. All right. We shall talk further. So Los Angeles temple, everything is all right?”

There were so many bank accounts by now that I’d lost track. Prabhupada’s little white bag was filled with checkbooks from a dozen banks, and he knew to the last penny what was in each account despite the sluggish pace of global information. Prabhupada was the ultimate businessman and banker, and he was about to lock horns with executives from Dai Nippon.

By the late 1960s, Dai Nippon was one of the largest fullservice printing companies in the world. With so many books and Back to Godhead magazines to print, Prabhupada wanted to work with a company that could give him the best quality printing at the lowest price for all his projects. Dwarakadish and Rayarama had first contacted Dai Nippon at their New York office in late 1967, beginning the long courtship that in true Oriental fashion took years to mature. Brahmananada had gone personally to Tokyo in June 1970 with the manuscript and illustrations for Krsna book. For three years now, Dai Nippon had been printing the majority of ISKCON publications, books like Teachings of Lord Chaitanya, the two-volume Krsna book, and about a million copies of BTG. But communication with them was slow, and there were frustrating delays in editing, the back-and-forth of final proofs, and shifting money overseas. Having Sudama in Tokyo had really helped grease the wheels, though. He often went to the Dai Nippon headquarters to iron out problems with the directors of the overseas division face to face.

Prabhupada had also visited the Dai Nippon offices in August of 1970, having lunch and schmoozing with guys like Maru Kugimoto and Yukio Ogata, finally placing a printing order for $52,000. When these executives met Prabhupada they could see he was for real, and so Dai Nippon’s services, prices, and credit terms had improved. By 1971, Karandhar had become ISKCON’s point man with Dai Nippon, and this was his second trip to Tokyo for that purpose.

I awake on the morning of April 22 with a terrible cold, but Prabhupada wants me to accompany him and Karandhar to the Dai Nippon negotiations. A Mercedes limo picks us up at noon in Hachioji. The car is so wide that it barely squeezes through the neighborhood’s narrow lanes. An hour later we are at the Dai Nippon offices in Shinjuku, downtown Tokyo.

Dai Nippon representative: Mr. Karandhar gave us suggestion that Dai Nippon would open a liaison office in Los Angeles in order to save time and in order to make a good communication. And our president, Mr. Tajima, would like to hear of your opinion or comment on that suggestion. Divine Grace, please allow that we are getting to the businesslike \[laughter\] conversation, but...

Prabhupada: One thing I shall request you, that our missionary activities—we are not exactly businessmen. You see? Our only idea is these books are published for missionary activities. So exactly we are not business power. So I will request your chairman that even sometimes you find discrepancies, you don’t mind it, because we are not strictly businessmen. But we are very sincere. We shall reciprocate very sincerely. But sometimes we are not strictly like businessmen.

Mr. Tajima, the head man at Dai Nippon, then speaks at length in Japanese, while another man translates.

Dai Nippon representative: What Mr. Tajima would like to tell you, that he is also businessman. So our talking is getting to be businesslike. So please allow him. But if we send one liaison officer in Los Angeles, we need some expenditure over there. You see? So we, Mr. Tajima, expects you to increase the publishing of your books more and more.

Prabhupada: Oh, yes, yes, that we shall do, certainly. We are very much enthusiastic to see more publication. We take this publication work as big drum. When we play drum, it is resounded within some quarters, but this drum is going from country to country, so it is bigger drum.

Even after several wars and a century of Western-style capitalism, the Japanese culture still maintains deep spiritual roots, a mix of Buddhist, Shinto, and Confucian ideas that permeate Japanese life, and when business discussions are over, the Dai Nippon executives switch to spiritual topics.

Dai Nippon representative: A year and a half ago, Mr. Tajima, he lose his son, twenty-eight years old, by traffic accident. And since then Mr. Tajima has been very sad. So how do you think about death from Krishna? How do you value death? How he can be relieved from such sadness?

Prabhupada: Oh. Yes. As I have explained, Krishna, He is as good as Lord Buddha. So our program is that the success of everything depends how Krishna is satisfied. So if you try to satisfy Krishna, then whatever you want, He will give you as benediction. I will give you one instance—it is stated in Krsna book: Krishna was a student of Sandipani Muni. So when Krishna finished His education, it is the system that disciple gives some gift to the spiritual master because he has been educated.... So when Krishna asked his teacher, “How can I satisfy you?” Sandipani Muni requested, “My dear boy, I lost my child very young. If You kindly bring him back, then I shall be very much pleased.” So Krishna went underneath the sea and brought his son back. This incident is there. So my point is that whatever you want, Krishna will give you. You try to satisfy Krishna. That’s all.

I’m sitting there, so sick and dizzy with a bad cough that I can barely focus, but trying to appear fresh and interested in a discussion which, for me, is becoming ironic.

Dai Nippon representative: Even you catch cold you don’t go to, you don’t consult with doctor, medical doctor?

Prabhupada: Yes, we avoid doctor’s bill as far as possible. And we, because we are vegetarian, hardly we get sick. If you take strictly vegetarian food, you will avoid so many doctors’ bills.

That night, my fever rages as I lie on the floor wrapped in blankets, coughing and spitting yellow phlegm into a bucket that Sudama has found for me. Someone brings over a small kerosene heater, which helps cut the chill. For nearly two days I lie almost comatose, with hazy recollections of eating a little rice, Prabhupada coming in to check on me once or twice, while Nanda Kumar types letters somewhere nearby. Finally a doctor comes and says that my temperature of 103° is too high. Two men wheel me out on a stretcher to a waiting ambulance, and I pass out, waking up later in a Tokyo hospital. They take an X-ray, and I’m relieved to hear I don’t have ТВ. Instead, they say I have “near pneumonia” and pump me full of penicillin.

I feel guilty, letting Prabhupada down by getting sick. I’d been with him continually for one and a half years, traveled around the world nearly twice, and while others had dropped like flies around me, I’d stayed pretty healthy, by Krishna’s grace, until now. Lesson learned: stay warm and try to sleep once in a while. Anyway, I recover quickly, and a couple of days later were off on another adventure.

**\* \* \***

Sudama had arranged a two-day program in Kobe, 320 miles southwest of Tokyo, on the same island of Honshu. We decided to take the bullet train, which moved at 100 mph and would reach Kobe in about three hours. As we sat on a bench and waited for our train, we watched officials shoving passengers into packed trains with a long, T-shaped tool with a three-foot-wide padded “pusher” on the end.

I bought a Time magazine at a station kiosk to catch up on world events. Once we’d boarded, we were cautioned to fasten our seat belts. As we whooshed through the outskirts of Tokyo and into the countryside with our hearts in our throats, the silence and high speed at which we traveled was so disorienting that at first there was little breath for conversation.

Srila Prabhupada broke the spell. “These poor Japanese, in two, three wars they attempted to expand. They are very poor in their land. Only by some technical knowledge they are maintaining. Otherwise they have no food, no shelter. I have studied. The Dai Nippon directors, they are living in a thatched house. And their system is big company, Dai Nippon, they supply everything—food, cloth, medicine, childrens education, and little salary. So people do not leave the post. Fully dependent on the employer. So whatever house they allot, they have to accept. So Japanese actually they are poor. Therefore their yen value—you go to purchase something, ‘One million yen, please.’ \[laughs\] We are traveling these three hundred miles and we see that the common man is living in matchboxes, 99 percent matchboxes.”

As we raced along, Sudama pointed out the silhouette of the famous Mount Fuji looming above us, and the Japanese Alps on the northern horizon. At one point, Sudama gestured out the window and said, “Those are the famous temples in Kyoto—Kyoto is the spiritual center, like the Vrindaban or Benares of Japan. We have some friends there, and they want us to open an ISKCON center in Kyoto.”

I read in Time that a man named Jacques Monod had just won the 1972 Nobel Prize for his book called Chance and Necessity. Monod concluded that life is the result of biological processes that occur by pure chance. When I brought this to Prabhupada’s attention, he had me read the article to him: “Jacques Monod ends the book with his fundamental conclusion that ‘The ancient covenant is in pieces; man knows at last that he is alone in the universe’s unfeeling immensity, out of which he emerged only by chance. His destiny is nowhere spelled out, nor is his duty. The kingdom above or the darkness below; it is for him to choose.’”

These were fighting words, and for the next several days Prabhupada focused on this article in conversations and lectures.

In Kobe, our host was Mr. Mahesh, a wealthy Sindhi cloth manufacturer and head of the Kobe Hindu Association. He gave us a flat on the third floor of an apartment house he owned, hinting that he might donate the apartment for an ISKCON center. In our rooms that night, Prabhupada launched right into Monod's theory:

Prabhupada: Just read out from dictionary what is the meaning of chance.

Pradyumna: “Chance. The way things fall out. Fortune, undesigned occurrence, absence of design or discoverable cause. Course of events regarded as a power, fate.”

Prabhupada: So chance can be adjusted with necessity. I want something. That is my necessity. And it will come by chance? Or I have to endeavor for it and then I get it? Shall I depend on chance? If I have to work to get something, then it is plan. Mayadhyakshena prakrtih suyate sa-characharam: “Under My plan, the nature is working.” Hetunanena kaunteya jagad vipari-vartate: “All these changes are taking place on account of My supervision.” So we do not take anything as chance. We got up on the train, and the train is running, and it is all chance? There is a huge management behind the train. Therefore we are comfortably seated and we come to the destination right in the time. All these are chances? Everything is done by plan, by prearrangement. By chance one is becoming millionaire, and by chance one is becoming a cockroach? This is sheer nonsense. This rascal, he is educated by a plan, by his parents. And therefore he is able now to talk nonsense and get the Nobel Prize for misguiding people.

Sudama: He won’t say, “By chance I have won the Nobel prize.”

Prabhupada: And the foolish people, they accept everything cheaply—and at once.

Shyamasundar: Cheaply and at once?

Prabhupada: Yes. The newspaper is costing ten cents.

The next night, Prabhupada was invited to speak to a large gathering of expatriate Hindus along with their “pet” guru, Swami Kaivalya, a bearded younger Indian man. There was only one vyasasan, and Sudama seated Prabhupada on it. Kaivalya Swami was apparently content to take a lesser chair.

Prabhupada starts off strongly, explaining the difference between a Vaishnava—who is a servant of the Supreme—and a Mayavadi—who thinks he is one with the Supreme. For nearly an hour Prabhupada delivers his very powerful, authoritative message that the highest form of human achievement is to be reunited face to face with God in unalloyed devotional service. The audience is a little stunned. They’ve never heard a holy man speak with such conviction. There is a long silence. Then the hall erupts into long, resounding applause.

Now it’s Swami Kaivalya’s turn. He speaks in Hindi, so we’re not sure what he’s saying, but Prabhupada sits erect, eyes closed, listening. After about thirty minutes Prabhupada suddenly opens his eyes and shouts, “Stop him! Stop him! Let us have kirtan!”

Later, Prabhupada explained to us that he couldn’t tolerate this so-called Swami telling people that God is formless, that we are the same as God, and that God is beyond worship. Needless to say, Mr. Mahesh and the Kobe Hindus were very distressed, and aboard the return train to Tokyo the next morning, Sudama laughed and said, “Well, so much for our Kobe temple!” Prabhupada replied, “This Kyoto city has better potential anyway.”

Back in Tokyo, Prabhupada fired off many letters, especially to his leaders in India, managing from afar the three temple projects so dear to his heart. He replied Dayananda in London, saying he would attend the UK Ratha-yatra that summer. He also accepted invitations for future programs in Mexico, San Diego, and Dallas.

In a letter to Bhakta das in San Diego, Prabhupada defined the moral standard for selling books to the public on street sankir-tana: “So far the meaning of the word cheating,’ there are only three things to be known: that Krishna is the Supreme Enjoyer, the Supreme Proprietor of everything, and the Supreme Friend of everyone, and we say that honesty is acting upon the knowledge of these three facts.... So if you apply this to your techniques for selling literature to persons in the Sankirtana party, then you will understand what is the meaning of the word ‘cheating.’”

Over the next week, Prabhupada spoke daily at the Tokyo temple on the themes of tapasya, austerity, and sacrifice, and of the guru’s chastisement: “It is the business of the teacher to find out your mistakes, not to find out your good things.” At first I was thinking, “Oh, Prabhupada, this may not be the right subject for these boys who are trying so hard,” but then I began to see how perfect his topic was for those steeped in a Zen-like master-pupil tradition.

On April 30, Prabhupada initiated two Japanese boys and gave Sudama sannyasa—he was now Sudama das Goswami Maharaja. It began to rain heavily, and a big program scheduled to be held in a park was rained out. We moved the festival to the small Hachioji temple, and Prabhupada spoke to the damp, huddled few as if they were thousands.

On May 4, we returned to the Dai Nippon offices, where Prabhupada finalized arrangements for the printing of the Indian BTG and some books in Hindi. After his meeting, Prabhupada sat patiently for an hour while a young Dai Nippon dentist fitted him with a pair of solid gold teeth, cleverly bridged to the remaining front teeth in his lower jaw. Prabhupada looked in the mirror and said, “Oh, this looks very nice,” then reached out and pulled the anxious dentist to his chest, hugged him, and messed up his hair. Emerging from the dentist's office, Srila Prabhupada flashed his glamorous new grin, ready for his trip to celebrity-conscious America.

On May 6 we took an early flight from Tokyo to Honolulu.

Srila Prabhupada was in high spirits after his recent victories: three huge temples underway in India, two in Australia, New Zealand, now a strong foothold in Japan. On the seven-hour flight he dictated letters and talked about one of his favorite places: Hawaii. Prabhupada had spent a month at the small ISKCON temple in Ka’a’awa, on the northeast side of Oahu, in March 1969. In August 1970, he stopped through Hawaii for five days en route to Tokyo, staying at the Ilikai Hotel in Waikiki. He said, “On this Hawaii island how many beautiful things, flowers, trees, and fruits—it is like a heavenly planet. The climate is milder and there is much fresh air from the ocean and sunshine, and the scenery position is also very beautiful. There is also strong inclination for spiritual birth, and many yogis take their birth in Hawaii.”

My own impressions of Hawaii had largely been shaped by a book my mom had received through her membership in Book-of-the-Month Club back in 1960. It was Hawaii, by James Michener. There was a movie of it, too, starring Max von Sydow and Julie Andrews—cinemascope panoramas of sweeping green canyons and red volcanoes, sun and rain, lush plantations, long-haired wahinas racing across broad white beaches to garland their menfolk fresh from the sea. Michener writes, “How beautiful these islands were! How shot through with harmony and peace! How the mind lingers on their pristine grandeur..... If paradise consists solely of beauty, then these islands were the fairest paradise that man ever invaded.”

Prabhupada was fond of the two devotees who had pioneered ISKCON Hawaii, and it was clear that he could hardly wait to see them again. Gary and Bonnie had joined us in San Francisco right at the beginning—distributing Mantra Rock posters was their first service—and they were married and initiated by Prabhupada as Goursundar das and Govinda dasi. In 1967, they had lived with Prabhupada at a beachside cottage in New Jersey and had nursed him through an illness. They had then been his personal servants for three months in Montreal. Theirs was a special relationship. Brooding Goursundar was deep and thoughtful, a philosopher, yet decisive and a man of action. Bespectacled Govinda dasi was energetic and deeply surrendered to Prabhupada. She was a sweet Texas belle most of the time but could read you the riot act if you crossed the line. Her mother/daughter mood with Prabhupada was much like Janaki’s: teasing back and forth with the Swami, as he scooted her cat out from under his feet, and making sure he was properly dressed.

Goursundar had come to Hawaii in September 1968, a month after we had left for London. Govinda dasi joined him in January 1969. Over the past three years, the two had preached strongly all over the islands, and the Hare Krishnas had emerged as established leaders in the hippie-surfer-yoga spiritual scene. Like magnets, these two devotees collected young seekers in paradise like driftwood cast up on the beach. People could see they had a spiritual method, a path, and were not speaking vague or empty words. They followed an approved and ancient process, and they were having more fun than anyone else!

**\* \* \***

Many sincere souls thronged to the Hare Krishna banner through Goursundar and Govinda dasi and quickly became strong devotees. Now, forty or fifty of them are here at the Honolulu airport with tons of flowers and wild kirtan to greet Srila Prabhupada, who mounts a vyasasan hastily assembled in an airport corridor.

Prabhupada glows with happiness as Goursundar and Govinda dasi come forward to garland him. Then he sees the boy named Sai, now Siddhaswarup das, and calls him forward and rubs the boy’s head as he bows before him. One by one, the mostly new devotees come forward and place garlands around Prabhupada’s neck, and after half an hour of this there’s a heap of garlands three feet high laying at his feet. Aloha.

After the airport greeting we pile into a big Chevy, and Goursundar drives us eighteen miles east on Pali Highway over the Ko’olau mountains—“This is the Honolulu Forest Reserve, Prabhupada,” Govinda dasi says. “Many sandalwood trees grow here.”

It’s so nice to be in a big American car again, driving through paradise, rainbows playing off showers to the south, big surf now lining up on our left as we descend to Waimanalo Beach on the windward side of Oahu.

And what’s this? “This is your home, Srila Prabhupada,” and Govinda dasi points to the large modern house with picture windows looking out on the Pacific. The vacation rental at 41-911 Laumilo Street has seven rooms, including four bedrooms, and sits on about a quarter acre of trees and tropical gardens. Hmm, only one bathroom, so we’ll have to share. Step out the sliding glass doors, walk across the Bermuda-grass lawn, and you’re on wide, six-mile-long Waimanalo Beach.

The next morning, May 7, we walk on the white, coral-sand beach. After breakfast, Goursundar drives us back to Honolulu for the Big Day, the installation of Pancha-tattva dieties and devotee initiations at the ISKCON temple on McKinley Street.

The Hawaii devotees had acquired a large, three-story house in the posh Manoa neighborhood, on a hill overlooking downtown Honolulu. They called the place New Navadwip. As we pulled up in front, dozens of brightly dressed devotees filled the street and lawns around the temple, and Prabhupada took his time moving up to the house, greeting everyone individually, and admiring the massive tulasi bushes, Krishna’s sacred plant, higher than our heads, that flanked the walkway. From the front stairs you could see Diamond Head off to the left, Waikiki Beach in the distance straight ahead, and that must be Pearl Harbor, with the big naval ships, over on the right.

First thing I see inside: Kartamashi. The same Krishna Deity we had bought at India Imports in San Francisco in February

1967! Govinda dasi has smoothly stolen Him away and brought Him to Hawaii, where it looks like He belongs—the Young Boss, hip-cocked, carrying a cowherd stick and draped in gardenias and jewels. The main room is packed, the ceremonial fire lit, and festivities will go on for hours. Before the installation Prabhupada initiates about thirty new devotees, many of whom are Sai’s followers.

Chris Butler was scarcely twenty years old and already a champion surfer. Over the past few years he had practiced intense yoga and achieved a high form of Brahman realization (I heard there were “kundalini scars” at the base of his lower back). Chris had adopted the name Sai, attracted a large number of followers, and tried to guide them toward spiritual perfection. In the summer of 1969, the Krishna devotees were living in a hut near Sunset Beach, the famous surfer haven where Sai also had an ashram. Sai met Govinda dasi and was immediately attracted to our Swami and his philosophy. Swamiji wrote to Sai from John Lennon’s place on October 27, 1969, explaining to him the various degrees of self-realization, that his own Brahman realization was just the beginning, and that there are yet higher forms of bliss: “I am very glad to learn from my disciple, Srimati Govinda Dasi, that you are reading our books and literature with some interest.... Just as sunshine, the sun globe, and the predominating deity on the sun globe are all full of light and high temperatures, similarly there are also degrees of transcendental bliss in the different features of the Absolute. Above all these, as they are described in Bhagavadgita, there is Purushottam, the Supreme Person. That is Krishna. In the Vedas it is stated that one who has understood the Supreme Person has understood everything. So if you kindly try to understand this philosophy of the Supreme Person you will understand our activities more clearly.”

Over a year’s time, Sai gradually understood the Krishna conscious philosophy. On November 4,1970, Prabhupada sent a letter to Govinda dasi from India stating, “They may be chanting Hare Krishna, but without taking instruction from one’s spiritual master it is just like milk touched by the lips of a serpent, it will have poisonous effect.” When Sai heard this, he immediately came into the McKinley Street temple and surrendered to Bhaktivedanta Swami, moving into the basement with the other brahmacharis. He broke his fifty disciples into small groups and dispatched them to ISKCON centers in San Francisco, L.A., India, and other places. Each group brought several thousand dollars as a donation to the local temple, and $28,000 was given personally to Prabhupada, along with properties in Maui, Kauai, and forty acres on the Big Island. Several of Sai’s disciples showed up in India about that time, like Tushta Krishna, his wife Tulasi Krishna devi, and Sudama Vipra. Although Prabhupada had not yet met Sai, he appreciated him very much through the energy and deep commitment displayed by his students. Prabhupada initiated Sai by mail and named him Siddhaswarup das, “servant of the Perfect Being.”

Govinda dasi: Sai had money, land, ashrams, disciples, beautiful girls adoring him—but he gave it all up almost immediately after hearing Prabhupada’s letter. His desire was to give everything to his spiritual master, not to simply take from him. You just knew he was a highly advanced yogi from a previous life. Krishna had sent him directly to Srila Prabhupada and, yeah, he was a little loose, wasn’t used to the strict regimen of traditional KC, but he was totally sincere and trying to adjust the unrestrained Hawaii surfer lifestyle to being a pure Vaishnava. I have always appreciated his surrender and depth of love.

Today, Srila Prabhupada initiates Siddhaswarup into the sannyasa order of life, renaming him Siddhaswarupananda Goswami, and the crowd goes wild with happiness.

But the best is yet to come. Devotees carry out the holy murtis of Lord Chaitanya and His four associates, Nityananda, Adwaita, Gadadhar, and Srivas, known as the Pancha-tattva, the five aspects of God. Govinda dasi and her team had been working for two years to sculpt these nearly four-feet-high lacquered papier-mache dieties under Prabhupada’s detailed guidance.

Golden and glowing, their arms upraised, and wrapped with a simple cloth around their waists, they are set before Prabhupada—and he nearly faints with pleasure. After anointing them with mantras and flowers and grains and colored powders and bathing with pitchers of water, milk, yogurt, and honey, the Pancha-tattva are dressed in elegant silks, wigs, jewels, and garlands and installed on the altar for all to admire and worship until the end of time.

Later, Goursundar handed me a bundle of mail for Prabhupada, and on our return trip to Waimanalo I read out the letters to him from the backseat of the car. In an aerogram postmarked London, Mondakini described her trip to Moscow, how she had met Ananta Shanti and his mother along with several of the heavily persecuted but determined underground Russian devotees. She said she would marry Ananta Shanti as soon as she had the proper visa to continue her service behind the Iron Curtain. As I read this, Prabhupada turned his head to us and Govinda dasi snapped a photo of his face wearing the biggest smile you ever saw, lit up with delight at this good news.

We drove back over the Ko’olau Mountains, and Prabhupada noted the double rainbows hovering between peaks and valleys, remarking that every color in natures palette is composed of green, yellow, red, and blue, and how nicely they combine to form the unimaginable varieties in Krishnas creation. Prabhupada told us that Hawaii is a remnant from a previous golden yuga, not yet enveloped by the ugly reign of Kali, and that the mode of goodness prevails here, prompting a strong inclination for spiritual life. “Of course, there is no real happiness anywhere, but if there is any happiness, it is in Hawaii,” he says.

James Michener: There was then, as there is now, no place known on earth that even began to compete with these islands in their capacity to encourage natural life to develop freely and radically up to its own best potential.

This is a time of rest for us, and the eighteen-mile distance between metropolitan Honolulu and sparsely populated Waimanalo, between leeward and windward, rain and sunshine, over the dividing mountain range, seems immense. Prabhupada unwinds, sets up his translating area in the big main room, and starts to work. Goursundar brings us another typewriter, so all day long Pradyumna sits clacking away, transcribing Prabhupada’s Srimad-Bhagavatam translations and purports from the night before while I type letters and philosophy tapes. My cassette tape recorder broke down just before we left Tokyo—it still plays but doesn’t record—so Goursundar is trying to get us another one.

Yesterday, in Honolulu, Balabhadra had slipped me a cassette of All Things Must Pass, so now I’m listening to it surreptitiously, hearing George’s “Beware of Maya” on earphones while typing Prabhupada’s letters to Bali Mardan, Satsvarupa, and the boys in India. Govinda dasi putters around the house all day, keeping Prabhupada fed and comfy, and she slings a makeshift curtain across the room to give him a little privacy. Bathroom schedules are worked out, and Prabhupada’s male servants soon learn to use the great outdoors. Goursundar shuttles back and forth to Honolulu every day, bringing heaps of mangos, papayas, bananas, and coconuts. When Prabhupada asks where he is getting so much abundance, Goursundar tells him he stops at the county dump on the way back here and loads his car with the excellent produce people throw away. “Just see,” says Prabhupada.

When Pradyumna joined us in Calcutta five months ago, Prabhupada’s translating work finally kicked into high gear. Now they sit together several times a day, the great sage and “Panditji,” going over the typed manuscripts of the Srimad-Bhagavatanis Third Canto, proofreading and lining out the progress. When he isn’t typing, Pradyumna sits and reads esoteric Sanskrit texts sometimes late into the night, riveted to this secret world. He giggles to himself or looks around at me or Nanda Kumar with a silly grin, his eyes alight with rare knowledge. One day he is especially sniggering over there, so I ask, “What’s so funny, Pradyumna?” He looks around, not sure where he is for a moment, then focuses on my curiosity and says, “Hey, get a load of this!” He then translates for me several paragraphs from an ancient Sanskrit text called the Surya Siddhanta, which states that the earth’s diameter is 1,018.6 yojanas, or 8,148 miles—almost exactly the same diameter reported by modern science. This book describes our heliocentric planetary system and the yearly orbit and daily spin of planet earth; it uses longitude and latitude (akhansa and deshantra) to map the earth, describing in great detail the seven dvipas (continents) known by Indian sages since the dawn of time, when my ancestors still thought the world was flat.

As the days roll by, Nanda Kumar, being a surfer-type from California, can’t keep his eyes off the big waves breaking just a hundred yards from our doorstep, and he asks Goursundar if there are any good places to bodysurf nearby. Goursundar tells him Makapu’u Beach has the best bodysurfing in the world, “and it’s just down the road.”

I pick up on Nanda Kumar’s excitement, and Prabhupada gives us the afternoon off. Someone left swimming suits in a drawer, so we grab towels and Goursundar drives us three miles south on Kalanianaole Highway to the bodysurfing mecca at Ma’akapuu Beach.

The waves are very high, maybe ten or fifteen feet—jeez, you can see fish swimming through vertical walls of water—and they break in a nice continuous set, one at a time, about fifty yards from the beach. I’ve never surfed in my life, but Nanda Kumar obviously knows what he’s doing, and he teaches me how to dive under oncoming waves and to work my way out just beyond the break. You wait for a swell and then plunge over the curl, ride diagonally down the wave on your side with right arm extended. He makes it look easy. I try it a few times and get small rides, but the thrill of maybe getting a big one keeps me going. At the bottom I look up and see Nanda Kumar—he looks so tiny and far away, twenty feet above me on a wall of churning water. Finally I do it. It’s like being shot from a cannon—an intense thrill!—and I bottom out, roll over with a big grin, waiting for applause, exhale, and then—Wham! A wave falling fifteen feet hits me in the face and I go deep under, slam the bottom, and black out.

I wake up on the beach spewing seawater from my lungs, Nanda Kumar pounding my chest. After a while I’m OK—and I know I should jump back on the horse that threw me, so I go back out. But I’m exhausted and my timing is way off, so I’m soon back on the beach sitting out the rest of the afternoon and watching these other lunatics.

We’re hungry after battling waves for hours, so we stop at a little store on the way back. I notice that milk costs $1 a quart—on the mainland the price is $1.69 a gallon. The price you pay for living in paradise. When we return to Prabhupada, he asks about our day sporting in the waves, laughs at my story of such pain for an instant of fun, and says we are not surfers we are “sufferers.”

**\* \* \***

Occasionally we went into Honolulu for programs. One engagement was at the Church of the Crossroads on University Avenue, just a few minutes’ drive south of the McKinley Street temple. This place reminded me of the Glide Church in San Francisco, where five years ago the Swami had spoken to a similar audience of young hippies on their vision quest in paradise. On another day Prabhupada spoke in the Kennedy Theater at the University of Hawaii, again, just a few minutes’ drive east of the temple. For this engagement he wore a bright saffron silk-satin outfit recently sewn by his loving disciple Kusha devi. He looked like the Prince of Paradise himself. These Hawaii devotees carried the early flavor of Haight-Ashbury. They were the Swami’s rebel army, his buccaneers, along for the magic carpet ride of Krishna’s inconceivable plan.

At the Kennedy Theater program, Prabhupada spoke about chaitya-guru, the personal form of Krishna dwelling in the heart of every living entity, giving direction—what we might call “instinct” or “intuition.” He used the example of the small sand crabs he’d observed on the beach that morning, how they knew when to scoot away from waves and the danger of approaching footsteps.

My birthday passed unobserved. On May 10, 1972, I was thirty years old.

Most mornings Prabhupada sat in his rocking chair in the yard at Waimanalo and gave an informal class to whoever was present. Whenever Siddhaswarupananda Swami was there, Prabhupada discussed Lord Chaitanya, why He took sannyasa, and from various perspectives what being a sannyasi really meant. Prabhupada told us that before Lord Chaitanya took sannyasa He preached to students. “But the proud student community did not become submissive. In a laughing manner they criticized the Lord. Lord Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, being omniscient, could understand the degradation of these students. Thus He sat at home, contemplating how to rescue them. He decided, ‘I shall accept the sannyasa order of life, for thus people will offer Me their obeisances, thinking of Me as a member of the renounced order.’”

There was a gorgeous flowering frangipani tree (plumeria) in the yard, and after his morning class Prabhupada would doff his clothes and relax on a mat spread on the lawn beneath this fragrant tree, wrapped in a simple loincloth for his daily massage.

О Prabhupada, how beautiful you are: the golden-limbed young man sporting in flowered gardens, joking, declaiming on every subject from butterflies to Brahmaloka, your eyes half-closed in rest and inner satisfaction. Never have I seen you so beautiful. This is what it must be like in the spiritual sky in Krishna’s place called Goloka Vrindavan—sheer heaven.

The tang of mustard and coconut oils being massaged over Prabhupada’s war-weary limbs, his grunting when Nanda Kumar or Goursundar hit a sweet spot; he sits upright for the boys to squeeze his head, working his scalp up and down—“Little harder,” he says, eyes closed.

Or sitting in his rocking chair, chanting japa for hours—each word snaps so clear and distinct—while a gentle breeze plays with his kurta, his senses alert to frangipani perfume and a few soft raindrops, seeing Krishna in every movement of bamboo rustling at the fence, in the play of wind across the emerald carpet of grass at his feet, in the sibilate susurrus of the insistent Pacific rolling back and forth on white coral sands. One day he points to a tree in the yard: “Just you take this coconut, for example. How perfectly Krishna is working. This water, salty water, is drawn up through the legs of the tree, and in the coconut it is distilled, pure water, stored far in the top of the tree. All the needs for human being are there: fresh water, food, even clothes and shade you can make from the leaves of this tree,” says our gorgeous spiritual master.

Goursundar has brought us a replacement tape recorder, so Prabhupada asks that we continue our philosophy book discussions, redoing some of the earlier sessions that were lost. So on the morning of May 12, Prabhupada and I sit on the lawn under the plumeria tree and plunge back into the sixteenth century to discuss the Renaissance philosophers.

Shyamasundar: European thinkers were just discovering the rules of natural science, like gravity and motion, so they were breaking with medieval Church philosophy, which was based on the Bible. Thomas Hobbes (1588-1679) declares, “Whatever exists is matter, and whatever changes is motion.”

Prabhupada: Spirit is not a combination of material conditions. If so, why don’t you combine matter in such a way as to produce living spirit, living forms? That you cannot do.

Shyamasundar: Nothing exists in the world but bodies, which we can perceive with our senses. God is “a most pure, simple, invisible, spirit corporeal.”

Prabhupada: Why invisible? He was certainly visible, for Arjuna was talking to Him face to face. God is invisible for one who is imperfect, but for one who is perfect He is certainly visible.

Shyamasundar: Like Machiavelli, Hobbes wants to justify the political position of the king and state. In his book Leviathan he states that the king is God’s representative and is there to enforce the social contract, the law.

Prabhupada: But who is the right man? Since men are always defective, how is this possible?

Shyamasundar: Under the social contract, man volunteers to restrict natural liberties for the sake of self-preservation.

Prabhupada: This is not natural liberty but ghostly liberty. In their unnatural condition haunted people are thinking, “I am God.” So many societies have come and gone. In the spiritual sky there is perfect society. Here we are just practicing.

Shyamasundar: Rene Descartes (1596-1650) was a French philosopher who proclaims that all truth can be derived from reason, which is superior to sense experience. Cogito ergo sum means “I think, therefore I am.”

Prabhupada: Yes, through our reason we can understand that there is God.
