We can reason, “I have my father, my father has his father, and so on; therefore there must be a supreme father.” In Sanskrit it is ahankara, “I am.” This is innate knowledge: I exist now, I existed in the past, and I shall continue to exist in the future.

Prabhupada liked Descartes’s two proofs for the existence of God: first, that an innate idea of an infinite being necessitates the existence of that infinite being; and second, that because we can conceive of perfection—in the mathematical sense, such as 2 +2 = 4—perfection must exist, and since God’s existence is the same as His essence, He too must exist.

But the guy Prabhupada really liked was Blaise Pascal (1623-1666), another French thinker, who declared that reason was fallible but God can be understood by the heart. Like Descartes, Pascal was a mathematician, and he proposed a metaphysical wager: there is a fifty-fifty chance that God exists; if you choose to believe in God, even if He does not exist, you have a better life—and if He does exist, you profit greatly. “Yes,” Prabhupada said, “we also advise people to chant Hare Krishna. Since you have nothing to lose and everything to gain, why not chant?”

The perfect days roll by. We start with a walk on Waimanalo Beach each morning, then a small class, breakfast, massage, baths, then back to work. We often pass through Prabhupadas curtain for philosophy book sessions, to consult on a passage in the Bhagavatam, to take him letters to sign, or to just schmooze on the state of the world. I peek in from time to time to see him working, whispering into his dictating machine hour after hour, his desk light still burning long after we’d crashed on the floor outside or retired to our rooms down the hall.

One day, Goursundar takes us on a five-hour road trip around Oahu, stopping at the Honolulu temple for lunch. As we pass Sunset Beach on the northern tip of the island, we pull over to watch the amazing spectacle of surfers riding giant waves, like ants on the far offshore breaks, Prabhupada admiring their courage but seeing it as wasted in folly.

We continue with the Renaissance rationalists:

Shyamasundar: Baruch Spinoza (1632-1677) sees God as the universal principle that binds together all the relationships in the material world. He states that the infinite universe is like a machine necessitated by the divine nature: God is the sum total of everything.

Prabhupada: Certainly God is everything, but if God is nothing but a principle, He has no personal activity. By saying that God is a principle, like light, we imply that God is a material thing. Everything may be like a machine, but a machine is devised by a person. If he says God is reason but is not a person, that is a contradiction.

Shyamasundar: Spinoza believes that the more we understand natural reality, the more we understand God.

Prabhupada: This is because God is reality and forgetfulness of God is illusion. Illusion is also God, but in illusion we forget

God; therefore it is not real. Sunshine and darkness are both reality because they exist side by side. How can we say that the shadow is not reality? In that sense, illusion or the unreal is also reality. This material world is called the unreal, but actually there is nothing unreal. As long as you are engaged in the service of Krishna, there is nothing unreal for you.

We have a four-hour discussion on Gottfried Wilhelm Leibnitz (1646-1716), spread out over two days. With Leibnitz, Prabhupada really gets into esoteric philosophical concepts.

Shyamasundar: What I’m trying to get at: can a hydrogen molecule desire to become part of water and be given that body? Does it have the independence to desire something and take a body accordingly? The hydrogen molecule—does it have a will?

Prabhupada: So far as we get Vedic information, andan-tara-stha paramanu. It does not say the soul is within but that Krishna is within as the Paramatma.

Shyamasundar: Leibnitz says that in each “monad,” or particle of matter, there is a kind of individuality.

Prabhupada: That individual is Krishna. Krishna knows that so many atoms will be combined and then another thing will be formed. It is not the individual soul, but Krishna directly.

Shyamasundar: But when you come to the living entities, then the individual soul is also there.

Prabhupada: Yes. Within the body. Krishna is there, and the individual soul is also there, both.

Shyamasundar: Leibnitz says that substance means to be capable of action, and that existence means action.

Prabhupada: Substance is original. Other things are categories.

Shyamasundar: So being capable of action, is that a good definition of substance?

Prabhupada: Yes. Substance means the original cause, so He is completely able to act.

Shyamasundar: Leibnitz says to be is to be active.

Prabhupada: Yes, to be means, to be active. Without activity, what does it mean to be?

When you hear Srila Prabhupada in these philosophy debates, you feel that he is telling only one percent of what he knows—his knowledge is so vast that you can safely say, “Prabhupada knows everything.” This is especially true when he talks about how universes are formed, how time and space are managed by the demigods, describing energies which we cannot conceive. If you try to figure out the forms and expansions of Vishnu, forget it. Prabhupada knows all this, but it’s far beyond my ability to follow. I had intensively questioned my professors at first-class colleges in the US and Europe; I had roamed the world in search of a person with all the answers, reading everything I could get my hands on. Now, after months of questioning Bhaktivedanta Swami on every aspect of philosophy, I have to conclude: Prabhupada knows everything. To have even found in my lifetime this one person who knows so much, what to speak of sitting at his feet on a daily basis—how rare is this opportunity! Out of billions of people on this planet, how lucky I am. It’s better than lifting a rock in your backyard and finding a 10,000-carat diamond, the most precious thing on the planet. How could this have happened to me?

One day, Prabhupada complains to Govinda dasi that there’s a “needle” poking him in his freshly upholstered rocking chair. She inspects it closely but doesn’t see anything wrong, so she sits down roughly. “Yeoww!” We hear her shout from the adjoining room, to the Swami’s great delight.

Prabhupada continues to direct his worldwide institution with letter after letter, writing thirty-eight letters in thirteen days. Most letters go to his team in India, who need his strong guidance. With the monsoon season coming up, he tells Tamal Krishna in Mayapur, “You have got now 100 tons cement in storage, so finish it as soon as possible, otherwise there is risk of flood and all the cement will be spoiled.” He tells Giriraja that “as far as possible we shall not deal in business,” and then goes on to write two pages of detailed business instructions for Juhu. What can he do? We are so poor at doing business, and he is the master of business. In other letters, Prabhupada invites his North American leaders to meet him in Los Angeles in the upcoming days, offering some of them induction into swamihood, the renounced order.

For a rain-bound kid from Oregon, seeing Hawaii with my spiritual master was another dream come true, another rhino made tame by Prabhupada’s unerring hand. We had seen the lands of kangaroos and kiwis and mysterious Hong Kong and Japan. Hawaii was the pot of gold at the end of the rainbow. After a grueling ten-month journey around the world, now it was time to return to Prabhupada’s comfortable Western headquarters in Los Angeles, to take the pulse of the American mission, to catch up with Prabhupada’s US generals, and to organize the next round-the-world tour.

**\* \* \***

May 18, 1972. On the six-hour flight from Honolulu, Prabhupada chanted on his beads while a movie played on the pull-down screen a few rows ahead—The Boyfriend, a silly 1971 musical comedy set in the 1920s, with lots of well-choreographed dancing. When I told Prabhupada I had met the female star, Twiggy, a couple of times at George Harrison’s house, he raised his eyebrows and said, “So?” I said she was too busy yakking with Pattie to talk about Krishna. I watched Prabhupada watch the film, no earphones, wondering what he thought about this lavish display of modern entertainment.

About a hundred devotees meet us at LAX, the most spectacular, operatic airport greeting yet—men in bright yellow, women in vivid swirling saris—what a scene! It’s like Lawrence of Arabia’s return to Aqaba—with three TV crews filming live for the 6 o’clock news. Seated on a dais in the airport VIP lounge, Prabhupada tells us: “The modern present human civilization is not in right order. I was coming here and I was seeing one cinema, The Boyfriend. Of course, young boy, young girl, making friendship, enjoying life—that is not condemned. Actually, that is life. Radha and Krishna are young boy and young girl. This young boy and young girl trying to unite for happiness is the natural inclination of the living entity. Radha-krishna-pranaya-vikrtih. So the thing as it is originally is perfect, but here, the same thing is only perverted reflection. Here, the boys and the girls meeting, it is just like a reflection of the tree on the river; it is just topsy-turvied.”

Right away Prabhupada gets into it, seeing all these beautiful female devotees, some with children, dancing around with married and unmarried men in the mix—Prabhupadas perfect camera-ready creation, his cast of thousands, right here in Hollywood. Surrounded by his loud, adoring fans like this I suddenly see Srila Prabhupada as the star of the Hare Krishna phenomenon that is sweeping the nation, a new-wave cultural icon. These are more than just fans; it’s like Beatlemania, girls fainting, guys who will do anything for a glance from their awesome, charismatic, cinematic hero.

Being with Prabhupada was like being in an endless movie. One moment you were part of an epic adventure-thriller like Ben-Hur or Where Eagles Dare, and the next moment you were in a newsreel, a documentary, or an evocative travelogue. You went from sidesplitting comedy to deep tragedy in a few frames of time. The content of letters written to Prabhupada ranged from juicy high drama to dry field reports; from soap-operatic Dear Abbeys to heavenly expressions of love. Prabhupada was the producerdirector for an ever-changing kaleidoscope of actors and scripts, and Los Angeles was his home base of choice. The weather was perfect here year round, and what is done in L.A. today, the whole world does tomorrow. From the Culver City temple it was just a few short blocks to the main gates of MGM Studios, where The Wizard of Oz was filmed and where “Gunsmoke” and “Jeopardy!” beamed out each week to millions of homes.

3764 Watseka Avenue in Los Angeles had become a burgeoning complex, devotees now occupying apartment houses and buildings for about three square blocks surrounding the temple, which was originally a Methodist church. Devotees in bright saris and saffron robes ran or strolled down the palm-lined avenue, often blocking the street with their colorful festivals. New Dwaraka had become the prototype metropolitan Hare Krishna community, where men, women, and children gathered for a single purpose: to worship the Supreme Personality in devotional service.

Right away Karandhar took Prabhupada on the grand tour, first to the new Golden Avatar sound and video studios, then to the Spiritual Sky incense factory. Next, he showed us his hi-tech command center, with its computer and telex machine. When Karandhar punched in the word Krishna, every instance of Krishna in all of Prabhupada’s lectures and books was displayed on the screen. Prabhupada was impressed. When a message from the New York devotees—“Please accept our most humble obeisances”—flashed in over the telex in real time, Prabhupada was elated.

Devotees from up and down the West Coast, from Denver, Detroit, and Texas, even from back East, were pouring in, along with several traveling sankirtan parties, for this rare visit from their spiritual master—and the temple was filled to overflowing, the devotees in rodeo mood.

Manjuali dasi: A couple of days a week you were working at the incense factory, a couple of days you were on sankirtan, and then between that and cooking offerings and distributing books as well, you were running all the time... Your life was full of Krishna, of service. There was a sense of community. With Prabhupada at the center, you were all spiritual pilgrims really on a path together, sharing prasadam together, and multitasking. It was really, really exciting.

Prabhupada settles into his comfortable quarters above the temple room, two rooms and a bath. I set up my office in a small closet at the base of the stairs leading up to his rooms, door open so I can hear the little brass bell when he rings for me. Pradyumna and I roll out our sleeping bags on the floor in the hall at night; Nanda Kumar sacks out with the brahmacharis somewhere. My office has a window looking south onto the alleyway between Watseka Avenue and Cardiff Street, open to pleasant afternoon sun. For the first time in months I have a desk and chair of my own, a place to store stuff, to get organized.

Right away, Prabhupada tells me we have to finish the philosophy book as quickly as possible—that we’ll meet every day until it is done. So I sort all the tapes and manuscripts—what has already been done, what has been lost, what remains—and I enlist two girls to help with the typing. They listen to the tapes and transcribe what they hear, leaving blanks and question marks for me to fill in later when they can’t hear something, like when a crow caws or a peacock shrieks just as Prabhupada is saying “Pl ...” What was that? Sounds like plain? Penury? No, must be plenary. Yes, it fits: “Plenary expansions of Krishna.” Plenary means absolute. As the pages pile up, I’m relieved to see this haphazard project finally taking shape.

On our first morning in L.A. we finish the British Empiricists with David Hume (1711-1776):

Shyamasundar: Hume was skeptical about achieving certain knowledge. He says there is no other source of knowledge except the senses.

Prabhupada: No. We don’t agree. The senses are imperfect. They cannot reach. Just like we cannot know what is there in the sun, but a geologist or astronomer, he can say, one who has studied.

Shyamasundar: Hume says that all that we are, all that we know, is merely a sequence of ideas.

Prabhupada: But behind the ideas there must be some fact. Otherwise, how we get the ideas?

Shyamasundar: He separates fact from idea. For instance, I may think this table is red, but it is actually brown. So my idea is incorrect.

Prabhupada: Your idea is one thing, but actually it has got a color, either red or brown. So if you have eye disease, you cannot see, actually, but one whose eyes are not diseased, he can see whether it is brown or red. Therefore we have to take knowledge from a person who is not diseased.

Shyamasundar: Hume distrusts any kind of authority and says that the only kinds of things that we can know for sure are mathematical proofs and immediate sense perceptions. Like we can perceive that there is time and there is space—like that. That is the only knowledge he will admit.

Prabhupada: And beyond the time and space? Shyamasundar: We can’t know anything.

Prabhupada: You cannot, but there is a process. Just like a small insect, he takes birth in the evening, and from evening to morning his birth, his marriage, his begetting children, everything is done, and in the morning he dies. So for this insect it is very difficult to understand that there is another animal which is called man who has got this insect’s lifetime in only twelve hours of his life. With your relative body, your duration of life, your knowledge, your perception, everything is relative. So you are teeny human being. What is impossible for you is not impossible for others. He is talking from the relative platform.

Prabhupada’s GBC men and other ISKCON leaders flew in one by one, and Prabhupada spent time with them in his room or on morning walks, hearing their reports, sorting out problems, and working with them on solutions. On May 19, Prabhupada wrote to Jagadish in Toronto: “Now I want to retire, and you all appointed GBC men must do the work that I am doing. Therefore, I have recommended this traveling extensively for the GBC men, just as I am doing it. If I did not travel there would be no nice organization. Traveling and preaching, that is the Sankirtana movement. But now I am old man; let me retire for writing books in my last years.”

He wants to retire. (“Yeah, right,” I’m thinking.) But first he wants to show his leaders how it’s done, traveling and preaching, and he sets me to work on his itinerary: first we will go to Mexico “around first June,” then up to Portland, Oregon, on June 8, San Diego after that, then New York, London for Ratha-yatra, maybe Amsterdam and Edinburgh, Paris for sure, New Vrindaban in late August, maybe Pittsburg and Dallas (“We’ll see”).

With no outside speaking engagements, the days quickly settle into a routine, starting off with Prabhupada’s 7 a.m. kirtan and lecture to more than a hundred devotees assembled in the lavishly decorated temple room. For the first time ever, these lectures will be filmed by a professional crew—Krishna Kanti and his Golden Avatar Productions staff—and Prabhupada does not disappoint. His subject matter is Srimad-Bhagavatam, Second Canto, third chapter: “The duty of a man on the threshold of death.” Each day he has Pradyumna read off today’s verse in Sanskrit and asks others to repeat the Sanskrit, grooming us in this amazing language. Over the following days, Prabhupada delivers a consistent series of lectures, a powerful exposition of this most essential of all Vedic knowledge, ready-made for TV. With all the flowers, devotee glee, and children coming forward for sweets from Prabhupada’s hand, it is a remarkable piece of transcendental show business.

The daily routine, starting at 6 a.m.: reply to letters, then morning lecture, a walk at Venice Beach or Cheviot Hills Park followed by meetings in his room with select devotees, then a philosophy book session, then massage, lunch, rest, GBC confabs or another philosophy session. In the late afternoons and early evenings Prabhupada sits in the small enclosed garden behind the kitchen and converses with devotees in the golden sunset afterglow, guru to the world training this new surge of devotees, endearing them evermore with his laughter and intimate talks.

We’re into the eighteenth- and nineteenth-century German Idealists now, and philosophy book sessions are becoming more intense. First up is Immanuel Kant (1724-1804), who is so important to modern thought that we spend much of May 20 and 21 discussing his Critique of Pure Reason.

Shyamasundar: Kant’s philosophy seeks to trace the relationship between a priori ideas, or those ideas of the mind that are independent of sense experience, and a posteriori ideas, or sense impressions. He wants to unify these two positions. Descartes claimed that all knowledge comes through innate ideas; Hume said just the opposite—that all knowledge comes from sense experience. Kant is trying to unify the two ideas, so he asks a fundamental question: “What is the source of knowledge?”

Prabhupada: Knowledge comes from intelligence, and the source of intelligence is transcendental. Transcendental knowledge means knowledge received from a source that is beyond the reach of my material senses. Thought comes from higher authorities. There are five stages of acquiring knowledge: direct perception (pratyaksha), receiving knowledge from higher authorities (paroksha), then apply your senses and come to some conclusion (aparoksha), then transcendental knowledge (adhokshaja), then spiritual knowledge (aprakrita).

Shyamasundar: Kant says the mind is aware that there is an ultimate reality, or a thing-in-itself, a noumenon, which produces each phenomenon. It is the nature of reason itself to find a total regularity for everything. So it must suppose that there is a Supreme Being in order to find that total synthesis. In fact, he recognizes three such ideals of pure reason: one is the soul, two is the ultimate world or reality, and three is God. He says that these three ideals are a priori to reason. They are born with us. We know these things.

Prabhupada: Yes, a priori means “before.” So in your preaching you can use this Kant’s statement, how he is confirming the statement of the Bhagavad-gita: buddhir jnanam asammohah. Krishna directly says, “I am the creator of intelligence and knowledge,” and Kant, as a philosopher, has found out that this is a fact by his reason. So this may help in our preaching work.

Shyamasundar: But Kant says that the mind is not equipped to perceive this ultimate reality. So the mind must remain forever content to be agnostic.

Prabhupada: Why should he remain agnostic? Suppose up on the roof there is some sound, and we may speculate, but we cannot ascertain what is that sound. But if somebody is actually there and he says, “This sound is due to this,” so why I shall remain satisfied with agnostic position, that I could not ascertain what is the sound, and therefore I shall remain satisfied? Rather, I shall say, “Is there anybody on the roof?” If somebody says, “Yes, I am here,” then “Will you kindly say what is that sound?” “Yes, it is this, that, this, that.” Therefore Vedic injunction is tad vijnanartham: for that which is beyond your senses you must approach a spiritual master. He will give you information.

Shyamasundar: Kant comes to the conclusion that these ideals of perfect knowledge are established, but they are unprovable and unknowable. There is God, there is soul, there is reality, but we cannot know anything more than that. We don’t have any more information than that. So the rest has to be done by faith.

Prabhupada: No. Faith, that is a compromise, you see. To have faith, that is also not perfect. Therefore the Western philosophers, they have created different faiths, and for them, religion means faith. Somebody may believe in one faith, others may believe in another faith. But that is not factual. The factual is this: if we are actually convinced that there is God, and God is omnipotent, so by His omnipotence He descends. These things are actually displayed. God reveals Himself, and devotees became encouraged. By hearing also we realize. Because God is absolute, therefore to see Him and to hear about Him, there is no difference. By seeing eye to eye or to hear about Him—the same thing.

Sometimes in these philosophy sessions we get really worked up, emotional. I adopt the role of each philosopher, really trying to defend “my” position, and Prabhupada becomes a lion, roaring at me. One moment he is the reasonable, gentle persuader and the next he rashly berates me for the fool I am.

On May 22 we moved on to Johann Gottlieb Fichte (1762-1814).

Shyamasundar: Fichte sees the unfolding of reality as the fulfillment of duty; one must always strive for what ought to be, and that will fulfill his duty.

Prabhupada: Duty means by superior order. That is duty. You cannot manufacture your duty.

Shyamasundar: We discern what ought to be, our duty, from the forces of nature around us—reality unfolding as a thing in itself.

Prabhupada: Who has prescribed that this is ought to be?

Shyamasundar: Well, the world order prescribes what ought to be.

Prabhupada: World order—what is that world order? Is it blind?

Shyamasundar: Harmony, whatever causes harmony...

Prabhupada: What is harmony? Who will define? You say this is harmony, I say this is harmony. Who are you? The question is, “Who are you?”

Shyamasundar: Hmm. Yes, I can see how this philosophy worked its way into the German mind. I can say, for example, that I am Hitler, I control the...

Prabhupada: That is “Might is right.” But Hitler is finished now. That anyone can say. The tiger can also say. “Might is right. I am powerful, so you must do as I say. That is your duty.”

Shyamasundar: Fichte says that philosophy must begin with the assumption that being is nothing but duty; duty is absolutely everything.

Prabhupada: That is nonsense. Without being, how you can do your duty?

Shyamasundar: Being doesn’t strive for what is; being is always striving to improve, to what ought to be.

Prabhupada: But you cannot do such. You commit mistake. Therefore you do not know what is ought to be or not to be. So we give that ultimate ought to be—that you will become surrendered soul to Krishna. That is ultimate ought to be.

Shyamasundar: Each individual must act according to his duty and his conscience in this world.

Prabhupada: No. A thief, he also prepares his conscience. When he goes to steal he says, “I must, because I have to maintain my family. I do not know any other business, so I must.” This is his conscience. The other conscious is, “No, no, no, I cannot steal. It is sin.” So where is the standard of conscience? You manufacture your own conscience. Therefore you have to take advice from Krishna conscience. That is real conscience.

Even in my solitary, one-on-one philosophy book debates with Prabhupada, it was hard enough to keep the thought process on track. But with all the heavy-hitters in town, devotees drifting in and out of Prabhupada’s rooms—and despite my dagger looks at Kirtanananda or Rupanuga—the philosophy sessions became almost public forums. This was really juicy stuff, so Prabhupada encouraged his big men to sit in on them. He’d show them how it was done. On May 23 we started on Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770-1831) before a packed room.

Shyamasundar: Hegel’s basic method is that he wants to synthesize all opposites to arrive at the truth. Remember, Kant was saying that the spirit expresses itself through the object, whereas Hegel says the objects themselves are the spirit expressing itself. There’s a distinction being made between the spirit within the object expressing itself or the spirit as the object.

Prabhupada: Object as it is, it is spirit. As it is. Because just like sunshine—sunshine is not sun, in one sense, but it is sun because in the sunshine there is heat and light and in the sun there is heat and light. So there is no difference. But still sunshine is not the sun. Therefore that is our philosophy, acintya bhedabheda, simultaneously one and different.

Shyamasundar: So in a sense this table is spirit.

Prabhupada: Yes. It is spirit, actually, but because I have no sense of Krishna, I am taking it as matter. Just like sometimes people criticize that “You are spiritualists, you hate materialism, then why you are using this table? Why you are using this typewriter, this microphone?” But our reply is that it is not matter; it is spirit. But when you use it for your sense gratification, then it is material.

Shyamasundar: Is this table Krishna?

Prabhupada: Yes.

Shyamasundar: Hegel’s method is called the dialectic. It means the synthesis of two opposing elements. First the thesis, then the antithesis, and then you arrive at synthesis.

Prabhupada: Yes, I understand. Dialectic means speculation. That means argument. You say something, I say something, and then you come to conclusion. First are the premises. Just like “Man is mortal. Mr. John is a man. Therefore John is mortal.”

Shyamasundar: No, but that’s the Aristotelian process, a syllogism. It deals with the “is-ness” of something. Hegel rejects Aristotle’s process. He’s more interested in the “becoming” of something. His thesis would be: “Man is immortal.” The antithesis is that “Man is not immortal.” So the synthesis would be the resolution of those two.

Prabhupada: All right, there are two processes. One is inductive and one is deductive. This Aristotle is deductive process. And there is another process, inductive. Now let us see whether man is mortal or immortal. We say in our Sanskrit language avaroha pantha, aroha pantha—knowledge trying to go up and knowledge coming from up, or ascending process and descending process. So we say that descending process is perfect.

Shyamasundar: Hegel says that the most essential dialectic of all is just this: there is a thesis of being and an antithesis of nothing. So how to reconcile these two?

Prabhupada: The reconcile is that the body is nothing and the spirit is something. This is synthesis. This is our proposal. The body is nothing, false, but I am real.

Shyamasundar: Hegel says this basic dialectic between being and nothing is the basis of becoming—that because these two things are always conflicting, we are always becoming.

Prabhupada: Therefore the question “becoming” means I am now in this awkward position that I am eternal and immortal but I have been entrapped by something which is mortal. Therefore I am always changing my position. So when I shall stop this taking of different positions? When I shall remain in my own being, that is the synthesis.

Shyamasundar: Does the condition of being entrapped, when I am released, does this enhance the appreciation of liberation?

Prabhupada: No. When the man who has fever recovers he enjoys equally with the man who never had fever. Therefore someone who has fallen into the material world, if he is liberated, he enjoys equally with the man who has never fallen into the material world. Neither he enjoys more—for instance, that he has learned some lesson, so therefore he enjoys more his freedom—nor does he enjoy less than the man who has never fallen.

This is really heavy stuff. The devotees observing are stunned by Prabhupada’s grasp of the word jugglery and ratified concepts of Western philosophy and how he can fire back answers in such a convincing manner with full and expert knowledge. Despite their occasional interruptions, I was so happy to see my godbrothers’ new appreciation for their spiritual master’s unimaginable philosophical depth and skill at debate, something I’d been able to observe for months.

Shyamasundar: So you say that form, or substance, precedes idea, not that idea precedes substance.

Prabhupada: Yes, we accept that. Substance precedes idea.

Shyamasundar: So Hegel says that these two things are opposing—idea and substance—they are thesis and antithesis. But the spirit contains both of them, so it is the synthesis.

Prabhupada: Yes. That we agree. Viruddha means opposing elements. Contradictory things can be adjusted in Krishna. Because what is idea is coming from Krishna, and what is substance, that is also coming from Krishna. Just like this example: cooler and heater. They are opposite, but they are both coming from electricity. That is called viruddha. Contradictory things adjusted in Krishna. Simultaneously one and different. That is our philosophy. In this material world it is impossible to think like that; therefore it is called “inconceivable.”

Shyamasundar: Everything we can conceive of—that must exist somewhere?

Prabhupada: Yes.

Shyamasundar: So somewhere must be the square root of minus one... and I can conceive of a building eight hundred stories high, so ...

Prabhupada: Eight hundred, eight million, stories! \[laughter\] Shyamasundar: It doesn’t exist.

Prabhupada: It exists.

On May 25, eight of Prabhupada’s GBC men gathered in his room to redivide the world into zones for better ISKCON management. The supreme commander was meeting with the majority of his generals for the first time since he’d appointed twelve men as Governing Body Commissioners in July 1970. He was pleased to see us all together. Now maybe we could take up the slack and relieve him of some of his management headaches. But we were so inexperienced, such greenhorns, and Prabhupada was a little impatient with us.

Prabhupada: So fix up the zone.

Shyamasundar: Well, we pretty much fixed it up, except...

Prabhupada: So write, write—take, take some paper. Here is paper. Here.

Shyamasundar: I think that, that the Southeast Asia ...

Prabhupada: Take this, take this map. First of all, find out the zone, what will be the zone.

Shyamasundar: The Southeast Asia and Australia we’ll join in one zone.

Prabhupada: So write, write that. What is one, two, three. What is the one zone, zone number one?

Shyamasundar: All right, Southeast Asia, Australia—if everyone is agreeable.

Prabhupada: They agree. Everyone will agree. It is not a question of ... \[laughter\] First of all, make the zone. Whatever I’ll say he’ll agree; That is not the problem. First of all make up zone.

Karandhar: Hong Kong, North and South Vietnam, Malaysia, Singapore, Indonesia, Australia, New Zealand, Fiji Islands, Samoa, and Tahiti.

Prabhupada: Make that one zone. Then next zone? First of all divide the whole world. It doesn’t matter who goes where. Divide the whole world into twelve parts. Or make fifteen zones—it is a world affair, after all.

Once we’d drawn the lines on the map and made a list, Prabhupada laid down his guiding principles and delivered our marching orders: “GBC means now they should tour very extensive. That is the first principle for GBC. Not sit down in one place and pass resolutions. No. They must be active. They must act like me. As I am old man, I am traveling all over the world. Now, to give me relief, I shall expand into twelve more so that they can exactly work like me. That is GBC. We must expand; we must expand. Now the framework of expansion is done by me. Now we have to push this movement. It is very important movement. It is not a farce. It is actually for the benefit of the human society. They are kept in darkness about God. And we are delivering God, ‘Here is God, His name, His address.’ So that must be pushed. What is your opinion?”

Prabhupada looks at us one by one, sees dazed faces as each of us grapples with the sudden immensity of our responsibil-ity—and then he goes to the heart of the matter, challenging us to assess ourselves for the battle to come. “We have to push on this movement with positive conviction that here is God. If you have got any doubt whether Krishna is positively God, then you cannot do it. That is the first qualification. If you are yourself not convinced, how you can convince others? Whether you are all convinced on this point, that here is positively God? What is your opinion?”

Silence. Confusion. Awe. Each wondering, Can I do this?

Prabhupada breaks the silence. “So far I am concerned, I have no opinion. I am convinced, \[laughter\] There will be so many questions, so many opposing elements. You have to face them by your arguments, by your knowledge. This is required. We are Brahma-sampradaya, Lord Brahma’s sampradaya. So push this movement and give me relief. Let me remain in the background. Let me finish writing my Bhagavata Purana. This institution, ISKCON, will give to the world so many valuable jewels. There is no comparison.”

I ask Prabhupada for the next afternoon off, and he agrees. I borrow a car and go alone to Venice Beach to soak up some rays and contemplate where my life is going from here. How can I be an effective GBC man and remain Prabhupada’s secretary? I have a wife and child but no money, no job—where is all this going? I’m sprawled out in the sand when I hear someone approach. I look up to see a skinny gal in a bikini, her hair a mess, mascara running from hollow eyes. Wait, I know her—it’s Peggy, Peggy Caserta, from my Haight-Ashbury days!

She turns, blinking into the sun, “Sa-yam?” she says, in her Loosiana way, recognizing me despite my shaved head, dhoti, and tilaka. “Is that you?”

We catch up. I note needle tracks up and down her bruised arms. She’s still with her girlfriend, Kimmy. They live in a motel in Hollywood. She asks if I have any money. My heart swells with pity, and I give her my last twenty dollars. Her car isn’t running right now, she says, can I give her a lift back to her motel?

In the car I explain how chanting works, how amazing Prabhupada is, but I can see her only thought is getting back now that she has money for a fix. I drop her at her fleabag motel in West Hollywood, go in for a minute to greet Kimmy and another strung-out junkie, who’s the daughter of a famous old-time movie star. As I back out, Peggy asks me to help them push-start her car, which is in the parking lot. It’s the same 1967 Mustang Shelby Cobra she’d bought five years ago, when she was rich and living the high life, but it’s now covered with dents, and the hood is missing, exposing the huge V-8 engine. We push the car, it starts with a rumble, and Kimmy races off to score. I hug Peggy and say I have to get back to my spiritual master, feeling very sad all the way down Venice Boulevard, thinking how lucky I am—there but for the grace of Krishna go I.

When I get back to the temple I tell Prabhupada what happened, and he is also sad, commiserating with the fallen children of this age. The next morning, when we start debating Schopenhauer, Prabhupada uses Peggy as an example of how maya is such a strong force.

Shyamasundar: Today we are discussing German philosopher Arthur Schopenhauer. His philosophy is exactly the opposite of Hegel’s rational, optimistic world order. Schopenhauer’s philosophy is often referred to as the philosophy of pessimism. He says that reality is blind, irrational, capricious, and whimsical, and that actually life is an evil situation.

Prabhupada: Man is called a rational animal. So how his irrational philosophy will be accepted by a rational animal?

Shyamasundar: Schopenhauer was the first Western philosopher to read the Bhagavad-gita and other Vedic scriptures. So he concluded that all phenomena are mere illusions, or maya. He uses that word maya. This world is simply illusory. Ultimate reality is the unfolding of will. That will is eternal, and because it never succeeds in perfection, will is always reincarnated in one body after another. Will is described as a force.

Prabhupada: Yes, will is ultimate reality, we also admit. Because we desire, we will become like this. First of all there is force and he is being forced. Prakriteh kriyamanani gunaih kar-mani sarvashah. Just like you met your friend, this Peggy, and she is forced. Nobody likes that, a wretched life, but she is forced because she has associated with certain material modes of nature. Just like these hippies—they are forced. They are coming from respectable family, there is no scarcity of money, and still they are lying on the street in wretched dress, wretched habits. So that this nature is forcing, “You do this.” Prakriteh kriyamanani.

Shyamasundar: Schopenhauer says that permanent happiness comes about when we lose our desire to live, when we deny the will to live.

Prabhupada: That is frustration. That is suicide. Just like one man who is very much suffering, he does not find any other means, then he cuts his own throat or hangs himself or takes some poison.

Shyamasundar: They desire a state of nonwillingness. Do you think it’s possible to stop willing?

Prabhupada: That is not possible. Even in a dream you are willing so many things.

Shyamasundar: Our desires are continuously frustrated; they are never satisfied. So he said that we are always suffering and that the more intelligent a person is, the more he suffers.

Prabhupada: Yes. Or by suffering, one’s intelligence becomes manifested.

Shyamasundar: Oh? Suffering sharpens the intelligence?

Prabhupada: Yes, the blessings of adversity. Just like a businessman. He loses some money in some attempt and he becomes more intelligent, that “This business should not be done.”

Shyamasundar: Schopenhauer says that because the world is so mad it could not possibly have any author. Because if there was a God, that God would have set the world in order.

Prabhupada: Then why he is mad? We have got experience that there are madmen, but there is hospital also for treating the madmen. The world is mad, that’s all right. But there is treatment also.

Shyamasundar: The world is an evil place.

Prabhupada: To some extent that is all right, because when you are in prison life you will find evil. But that evil is good for you—so that you can learn some lesson—and when you are out of the prison you will not come again. That is the blessings of evil.

Shyamasundar: The blessings of evil.

Prabhupada: Yes.

**\* \* \***

On May 27, Prabhupada conducts a grand ceremony in the temple. To prepare his leaders to share what has become for him a nearly overwhelming burden, Prabhupada has offered some of them sannyasa—to make them swamis. Four men come forward and agree to a lifetime of renunciation: Rupanuga and Bali Mardan from New York, Satsvarupa from Dallas, and Hridayananda from Houston. Prabhupada tells them, “According to Vedic system, one takes sannyasa at the fag end of life. Just like I have taken. While I was going to die, I took sannyasa. Something is better than nothing. And now you have taken sannyasa in the prime of youthful life. So you are all far better than me. You have got enough opportunity to serve Krishna and His mission. I am old man. I may pass away at any moment. The writing is on the wall. So you remain and preach this cult. Try your best.”

Two days later, in the morning of May 29, we finish the German Idealists by discussing Friedrich Wilhelm Nietzsche (1844-1900).

Shyamasundar: Schopenhauer said that blind will is the force that binds us to existence, but Nietzsche writes about der Wille zur Macht, “the will to power” that the philosopher and others like him can use to master the lower self.

Prabhupada: This is called spiritual power.

Shyamasundar: Nietzsche says that the man who uses this higher power to become self-possessed and fearless is called an Ubermensch, or “superman.”

Prabhupada: We accept the guru as the superman. Guru means swami or goswami, one who can control the senses. Vacho vegam manasah krodha-vegam. One who can control the mind, the urge to speak, anger, and demands of the belly and genitals, he is goswami, superman. But this is not possible for an ordinary man.

Shyamasundar: Nietzsche is famous for proclaiming “God is dead,” meaning the basic tenets of Christianity no longer work.

Prabhupada: Yes. If you create an artificial God, it is better that he is dead so that he cannot inflict more injuries.

A young Indian devotee has been sitting in on the philosophy sessions, and although he is quiet and introspective, you can see he burns with humble intensity and intelligence. His name is Swarup Damodar das, initiated by Prabhupada the previous year, and he is working on his doctorate in chemistry at UC Irvine, down in Orange County. I encourage him to join us each day if he can, knowing how Prabhupada appreciates the insights of learned men. “Please come tomorrow,” I say to him after our session on Neitzsche, “and feel free to speak up if you have a comment, because tomorrow we’ll be discussing the biggest rascal of them all, Charles Darwin.”

Shyamasundar: Darwin proposes the doctrine of natural selection, or survival of the fittest. Nature selects different species that can best survive. For example, if you put a certain animal in a cold climate, it will develop hair to protect its body against the cold, and it will pass on this characteristic to its offspring.

Prabhupada: The people in Greenland—do they develop hair?

Shyamasundar: They don’t have so much hair, but they develop fatty tissues and their eyes are slitted against snow and bright light.

Prabhupada: Then development of hair is not the only condition. There are other many conditions. You cannot say that development of hair is due to natural conditions. That is not a fixed-up...

Shyamasundar: I was just using that as an example of how a species can adapt to its environment.

Prabhupada: The question is: is there any plan that this body should exist in certain condition of nature and therefore he must have these equipments, either you say tissues or eyes or hair? Who has made these arrangements? That is the question.

Shyamasundar: His answer to that is chance variation.

Prabhupada: That is nonsense. He examines what is already existing. But our question is: who has made these different circumstances for the existence of different animals? That is our question. Nature’s law is that the big snake eats up the small snakes. Otherwise, there will be disturbance. That is nature’s law. But behind this nature’s law there is brain. That is our proposition: nature’s law is not blind. There is brain, and that brain is God.

We argue for an hour about natural selection. Finally, Srila Prabhupada admits that, yes, there is selection in nature, even evolution. I begin to feel I have won at least a partial victory. But then Prabhupada says that every one of the 8,400,000 species already exists somewhere, and that a certain body will take birth on a planet when favorable conditions exist for that type of body. For example, dinosaurs are now gone from this planet, but they still exist on some other planets where temperature, solar radiation, and the atmosphere are perfect for dinosaurs.

Prabhupada: Natural selection is there, but how the natural selection is working he does not know that. Evolution we accept, but our point is: all the species of life—they are existing simultaneously. It is not that one is missing, one has gone away, and another has come, and for millions of years there was no human being. This is all nonsense. He cannot find remains in the layers of dirt, but that is not sufficient evidence.

Shyamasundar: So do you agree that from simple forms of life more complex forms evolve?

Karandhar: No. From the Vedic information we learn that the most complex living entity, Lord Brahma, manifested first, and he created all the variations. So from the most complex the most simple was evolved. So if you have the wrong information, you could look at it and say it was the opposite, that from the most simple the most complex evolved. The sequence is there, and if you observe it in the wrong way, you may conclude it’s going in the opposite direction.

Prabhupada: Yes. All forms of life are always existing. They are not extinct. That is the point.

Shyamasundar: All right. But on this planet now, if we could examine this planet—

Prabhupada: Again you come to this planet. Why you are sticking to this planet?

Shyamasundar: So if you accept that there is an evolution, do you accept that the bodies change because of changing conditions of the natural surroundings?

Prabhupada: Body is not changing. The body is already there. The soul is changing bodies, transmigrating from one body to another.

Karandhar: Just like if you go to an open marketplace, at any given point you can go through the marketplace and see that there’s this kind of person, that kind of person, and he may go away from the marketplace. So because he goes away, you can’t say that that person doesn’t exist anymore because he’s not observable there.

Prabhupada: Yes, yes.

I’m shot down again. The Darwin discussions become heated at times, and Karandhar and Swarup Damodar join Prabhupada to gang up on me. Three hours later we call a halt.

We pick up again with Darwin’s Origin of Species on the morning of May 31. It’s Prabhupada’s contention that humans have existed on this planet since time immemorial. Over and over I demand the evidence that proves this.

Shyamasundar: Darwin’s theory is that the human body and the monkey body have evolved from a common ancestor.

Prabhupada: So if men came from monkeys, why don’t we see it still happening? Why don’t we see sometimes a monkey who has human features?

Shyamasundar: They are related. They are like branches of the same tree.

Prabhupada: Related? Everything is related. That is another thing. But if the monkeys body is developing into human body, then after development of human body, why the monkey species does not cease to exist? We say that from the beginning of life, at that time also there were human beings.

Shyamasundar: We find no evidence of very early human beings.

Prabhupada: Why no evidence?

Shyamasundar: In the ground. There’s no fossil evidence in the ground.

Swarup Damodar: There is one physicist named Heisenberg. He produced the so-called Theory of Uncertainty—that in every physical observation there is always uncertainty.

Prabhupada: Basic principle is uncertainty, and on this they are building big, big buildings.

Shyamasundar: Darwin calls the common ancestor of humans and monkeys the “missing link.”

Prabhupada: You say “something missing.” That is evasive. Why something should be missing if you are in knowledge? If you say this thing is missing, then you have no knowledge.

Karandhar: It is an axiom that if any part of knowledge is imperfect, then the whole knowledge is imperfect. So if Darwins theory is at all correct, then why doesn’t it conclude itself by its own logical deduction? Why would it have to allude to something missing, some missing factor?

Prabhupada: That missing link—let him learn from us. We can give him the missing link.

Jeez, I just can’t get anything on these guys. They keep shooting down each of my convincing arguments one by one. I keep going back to evidence—where is your evidence that humans existed millions of years ago on this planet?

Prabhupada: It is just like this Los Angeles City. Suppose there is a havoc of flood from the ocean and all men die. That does not mean they are extinct. The men are there somewhere else. You cannot say that human species is now extinct because your study is limited.

Shyamasundar: But the point is that they have determined that there are rock structures in the earth that are very, very, very, very old and that these contain no evidence of any complex forms of life. So if you say that there were higher forms of life millions of years ago existing on this planet, there has been no rock evidence ever found of that.

Prabhupada: So why they’re trying to find out evidence from the rocks, not from any other source? I am a human being; I am the remaining. I am a descendant.

Shyamasundar: But your forefathers made no tools or had no house?

Prabhupada: I can make tools. Naturally, my grandfather—he can make too. What does making tools have to do with anything?

Shyamasundar: But why weren’t there any tools left behind for us to find? Remnants, evidence, in the soil?

Prabhupada: What is the use of tools? Tools are used by the carpenters, and we are not carpenters.

Shyamasundar: But if there were high forms of men living—

Prabhupada: Then you are concerned with the carpenters, not the philosophers. Our evidence is intelligence, not with tools and houses. Our evidence is intelligence. We get Vedic information by disciplic succession, highly intelligent information. So that is our evidence. Not the tools. Many such things have been discovered, tools and houses, but they will not tell you. And besides that, they are searching after dead bones and we are searching after living brains. So which should we consider better? Now this Valmiki Ramayana, for example, it is perfect knowledge, entire history of this planet, and it was written at least 805,000 years ago.

Shyamasundar: I must admit that in our Western history they never refer to the ancient Indian civilization.

Prabhupada: Because they will be defeated. They do not recognize the ancient Vedic civilization. That was the British policy, to minimize ancient India in order to control ... But this point should be stressed—that Darwin is dealing with dead bones and we are dealing with living brains.

Shyamasundar: Just like the Bhagavad-gita is so perfectly written, so perfectly conceived.

Prabhupada: Yes.

Discussion of Darwin leads us into a number of current scientific topics, such as Carbon-14 dating, African lungfish, mythic tunnels from Asia to South America, horse fossils, the expanding and contracting universe. Emotions are high, and Prabhupada is energized to attack modern science in the presence of so many of his leaders. On the morning of June 1 we discuss the latest scientific theory about how our universe was formed, the so-called “hierarchy of condensations” theory.

Shyamasundar: I’ll read what they say. There was a “frozen equilibrium and a spontaneous breakup of primordial nuclear fluid. The original state of matter is assumed to be a hot nuclear gas. After the first complement of the atomic species had been formed during the first hour of expansion, nothing of particular interest happened for the next thirty million years.”

Prabhupada: Where is the evidence that he is speaking the truth?

Karandhar: For most people it is just his word. Whatever his contemporary scientists conclude, he offers some insignificant evidence.

Prabhupada: If words are to be accepted as true, why not accept the words of Krishna? We do not know what it is, but you are presenting there in bombastic words, and we have to accept your word, is it not? So I will say that instead of accepting your words we will accept Krishnas word. He is a greater personality.

Shyamasundar: Scientists are constantly changing their theories.

Prabhupada: This must be changing because the instruments by which we acquire knowledge—they are imperfect. So by our so-called research and sensuous acceptance of knowledge, that is never perfect. It cannot be perfect. Our source of knowledge is different. We do not depend on experimental knowledge.

How can you argue with that?

Shyamasundar: OK, Srila Prabhupada, were almost caught up on the philosophy book—from Plato to Sartre. We’ve got a few more to do, such as the modern psychologists, like Freud. Maybe we can finish these when you come back to L.A.? I’ve got two girls typing all these discussions—they might be finished by the time we get back from Mexico and Portland.

It’s June 1, and I’ve been on the phone back and forth with Chitsukhananda in Mexico City for the past few days. One Mexican admirer has purchased airline tickets for Prabhupada and his three servants, and were scheduled to depart Los Angeles tomorrow, June 2. Chitsukhananda reminds us that Prabhupada needs a visa for Mexico, so Karandhar runs upstairs and grabs Prabhupada’s passport, hurries to the Mexican Consulate General in downtown L.A. and, in the nick of time, gets him a fifteen-day tourist visa. Were due to return to L.A. late on June 7, then go on to Portland, Oregon, on June 8. It’s going to be tight.

**\* \* \***

From mid-1966 to mid-1970, in just four years’ time, Bhaktivedanta Swami had built his International Society for Krishna Consciousness into a major spiritual force in North America and Europe. On August 2, 1970, Srila Prabhupada flew from Los Angeles to Tokyo to commence his first round-the-world preaching tour, returning to L.A. in late June 1971, having planted the seeds for ISKCON in Japan, India, Russia, Malaysia, and Australia. For his second round-the-world tour, Prabhupada left Los Angeles on July 16,1971, and now, in late May of 1972, after his heroic success in London, Nairobi, India, Australia, New Zealand, Hong Kong, and Japan, Srila Prabhupada is back in L.A., revving up for his third round-the-world adventure.

He’s met his with his North American ISKCON leaders, organized them, prepared them, and now he’s going to show them how to get things done, starting with Mexico. Armed with fresh cassette tapes, I jump in the front seat of Prabhupada’s car as we race to LAX for our flight to Mexico City—chasing another rhino with the Swami.
